# Sūrah Yūsuf (Chapter 12) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**
**The Sūrah That Is One Whole Story**

Sūrah Yūsuf is the twelfth sūrah of the Qur'an and the only one named after a single prophet in his own unqualified person: *Sūrat Yūsuf*, "The Sūrah of Joseph." It contains 111 verses by the counting of Baṣrah, and it holds a distinction no other chapter of the Qur'an exactly matches: verses 3 through 101 form the longest single continuous narrative in the entire Book. Elsewhere the Qur'an tells its stories in fragments — Moses appears in Exodus-like bursts across a dozen sūrahs, each passage lighting one hour of his life; Abraham is presented in scenes that are not sequenced; Yūsuf alone is given to us as a life, beginning with a boy's dream in a Canaanite tent and ending with an old man's prayer in an Egyptian palace. The sūrah's own opening announces why this matters: the story is not decoration, it is *āyah*, sign (12:1, 12:7).

**Its Place in the Muṣḥaf and in the Chronology of Revelation**

In the ordering of the written copy the sūrah stands immediately after Hūd, the sūrah whose closing verse commands the Prophet to "stand firm as you are commanded" (11:112) — and Yūsuf is that command illustrated in the life of a man who stood firm through slavery, false accusation, and years of forgotten imprisonment. It opens the tenth portion (juzʾ) of the thirty-part division. The classical introductions to the sūrah — al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr among them — transmit a report from ʿĀʾishah that it was sent down in the thirteenth year of the Prophetic mission, in the so-called Year of Sorrow, when Khadījah and Abū Ṭālib died and the Prophet was stoned at Ṭāʾif: a revelation whose reported occasion was, if the report is accepted, grief itself. The chain of that narration is disputed and many hadith masters treat it as weak, so it should be held as edifying tradition rather than settled history — but it is not at all out of keeping with the sūrah's texture to describe it as a chapter of consolation. Its own verse states the plain historical claim that matters more: before this revelation the Prophet ﷺ was "among the heedless" of this story (12:3), with no access to the Jewish scroll that carried it.

**The Disconnected Letters and the Series They Open**

Yūsuf is the third sūrah in a run of five — Yūnus, Hūd, Yūsuf, Ibrāhīm, al-Ḥijr — whose openings begin with *alif lām rāʾ* and then speak of the Book. The reader who meets Yūsuf after Hūd has already heard the challenge of those letters twice. Their ultimate meaning is, as the majority maintain, known to God; their function in the ear of the hearer is nonetheless observable: they stop, they summon attention, and they announce that what follows is recited, not composed. Verse 2 will then state the medium: "an Arabic Qur'an," and verse 3 the gift: "the most beautiful of stories." The sequence is deliberate — letters, then language, then narrative.

**The Ask of the Sūrah's First Hearers**

The commentaries record two occasions of revelation, and both are instructive. According to one report transmitted by ar-Rāzī and others, certain Jewish scholars in Makkah prompted the idolaters to interrogate the Prophet about particulars of Joseph's life — where the treasure was, what became of the shirt, how the years of famine were managed — expecting that a man who could not answer would be exposed as a fabricator. According to a report cited by al-Ṭabarī, the sūrah came instead after the Companions asked the Prophet to tell them a story. Either way the shape of the answer is the same: the request was for information, and what descended was *evidence wrapped in narrative* — a retelling that corrects, compresses, and moralises the received account while never naming the source it corrects. Where Genesis 37–46 is the closest parallel text, the differences are conspicuous and are noted throughout the commentary below: the Qur'an omits the deception of Jacob with the bloody coat's fabrication as an outright forgery by the brothers (it keeps the coat, but Jacob's response is dignified patience rather than being tricked into grief), omits the identification of Judah's sons, and, above all, refuses the slightest excuse for the brothers' crime — while making their eventual repentance real and accepted.

**Seven Movements of a Single Drama**

For orientation, the narrative falls into seven natural acts: the dream and the family's jealousy (4–18); sale, purchase, and upbringing in Egypt (19–25); the attempt at seduction, the torn shirt, and the women's cut hands (26–34); prison, the two fellow inmates, and the king's dream (35–49); exoneration, office, and the seven lean years (50–56); the brothers' arrivals, the cup in the saddlebag, and the collapse of their excuse (57–86); confession, the shirt restored to Jacob's eyes, the family's reunion, and the fulfillment of the first dream (87–100). Verses 101–111 are the sūrah's own epilogue: Joseph's prayer, the Qur'an's claim about its status, and the warning to the hearer. Reading a verse in isolation is therefore always a risk; this commentary marks the echoes forward and back, because in this sūrah the meaning of verse 4 is not visible until verse 100 has been read, and verse 100 would be unintelligible without verse 4.

**Theology Written as Plot**

The sūrah's most distinctive feature is not its characters but its camera angle. Human beings in it scheme, and God in it is repeatedly named as the one whose will quietly overrides the scheme: "Truly my Lord is Latīf (subtle, gentle) over whom He wills" (12:100); "He raises degrees whom He wills, and He is the ʿAzīz, the Ḥakīm" (12:76); "but God is Ḥakīm, ʿAlīm" (12:83); "He brings not to nothing your plot" is the iron promise of 12:100's world, and "indeed, my Lord is Latīf" the experience of it. God's nine-and-ninety-fold work in this chapter is never a miracle that suspends events: a caravan happens to pass, a price happens to be two silver dirhams, a king happens to need an accountant. The sūrah's doctrine of providence is that God writes the plot inside the plots, which is precisely why the chapter can be titled a sign for "the inquiring" (12:7) rather than for the astonished.

**Why It Is Called the Most Beautiful of Stories**

Commentators have long listed the reasons for *aḥsan al-qaṣaṣ* — that it contains prophets and prophets' fathers, chaste love and treacherous love, envy and forgiveness, kingship and slavery, dream and its realization, a torn shirt and a restored one, a young man's temptation and an old man's patience; that its language is exquisitely ordered and its lessons cover both the religion and the worldly affair (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī); that it moves from a dream of prostration to prostration fulfilled, so that its beginning is its end. Al-Qushayrī and, later, al-Maybudī read it as the soul's exile from its Divine Origin and its return; Suhrawardī, in *The Philosophy of Illumination*'s orbit, used its figures — Yūsuf's beauty, Zulaykhā's longing, the jail that was really a refuge — to speak of love as an ontological pull; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Jāmī turned the episode of seduction into a 4,500-couplet Persian romance. This commentary keeps to the exoteric sense for which the chapter itself argues, while noting where the classical interpreters found the verse open to spiritual reading — because the sūrah's own vocabulary (*ḥusn*, *jamāl*, *ʿishq* in 12:30) invites it.

**The Human Material**

Set aside from the miraculous frame, the chapter is a study in ordinary, recognisably modern failures: a father's indiscreet love; siblings comparing their worth in returns; a woman with power and no prospect of accountability; a society that prefers scandal to investigation; an ex-inmate told to forget that he was ever in prison; two men who break a promise; a minister's household that cannot tell whether a man is a spy; brothers who keep their best excuses ready in case the truth is inconvenient. That the sūrah treats all of this without moral panic — and with one of the Qur'an's most precise psychological sentences, "the self is commandingly evil" (12:53) — is why generations of adab and spiritual writers used it as a manual.

**How This Commentary Reads the Source**

The verse-by-verse expansion below is built on the study-notes translation and commentary in `initial/012.md`, whose sigla (Ṭ, Kl, Q, R, Z, My, Bg, Bḍ, IJ, Qu, Aj, N, Ṭs, JJ, I) are the standard shorthand of that annotated edition for al-Ṭabarī, al-Kātibī / al-Kashshāf, al-Qurṭubī, Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, al-Zamakhsharī, al-Marāghī's *al-Manār* commentators, al-Baghawī, al-Bayḍāwī, Ibn al-Jawzī, al-Qushayrī, Abū Muslim al-Iṣfahānī, Nāṣir, and al-Ṭabarī's *Jaamiʿ* traditions. Where the source gives only a cross-reference ("see 2:1c"), that reference has been worked out and its substance carried into the discussion. Qur'anic quotations are given in English with their verse numbers, ḥadīth with their collections and an honest note where graders have judged them weak, and the opinions of the classical commentators are attributed by name so that the reader can tell consensus from a single voice. Arabic words are transliterated and then unpacked as they appear; no prior knowledge of Arabic is assumed. The unit of treatment is the verse: every verse of the sūrah, including the verses that older commentaries grouped together, is handled individually below, so that no verse is traded for the sake of finishing.

**A Final Word on the Reader**

The sūrah ends by addressing two audiences at once: "How many a sign they pass by in the heavens and the earth, turning away from it" (12:105), and "say: this is my way, I call to God upon insight" (12:108). Between those two sentences the chapter asks its reader to decide what kind of person he takes himself to be — one who passes, or one who asks. The commentary that follows is written for the latter.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:1

> **1** *Alif. Lām. Rāʾ. These are the signs of the clear Book.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Door Opened With Letters, Not Words**

The three letters that stand at the head of this sūrah — *alif lām rāʾ* — are the same opening that has just been heard in Yūnus and Hūd, and they recur again at Ibrāhīm 14:1 and al-Ḥijr 15:1. They belong to the "disconnected letters" (*al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿāt*), which appear at the beginning of twenty-nine sūrahs and which the majority of commentators, as our source records, hold to have an ultimate meaning known only to God. It is the position associated with Ibn ʿAbbās, with Mālik in one report, and with al-Shāʿbī and Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah: these belong to the *mutashābih*, the verses whose full sense is reserved, and the correct response is to recite them, believe them to be from God, and leave the weighing.

But the commentators did not stop at reverent silence, and their refusal to stop is itself instructive. Al-Ṭabarī gathers the attempts at meaning and, having gathered them, declines to bind any single one. Ibn Kathīr lists nine or ten of the proposed senses — that they are names of the sūrah, names among God's names, an oath by which God swears, a numerical notation of the years between the hearers and some event, letters whose sound intimidates because the pagans' pride was built on eloquence. Al-Zamakhsharī in al-Kashshāf and Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī both treat the letters as a *taḥaddī*, a challenge compressed to its shortest possible form: your speech and mine are made of the same twenty-eight letters; here is a Book built out of them, and you are invited. Al-Rāzī adds a subtler observation that deserves the reader's attention: whoever reflects on how these few sounds, arranged and re-arranged, generate the whole range of articulable meaning, arrives at a demonstration of the possible poverty of a creature and the possible wealth of a Creator. The letters are the alphabet laid bare, and the Book built from them is then named — in the very next clause — *mubīn*, clarifying.

**Why Silence Is the Correct First Answer**

There is a discipline in not decoding everything. The sūrah's own epistemology, which will be spelled out in verses 111 and the surrounding lines, is that the Qur'an elaborates all things *for a people who believe* (12:111), and that understanding is promised to those who use their reason (12:2). A opening which refuses to be pinned to one meaning trains the reader in the right posture before a text that claims to be from God: attention without presumption. It also protects against a common error in devotional reading, which is to convert a sign into a code. Codes are for people who want to be in the know; signs are for people who want to know. Al-Qushayrī notes that the letters teach the servant that not every opening of God's speech is intended to be entered from the side he prefers.

**"These Are the Signs" — A Demonstrative That Points at Dignity**

*Tilka āyāt* — "these are the verses/signs." The word *āyah* in Arabic means a mark, a token, anything whose visible presence points beyond itself to something else: a milestone, a treaty-sign, a piece of evidence in court. When the Qur'an calls its own verses *āyāt*, it relocates them from the category of "information" into the category of "exhibit." An *āyah* is not consumed when it is understood; it continues pointing. This is why the same word is used in this sūrah for the evidence of the shirt (12:18, 12:26-28), for the signs in the heavens and the earth (12:105), and for the pieces of money in the brothers' saddlebags (12:65) — the vocabulary of this chapter treats providence as a set of exhibits.

The demonstrative *tilka* is the form used for something at a distance, and Arabic rhetoric holds that distance here is not spatial but honorific: "those," meaning those exalted, those far above being treated as ordinary speech. Compare *dhālika al-kitāb* in 2:2, "that Book," where the same distance expresses eminence. God could have said "the signs of a book"; He said "the signs of *the* clear Book," and pointed at it from far off.

**"The Clear Book" — Two Readings and Why Both Are True**

*Al-kitāb al-mubīn* is grammatically ambiguous in a way the commentators noticed and exploited. *Mubīn* is the active participle of *abāna*, "to be or make clear," so strictly it means "the Book that makes things clear." That is the reading of al-Ṭabarī and of Ibn Kathīr here: it clarifies the road, the halal and haram, the stories of the peoples, the names of God; it is "clear" in the sense that a roadbed is clear of obstruction. The source notes the same claim made elsewhere in identical words — "these are the verses of the clear Book" at 26:2 and 28:2, with the near-equivalents at 43:2 and 44:2, where al-Zukhruf says:

> *"Truly We have made it an Arabic Qur'an that haply you may understand."* (Qur'an 43:3)

Other grammarians permit the participle to carry the sense of its passive — *mubān*, that which has been made plain — and some read "the Book made clear," as at 52:2, "and the cleared (*mubīn*) scroll." Both readings land in the same place: clarity is not an accessory property of this Book but the whole manner of its existence. The grammarians allow that a second noun may stand as the attribute of the first, so the phrase may even mean "the Book, the evident," the evidence residing in the Book's own self-evidence, which is how a first reader recognizes any genuine thing. Our source glosses the adjective as "its capacity to render the truth manifest" — a Book which is clear and which clears.

**Or the Preserved Tablet?**

Our source records the alternative preferred by some of the earlier exegetes: that "the clear Book" is the Preserved Tablet (*al-lawḥ al-maḥfūẓ*) mentioned at 85:22 and 56:77-79, "in a hidden Book which none touch save the purified," so that the verse would mean: the signs you are hearing are transcribed there. This is a minority view and the context resists it — the following verse says "We sent *it* down," and a descent implies that which reaches the earth, not that which remains above. But the reading is worth recording for what it teaches about the Qur'an's self-awareness: the recited Book in Makkah and the written Book in heaven are, in the sūrah's imagination, the same document at two addresses. See also Yūnus 10:1, where the same letters are followed by the assertion "that is why your Lord is worshipped," and where the adjective "wise" was taken to indicate that the Book is clear in its own wisdom.

**A Story That Announces Its Credentials First**

Finally, and most usefully for reading the next 110 verses: this sūrah is about to narrate a love story, a fratricide, a false accusation, a prison, and seven years of economic planning in a pagan empire. Nothing in that subject-matter is obviously scripture. So the chapter begins by declaring its warrant. Before it tells anything, it states that what it tells consists of *signs* from a *clarifying* Book, sent down in a language the hearers actually speak (12:2), from a source its narrator could not have consulted (12:3). A modern reader may notice that this is exactly the order of good epistemic practice: identify the provenance of a claim, then weigh its content. The sūrah refuses to let its beauty be received as mere beauty. Verse 3 will offer "the most beautiful of stories," but only after verse 1 has refused the reader the option of treating the story as a story.

**Practical Reach for the Reader**

The verse is also a small lesson in how to begin. The Prophet ﷺ taught the Companions to preface every weighty matter with the Name of God and with affirmation that guidance comes from Him; the sūrah's opening performs the same. Al-Ḥakīm al-Tirmidhī, in *Khātam al-Awliyāʾ*, makes a further observation on this verse that later spiritual writers repeat: that God opened this sūrah with letters so that the servant learns to begin his own affair with the acknowledgment of need — the letters are, as it were, God taking the reader's hand at the threshold, since a hand can be taken only by someone who first stops talking. The verse, at eleven words in Arabic, thus functions as a covenant of attention between the Book and its reader: what follows is evidence, not entertainment; and the reader who treats it otherwise will get from the story of Yūsuf exactly what a reader gets from a newspaper item about a minister's household — nothing that outlives the sitting.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:2

> **2** *Truly We sent it down as an Arabic Qur'an, that haply you may understand.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Royal "We" and the Verb of Descent**

*Innā anzalnāhu Qur'ānan ʿarabiyyan* — "It is We who sent it down as a Qur'an, Arabic." The first thing to register in the Arabic is the verb's form. The Qur'an uses two words for its own coming: *anzala*, sent down as a unit, and *nazzala*, sent down in repeated instalments. Here the simple form is used, and the sūrah's own narrative will show why both are true of the same Book. The gradual form carries the Qur'an's answer to the objection of the pagans in al-Furqān:

> *"And those who disbelieve say: 'Why has not the Qur'an been sent down upon him all at once?' Thus, so that We may make it firm thereby upon your heart, and We have recited it in a [due] recitation."* (Qur'an 25:32)

The revelation came in pieces across twenty-three years, arriving in response to questions, consolations, battles, and domestic cases; yet in its totality it is one descent, and this sūrah — one of the earliest, delivered in a single breath to a single grieving audience in Makkah — is a specimen of that totality. The plural "We" in divine speech is the plural of majesty and of the angelic mediation that carries the message, as at 26:192-194, "Truly it is a descent (*tanzīl*) from the Lord of the worlds, which the Trusted Spirit has brought down upon your heart." Our source's note that "sent it down" refers to God's sending, not to Moses' or any human narrator's, matters because in this chapter the same verb will appear again at 12:21 in a wholly worldly register — "he who bought him from Egypt" — and the echo is intentional: the one who sends down the Book is the one who places Yūsuf in the house of the minister.

**What "Qur'an" Means as a Name**

*Qur'ānan* is a verbal noun from *qara'a*, "to recite, to read aloud," and its form *fuʿʿāl* carries intensive and frequent sense: it is "recitation" in the mode of "that which is endlessly recited." A view transmitted from al-Shāfiʿī links the word instead to *al-qarīnatān*, "the two companions," on the ground that the sūrahs accompany one another; and others, in the etymological literature of Abū ʿUbaydah and Ibn Fāris, derive it from *al-qarʾ*, "gathering," since the Book gathers up the scriptures before it and the sciences within it. All these etymologies converge on one fact: the Book is made to be recited, and what is recited is retained. This is not a trivial philological point in a sūrah whose entire transmission was oral. A written document can be lost; the *Qur'ān*, being the thing recited, is preserved in the very mode in which it exists, and this sūrah will itself make that claim at verse 2: the story is "recounted" by God — nuqīṣṣu — to a man who cannot read it.

**"Arabic" — Language as an Act of Consideration**

*Lisanan ʿarabiyyan mubīnan*, "in a clear Arabic tongue" (26:195). Our source points to the four parallel assertions — 20:113, "and We have sent it down as an Arabic Qur'an," 39:28, "an Arabic Qur'an without crookedness," 41:3, "a Book whose verses are detailed, an Arabic Qur'an for a people who know," and 43:3 — and the variation in the companions of the word is the point: in each case "Arabic" is joined to whatever the hearers needed to hear. In al-Zukhruf the phrase is "Arabic Qur'an" against those who would have preferred a Book in another language; in Fuṣṣilat it is paired with "detailed," because detail demands a language capable of bearing it.

The earliest exegetes gloss *ʿarabiyyan* as "in the language of the Arabs," and a saying transmitted from Mālik ibn Anas derives a rule of law from the verse: "It is only the language of the Arabs that is meant here; so whoever does not understand their speech does not understand the Book of God." This is the ground of the whole science of *fiqh al-lugha*, and it is why, down to the present day, the jurists insist that the syntactic habits of 7th-century Arabs — how they used the accusative for emphasis, how they left a verb unexpressed, how they used a plural for a dignified singular — are legally binding data, not decorative lore.

**But Why This Language? The Logical Question**

Verse 2 seems simple — the Book is in Arabic, so you can understand it. The question a reflective reader will ask is the one al-Rāzī asks on behalf of an objector: if the Book is for all mankind, why is it for one people's tongue? The answer that emerges from the sūrah's own practice is in three parts.

First, intelligibility has to be actual, not theoretical. God could have sent a language that every nation half-understood and none fully; He chose one in which the address could be exact — where a single case-vowel shifts "the servant of Zayd" into "Zayd's servant," where the difference between *faʿala* and *fāʿala* fixes whether an action was done once, repeatedly, or reciprocally, and where a whole vocabulary of inner states — *khushūʿ*, *wajal*, *hashya*, *raʾbah*, *rahmah*, *shafaqah* — distinguishes reverence that includes fear from fear that includes love. A Book that legislates, console, and diagnoses the soul needs that precision, and it had it in the language of the man who was to carry it.

Second, the choice was strategic in the world as it was. The Arabs held the trade corridors between three empires, had no established priestly class that could monopoly the text, and a market culture in which poetry was mass media. A Book that became Arabic became, through the very networks that carried cloth and incense, translatable and portable.

Third — and this is the sūrah's own logic — a determinate source text is a necessary condition of translation. What is vague can be copied but not translated; what is precise can be rendered, checked, and re-rendered. Every English, Turkish, Malay, or Hausa reader of this commentary, including the reader of these pages, is downstream of *bi-lisāni qaumihi*, "in the tongue of his own people" (14:4):

> *"And We sent not any messenger except in the tongue of his people, that he might make it clear to them."* (Qur'an 14:4)

**"That Haply You May Understand" — The Purpose Clause**

*Laʿalla-tuʿqilūn*, "so that you would use your reason." The particle *laʿalla* in Arabic expresses expectation and hope, and our source's translators render it "haply," which is exactly right for the human side of it and was felt by grammarians to require comment when the Agent is God. Al-Zamakhsharī states the standard answer plainly: *laʿalla* here is *taḥqīq*, "assurance" — when God says "so that perhaps," the "perhaps" belongs to the creature's experience of hope, not to the Divine uncertainty. The servant hopes; the Lord intends. It is not possible for God to hope for what He has decided, but it is merciful for Him to let the servant hope, since hope is what keeps a person in the room with a text he has not yet mastered.

The verb chosen is *taʿqilūn*, from *ʿaql*. Its root meaning is "to bind": the *ʿiqāl* is the cord that hobbles a camel. To be *ʿāqil* in Arabic is not primarily to be clever but to be one who holds himself in, who ties down his own impulse so that it does not bolt with him. That is why the same word means both "reason" and "the pledge given to a killer's heirs," and why the jurists called a person of mature responsibility *ʿāqil bāligh*. The purpose of this Arabic Book, then, is not that you decode it but that you be restrained by it. The connection to 12:1 is immediate: the letters that cannot be decoded are given to a mind that must use them as reins.

**Understanding Is the Command, Not the Reward**

Al-Marāghī and the *Manār* school draw from this verse a rule for how a Muslim should relate to the text: recitation that does not reach *taʿaqqul* does not achieve the verse's stated aim, and the sūrah does not say "that you may recite it" — it says "that you may understand." They note that a people who recite fluently and act on nothing have inverted the order of the verse, and that the revival of a community begins where this verse says the revelation begins: with comprehension in the reader's own language. Hence the legitimacy — indeed the obligation — of translation, commentary, and teaching in every tongue, of which the very book this commentary sits inside is an instance.

**A Reader's Reassurance and a Warning**

Two last things the verse gives. It reassures the non-Arab: you are not excluded by your ignorance of a seventh-century dialect; the purpose clause is addressed to "you," whoever you are, and the entire science of *tafsīr* exists because the *ʿarabiyy* is a medium, not a barrier — God sent it down to be understood, not to be admired. And it warns the Arab: his native fluency is a privilege of access, not a badge of standing, and this sūrah's own audience in Makkah were native speakers who "passed by" its signs turning away (12:105). Language gets you to the door; *ʿaql*, the binding of the self to what is known, is what walks you through it.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:3

> **3** *We recount unto thee the most beautiful of stories by Our having revealed unto thee this Quran, though before it thou wert among the heedless.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verb Chosen From the Root of Tracking**

*Nuqīṣṣu ʿalayka* — "We recount to you." The verb is from *qaṣṣa*, whose primary sense is to follow a footprint, to track: in Ṭā-Hā, Moses is told to cast down his staff and "fear no consequence (*qaṣaṣ*),"; and in al-Qaṣaṣ, when Moses' mother tells his sister to go after him, the word is exactly this — *"follow (uqṣuṣihī) him"* (28:11). *Qiṣṣaṣ* is therefore not "storytelling" in the sense of inventing an entertaining sequence; it is retracing, step by step, an account whose tracks already exist. Al-Zamakhsharī notes that God chose the word that implies following, not the word (*ḥakā*, *dhakara*) that implies describing; and the choice fits a sūrah which begins with an assertion about a Book that "clarifies," because a track is what you follow when you want to arrive somewhere and not merely to be amused.

The preposition *ʿalā* — "upon you," "to you" — is also deliberate. Arabic uses *qaṣṣa ʿalā* when the recounting is authoritative and complete, an account laid out before someone in full, and *li-* for a report merely delivered. Ibn al-Qayyim, in *al-Fawā'id*, observes that God's *qiṣṣaṣ* upon the Prophet is accompanied by the phrase *bi-māūḥaynā* — "by what We reveal to you" — so the source of the account is revelation and its manner is *tafsīl*, unfolding: the account does not come to him as rumour, it comes as the very act of revealing.

**"The Most Beautiful of Stories" — Superlative in What Sense?**

*Aḥsan al-qaṣaṣ*. The partitive construction *aḥsan + al-qaṣaṣ* is the same shape as 39:23, which our source immediately cites:

> *"God has sent down the most beautiful of discourse (aḥsan al-ḥadīth), a Book consistent, reiterate in its likenesses."* (Qur'an 39:23)

The sūrah therefore claims a superlative twice, once for the whole Book and once for this episode inside the Book, and the relation between them is that this story is a specimen of the Book's beauty, not a rival to it. Commentators have enumerated four senses in which it is *aḥsan*.

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī take the beauty to be of content: the story contains in concentrated form everything a human life needs — prophethood and fatherhood, temptation and chastity, envy and forgiveness, kingship and slavery, a father's grief and a son's patience, and it ends in faith rather than in tragedy; other stories amuse and end, this one instructs and continues. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī, in a discussion that is among his finest, lists this first and then adds a second sense: that *aḥsan* refers to the beauty of its *words* — the language, the sequence, the restraint, the way one scene hands off to the next without the reader losing the thread, so that a Makkan audience whose pride was poetry was given a narrative whose craft they could not match. A third reading, transmitted from al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī and others in the early commentaries, is that it is the most beautiful because its outcome is beautiful for its people: what looked at every stage like disaster was, in the end, a settling of a family and the saving of a region. A fourth, cited by al-Qushayrī and later Sufi readers, is that it is beautiful because it is a story of return — exile, separation, and reunion — which is the true shape of every soul's history.

None of these four is exclusive, and the sūrah's own vocabulary supports all of them, since *ḥusn* in this chapter keeps shifting between the beauty of a face (12:31), a word (12:78, "my father, do not be distressed"), a patience (12:18, *ṣabrun jamīl*, and 12:83, *fa-ṣabrun jamīl*), and an outcome (12:90, "whoever is mindful and patient — truly God does not lose the wage of the beautiful-doers").

**"Aḥsan," Not "Ḥasan" — Why Comparison Is Not the Point**

A subtle observation from al-Rāzī is worth the reader's attention: the verse does not say this story is *good* and other stories bad, but that it is the most beautiful of stories, which concedes that there are other *qaṣaṣ* in the Qur'an and that the genre itself is legitimate. The Book does not disdain narrative; it competes in it. This is why Muslims produced histories, *Qiṣaṣ al-Anbiyāʾ* literature, and moral romance for a thousand years under the banner of this verse rather than as a departure from it — and why the *Qiṣaṣ al-Anbiyāʾ* attributed to Ibn Kathīr bears the sūrah's own title. The superlative is an invitation to read all stories comparatively and to ask of any story, including a story told about oneself, whether its beauty is in its craft, its outcome, or its truth.

**"Though Before It Thou Wert Among the Heedless"**

*In kunta min ghāfilīn*. Two readings compete in the commentators and both are recorded in our source: that the Prophet ﷺ had no prior knowledge of this story, and therefore could not have composed it (this is al-Kalbī's and most modern readings), or that he was, before revelation, in the general state of his people's heedlessness, which 42:52 names: "and you did not know what is the Book, nor what is faith." The first is the sense preferred by the commentators our source cites, because the second would require the unusual claim that the Prophet was blameworthy in a way that the verse would not then resolve; and the parallel verses are decisive for the first reading:

> *"That is of the news of the unseen which We reveal to you. You were not present beside them when they cast their pens [to see] which of them would be the guardian of Mary, and you were not present among them when they disputed."* (Qur'an 3:44)

> *"And you were not at the side of the western slope when We recited to Moses the story, but you are a mercy from your Lord, that you may warn a people to whom no warner came before you, that haply they may mind."* (Qur'an 28:86)

The argument from these three verses — 3:44, 28:86, and 12:3 — is a logical one of the strongest kind. The Qur'an repeatedly grounds the Prophet's credibility in his *lack* of access, which is a claim that can be checked against his biography, and in this case the check is unusually simple: the story of Yūsuf was not in the Arab market of tales. The details it supplies — the mechanics of the Egyptian storehouse, the stratagem of the cup in the brother's saddlebag, the particular form of a ruler's dream and its seven-plus-one structure, the practice of a king's household with the phrase "O chief minister, truly your children are a group of thieves" (12:78), the custom of taking a pledge "as you took your pledge from your brother" (12:66) — belong to no Arabian repertory. Whoever "recounts it upon him" either had access to the People of the Book, which the whole community of Makkah knew he had not, or he was told.

**The Word "Ghāfil" and Its Lesson for Us**

The root *gh-f-l* means to leave a thing unattended, as one leaves a garment lying and it gathers dust; *ghaflah* is not ignorance but inattention — the knowledge is there or available and the mind is elsewhere. That is why it is the Qur'an's favoured word for the failure that ruins people: 21:1, *"draws nearer to the people their reckoning while they are in heedlessness, turning away"*; 7:179, hearts that do not understand, eyes that do not see, ears that do not hear — "those are like cattle, rather they are more astray" (7:179). And on 30:55: *"the day the Hour arrives, the criminals will swear they remained but an hour"* — because a life spent in *ghaflah* compresses, at the end, into a single regretful instant of elapsed time. The law itself takes account of the state: the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The pen is lifted from three: from the sleeper until he wakes, from the child until he reaches puberty, and from the one whose mind is taken until he recovers."* (Abū Dāwūd 4403; al-Tirmidhī 1400; al-Nasāʾī 3432; Ibn Mājah 2041)

Accountability attaches where attention was possible — which is why "you were among the heedless" is a statement about a man before his commissioning, not after it, and why the same verse functions as a warning to anyone who possesses the Book and does not attend to it.

Ibn ʿAbbās's much-cited counsel that this sūrah was revealed as consolation to the Prophet in his grief at Makkah — the report in al-Ḥākim's *al-Mustadrak* and elsewhere, "the most excellent of stories" and the tradition in Aḥmad: *"Tell of the prophets, and it is no harm if you narrate from the Children of Israel"* (Aḥmad, Musnad; graded variously) — belongs here too: the sūrah consoles *because* it demonstrates. A man told that his own suffering, like his namesake's, is a plot device in the hand of the Latīf, is consoled by knowledge, not by sentiment.

**Why the Verse Is Placed Where It Is**

Notice the order. Verse 1: the Book is a set of signs and it clarifies. Verse 2: it has come down in your language so that reason can bind you to it. Verse 3: and here, now, is a story — the most beautiful of them — and its provenance is God, because its narrator could not have had it. Only after that does the narrative begin (verse 4: *"idh qāla Yūsuf"*), in the abrupt, unframed way of a story whose teller does not need to introduce himself. A hearer who has accepted 1-3 will accept the story as evidence; a hearer who has not will hear a charming tale and, as verse 105 says, pass by it "turning away." The sūrah's structure is thus an argument in three premises followed by a demonstration — and the demonstration is, uniquely in the Qur'an, continuous enough to be checked: eleven verses of dreams and betrayal, ten of Egypt, five of the torn shirt, and one old man's eyes restored by the smell of a shirt, with the whole arc closing, at verse 100, on the prostration of verse 4.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:4

> **4** *[Remember] when Joseph said unto his father, “O my father, truly I have seen eleven stars, and the sun and the moon. I saw them prostrating before me.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Abrupt Opening for an Old Story**

The verse begins with *idh*, "when," without a verb governing it — "when Joseph said" — because the sūrah's audience was expected to fill in "recall," "mention," or take the sentence as continuing directly from verse 3's "We recount to you." This is a technique the Qur'an uses often and it is worth noticing: the narrative does not set a scene, it drops the reader into a conversation already in progress. The commentators on this verse, from al-Ṭabarī onward, supply what the text withholds — that Joseph was a boy of about seventeen according to most transmitted reports, that his father was Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm, all three of them prophets, as our source notes by pointing to the Abrahamic chain at 6:84 — and the point of the omission is that the chapter is not about his childhood. It is about what a promise does to a family.

**"Yā Abati" — A Son's Grammar**

The address is *yā abati*, "O my father," with the first-person suffix in its softened form. In this sūrah the phrase occurs eleven times on the tongues of Yūsuf and Banyāmīn, while the brothers say *yā abānā*, "O our father." The difference is not accidental and the classical stylists made much of it: the singular possessive is a child's word for a parent, intimate and pleading, while the plural is the word of men presenting a family position. Abū Ḥayyān in *al-Baḥr al-Muḥīṭ* and al-Sharīf al-Raḍī in *al-Khaṣāʾiṣ* both note the effect — you hear, in *yā abati*, the voice of someone who still expects comfort from the man he is addressing, and you hear in *yā abānā* the voice of men who expect a decision. This one grammatical fact does more to prepare the reader for verse 16 and verse 84 than any number of adjectives could.

**"I Saw" — The Vocabulary of a True Dream**

*Innī raʾaytu* — "I saw," with the intensive *inna* to underline the reality of it. In Arabic, the language of dreams is sharply divided. *Ru'yā* is used for a vision that is true, and the Arabs do not say *raʼaytu ru'yā* for a fantasy; for the meaningless or disturbed dream the word is *ḥulm*, and the Prophet ﷺ fixed the usage:

> *"The true vision (ru'yā) is from God, and the ḥulm is from Satan; so if one of you sees something he dislikes, let him spit to his left and seek refuge with God from its evil, for it will not harm him."* (Bukhārī 7043; Muslim 2262)

> *"When the End Times draw near, the believer's dream will hardly ever be false; and the truest of dreams is that seen in the last part of the night."* (Aḥmad's Musnad; al-Ḥākim graded it ṣaḥīḥ, and al-Dhahabī and others noted weakness in its chain — it should be cited as a widely transmitted report, not a proof-text)

The boy has used the verb of sight in the language of veridical vision, which is why the commentators open their treatment of this verse with the categories of dreams, and why our source records the accepted view that this was a true dream, with the additional scholastic note that the dreams of prophets are a form of revelation, while others place the beginning of Yūsuf's prophethood later, at 12:15's "We inspired you," so that this vision would be a portent of an office rather than its possession. The distinction matters for the sūrah's theology of childhood: Yūsuf is not shown receiving law, only being shown his future.

**Eleven Stars, a Sun, a Moon**

The symbols are astronomical, and the interpretation is not given by God in the verse — it is given by the family at the end of the sūrah, when the parents and eleven brothers "enter upon Yūsuf and they prostrate" (12:100). The eleven stars are, on the near-unanimous reading of our source's authorities (al-Baghawī, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī, al-Ṭabarsī), the eleven brothers; the sun and moon are the father and mother in one order or the other, with the commentators differing on which is which. The brothers' mother is identified in the transmitted genealogies as Rāḥīl (Rachel), and Jacob's other wives as the mothers of the rest — a family structure the sūrah itself makes visible when it calls Banyāmīn "his brother" (12:8) and never once gives him a separate mother's name.

Al-Rāzī adds an observation that is beautiful and precise: the word for star, *kawkab*, is from *kawkaba*, "to set, to go down," as a star sinks in the west — so a reader is being told, in the very nouns, that these "stars" are destined for descent and eclipse before their rising. The image is not of fixed points of light but of things that set. And since the brightest lights in the dream are set in motion toward the smallest figure in it, the dream's real content is not honour but disturbance: the family order is about to be overturned.

**Why Prostration Reads as a Threat**

Jacob does not congratulate him. Our source preserves the classical explanation: a created being does not prostrate to another created being, so prostration seen in a dream signals that the one prostrated to will be tried (as our source records, comparing Genesis 37:10, where the father rebukes him outright). The paradigm is Adam, to whom the angels prostrated by God's command (2:34) and who was immediately sent down; "for the fall of Adam see 2:35," says our source, and the reference is the key to the whole chapter. Exaltation in this Book is always preceded by exposure. Yūsuf will be lifted into the minister's house and out of it into a cell, lifted from the cell to the treasury and out of it into his father's eyesight, and each ascent is preceded by a stripping.

A second, complementary reading is given by al-Ṭabarī from several early exegetes: that prostration here was a form of honourable greeting lawful in their dispensation, as the angels' bow was for Adam, and thus not sinful but astonishing. Al-Saʿdī harmonises: Jacob feared that the brothers would understand it as a claim of superiority and act on it. Both readings yield the same fatherly conclusion — a dream that announces a rank invites attack — and Jacob acts on the more dangerous one.

**The Prophet ﷺ and the Dream That Begins a Sūrah**

Two things in the Sunnah belong here because they explain why the text bothers to report a boy's dream at all. The first is the status of the true dream in the life of the community after revelation ended. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"There remains none of prophethood except the good news (al-mubashshirāt)." They asked: "And what are the good news?" He said: "The true vision."* (Aḥmad's Musnad; al-Ḥākim in al-Mustadrak; its chain contains al-Sudāy, whom the judges of ḥadīth criticised — it is adduced for its meaning, which the sound reports in Bukhārī and Muslim support)

The second is that the Prophet ﷺ's own mission began with a vision: the arrival of the angel in Ḥirāʾ, and — as the sīrah literature records, with the chain analysed by Ibn Ḥajar in *Fatḥ al-Bārī* at the opening of his book — Khadījah's prior dream of her own reassurance, and the true dream which "fell upon him like the break of dawn." Our source's note that Yūsuf's vision was "as was the case with the Prophet Muḥammad" is exactly this parallel: a dream that is not yet a commission, given to prepare the dreamer.

**A Family System in One Sentence**

Finally, this verse is a study in what disclosure does. Yūsuf tells what he has seen to the person he trusts, and the verse never says he was wrong to; it says his father told him what to do with it next (12:5). The psychology of the sūrah is precise about this: the danger is not the dream but the audience. Envy, which the sūrah will expose in verses 8 and 15 as a self-justifying force, is nourished on information. A household that has heard of a child's exaltation will begin to test the prophecy by trying to falsify it — and here, the eleven stars respond by trying to make the boy disappear, which is exactly how the dream comes true. Every stage of Yūsuf's elevation is set in motion by somebody's attempt to prevent it, a structural irony that al-Qurṭubī flags at verse 100 and that the reader should keep in mind now, at the beginning: the well is the road to the palace; the accusation is the road to the treasury; the famine is the road to the family's reunion. Whoever tells a family its future must know whether the family is large enough to bear it. Yūsuf's was not — and God's plan did not require that it be.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:5

> **5** *He said, “O my son! Recount not thy vision to thy brothers, lest they devise some scheme against thee. Surely Satan is a manifest enemy unto man.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Yā Bunayya" — Diminutive as tenderness**

Jacob's reply begins *qāla yā bunayya*, "O my little son," the vocative with a diminutive of endearment. The Qur'an uses this form only in the mouths of fathers addressing pious sons: here, and at Luqmān's teaching scene — *"When Luqmān said to his son, while he was advising him: 'O my little son, do not associate anything with God'"* (31:13) — and at Abraham's address to the son he is commanded to sacrifice (37:102). Al-Zamakhsharī notes the rhetorical economy: a father who needs to forbid uses the affectionate diminutive first, so that the prohibition arrives wrapped in love. This is the same father who will be called back to the diminutive in verse 80, when he says *yā bunayya* about the last surviving son and then cannot contain the grief he is trying to manage. The word tells us that Jacob's counsel is not strategy detached from feeling; it is feeling disciplined into strategy.

**"Do Not Recount" — the Same Root as the Sūrah's Title-Claim**

*Lā taqṣuṣ ʿalayhim ru'yāka*. The verb is the very one the reader met two verses ago, in God's mouth: *nuqīṣṣu ʿalayka aḥsan al-qaṣaṣ* (12:3). The correspondence is unmistakable, and the commentators — al-Biqāʿī in *Naẓm al-Durar*, who built his entire reading of the Qur'an's coherence on such symmetries — draws out its lesson. God recounts the story to a prophet as a mercy; the father forbids the boy to recount it to his brothers because the same act, in the same words, with a hostile audience, is a hazard. The content of a revelation, a gift, or a piece of good news does not determine its effect; the hearer's state does. Yūsuf's account would have been true, and it would have been dangerous. The verse therefore teaches a discipline that the Islamic tradition called *kitmān al-niʿma* at times and *ḥifẓ al-sirr* at others: the concealment of what invites envy until it can be safely borne.

The Prophetic reports on this theme must be reported honestly. Widely cited in the adab literature is:

> *"Seek help in fulfilling your needs by keeping them secret, then trim the wings of each one [i.e. do not let the news fly]."* (al-Ḥakīm al-Tirmidhī, *Nawādir al-Uṣūl*, and cited by al-Suyūṭī — a report with a weak chain, quoted by scholars of conduct, not relied upon as law)

But the sound material teaches the same ethic without needing that chain: the Prophet ﷺ forbade the *tamannī*, the wishful announcing of what one does not possess (57:23 condemns pride in what has not yet come), and he said:

> *"How excellent for some of you to conceal his deed of good, just as he conceals his deed of evil."* (al-Ḥākim, *al-Mustadrak*, from Anas; graded differently by later judges)

More directly relevant is the counsel the same father gives in the same sūrah, which is a policy of not stacking the family's vulnerability in one place: *"O my sons, enter not by a single gate, but enter by separate gates"* (12:67). Verse 5 and verse 67 are one mind: Jacob is a man who reduces exposure. The verse's second half adds the reason he cannot control — the existence of a competitor for the loyalty of hearts.

**"Lest They Devises a Scheme" — Kayd and Its Mechanism**

*Fa-yakīdū laka kaydan* — "so they would devise against you a scheme," with the *fa* of consequence and the preposition *li* ("for you") standing in the sense of *ʿalā* ("against"), a usage Arabic permits with verbs of harm. The word *kayd* is used in the Qur'an for the kind of action that works by indirection — pulling the ground out, arranging circumstances so that a result appears to have happened by itself. Its near-synonym *makr* is used for what is planned; *kayd* for what is *engineered*, and the pairing in this sūrah is deliberate: verse 9 shows the brothers' plan's frankness ("slay Yūsuf"), verse 33 shows the women's *makr* (the pressure of a social situation), and verse 76 shows the stratagem with the cup, which the sūrah itself describes in God's own register: *"thus We devised (nikīdu) for Yūsuf"* — the divine *kayd* that defeats human *kayd*, with the crucial qualification attached in the same sentence: *"except that God wills."* The classical question, whether God can be described as a *mākidaḥ* or *kāʾid*, is answered in the tradition by distinguishing retribution from deception: al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr on 3:54, *"they devised, and God devised, and God is the best of devisers,"* both explain that God's *makr* is the exposure and defeat of a plan by letting it run into the consequences it was designed to avoid — never the concealment of truth from one entitled to it. The brothers, then, are not warned about a possible enemy's cleverness but about the mechanism they are already using: the man who is going to hurt you will not usually announce that he is hurting you; he will arrange the world so that your hurt looks like an accident.

**"Surely Satan Is a Manifest Enemy to Man"**

*Inna al-shayṭāna li-l-insāni ʿaduwwun mubīn*. Our source flags the formula's appearance at 2:168 and 2:208, 6:142, and 7:22, and each of those contexts is instructive. In 2:168 it is attached to eating — "do not follow the footsteps of Satan" — and there the enmity is precisely that Satan's suggestions arrive disguised as appetite; in 7:22 it is the whisper to Adam about the tree and the exposure of nakedness, i.e. the temptation that promises adornment and delivers shame. Both are relevant here, because in verses 23-33 the same enemy will work through desire, and in verses 8-18 he works through envy; in each case the mechanism is to make sin look like a remedy.

The adjective *mubīn* is grammatically active, so "clearly evident," and the commentators point out the asymmetry that a *manifest* enemy is a strange piece of news — everyone would say their enemy is hidden. The answer of al-Bayḍāwī and of al-Rāzī: his enmity is evident in its *outcome*, not in its *presentation*. That is what makes him dangerous and what makes the word a warning rather than a piece of information. The enemy you cannot see is not the threat; the enemy whose offers you cannot distinguish from offers of your own well-being is. Jacob, quoting this general rule over a particular boy, is teaching a method: when a person close to you begins to speak in terms that flatter and alarm at once, the sūrah says, name the third voice in the room.

**Why This Is Called "the First of Yūsuf's Trials"**

Ibn ʿĀshūr notes that the trial begun in this pair of verses is not the well but the *telling*: the earliest hardship the chapter records for Yūsuf is that his gift made him a target inside his own family, and the sūrah will repeat that pattern at each elevation — the minister's household, the women of the city, the king's dream. The principle, drawn out repeatedly by the commentators on this verse, is that a talent is socially destabilising before it is personally useful, and that a person who has just discovered his own gift has not yet learned the first skill it requires, which is discretion about it. A modern reader can see the mechanism in any family or office where a promotion has just been announced: the trouble is rarely the promotion, it is the promotion having become a topic. The verse tells a sixteen-year-old, in effect, that the news will be received by eleven people who have been measuring his worth against theirs since he could walk, and that they will not weigh the news, they will act on it.

**What the Verse Leaves Out on Purpose**

The text does not say that Jacob hid the dream, nor how long he kept it. That silence is functional: the sūrah is not a chronicle. It leaves to the reader the ordinary and very serious question of how to handle a promise made over a child, and it gives one clear answer to a more urgent question — what to do with a blessing you have been given and cannot yet defend. Not to suppress it; Yūsuf loses nothing of what he was shown. Not to distribute it indiscriminately. Hold it, work on it, and wait for an audience that can afford to be pleased for you. That is the whole ethic of verses 4-5 read as a unit, and it explains why the sūrah, which in verse 12:100 will show the dream fulfilled in front of the same eleven men, never lets the brothers be the ones who decide what Yūsuf's life will be.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:6

> **6** *Thus shall thy Lord choose thee, and teach thee the interpretation of events, and complete His Blessing upon thee, and upon the House of Jacob, just as He completed it upon thy forefathers, Abraham and Isaac. Truly thy Lord is Knowing, Wise.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Thus" — the Promise Tied to the Vision**

*Kadhālika yajtabīka rabbuka*. *Kadhālika* means "in this manner," and it reaches back to the dream in verse 4 as a cause is attached to its effect: just as your Lord has shown you this, so He will do these things. The word is important because it makes the vision the grounds of the promise rather than a mysterious ornament; Yūsuf's future is not inferred from astrology or from his father's hopes, it is derived from what God has already done in showing him. Al-Rāzī observes that the sūrah thus places revelation and election in a single sequence, and that it is God who is the subject of every verb in the verse — "your Lord will choose you, and will teach you, and will complete." Not a word in this promise depends on the brothers, the father, the caravan, or the market, and the whole chapter will keep that structure: men will act, and the verbs of the verse will still be God's.

**"Choose" — What Ijtibāʾ Means**

*Yajtabīka* is from *jubāyah*, "to collect, to pick out," the verb of selecting the best portion and leaving the rest: the same root gives *jibāyah*, the collection of tax, and *mujtadā*, one elected. The Qur'an uses it of Adam (3:33), of Abraham, and of Mary, whom God "chose above the women of the worlds" (3:42). The word carries no sense of favouritism; in every usage the elected one is given a task, not a privilege. Our source's note (al-Rāzī) spells this out: "just as God had chosen Yūsuf to have such an exalted vision, so too would He choose him to accomplish great tasks," and for al-Bayḍāwī and al-Qurṭubī that means specifically prophethood. It is a small but consequential doctrine of the verse that the boy's dream is not a promise of career but of commission; and the sūrah will, when it needs to describe the office, use not "king" but "God chooses whom He wills of His servants" (12:37, 12:40).

**"The Interpretation of Events" — Ta'wīl al-Aḥādīth**

*Yulimmuka ta'wīl al-aḥādīth*. Three words deserve unpacking.

*Yuʿallimu* is "teaching" in the mode of *taʿlīm*, with the intensive form indicating repeated, orderly instruction. That a prophet's gift should be described as a *taught* skill — not a flash of insight — is, as Ibn ʿĀshūr points out, a general rule of the Qur'anic epistemology: knowledge is acquired by being instructed, even for prophets, and 96:5 states the principle in the very first revelation: "taught man what he knew not."

*Ta'wīl* is from *āla*, "to return," and means the end-point to which a thing returns, hence the true outcome of a matter, and hence the correct interpretation of a speech-act or a dream. The Qur'an itself defines the field in a verse our source cites for the wider sense (3:7): the *muḥkam* verses are "the mother of the Book," and "none knows its *ta'wīl* except God" — according to the reading that stops the sentence there; while "those firmly rooted in knowledge" say "we believe in it." So *ta'wīl* is not arbitrary allegory: it is the disciplined search for the meaning to which the text returns, and the verse here pairs it with *taʿbīr*, which was the near-synonym used in the Prophet's own time for dream-reading (as in the well-known report on the *ru'yā* being interpreted in accordance with the interpreter's standing — reported by Ibn Mājah 3905 and Aḥmad from Thawbān, a chain which the tradition's judges regard as weak but which circulates precisely in the literature on *taʿbīr*).

*Al-aḥādīth* is plural of *ḥadīth*, "a new, an account, an event." The phrase thus means "the interpretation of accounts," and the sūrah itself proves the range of the gift: Yūsuf interprets the two prisoners' dreams inside a cell (12:36-41), the king's composite dream about the seven fat and lean cows (12:43-49), and the brothers' case of the cup (12:76-79), and the sūrah closes his career with the word in the exact phrase he uses for his own childhood dream: *ta'wīl ru'yāya* — "the interpretation of my vision" (12:100). Our source notes all the sites (vv. 21, 44, 47-49), and this is the single best demonstration of the sūrah's method: it defines a gift by showing it working four times, and then has the recipient thank God for it by name at the end.

**Traditional Psychology: Whose Dream Is It?**

Our source records the doctrine of discernment (al-Bayḍāwī and the *ḥadīth* sciences): true dreams come from an angelic source, false ones from the *nafs* or from a diabolic one. The standard classification in the Sunnah is the three-part one transmitted from Abū Hurayrah:

> *"The vision is three: a glad tiding from God, a discouragement from Satan, and a dream from a man's own self; so when you see something you dislike, do not tell it to anyone, and stand and pray."* (Muslim 2263; the same threefold division is given in the Book of Dreams of Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī)

The discipline this creates is not superstition but self-examination: before acting on a stirring "vision," the tradition asks the claimant to establish its source — whether it flatters, frightens, or arose from the day's business. Al-Ghazālī, in the *Iḥyāʾ*'s book on sleep and waking, makes the same test a moral one, since a true dream is, in his account, a byproduct of a heart gone quiet; and this explains the placement of the gift here, in a sūrah whose hero is repeatedly described in terms of inward composure — *ṣabr*, *taqwā*, *iḥsān*. One is taught *ta'wīl* not because one is clever but because one has been made still, and Yūsuf's stillness is proven at each of the tests that follow.

**"And Complete His Blessing upon You and upon the House of Jacob"**

*Wa yutimma niʿmatahu ʿalayka wa ʿalā āli Yaʿqūba*. Two classical readings are preserved in our source and both are needed.

The first (al-Bayḍāwī, al-Rāzī) takes *niʿma* as prophethood: as it was completed on Abraham and Isaac, so on Yūsuf and on the whole house — a religious consummation. The second (also al-Bayḍāwī's second gloss, and preferred by many because the sūrah visibly narrates worldly as well as spiritual elevation) takes it as the *combination* of blessings of this world and the next: deliverance, rank, knowledge, family restored, and a good end. In this second sense the verse's own reference to the forefathers is unpacked by the commentators as follows: to Abraham, the intimate friend (4:125: *"and God took Abraham as an intimate friend (khalīl)"*) and the one saved from the fire — *"they plotted against him, but We made them the greatest losers"* (37:98, with the rescue at 21:69: *"O fire, be coolness and safety for Abraham"*); to Isaac, the one who consented to be sacrificed and was ransomed (37:102-107). Both readings converge on the same fact: that in this family blessing is a *completed* thing, not a partial one — and that it reaches the members who are not individually exceptional, since "the House of Jacob" (āl Yaʿqūb) includes the eleven who are about to sin.

The last phrase is the most quietly remarkable part of the verse. The promise that blesses Yūsuf is expressly extended to his *family*, and by the end of the sūrah the beneficiaries of his grain, his protection, and his forgiveness will be precisely the men of verses 9-18. This is the logic of 6:165 applied within a household: "He has made you stewards upon the earth." A prophet's elevation is not a private reward; it is infrastructure.

**Why Isaac and Not Ishmael**

Readers of the verse often ask why the line runs through Ibrāhīm and Isḥāq, when the Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ descends through Ismāʿīl. The answer is direct: because Yūsuf is a son of Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq, and the sūrah names the actual line of the household being blessed. This is not a deprecation of Ishmael's line — 2:129 and 62:2 both describe the raising up "from among them" of a messenger — but it teaches a point of *ʿaqīdah* the sūrah will hammer in verses 38-40: election is God's to give and is not a property of blood. Yūsuf is honoured, not because his grandfather was honoured, but because God chose him; and the mention of the forefathers exists to say that the same God who was Latīf to them is being Latīf to him. The chain of blessing is real, and it is not the cause.

**"Truly Your Lord is Knowing, Wise" — the Ground of Every Promise**

*Inna rabbaka ʿalīmun ḥakīm*. The closing formula binds the promise to two attributes and is repeated in this sūrah at moments of maximum uncertainty (12:83: *"indeed, my Lord is Knowing, Wise"*; 12:100: *"indeed, my Lord is Latīf"*). The logic: a promise about the future requires two things of the one who makes it — he must know what will be (*ʿalīm*), and he must place things where they belong (*ḥakīm*). *Ḥakīm* is from *ḥakama*, "to govern, to restrain," and is used of a bit placed in a horse's mouth; a *ḥakīm* is one whose acts are correctly fitted, and whose restraint is as wise as His action. For the reader in the middle of a bad situation, the formula is not a compliment about God but a statement that the sequence of his circumstances has an author with both foreknowledge and design, which is the only combination that makes patience rational rather than fatalistic. Ibn Taymiyyah, in *Majmūʿ al-Fatāwā*, draws on this class of verse for his general rule that God's acts are never *ʿabath*, purposeless (play, absurdity), being joined always to knowledge and wisdom — an assertion the whole of Yūsuf's life is structured to demonstrate.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:7

> **7** *Certainly in Joseph and his brothers there are signs for the inquiring.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Tells You How to Read the Next Ninety-Four**

After the promise to Yūsuf, the sūrah steps outside the story and issues a notice: *la-qad kānat fī Yūsuf wa-ikhwānihi āyātun li-l-sāʾilīn*. The lam is the lam of oath, and *qad* with the perfect verb asserts that this has already been settled; the sentence has the shape of a guarantee, not a suggestion. The commentators class it among the "framework" verses (*iʿtirāḍiyyāt*) that interrupt the narrative to instruct the listener, exactly as 54:17, 54:22, 54:32 and 54:40 repeat "and We have made the Qur'an easy to remember" between the fragments of its stories. Al-Biqāʿī's analysis of this sūrah's arrangement makes the placement precise: after the promise (v. 6) the sūrah tells the hearer what the story is for, so that the accounts of the well, the tempted man, the prison, and the granary are heard from the outset as *evidence*, not as plot.

**"Āyāt" and Not "ʿIbar" — a Difference That Matters**

The parallel verse in Ṣād is worth setting against this one:

> *"A Book We have sent down to you, blessed, that they might reflect on its verses, and that those possessed of intellect (ulū al-albāb) might take heed."* (Qur'an 38:29)

The same story will be summed up at the end of this sūrah with the other word, so that the chapter's first and last interpretive notices form a pair — *āyāt* for the inquiring here, *ʿibrah* for those possessed of intellect at 12:111: the lesson is for whoever will look for it, the sign for whoever will acknowledge the One who set it.

And in the sūrah now being read, the same word *āyah* reappears at the end of the story with the sharpest of warnings attached:

> *"And how many a sign in the heavens and the earth do they pass by, while from it they turn away."* (Qur'an 12:105)

The word *ʿibrah* means an extractable moral, something you lift from a case and apply elsewhere; *āyah* means a sign, which points beyond itself to the one who placed it there. Our source glosses *āyāt* as "lessons" and as bearing "the universal import of the story" (al-Bayḍāwī, and Rāzī), and in doing so it preserves the technical difference: a story is a *ʿibrah* if all you learn from it is what not to do; it is an *āyah* if from it you learn who God is. This sūrah's choice of *āyāt* is why, as the commentary on every verse below will show, the chapter's real subject is not Yūsuf's fortunes but God's *lutf* — His subtlety — with Yūsuf as the case file.

**"For the Inquiring" — the Story as an Answer to Questions**

*Li-l-sāʾilīn*: "for those who ask." Our source explains that these are those who wish to gain true knowledge and to learn the story's universal import, and the choice of word is unusual; a more expected ending would have been *li-l-mutaʾammilīn*, "for those who reflect," or "for people of intellect," as at 38:77 and 5:100. Ibn ʿĀshūr draws the consequence: the sūrah is addressed to a *questioner*, and a question is a self-disclosure. To ask is to admit that one does not yet possess the answer, and so *sāʾil* names not curiosity but its precondition — the recognition of need. The Qur'an's own phrase for the same posture is:

> *"So ask the people of the Reminder, if you do not know."* (Qur'an 16:43; cf. 21:7)

There is a second, historical layer to the word which the occasion of revelation reports supply: certain Jewish scholars in Makkah, as our source's introduction notes, prompted the idolaters to ask the Prophet ﷺ about details of Yūsuf's life, and al-Ṭabarī's report says the chapter came in response to a request for a story. In both cases the verse says the same thing about the answer: this chapter is an answer to a question, but only those who asked will treat it as one. Anyone can hear a story; only a questioner hears an answer.

**Rāzī's Moral: the Futility of Envy, and the Fate of Two Prophets**

Al-Rāzī, in a passage our source records, draws the first *āyah* as a warning about *ḥasad*: the brothers' envy accomplished exactly nothing for them and everything against themselves, and the same pattern repeated in the Prophet ﷺ's own lifetime, when his kinsmen in Makkah, moved by the same competitive feeling, rejected and injured him — and in both cases God aided His servant and the enemies ended beneath his authority. Yūsuf's brothers ate from his granary; Quraysh submitted within a decade. This is not a promise that every envied person is a prophet, but a law about the structure of envy: it attacks what it cannot become, and the attack supplies the conditions of the very elevation it feared — the well being the road to the throne, as the whole sūrah proves.

The ḥadīth our source cites here is transmitted in the following words:

> *"Beware of envy, for envy consumes good deeds as fire consumes firewood."* (Abū Dāwūd 4903; Ibn Mājah 4210, from Abū Hurayrah)

The chain is disputed — several of the ḥadīth judges criticise a narrator in it, and al-Albānī judged it weak in itself but supported by corroborating meanings — so it should be cited for its admonition rather than as an independently established text. The meaning is sound and Qur'anic in any case: envy is corrosive because it turns the blessing it observes into evidence against one's own worth; the envious man does not merely want what another has, he concludes that he has been wronged by what another has received, which is the exact inversion of gratitude. The sūrah itself will put the correct alternative in the mouth of Yūsuf at verse 87: *"I hope to obtain from God what you despair of."*

**Two Signs for Two Situations: Patience and Gratitude**

Al-Qushayrī, quoted in our source, specifies the practical yield: the story counsels "patience in times of tribulation" and "gratitude for God's blessing." The pairing is the Prophet's ﷺ own, and the ḥadīth that gives the formula for the believer's relation to events is sound and belongs here word for word:

> *"The affair of the believer is all good, and that is for no one except the believer: if prosperity reaches him and he is grateful, it is good for him; and if hardship reaches him and he is patient, it is good for him."* (Muslim 2999, from Ṣuhayb)

Read against 12:7, this ḥadīth explains why the sūrah is "signs for the inquiring" rather than advice for the settled: a person's stance toward the same sequence of events — elevated then stripped, exalted then accused, forgotten then remembered — is precisely the thing he must ask about, since the sūrah gives him no way to control the events and a great deal of evidence about how to be in them.

**"God's Love Is Realised Through Trial"**

The final note in our source on this verse is the most theological: that the story shows "God's gentle taking-care (lutf) of His friends through His protection of them," and that "God's Love is realised through trial and tribulation." This is the sūrah's own later wording, put in Yūsuf's mouth at 12:100 — *"my Lord has been Latīf to me"* — and *lutf* in the theological vocabulary means kindness so subtle that the recipient discovers it only afterwards, often by seeing that the harmful thing was the necessary one. The classical theodicy drawn from verses like this one is not that suffering is good but that God's protection does not exempt from pain; it works through it. The relevant sound ḥadīth is:

> *"The people most severely tested are the prophets, then the most resembling, then the most resembling; a man is tried according to his religion."* (al-Tirmidhī 2398; Ibn Mājah 4023, from Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ — graded ṣaḥīḥ)

And in a second report on the same theme, transmitted from Anas:

> *"When God wills good for His servant, He hastens for him the punishment in this world; and when God wills evil for His servant, He withholds from him his sin, so that he meets Him on the Day of Resurrection with [the full weight of] it."* (al-Tirmidhī 2396 — its chain contains Sulaym al-ʿAʾmash, whose precision is disputed; the report is cited by the commentators, including al-Munādhawī with qualifications, and not as a proof-text)

The pairing of a sound and a contested report is deliberate: the doctrine of trial-as-cure is Qur'anic in general (2:155-157; 29:2-3: *"Do people suppose that they will be left to say 'we believe,' and not be tested?"*), and a commentator who cites the weaker narration honestly marks its standing, because honesty about evidence is itself one of the *āyāt* this verse promises the questioner.

**How a Modern Reader Should Use This Verse**

The chapter asks to be read as a case record, and a case record is used by people who bring questions: how does one keep faith when the accusation sticks? how does one handle power after humiliation? what is a father owed by sons who lied? how does one forgive people who cannot pay you back? These questions are answerable from verses 18, 23-33, 53-55, 86-92 — that is why the sūrah can say its story is a store of *signs for the inquiring*, since the answers were composed for those very questions and arrive only to whoever asks them. The reader who comes to a story for entertainment receives the entertainment; the reader who comes with an honest question receives the answer. The verse is, in effect, the sūrah's user's manual: ask before you read.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:8

> **8** *[Remember] when they said, “Truly Joseph and his brother are more beloved unto our father than are we, though we are a group. Surely our father is in manifest error!*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Overheard: the Qur'an's Use of Unvoiced Speech**

The verse does not narrate a decision; it quotes a private conversation. *Idh qālū* — "when they said" — gives the reader direct access to what the brothers said among themselves, before and apart from anything they said to their father, and the sūrah will keep doing this: 12:16 ("what do you think of the punishment of God, if it comes to you"), 12:29, 12:31, 12:68, 12:76, 12:91 are all quoted interior speech. Al-Zamakhsharī and Ibn Kathīr both note the rhetorical gain of the technique — the audience is made a confidant, and the point of disclosure is not to convict them later but to show that nothing in the story is hidden from the One who is telling it, since if God can quote a private council, He also knows the councils of Makkah. The reader is thus given, before the crime, the motive; there will be no surprise verdict at the end, and no euphemism either.

**"Joseph and His Brother": the Wound Named Precisely**

*Yūsufu wa-akhūhu*. The brother is Banyāmīn, Yaʿqūb's youngest and Yūsuf's only full brother, as our source records from the classical exegetes. The reason the two are bracketed together by the brothers is stated plainly: they were the young children of the same mother, so the affection of the old father gathered on them (Ṭs). Two facts of Arabic usage should be noticed here. First, the sūrah consistently calls Banyāmīn "the brother of Yūsuf" and never once "our brother" in the brothers' speech: their own grammar performs the exclusion they are complaining about. Second, when the moment comes at 12:76 for Yūsuf to keep his full brother under the king's law, the word becomes *akh* in the singular ("we found a wallet in the saddlebag of one of you... *akhūhu*"), the technical use for a full brother — and the lexical literature observes a tendency in the Qur'an's usage to mark full siblinghood with the plural *ikhwa* and wider kinship with *akhwā*. This is a fine example of the sūrah's precision: the family's fracture line is visible in its morphology.

**"More Beloved to Our Father Than We" — a Complaint in the Grammar of Degrees**

*Huwa aḥabbu ilā abīnā minnā* — literally "he is more beloved to our father than we." The comparative construction concedes a fact and treats it as an injustice; it says nothing about whether more love is deserved, because the complaint is not that love was wrongly given but that it was not given to them. The sūrah lets this stand with no authorial comment, which is characteristic of its moral method. It does not have to call them wrong; it has already shown, in verse 4, that their father had real and specific grounds for preferential attention — a boy granted a vision of providential significance is a child a father cannot treat as merely one of twelve — and in verse 6 that God, not Yaʿqūb, had chosen Yūsuf. The brothers' error is not in perceiving a difference but in attributing the difference to a preference when it is, in the sūrah's view, a divine allocation. Envy, in the Qur'anic diagnosis, is the refusal to accept that another's share was assigned by someone with a better claim to decide than oneself.

**"Though We Are a Group" — the Arithmetic of Resentment**

*Wa-naḥnu ʿuṣbah*. Our source explains *ʿuṣbah* as "a large group, usually between ten and forty" (al-Baghawī and others), and reads the clause as an expression of astonishment: how can this be, when we are so many and so useful? Al-Bayḍāwī and al-Zamakhsharī both make the logic explicit — in a tribal setting, grown sons are a father's defence and his name; a cluster of able men ought, by every calculation, to be the centre of an old man's hope. The word itself carries the argument: *ʿuṣbah* is from *ʿaṣaba*, "to encircle, to clasp," and the ʿaṣabah in law are the paternal kinsmen who surround a man for support and who inherit in their own right. So the sentence means: we are the ring around you; we are the ones who would defend the name. The premise exposed by the sūrah is the premise of all such resentment: that love is a return on usefulness, and that a father should weight his children by what they can do for him.

Two lines from the sūrah's own later material refute that premise inside the chapter. The "group" fails to protect anyone: they cannot keep Yūsuf, cannot keep their own promise about Banyāmīn (12:66, 12:81-82), and end up begging the youngest brother for grain. And the Qur'an's general verdict on the arithmetic of numbers is unambiguous:

> *"And if you obey most of those upon the earth, they will lead you astray from the way of God."* (Qur'an 6:116; cf. 12:103: *"and most of mankind, though you strongly desire it, will not be believers"*)

A majority is a fact about bodies, not a fact about desert. The brothers' "though we are a group" is thus presented by the sūrah as the first of its case-studies in a fallacy that every community repeats — the claim that the size of a party is evidence for its rightness.

**"Our Father Is in Manifest Error" — the Same Word, Reversed**

*Innā abākum la-fī ḍalālin mubīn*. They call their father *ḍāll*, lost — the word's root meaning is that of a stray animal wandering off, and in the Qur'an it is the standard term for a misdirection that is not merely intellectual but practical, the error of someone whose compass is off rather than whose arithmetic is wrong. The irony is built out of the sūrah's own vocabulary: *mubīn*, the word used in 12:1 for the Book that makes things plain, is used here by the brothers for the obviousness of a father's error. The chapter's first sentence announced a *clarifying* Book; its eighth verse shows unclarified hearts confidently diagnosing a clear mistake in a prophet. Al-Rāzī draws the point: the same adjective describes the criterion and the counterfeit, and the reader must decide which is which by content, not by confidence.

Two things follow about the man accused of *ḍalāl* by his own sons. First, the sūrah will never let Yaʿqūb be wrong about his sons; his judgment in verse 5 and verse 18 ("your selves have embellished (*sawwasat*) an affair for you") is right every time, and he is vindicated by events rather than by argument. Second, he is not depicted as an ascetic who cannot be hurt by it: the same father who calls his sons' affair "an affair your selves have dressed up" will say, of his own grief, "I only complain of my grief to God, and I know from God what you do not know" (12:86), and his eyes go white with sorrow (12:84).
