The sūrah permits a righteous man to be wounded by his children while remaining right about them — which is one of the more quietly remarkable psychological statements in the Book.

**The Ethics of Favouritism: What the Verse Does and Does Not Endorse**

That the brothers articulate a real phenomenon is not denied. Our source cites the report that Yaʿqūb kept Yūsuf with him night and day, and the tradition in al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī that the visible marks of preference multiplied the envy after the dream. Yet it is exactly worth observing what the sūrah omits: Genesis 37:3 states that Israel "loved Joseph more than all his children, because he was the son of his old age, and he made him a coat of many colours," and that coat becomes the brothers' prop. The Qur'an keeps the coat — but only as the shirt of verse 18, the shirt of verse 26, the shirt of verse 93, all of them instruments of evidence rather than gifts of favour. The text never tells us that Yūsuf received a token that made him conspicuous. That silence is doctrinally load-bearing: the sūrah declines to make a father's love the cause of a crime, and locates the cause in the sons' comparison of worth.

The Prophetic teaching on the matter is direct and belongs to the commentary on this verse as a rule for households:

> *"Fear God and treat your children with equity."* When the man who gave one of his children a gift was told to give all of them the like, the Prophet ﷺ said: *"I do not testify to an injustice."* — and in another wording: *"Do not bear me witness to an injustice; for truly it is not fitting that I should testify to the preferment of some of your children over others. Act equitably between your children as you would wish them to act equitably among themselves in kindness."* (Bukhārī 2587; Muslim 1623, from al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr)

The tradition's concern is not that a child may be loved more — that is not in anyone's power — but that *gifts and public preference* are in one's power, and that inequity in them is injustice precisely because it manufactures in innocent children the very comparison this verse records. Where a sūrah's entire tragedy begins with "more beloved to our father than we," the Sunnah's rule about equity in the visible is its practical footnote.

**The Psychology the Verse Describes, Named**

Modern developmental research on families converges with this reading on one finding: what damages siblings is not differential treatment as such but *perceived* differential treatment — the sense of being the less-favoured one, which predicts conflict, secrecy, and estrangement into adulthood more reliably than actual distributions do. That is precisely what verse 8 encodes: the brothers' claim is about what their father loves, an internal state they cannot observe, and their conclusion is a verdict on his rationality. The classical definition of *ḥasad* — the wish that a blessing should pass away from the one who has it, as al-Mubārakfūrī glosses it in his commentary on the ḥadīth quoted above — explains the brothers' arithmetic: they do not say "I want what he has" but "I want him to lose it" — an envious heart's arithmetic subtracts from the other rather than adding to the self, and it will therefore accept a plan (verse 9) that cannot possibly increase anything it possesses. Verse 8 is the diagnosis; verse 9 is the symptom.

**Why the Sūrah Begins Its Human Antagonism Here**

Read as a unit, verses 7-8 answer verse 1's promise of a clarifying Book in an unexpected way: the chapter's first move is not a miracle but a household's resentment, quoted verbatim and analysed by its grammar. The sūrah thereby teaches its reader to look for the beginnings of destruction where they actually occur — not in the act, but in the sentence a person says to himself about another's good, complete with its "though we are a group." Every subsequent event in the chapter, from the well to the accusation of theft, is downstream of that one clause; and the whole of Yūsuf's later conduct — "no reproach upon you today" (12:92) — is the sūrah's demonstration that a man can be the rightful object of such resentment and still be the one who cancels it.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:9

> **9** *Slay Joseph, or cast him out to some land, that your father’s concern might be for you. And be, thereafter, a righteous people.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Council That Speaks in Imperatives**

*Uqtulū Yūsuf* — "kill Joseph." The brothers' counsel is reported as a command addressed to one another, in the imperative, with the object named bare: not "Yūsuf" with the honorific or with the kinship word, but the bare name, in a construction the Arabic rhetoricians call *al-ḥadhf li-l-ihānah*, the dropping of a title for contempt (Ibn ʿAṭiyyah and al-Alūsī both note it). Al-Zamakhsharī makes a second observation about the phrasing that the source does not record but the syntax compels: the verse says "kill *Yūsuf*" while the previous verse said "Joseph *and his brother*" — and the resolution is that the plan in council names the target, and the plan as executed (verse 15) drops the brother entirely. The brothers overreach in the deliberation and under-deliver in the deed; the text marks the difference without comment, and the reader should notice that men who intend to murder end up throwing a boy into a hole.

**Two Options, and What Having Two Means**

*Uqtulū Yūsuf aw alqūhu arḍan* — "kill Joseph, or cast him into some land." The disjunction is the sūrah's own piece of moral reporting. Al-Bayḍāwī and Ibn ʿĀshūr both point out that the second alternative is not added as a synonym but as a concession: their counsel admits an option that leaves the boy alive, and the sūrah will show that the option was chosen — not from mercy, but because the first was unthinkable to them. The classical exegetes often remark that this is the anatomy of a compromised intention: a plan that says "either do the unforgivable thing or do something nearly as bad" is a plan whose author is already bargaining with himself, and the bargaining is where the real decision gets made. The verse thus tells us something about the brothers before it tells us anything about Yūsuf: they are not yet hard enough to do what they say, and the sūrah will keep them at that stage, never quite as evil as their own deliberation, right up to their repentance at 12:91-92.

**"That Your Father's Concern Might Be for You" — the Purpose Clause**

*Yakun lakum wajh* — literally, "that the face may be yours." The idiom is a visual one: a man whose affair is attended to has his "face" in the room; the one with no claim is turned away. Their goal is not their father's love in any private sense but the exclusive possession of his attention — the practical, tribal reality that a father's regard is a family's economy: it decides who is kept near, who is sent out, whose word counts at the well and in the market. Our source's gloss is exact: they believed that removing Yūsuf would remove him from Yaʿqūb's attention so that the father's concern would then be for them alone. The hidden premise, exposed by the whole sūrah, is that a heart can be made to love by subtraction; and the chapter's answer is that Yaʿqūb's love does not move when Yūsuf disappears — it merely loses its object and turns into a permanent, whitening grief (12:84). Nothing was gained; a second son was nearly lost; the grain of Egypt had to be bought.

**"And Be, After That, a Righteous People" — the Deferred Repentance**

*Wa takūnū min baʿdu qawman ṣāliḥīn*. Our source records the reading of the early commentators: the brothers intended to repent afterwards, or — in another gloss — to repair their relationship with their father once the difficulty was cleared out of the way. Either way the structure is the one thing the Qur'an names most often as a self-deception: a sin undertaken on the strength of a future act of righteousness. This is *taswīf*, the postponement of repentance, which the tradition treats as a disease of the heart rather than a scheduling problem. The relevant teaching is:

The promise side of the rule is established in sound texts, and there is no need to reach beyond them:

> *"God is more pleased with the repentance of one of you than a man who is riding in a barren land, and his mount runs away from him carrying his food and his water, and he despairs of it, and then it stands in front of him: God is more pleased with the repentance of His servant than that man with his mount."* (Bukhārī 6309; Muslim 2744)

> *"God stretches out His Hand at night so that the one who sinned by day may repent, and He stretches out His Hand by day so that the one who sinned at night may repent — and that continues until the sun rises from the place of its setting."* (Muslim 2759)

But the sūrah's concern here is not whether the door is open; it is whether a person may legitimately plan to walk through it later. The Prophet ﷺ described the difference in a single image:

> *"The believer sees his sins as though a mountain were suspended over him, fearing that it will fall upon him; and the wicked one sees his sins as though a fly were on his nose, which he brushed away."* — and in the wording of the *ta'liq* narration: *"The one who repents from sin is as one who has no sin."* (Bukhārī 6308 for the mountain-and-fly narration, from Anas; the second wording is transmitted by Ibn Mājah 4250 with a weak chain and is cited by the scholars as an admonition, not as a proof-text)

The sūrah's point is that repentance which is *planned as a companion to a crime* is not repentance, because what is repented of must be, in the intention, something one is abandoning — not something one is borrowing against.

**What the Islamic Definition of Repentance Excludes Here**

The classical jurists and ethicists set conditions for *tawbah* in a way that measures the brothers' plan exactly. Al-Ghazālī in the *Iḥyāʾ* (book of repentance) and Ibn Rajab in *Jāmiʿ al-ʿUlūm wa-l-Ḥikam* list its constituents as: regret for what was done, immediate cessation, firm resolve not to return, and — where the sin involved another's right — restoration of that right or seeking the owner's forgiveness. The brothers' projected "righteous people" fails at the first and last: the plan cannot be regretted, since it is the plan they like, and the right of Yūsuf cannot be restored after death or disappearance. What they have proposed is not a sin followed by repentance but a sin with a wish attached, and the sūrah lets the wish stand as the sentence that indicts it.

That is also why the chapter's treatment of them is not finished here. The Qur'an does, remarkably, accept their repentance — but only after the sequence in which they *ask to be forgiven* and are told, in the language of verse 92, "no reproach upon you today," and only after the years in which they could not have engineered their own moral improvement. Al-Rāzī's note in our source is the fitting summary: envy's "ultimate futility" is that it never produces the thing it aims at, since the brothers' plan was to secure their father's exclusive regard and it produced instead a father who turned from them with his eyes full of what he refused to say about them.

**The Command Form and the Question of Imitation**

A technical point of *uṣūl al-fiqh* deserves a place, because this verse is a standard example in the discussion of reported speech. The imperative *uqtulū* is a grammatical command, but it is not a divine command; it is the Qur'an quoting a human command within a narrative. The jurists — al-Āmidī, al-Sarakhsī in *Uṣūl*, and Ibn al-Ḥājib's *Mukhtaṣar* — hold that a command in the context of *ḥikāya* (reported speech) carries no ruling; and the exegetes add the safety rule that narrative passages in the Qur'an are not, on their own, proof-texts for action, which is precisely why 12:9 can be recited, memorised, and taught without any risk that it licenses what it contains. The same discipline applies to the Prophet's ﷺ words reported in the Qur'an in the mouths of Pharaoh and of the jINN in 72:6. A reader with some literacy in Arabic rhetoric will see that the sūrah is depending on this principle: it tells a story in which sin is quoted in its own vocabulary, at full length, with no editorial comment, and trusts the hearer to know that quotation is not endorsement. The whole chapter's ethics, including Yūsuf's refusal in verse 23 ("God forbid"), are conducted in this register — the text is willing to be honest about the interior life of bad men because it is confident that honesty is clarifying, which returns us to verse 1's "clarifying Book."

**The Modern Shape of Verse 9**

The form of the brothers' reasoning is familiar enough to be worth naming plainly: an outcome is desired (attention, standing, the removal of a rival), an action is identified that will certainly produce harm, and a future self is invented who will clean up the moral consequences. In one's own life it appears as "I will take this shortcut now and make it up later," "I will win this argument with my spouse and be kind about it afterwards," "I will take this money from the trust that has no one else in it and repay it when the year is good." The verse is placed here, in the sūrah's very first act, as a specimen: notice how quickly the arithmetic of a good man's household becomes a proposal to eliminate a person, and notice that the proposal's own internal weakness — its "or," its deferral, its bargaining — is what actually saves the boy. In the sūrah's providential grammar, Yūsuf's life is preserved at this moment not by his father's plan or by his own merit but by the hesitation of the men who mean to end it, and the reader is entitled to draw from that the general rule the chapter will repeat: God's *lutf* often operates through the incomplete malice of other people.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:10

> **10** *One among them said, “Slay not Joseph, but cast him into the depths of the well, that some caravan might pick him up, if you would take some action.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Dissent Recorded Without a Name**

*Qāla qāʾilun minhum lā taqtulū Yūsuf*. The sūrah does not say which brother spoke. Our source records the identification attempted by the commentators — our source identifies him as the eldest brother, and al-Rāzī and al-Bayḍāwī compare his action to that of Judah or Reuben in Genesis 37:21-27 — and the silence of the text is one of its deliberate features. The Qur'an's narrative technique is famously uninterested in the names of its incidental characters (the king of Egypt is *al-malik*, "the king," never *Firʿawn*; Yūsuf's master is *al-ʿazīz*; Jacob himself is never named in this chapter, only "father"), a discipline that keeps the reader's attention on the moral structure rather than on the cast list. It is a well-observed feature of the Qur'an's narrative technique that where it names, the naming carries weight — Pharaoh, Hāmān and Qārūn are named for infamy — and where it does not, the individual has dissolved into a function. Here the function is *the one who restrains*.

**The Ethics of the Minority Voice**

Whatever his identity, this brother's intervention changes the story's direction, and the sūrah records it in order that we notice: inside a council resolved on a crime there is usually at least one voice urging the lesser of the options, and the lesser option — a boy thrown into an abandoned well in a caravan route — is still an attempt on his life. So the verse is simultaneously a vindication of dissent and a limit on its moral worth. The Prophet ﷺ stated the obligation in the terms the brother was operating under:

> *"Whoever among you sees an evil, let him change it with his hand; and if he cannot, then with his tongue; and if he cannot, then with his heart — and that is the weakest of faith."* (Muslim 49, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī)

The brother could not change it with his hand; he changed it with his tongue, and by the sūrah's own account the boy lived. This is the practical theology of the chapter's providence: God does not need a hero to block evil; He needs a hesitation inside it. Ibn ʿĀshūr draws the same conclusion, that the counsel of restraint was itself an *āyah* — a sign that a household is never as unified in its sin as it pretends to be, and that the "group" (ʿuṣbah) of verse 8 was, at the moment of action, a room of men with different limits.

**"The Depths of the Well" — a Lexical Descent**

*Giābat al-biʾr* (also read *gidāb*), "the bottom of the well," where the water collects. Al-Ṭabarī glosses *gidāb* as *qār al-biʾr*, its settled depth, and the lexicographers (Ibn Fāris, *Muʿjam Maqāyīs al-Lugha*; al-Azharī, *Tahdhīb al-Lugha*) connect the root to coldness and to the hollow of a thing — *jidād* being the cold water at the bottom of a well. The word for the receptacle, *biʾr*, is used in the Qur'an only here and in a handful of places, and is the ordinary word for a dug water-pit; the sūrah's choice of the *bottom* and the *hidden* (the word is cognate with *ghayāb*, absence, concealment) is what makes the plan workable in the brothers' own minds: the well hides, the desert finishes what the well begins.

**"That Some Caravan Might Pick Him Up" — the Language of a Found Object**

*Yaltaqiṣhu baʿḍ al-raybān* — "may pick him up." *Iltiqāṣ* is the verb of gathering up something lying on the ground, and its noun *luqṭah* is the technical term in Islamic law for lost property found by a passer-by. The sūrah thus describes Yūsuf, in the brothers' own phrase, as a foundling-to-be: a person reduced, in the vocabulary of the plotters, to an object of opportunity. But the echo carries a judgment as well as a picture, because the law of *luqṭah* is a law of publicity and care: the Prophet ﷺ, when asked about lost property, said:

> *"Know its container and its cord, then announce it for a year; if its owner comes, give it to him, and otherwise you may make use of it."* And he said of lost sheep: *"Take it, for it is yours, or your brother's, or the wolf's."* — and of lost camels: *"What have you to do with it? It has its water-skin and its feet; it will reach its owner and drink where it drinks."* (Bukhārī 91-92; Muslim 1722)

A foundling is, in that law, the most protected category of the lost, for he has no owner to come; and the sūrah will show the caravan doing the opposite of what the law of *luqṭah* requires — appraising him, bargaining over him, and selling him (12:19-20), with the buyer's instruction to "make his lodging honourable" (12:21) arriving as a small, unforced mercy inside an unjust transaction. The brothers intended to make Yūsuf a lost object; the sūrah quietly notes that the law of God treats lost objects as trusts.

**"If You Would Take Some Action" — a Conditional That Wounds**

*In kuntum fāʿilīn* — "if you are going to do anything." The conditional is rhetorical, and the commentators mark its function: a conditional addressed to men who have just proposed a murder does not encourage the deed, it discounts it — "if you insist on acting at all." The syntax itself reproaches them. This is also the second time in three verses that the brothers' plan has been softened by an "if" or an "or": verse 9's "kill him *or* cast him out," verse 10's "*if* you are to do anything." Al-Rāzī's note is that the plan of a household on the verge of sin is usually accompanied by such hedging, and the hedging is the place where grace enters.

**What the Commentary Literature Adds, and How It Must Be Treated**

Our source states that the brothers did not accept the proposal at once (12:15c), and the transmitted accounts of their hesitation — that the eldest reminded them of a promise, that one of them was moved to pity when Yūsuf clung to him — belong to the genre the tradition calls *akhbār al-tafsīr*, narrative supplementation drawn largely from earlier scriptures and popular retelling. Ibn Kathīr's treatment of the story is the model for how to handle them: he relays the reports, and where they come from the People of the Book he says plainly that they are neither believed nor disbelieved, since the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Do not believe the People of the Book and do not disbelieve them, and say: 'We believe in God and in what has been revealed to us and in what was revealed to you.'"* (Bukhārī 4485; Muslim 2667)

The bloody-shirt scene of Genesis 37:31-33, Jacob's refusal to be comforted, and the sale to the Ishmaelites are parallel material; the Qur'an keeps the shirt, omits the deception of the father by the brothers' fabrication of evidence, and inserts the one un-named dissenter. That edit — a restraint added inside the crime and a lie removed from the prophet's family — is itself part of the sūrah's theology of prophetic households, and the reader who compares the accounts should read it as the chapter's own commentary on verses 9-10.

**The Verse as a Rule for Real Life**

Verse 10 teaches two hard, useful things. First, that when a group has decided on harm, the achievable good is often not the prevention of the harm but the reduction of it, and reducing it is not surrender — a man who cannot stop a plan may still change its shape, and a change of shape saved a prophet. Second, that a plot designed to look like an accident is a signature of *kayd* (compare 12:5 and 12:33, 12:76), and the sūrah invites the reader to ask, whenever a misfortune arrives with suspicious tidiness, who advised the tidiness. The Qur'an's answer to the "accident" is not conspiracy-mongering but the certainty stated in this chapter's closing verses: nothing in the sequence escaped the knowledge of the *ʿAlīm Ḥakīm* (12:83), and the well that was meant to end a life was the first stage of a road to a throne.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:11

> **11** *They said, “O father! What ails thee that thou dost not trust us with Joseph? Truly we wish him well.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Offence Taken as a Tactic**

*Qālū yā abānā mā laka lā ta'tamanā ʿalā Yūsuf*. The form is an interrogative of reproach — "what is the matter with you, that you do not trust us?" — and the sūrah's placement of it right after the brothers' private council (9-10) lets the reader see the machinery: men who have just debated killing a boy approach their father with an air of wounded honesty. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the rhetorical order of their whole speech in verses 11-12, which is a textbook case of the four moves of a persuasive falsehood — (1) a reproach that puts the listener on the defensive ("what ails you?"), (2) an assertion of good intention ("we are for him well-wishers"), (3) a modest, concrete request with a benefit attached ("send him with us to pasture and play"), and (4) a guarantee ("we shall surely keep him"). Each element is, standing alone, entirely plausible; that is what makes the sequence dangerous. The sūrah's comment on this is silence — it does not call them liars here, because verses 15-18 will do it better.

**"Trust" (Amānah) and Its Weight in the Qur'an**

The verb is *i'timād* from *amāna*, the same root as *amānah*, trust. The Qur'an uses it of God's charge to mankind and the heavens (33:72: *"We offered the trust to the heavens and the earth and the mountains, and they refused to carry it — and man carried it"*), of withheld property (4:58: *"God commands you to render the trusts (al-amānāt) to those to whom they are due"*), and of persons entrusted to one's care — as in 26:107, where the messenger says "trustworthy (amīn)"; and the exact two-word pairing occurs in the Moses cycle, where his sister says *"shall I not direct you to a household that will take him up (yakfulūhu) on your behalf, and they will advise him (nāṣiḥūn)?"* — care and sincerity in the same breath, the pairing the brothers use about Yūsuf (12:11-12). The correspondence is close enough to be read as the Qur'an's own cross-reference: the child Moses is handed to the household of his enemy by a sister who uses the words of sincerity, and is returned to his mother under that household's roof (28:7-13; 20:39). Both stories teach the same thing — that God's protection of a chosen child often runs through the hands of people who do not know they are being used, and whose intentions are not what will finally happen.

**"We Wish Him Well" — the Misuse of Naṣīḥah**

*Innā la-nāṣiḥūn*. *Nāṣiḥ* is from *naṣaḥa*, of which the lexicographers give two related images: mending a garment so that its seams hold, and straining milk so that nothing is mixed with it; Rāghib's *al-Mufradāt* derives both from the sense of purity and repair — a *nāṣiḥ* is one whose wish for you is unmixed, like strained milk, and one whose advice mends rather than tears. The word is the one the Prophet ﷺ chose as the summary of the whole religion:

> *"The religion is sincere counsel (naṣīḥah)." We asked: "For whom?" He said: "For God, for His Book, for His Messenger, for the leaders of the Muslims, and for their common folk."* (Muslim 55, from Tamīm al-Dārī)

The brothers therefore utter the religion's compressed definition as a cover for a murder, and the sūrah's ear is sharp enough to preserve the word. What follows from this is not cynicism about sincerity but a rule of testing: the claim to be well-wishing cannot be accepted on its own utterance; the *nāṣiḥ* is known by whether the counsel costs him anything and whether it survives being refused. Yaʿqūb's answer (verse 13) is precisely a refusal, and the sūrah shows that the men who insist their motives are pure become indignant the moment they are declined — a pattern the chapter will repeat in 12:16-17 ("if we are destroyed, then we are among the losers" — the language of self-pity retained even after the deed is done) and again in verse 63 and verse 97, where the same two words *nāsiḥūn* and *ḥāfiẓūn* are recycled verbatim for the second, more honest appeal about Banyāmīn. Read as a pair, verses 11 and 63-64 are the sūrah's own comparison of two versions of the same pitch: one that fails to be believed, and one that is.

**"O Father": the Plural Address as a Family Claim**

They say *yā abānā*, "our father," not the singular of the sons who were loved (see verse 4 above on *yā abati*). Al-Biqāʾī and after him al-Shanqīṭī observe that the plural possessive is deployed for collective pressure: it is the address of men who speak as one body and who have made their decision already, and the father will meet the plurality with a singular in verse 64 — "I will not send him down with you until you give me a covenant *in God's name*." Their "we" is a bloc; his reply is a binding of each of them to an oath. This is the sūrah's first lesson in how a group uses its number as a moral argument, a theme announced in verse 8 ("though we are a group") and answered in verse 103 ("most of mankind will not believe").

**What the Verse Says About Being Distrusted**

Finally, the brothers' question — "what ails you that you do not trust us?" — is a question the Qur'an elsewhere treats with unusual seriousness, because trustworthiness is the one thing a person is judged on and cannot assert for himself. The sūrah's own verdict will come in Yūsuf's words to the king's envoys: *"return to your lord and ask him what is the matter of the women who cut their hands — my Lord knows his scheme"* (12:50), that is, vindication is asked for from God rather than demanded from people; and the sound ḥadīth fixes the same order in the believer's life:

> *"The signs of a hypocrite are three: when he speaks he lies, when he promises he breaks it, and when he is entrusted he betrays."* (Bukhārī 33; Muslim 59, from Abū Hurayrah)

The brothers exhibit all three in the next two verses. Their father, who knows them, asks for a covenant; the text, which knows the reader, asks him to notice that the people who most loudly claim to be *nāsiḥūn* are frequently the ones who have already decided what they will do with the trust.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:12

> **12** *Send him forth with us tomorrow, to frolic and play. We shall surely be his keepers.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Request in the Shape of a Reasonable Proposal**

*Arsilhu maʿanā ghadan nartʿa wa-naltabib*. Grammatically this is a plea framed as a benefit, and it contains the smallest possible ask: one day, a nearby pasture, an activity the family already does. Al-Rāzī's note in our source gives the background that makes the request credible — it was the brothers' custom to go out to the grazing-grounds and spend time there in leisure, so the invitation was not unusual; unusual was only that Yūsuf was asked. Deception of this kind rarely proposes something unprecedented; it proposes the ordinary thing, one time, with the person added. The word *ghadan*, "tomorrow," is the pressure point: a deadline set by the party that wants the thing, so that deliberation is squeezed out. Jacob will not grant it without a covenant (verse 17), which is the sūrah's practical lesson in what to do with a time-limited request from people whose motives you have reason to doubt.

**"To Graze and to Play" — Two Readings of a Verb**

*Nartʿa* is from *raʿā*, to pasture or to feed on pasture, so that the verb may mean "we shall let the flocks graze" or, on the reading transmitted from ʿAlī and Ibn ʿAbbās in al-Ṭabarī — *yarʿa*, "he shall graze" — the boys take him so that *he* grazes, i.e. is fed and free. *Naltabib* is "we shall amuse ourselves," from *al-lahw*; a reading attributed to al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī gives *nalʿab*, the more common Qur'anic word for play. The two variants divide the sentence between care and amusement, and both are in the verse's spirit: the promise is that the boy will be fed and entertained.

Now notice the vocabulary. *Laʿb* and *lahw* are the Qur'an's standing words for the self-occupying, unexamined life:

> *"The life of this world is nothing but play (laʿb) and amusement (lahw); and truly the abode in the Hereafter — that is the life, if they only knew."* (Qur'an 29:64; cf. 6:32; 57:20)

The sūrah lets the brothers use, about a child's afternoon, the exact word God uses for a world without account. That is not an accusation that play is sinful — the ḥadīth of ʿĀʾishah watching the Abyssinians play in the mosque, and the Prophet ﷺ standing to screen her, settles that (Bukhārī 5236; Muslim 892) — but it is a piece of the chapter's irony: the word for harmless amusement is spoken by men for whom the amusement is a screen, and the sūrah uses the same noun elsewhere for the condition of people who do not ask what their activity is for.

**"We Shall Surely Be His Keepers" — the Claim and the Account**

*Wa innā la-ḥāfiẓūn*. The root *ḥ-f-ẓ* is guarding with attentiveness, in the sense of keeping a thing from being lost to you; it is used of keeping animals in a fold, of preserving a text in the memory, of guarding the prayers (2:238: *"guard attentively the prayers and the middle prayer"*), and of guarding the chastity of others (70:29). The attribute of God Himself is *al-Ḥafīẓ*, and the sūrah's own theology of it appears in two places: Jacob's reply at verse 64, *"except that you bring him to me unless God wills"* and — in the verse the brothers will not quote — the fact that God's guarding does not fatigue:

> *"His Footstool extends to the heavens and the earth, and their preservation (ḥifẓuhumā) does not weary Him."* (Qur'an 2:255)

The claim made here is therefore a claim to a divine quality, exercised over a child, by men who have already agreed among themselves to be rid of him. The sūrah's legal and moral treatment of such a claim is summed up in the Prophet's ﷺ rule about the very word *rāʾin*, "shepherd, guardian":

> *"Every one of you is a guardian (rāʾin), and every one of you is asked about his charge: the ruler is a guardian, the man is a guardian over his household, the woman is a guardian in her husband's house; so every one of you is a guardian and every one of you is asked about his charge."* (Bukhārī 893; Muslim 1829, from Ibn ʿUmar)

The brothers volunteered for an office whose definition includes being questioned about it, and they are questioned in this sūrah at verses 16, 63, 66, 81-82 and finally at 12:91, where the question is answered by themselves. That is the structure of the chapter's treatment of promises: they do not need to be broken for the reader to see them clearly; they only need to be remembered.

**The Same Pitch, Twenty-Four Verses Later**

Verses 11-12 have a twin, and the sūrah invites the comparison by repeating their wording almost exactly. When the brothers come back from Egypt with their merchandise returned to them and ask for Banyāmīn, they open with the same sentence: *"O our father, what is the matter with you, that you do not trust us with Yūsuf? And truly we are sincere advisers to him"* (12:63), and they add the same undertaking in the same root: *"we will guard our brother and get a camel-load more"* (12:65). Jacob's reply (12:64) is the sūrah's own commentary on verse 12 above: *"How could I entrust him to you except as I entrusted his brother to you before? So God is the best of guardians (ḥāfiẓ), and He is the most merciful of the merciful."* He does not dispute their sincerity; he cites the record. The word they offered as a promise — *ḥāfiẓ* — he hands back to God, who alone can be called by it without being questioned about it. Only when they are hard-pressed does he accept an instrument instead of a claim: *"I will not send him down with you until you give me a binding pledge (mawthāq) in God's name that you will bring him back to me, unless you are overpowered"* (12:66) — and even then he closes by appointing God as the one who supervises the agreement: *"God is the trustee (wakīl) over what we say."* The escalation is instructive: the first time, a bare assertion ("we are keepers") was met with a fear (verse 13); the second time a covenant sworn in God's name is demanded, and the demand is itself treated as insufficient. The reader who has been following the sūrah sees that this is not miserliness in a father but a theology: no instrument binds a man who has decided, and a pledge is worth what its giver's fear of God is worth — which is why Jacob's *mawthāq* will be broken (12:69-76) in precisely the circumstance he had foreseen, "unless you are overpowered," and why the sūrah puts that exception in his mouth in advance.


**A Note on the Reader's Own Use of This Verse**

Muslim ethicists read verses 11-12 as a case study in *ghishsh* — the fraud that works not by lying about facts but by selecting a true description of a harmless activity and attaching it to an untrue intention: to graze and to play is what they will do; to be keepers is not. Al-Ghazālī, in the *Iḥyāʾ*'s book on truthfulness, discusses how the worst deceptions are constructed of accurate details — the pasture is real, the camel is real, the day is real — which is why a judge cannot act on the details alone and why a father cannot. Its counsel, read together with Jacob's behaviour in verses 13 and 66, gives a rule for anyone being persuaded: do not argue with the accuracy of a proposal, ask about the intention behind it, and require that the intention be made costly — an oath, a bond, a witness. A person who resents the cost of being answerable has told you more about his purpose than his assurances have.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:13

> **13** *He said, “Truly it grieves me that you should go with him. And I fear that the wolf may eat him, while you are heedless of him.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Verbs for Two Different Ailments**

*Qāla innī la-yaḥzununī an dhahabtum bihi wa-akhāfu an yaʾkulahu al-dhiʾbu wa-antum ʿanhu ghāfilūn.* The verse puts two states of the heart side by side, and Arabic keeps them distinct in a way the English "grieve" and "fear" only partly carry. *Ḥuzn* is grief for something present or already lost — the ache of separation now; *khawf* is the apprehension of a harm still to come. The Prophet's ﷺ supplication preserves precisely this pairing, and the commentators of Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (Ibn Ḥajar, *Fatḥ al-Bārī*, on the book of *daʿawāt*) explain the distinction while quoting it:

> *"O God, I seek refuge with You from anxiety (hamm) and grief (ḥuzn), from incapacity and laziness, from cowardice and miserliness, and from the crushing of debt and the domination of men."* (Bukhārī 6369, from Anas)

*Ḥuzn* is for what has happened; *hamm* is for what one dreads will. Yaʿqūb names both: the departure would grieve him, and the possibility of harm frightens him. That a prophet should state both without embarrassment is one of the sūrah's quiet contributions to the Qur'anic anthropology of feeling: grief is not a deficiency of faith. The same Prophet ﷺ, holding his dying infant son Ibrāhīm, said:

> *"The eyes shed tears, the heart grieves, and we say only what pleases our Lord; and truly, O Ibrāhīm, we are grieved by that which parts us from you."* (Bukhārī 1303; Muslim 2315)

**"While You Are Heedless" — the Chapter's Key Word Placed in a Father's Mouth**

The closing phrase, *wa-antum ʿanhu ghāfilūn*, reuses the word of verse 3: "you were among the heedless (ghāfilīn)." The link is not decoration. There, God addressed the Prophet ﷺ about a lack of knowledge that was no fault of his; here, Yaʿqūb charges his sons with the same word for a failure of attention that is theirs, because they have chosen it — a group of grown men cannot be ignorant of a boy they have taken into the desert, and the only way their charge can be discharged is by not looking at him. Ibn al-Qayyim's treatment of *ghaflah* in *al-Fawā'id* makes the same moral observation from the other side: heedlessness is not the absence of information but the presence of neglect, and the person who is heedless has usually decided to be.

This is the moment at which the sūrah also makes a statement about providence, because the father's fear of *their* heedlessness is answered in the same breath by God's non-forgetfulness. The verse Yaʿhā refers to when He answers Zakariyyā's fear of being "never wretched in supplication" is:

> *"My Lord, indeed I have called upon You, and I have never been unhappy in my supplication to You, my Lord."* (Qur'an 19:4) — and the principle is stated at 20:52: *"My Lord does not err and does not forget."*

The point of these verses in connection with our own is: a prophet's father may rightly fear the negligence of his sons, because a person is required to use the causes he can see, and yet nothing escapes the keeping of the One who neither misses nor forgets. Verse 15 will supply the practical conclusion: the brothers act in the belief that they are unobserved, and God reveals to the boy in the pit what they cannot see coming.

**"The Wolf" — Real Animal or Figure of Speech?**

Our source records the dispute, which is one of the more interesting exegetical problems in the sūrah. On one reading, Yaʿqūb means an actual wolf, the most feared predator of the pasture, with the definite article pointing to a particular animal ("*the* wolf," as in the later speech of the brothers). On another, the wolf is a figure: in a transmitted account ten wolves surrounded the boy and one withdrew from them before the earth opened and swallowed him, and the ten are the brothers, the withdrawing one the dissenter of verse 10, and the rift in the ground the well (cf. 12:10 and 12:15). The second reading is a *taʿbīr* narrative rather than an established report, and Such material is transmitted in the commentaries with caution and should not be relied on as history; it is worth recording here because the brothers' behaviour does exactly what wolves do in Arabic proverb — they circle, they take the weakest, and they let one of their number object and be overruled.

What is decisive is not which animal Yaʿqūb had in mind but what he has chosen to say. He cannot accuse his sons of the intention he suspects — that would tear the family open with no evidence — so he names a danger outside them, in a form they can dismiss with a boast. The sūrah's psychological realism is exact here: a man who is afraid for a child in the care of people he does not fully trust reaches for a reason the others cannot prove false, and the verse gives them the chance to prove him false with their own strength. They do (verse 14). The excuse he has offered is the excuse they will use, and that is the point at which the reader should note a fact the chapter will not spell out: Yaʿqūb will later say, "truly nothing has made you do this except your own selves' embellishment" (12:18) — a conclusion he could not have had from any evidence in the text, which the commentators take as a piece of the prophetic *firāsa* of a man who knows his sons, and whose fear was right on every count except the name of the animal.

**The Vocabulary of the Wolf**

*Al-dhiʾb* occurs in the Qur'an only in this sūrah, twice (12:13 and 12:17), and the pairing of those two verses is a lesson in rhetoric. The first is a hypothetical in a father's mouth; the second is the report of an accomplished fact in the sons' mouths — *"he has been eaten by the wolf (akalahu al-dhiʾbu)."* The lie has the same nouns and the same verb as the fear, with only the tense changed. Al-Zamakhsharī's observation on fabricated speech in this sūrah holds generally: a lie is usually a true sentence with the wrong verb form, and the chapter's ear is trained on verb forms. Lexically, the root also yields *ḍibʿ*, greed or rapacity, and Arabic writers used "the wolf of your own self" for avarice — a usage the tradition applied to exactly this family, whose "wolf" was their own *ḥasad* (see the discussion of 12:8 above, and 12:53's "the self is commandingly evil").

**A Father Who Is Right and a Father Who Is Overruled**

Two doctrinal notes belong to this verse. First, about *khauf* itself: the Muʿtazilī and Ashʿarī discussions of trust in divine decree (tawakkul) both take this verse as evidence, because Yaʿqūb is not reproached for his fear; and the sound practice of the Prophet ﷺ was to combine caution and reliance, as in the report on the unruly mount: *"Trust in God — and tie her"* (al-Tirmidhī 2517, from Anas: the chain contains a narrator whom the ḥadīth masters criticised, so it is cited for its meaning, which is Qur'anic — "and prepare against them whatever you are able of power and of steeds of war" (8:60)). Second, the verse is a specimen of *naṣīḥa* refused: the father states an objection, the sons rebut it with a stronger-sounding one (verse 14), and he yields — not because they persuaded him, but because he has no authority over adult sons and a promise made to him by their own father's father (see the note on the pledge, 12:66) is the only leverage left. The sūrah does not say he lacked faith; it says he grieved.

**The Spiritual Reading Preserved by the Source**

Our source records a spiritual gloss on this verse which is worth setting down because it is representative of the chapter's place in devotional literature: as Yaʿqūb could not bear separation from the boy for an hour, so the believer should not be heedless of the Beloved for a moment, since heedlessness *is* the separation and remembrance *is* the company. The support for the second half of the claim is a sound ḥadīth qudsī, which is why such readings, when they are marked as *ishārāt* and not as the verse's plain sense, are legitimate:

> *"God the Exalted says: 'I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he mentions Me; if he mentions Me to himself, I mention him to Myself; and if he mentions Me in a company, I mention him in a better company; and if he draws near to Me a handspan, I draw near to him a cubit.'"* (Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675, from Abū Hurayrah)

The rule of procedure that governs such glosses applies here: the plain sense of 12:13 is a father's fear for a child, and the devotional reading is an application of that sense, not a substitute for it — a distinction that keeps the chapter's moral force and its spiritual reach from being confused with one another.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:14

> **14** *They said, “If the wolf should eat him, while we are a group, then we would surely be losers!”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Boast Repeated Is the Boast Examined**

*Qālū laʾin akalahu al-dhiʾbu wa-naḥnu ʿuṣbah inna-idhan la-mina al-khāsirīn.* The brothers repeat the word they used two verses earlier: "though we are a group" (12:8) has become "while we are a group" (12:14). Repetition in the Qur'an is rarely filler, and al-Biqāʿī's analysis of the sūrah's arrangement treats such echoes as signposts: what they are certain of is their number, and what they are certain of about their father is that he is wrong (12:8). The sūrah does not need to refute the boast. It has already arranged the plot so that the boast answers itself: the eleven "ʿuṣbah" will fail to guard one boy, will fail to keep their second brother (12:66-76), and will be fed by the boy they lost. The verse therefore functions as an internal warning about over-confidence in collective strength — a theme the Qur'an states in general terms elsewhere:

> *"And do not be like those who went forth from their homes boastfully and to be seen by the people, and who bar from the way of God."* (Qur'an 8:47)

**"We Would Surely Be Losers" — the Qur'an's Own Definition Closes the Circle**

They swear, in effect, that if a wolf took Yūsuf while they were many, they would be *khāsirūn*, losers. The conditional is the language of a wager: "if that happens, then we are of the lost" — a man staking his standing on an outcome, exactly as a merchant stakes capital. That is precisely the register of the word: *khusūr* in Arabic is a term of trade, the diminution of capital (Rāghib, *al-Mufradāt*; the same root gives the *mukhāṣarah*, the mutual loss of a transaction), and the Qur'an's uses of it are almost always commercial — the "trade" of 61:10 ("Shall I guide you to a commerce (tijārah) that will save you from a painful punishment?"), the "loss" of those who "sold the signs of God for a small price" (2:174), and the traders of 83:1-3, "Woe to those who give short measure, who when they take the measure from the people exact it in full, and when they measure or weigh for them give less." That last is a devastating gloss on this verse, because the brothers' loss is a short measure of their own brother's worth.

But the sūrah's word is also used, elsewhere in the Qur'an, in a formula that reads like an answer to this very boast:

> *"Do the people of the towns then feel secure (fa-amina) that Our punishment will come to them by night while they sleep? Or do the people of the towns feel secure that Our punishment will come to them in the forenoon while they play? Did they then feel secure from the plan (makr) of God? But no one feels secure from the plan of God except the losing people (al-qawm al-khāsirūn)."* (Qur'an 7:97-99)

Here the *qawm khāsirūn* are defined not as those who suffer a loss but as those who are confident they will not. The brothers believe they have said the opposite — "if we fail, we are losers" — and the Qur'an's own vocabulary lets the reader hear the double sense: a man who is certain that a harm cannot reach him through his own strength has already described the condition of the *khāsir*. Verse 14 is thus, structurally, the moment of the brothers' fall, not verse 15: the act is still only a plan, but the presumption is finished and recorded.

**The Grammar of a Sworn Hypothetical**

*Laʾin* — the lam after the conditional particle is the lam of an oath, so that the sentence is formally a sworn conditional ("by God, if the wolf eats him…"). Al-Zamakhsharī notes how common this is in the Qur'an's reporting of human speech, and how it is used by people who want their certainty to be believed. Two observations follow. First, an oath attaches to what is not at issue: no one is disputing whether eleven grown men are stronger than a boy, and that is exactly what they swear to; the unexamined premise — that strength determines what will happen to a person — is smuggled through on the strength of an oath about the periphery. Second, the verb form chosen, *la-mina al-khāsirīn* with *min* ("we would be *among* the losers"), softens the claim into self-classification: not "we would be destroyed," but "we would be of the ordinary run of the unlucky." The Qur'an's use of "among" in such places is one of the finer markers of its realism about moral language, and the verse's structure is a case-study in how a person commits himself to an idea by couching it as an unremarkable instance of a general class.

**"If the Wolf Should Eat Him": Their Word, Their Later Excuse**

The sūrah makes the brothers, not their father, use the word *dhiʾb*. Their father had named the wolf as a fear (12:13); they name it as a hypothesis to be dismissed; and at 12:17 they will say it as a fact: *"a qabīḥatu... akalahu al-dhiʾb"* — "the wolf has eaten him." The three appearances form the sūrah's own demonstration of how a lie is manufactured: a true possibility is offered, mocked, and then promoted into a report. Al-Qurṭubī's observation that the Qur'an sometimes lets a liar's vocabulary become his confession is apt here — the text supplies no commentary on verse 14, and needs none, because the ending of the sentence is supplied by verse 17, in the same words.

**A Psychological Rule the Verse States in Passing**

The structure of the brothers' confidence deserves naming, because it is the ordinary shape of risk-denial: they reason from the size of the party ("while we are a group") to the unlikelihood of the event, without ever addressing the question that decides the event — what the party intends to do. A wolf is not the danger; the danger is that the eleven have already agreed on what to do with the boy, and their father's objection is answered not by evidence but by volume. The Qur'an, in its general teaching on *ghurūr*, self-delusion, describes exactly this:

> *"O you who believe, let not your wealth or your children divert you from the remembrance of God; and whoever does that — those are the losers (al-khāsirūn)."* (Qur'an 63:9)

Ibn Kathīr and the other commentators of this sūrah note that the brothers' sentence is a real conditional — the form in Arabic that presents what the speaker treats as a live possibility, not an idle one — and that its rhetoric therefore conceals an admission: they have already placed the wolf's meal among the outcomes they are prepared for, which is a way of planning to be surprised by nothing. The practical lesson is the plain one: a person who says "it could not happen to us, because we are many" has not reduced the risk; he has reduced his own attention. The next verse shows how little attention costs.

**How the Verse Is Used in the Sūrah's Ethics of Power**

Commentators on this verse also draw the political reading, since the sūrah will soon show Yūsuf governing a country and the same *ʿuṣbah* logic in the form of a group's "we are too strong for this" applied to a ruler and his court. Al-Marwazī and later writers on statecraft take the pair 12:13-14 as a parable of the difference between two kinds of security: the security that prepares (a father who names the danger and demands a covenant) and the security that boasts (a group that names the danger as impossible). The first survives; the second, in this story, loses a brother for twenty-odd years and has to be fed by him. The sūrah's verdict is delivered not in an authorial sentence but in arithmetic: eleven men plus a wolf equals one lost son, and the verse that reads like a swagger is the verse in which they are finished.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:15

> **15** *So when they went with him, and agreed to put him in the depths of the well, We revealed unto him, “Verily thou wilt inform them of this affair of theirs, when they are unaware.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Contains Everything and Narrates Almost Nothing**

*Fa-lammā dhahabū bihi wa-ajmaʿū an yajʿalūhu fī ghayābat al-biʾr awḥaynā ilayhi la-lanubatti'annahum bi-hādhā al-amri wa-hum lā yashʿurūn.* Two verbs carry a whole childhood: *dhahabū bihi*, "they went with him," and *ajmaʿū*, "they resolved." Between them the sūrah says nothing, and the nothing is deliberate. The commentators fill the interval with the details our source records — that they took turns carrying him on their shoulders, that once out of their father's sight one of them threw him down, that each brother struck him when he appealed to him, that he was beaten nearly to death, that they tied his hands and stripped his shirt, that he cried out for the shirt to cover himself in the water and they answered by mocking his dream: "call the sun and the moon and the eleven stars to comfort you" (from the reports in al-Ṭabarī and al-Ṭabarsī). The material is a mixture of scriptural parallel and popular amplification; the Qur'an omits the beating, the thirst, and the mockery entirely, and it is worth pausing on why. A text that intended to move its audience by pathos would have kept it; the sūrah keeps instead the *resolution* and the *revelation*, because its subject is not the injury but the presence of God inside it. Al-Zamakhsharī's rhetorical principle — that the Qur'an's concision is a form of moral seriousness — is nowhere more visible.

**"They Resolved" — Ijmāʿ Used of a Sin**

*Ajmaʿū*, from the same root as *ijmāʿ*, consensus: the verb means to gather one's will into a single determination, and the sūrah applies it, strikingly, to eleven brothers agreeing on a crime. Al-Rāzī notes the usage, and it deserves to be kept in mind whenever the word "consensus" appears in religious discussion: unanimity is a fact about people, not a property that sanctifies. The verse puts the sūrah's own answer beside it: their *ijmāʿ* was immediately followed by revelation to the victim, so that the chapter's earliest lesson about collective agreement is that no assembly, however unanimous, has the last word about a person's fate. The next use of a "group" word in the sūrah is the brothers' "we are a *ʿuṣbah*" (12:8, 14), and the pattern holds: the group is real and it is not final.

**"The Depths of the Well" — and What the Pit Means**

*Ghayābat al-biʾr*: the hidden bottom, the place where what is thrown there cannot be seen. *Ghayāb* is from *ghāba*, "to be out of sight" (it gives *ghāʾib*, the unseen, and *ghayb*), and the sūrah's choice of the word rather than *qār* (bottom) or *quʿr* (depth) puts the emphasis on concealment, which is the only part of the plan the brothers could rely upon: they could not control whether he died, only whether he was seen. Al-Ṭabarī reports that the well was "spacious at the bottom, narrow at the top," details of a kind the text itself does not supply.

The Qur'an's other pits, wells, and enclosed places form a set with this one, and the set is a doctrine: the belly of the whale that holds Yūnus (21:87; 37:142), the basket on the water of Moses (20:39; 28:7), the cave of the two in flight (9:40), the fire of Abraham (21:69). In each case the enclosure is intended as a grave and is used as a transport; in each case the one inside is addressed in a way that changes what the enclosure is. Ibn al-Qayyim, in his *al-Jawāb al-Kāfī*, argues that God's protective care is most often exercised inside the very thing that was meant to destroy, and this verse is his proof-case: Yūsuf is not lifted out of the well before he is spoken to; the revelation comes while he is in it.

**"We Revealed to Him" — the Modes of Waḥy**

*Awḥaynā ilayhi*. The verb of inspiration is used here of a boy in a hole, and our source's gloss is careful: this came "through the angel or by inspiration (*ilhām*)," and some of the commentators (al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarsī) held that this was the moment Yūsuf's prophethood began, since *waḥy* in the technical sense is what makes a prophet. The sūrah's own general teaching is that God's speech to a human being comes in graded modes:

> *"And it is not fitting for a human being that God should speak to him except by inspiration (waḥyan), or from behind a veil, or that He send a messenger who reveals by His permission what He wills. Indeed, He is Exalted, Wise."* (Qur'an 42:51)

The three modes named there — direct inspiration, speech from behind a barrier (Moses at the burning bush, 20:11-12), and a sent messenger (Gabriel, 2:97; 81:19-25) — are the map by which the commentators locate this verse, and they use the parallel case of Hājar's and Moses' mother, to whom God "revealed" (*awḥaynā*) instruction that is not law but care (28:7, "and We inspired to the mother of Moses: 'nurse him'"). Al-Qurṭubī and Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī both discuss whether such *ilhām* counts as prophethood, and the practical upshot they draw is the one this sūrah needs: the one who is sinned against is not left without a teacher. Whatever the classification, the boy in the well is being instructed by God, which is the sūrah's point and not a secondary one.

**"You Will Surely Inform Them of This Affair" — a Promise with Two Edges**

*Lataunabbi'annahum bi-hādhā al-amr* — the double lam (oath and answer), the verb of "informing," the demonstrative attached to *amr*, "this affair." Two edges are visible in the sentence, and the commentators note both.

The first is consoling: the promise is not "you will be avenged" but "they will be told," and being told is a greater thing for a boy in a well than being revenged would have been for a man in a palace, because it means that the truth survives and comes back to him intact. Yūsuf will indeed tell them, at 12:89-90 — *"Do you know what you did with Yūsuf and his brother, while you were unaware?"* — and the address then will be an interrogation that leads to forgiveness rather than to humiliation. This is a rare scriptural use of a promised disclosure: it is scheduled as mercy.

The second edge is the qualifier: *wa-hum lā yashʿurūn* — "while they are unaware." Our source preserves the two readings. On the first, taken by al-Ṭabarī and many, "unaware" attaches to the future moment: they will be confronted at a time when they have no idea it is coming, which the sūrah fulfils with unusual precision at 12:58: *"And Yūsuf recognised his brothers, while they were unrecognising of him (wa-hum lahu munkirūn)."* The power he gains is a power to see without being seen; their loss is that they never see what is in front of them until they are told. On the second reading, "while they are unaware" attaches to the moment in the well, and the sense is that God was revealing to Yūsuf what the brothers did not perceive even as they did it — a plain statement of the sūrah's providence: a plot is always being run inside a plot. Al-Rāzī's note on the verse explains why the disclosure is withheld from the brothers rather than announced to them: had they known that the boy they threw into the well was being addressed by God, their envy would have turned to killing. The concealment of revelation is thus itself an act of protection.

**The Root of "Unaware" and the Chapter's Epistemology**

*Yashʿurūn* is from *sh-ʿ-r*, "to feel, to perceive," the root that also gives *shiʿr*, poetry, because the poet was held to perceive what others do not. This is more than an etymology in this sūrah, which is built on perception-tests: Jacob knows something his sons do not and says so ("I know from God what you do not know," 12:86); the women of the city see Yūsuf and cut their hands without knowing they have done it (12:31); the brothers weigh their money and find it in their sacks and read it as any omen but the true one (12:65); Potiphar's wife's story is believed by everyone and disproved by one torn garment (12:26-28). *Ghayābat al-biʾr* and *lā yashʿurūn* belong to the same field of vision-language: the well hides, the sūrah exists to undo that concealment — "they will be informed" — and the reader is being trained, from verse 1's "clarifying Book," to perceive what a careless eye passes over (12:105).

**Practical Reach: What a Promise Does for a Person in a Pit**

The sūrah's psychology of wrongful harm is compressed into the middle of this verse. Yūsuf is not given an escape plan, a rescuer's name, or a timeline; he is given a certainty about the ending — *what has been done to you will be told, and they will not have seen it coming*. The classical writers on *ṣabr* draw the general rule from this: relief in a crisis is usually administered as meaning before it is administered as rescue, and a person who knows where his story ends can bear the chapter he is in. That is also why the verse has been read at funerals and in prison cells in Muslim communities for fourteen centuries: not as a promise that the innocent will be avenged, but as a promise that the innocent will not be the last word — and that the ones who buried the truth will find it standing in front of them, at a moment they do not sense.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:16

> **16** *And in the evening they came weeping unto their father.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"At Nightfall" — the Timing of a Performance**

*Jāʾū abāhum ʿishāʾan yabkūn*. The time is stated before the emotion, and it matters. *ʿIshāʾ* is the darkness of early night (the root gives *ʿashiy*, evening, and names the night prayer), and a household in that light sees less: faces are harder to read, tears are less checkable, and no one else is present to ask a second question. The sūrah's commentators note the arrangement without editorialising, and the point is exact: a lie that must be performed chooses the conditions of its performance. It is the same discipline the brothers showed in choosing a well that hides (12:10, 12:15, where *ghayāb* and *ghaflah* are the operative words) and in asking for a single day (12:12, "tomorrow"). Al-Zamakhsharī, whose commentary is unusually attentive to such staging, treats *ʿishāʾan* as the sūrah's first piece of evidence against them: an innocent party comes with news as it is; a guilty one comes at an hour and with a manner.

**"Weeping" — a Verb in the Unfinished Tense**

*Yabkūn* is the imperfect, which in Arabic indicates that the accompanying action is continuous or recurrent at the time of the main verb: they came *in a state of weeping*, not "they wept once." The grammar alone tells the reader that crying is being presented, displayed, sustained. The rhetorical tradition treats this as a small masterpiece of exposure: the Qur'an never says "they pretended to weep," because it does not need to — the presentation of weeping as an accompanying circumstance, together with the verse's omission of Yūsuf, is enough.

Al-Qushayrī, in a passage our source records, draws the harder lesson: the ease of the performance shows how far they had travelled. It is his observation that when hypocrisy becomes total, a person acquires command over his own body — even over his tears — so that what in an honest man is an involuntary sign becomes in a corrupt one a tool. The ethical principle behind it is stated in the Qur'an as the mechanism of hardening:

> *"And when Moses said to his people: 'O my people, why do you harm me, while you know that I am God's messenger to you?' So when they deviated, God deviated their hearts. And God does not guide a people who are lawlessly wicked."* (Qur'an 61:5)

That is the sūrah's soberest claim about the brothers in this verse: they are not shown agonising over the lie but executing it smoothly, and the smoothness is the diagnosis. The tradition's sound counter-image is worth setting beside their tears, because it measures the difference between a performance and a condition:

> *"Two eyes that the Fire will not touch: an eye that wept from fear of God, and an eye that stayed awake in God's cause, guarding on the frontier."* (al-Tirmidhī 1639, from Ibn ʿAbbās — graded ḥasan)

**A Father Who Reasons: Jacob's First Question**

Our source records what the commentators extract from the next verse: that Yaʿqūb, seeing them weeping, asked first whether something had happened to their sheep in the pasture (al-Baghawī, al-Qurṭubī, al-Ṭabarī), and when they answered nothing had happened to the flock, he pressed them for what had happened to Yūsuf. The order is a piece of reasoning by elimination that the text quietly approves: a wolf comes for the flock; if the flock is whole, then the animal was not the wolf they feared; therefore the report is about something else. The Qur'an shows a prophet arriving at a suspicion through evidence, which is one of this chapter's more consequential features for Islamic thought — the commentators of *uṣūl* point to it in discussing how knowledge is built from indications (*amārāt*) rather than from pronouncements. The same forensic instinct will appear twice in the next two verses: at 12:18 (a shirt unrent by fangs) and at 12:26, where Yūsuf himself settles the case of the torn garment by asking which way it was torn.

**Why the Brothers Feared Belief and Why They Needed It**

The mistrust that makes their performance necessary was itself announced by them: "what is the matter with you, that you do not trust us?" (12:11). Our source's note here is important theologically: Yaʿqūb's refusal to believe them, unlike the Genesis account where Israel accepts the brothers' report and mourns (Genesis 37:33), preserves for the prophet a knowledge appropriate to his household, and the note adds that this very suspicion will later cost them — because when they come back with the true report about the returned merchandise (12:65) and with a truthful plea (12:81-83), the father still cannot credit them. The sūrah's observation about liars is not that they are caught but that they are disqualified: a person who manufactures suspicion has produced a fact about himself that will outlive every subsequent truth he tells. That is why verse 17's line — "you would not believe us even if we were sincere" — is not a quip but the criminal's own verdict on his credibility, and the law of testimony in Islam builds on the same psychology, requiring the witness to be *ʿadl*, of established uprightness, because a known falsehood permanently degrades the evidentiary value of a man's speech.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

Two omissions should be registered, because they shape how Muslim readers have used this sūrah. The text says nothing about Yūsuf in the well at this moment — no thirst, no darkness, no crying — although the exegetical literature is full of such details, most of them popular amplification rather than transmitted knowledge. And it says nothing of any miracle of rescue: the boy has already been told (12:15) that the affair will be recounted, and the sūrah's silence at the point of maximum drama is its way of insisting that the story is not about what happened to him in the pit but about what God was doing while it happened. Both omissions give the reader an ethic: the wronged man's suffering is not spectacle, and the report of the wrong, in this Book, is always told from the side of the one who was lied to rather than the one who lied.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:17

> **17** *They said, “O father! We went racing with one another, and left Joseph with our things, and the wolf ate him. But thou wouldst not believe us, even if we were truthful.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Anatomy of a Convincing Falsehood**

The brothers' statement has four parts, and each is true enough to be checked: they did go out; they did race; they did leave Yūsuf behind; a wolf is a real danger in pastureland. Only the conclusion — *fa-akalahu al-dhiʾb* — is invented, and it is invented in the father's own words (12:13). This is the sūrah's presentation of how deception actually works: not a tissue of fictions but a true narrative with one false hinge, positioned where the listener's inference will run most naturally. The commentators of the *Maqāṣid* tradition draw the legal consequence: a statement composed of verified details is not therefore verified, and a judge does not establish the unproven part of a claim by counting its agreeable parts.

Al-Zamakhsharī notes the connective *fa* in "so the wolf ate him": the particle is used to make the wolf's arrival look like the consequence of their racing, as though the incident followed of itself from an innocent afternoon. The sūrah, which in verse 15 has already told us what they agreed to do, lets the conjunction stand and thereby exposes the technique: the small word *fa* is doing the lying.

**"We Went Racing" — a Verb the Book Uses for Better Purposes**

*Natāsābaqu* is from *sabq*, "to outrun," and in the Qur'an this verb-cluster is overwhelmingly devoted to competition in good:

> *"…so race to good deeds (fa-fastabiqu al-khayrāt); unto God is your return all together, and He will inform you about that over which you used to differ."* (Qur'an 5:48; cf. 2:148; 23:61)

> *"Race (sābiqū) to forgiveness from your Lord and a Garden as wide as the heavens and the earth."* (Qur'an 57:21)

Even the sun and moon are described with it: *"each swimming (yasbaḥūn) in an orbit"* (36:40). The brothers' race is therefore a parody-instance of a noble verb, and the sūrah's diction draws the reader's attention to what the race was *for*. Genesis 37:15 has the men turn aside to feed a flock and a man directing them; the Qur'an keeps the competitive sport and adds the detail that its purpose was training — for warfare and for the wolf, as the reports in our source state (Rāzī: they raced "to train themselves so they could fight enemies and the wolf which had been carrying off their sheep"). This gives the sūrah's audience an extra irony: they are drilling for a combat with the very animal they will name as the murderer of the boy they handed over. Al-Qurṭubī's and al-Rāzī's treatments of such narrative details always return to the same hermeneutic caution — these reports are edifying, not established, and the verse's plain point is that sport and training are no excuse for what is planned in the intervals of them.

**"We Left Yūsuf by Our Goods" — a Person Downgraded to Equipment**

*Tarakhnā Yūsufa ʿinda matāʿinā*. *Matāʿ* is goods, baggage, the things one travels with; and in the Qur'an it is also the standard word for the enjoyments of this life, precisely because they are portable, temporary and unthinking:

> *"Say: the enjoyment (matāʿ) of this world is brief, but the Hereafter is better for one who is mindful of God."* (Qur'an 4:77; cf. 11:3: "your Lord is Forgiving, Merciful — and He gives you enjoyment (yumattiʿkum matāʿan maʿrūfan)"; and 3:197 "a little enjoyment (matāʿun qalīl)")

So the sentence says, in its own noun, what the brothers meant to conceal: they treated a person as part of the kit. It is the same reduction the caravan will complete in the next two verses, when Yūsuf becomes *biḍāʿah*, merchandise (12:19) — a word-play the sūrah appears to arrange deliberately, since the same commercial vocabulary will return when Yūsuf as *al-ʿAzīz* weighs out grain to men who do not recognise him (12:59-60, 12:88). The chapter's economics of human worth are thus opened here, at the point where a family's carelessness is described as a piece of luggage, and closed at 12:90 where the same men are told that "God has been gracious to me" — the one they appraised at a few dirhams being the one through whom they are fed.

**"And the Wolf Ate Him" — the Father's Word Turned into a Report**

The single change in the sentence is from *an yaʾkulahu* ("that the wolf might eat him," verse 13) to *akalahu* ("ate him"). This is why the sūrah keeps the father's odd, specific fear in verse 13: it provides the raw material of the lie and lets the reader watch it being assembled. Al-Bayḍāwī and after him al-Alūsī remark on the same device in the brothers' later plea, "if we are destroyed, we are among the losers" (12:14), which similarly converts a fear into a boast. A repeated word is the fingerprint of a prepared story, and the sūrah is willing to convict on such evidence.

**"You Would Not Believe Us Even If We Were Truthful" — a Confession in Disguise**

*Wa-mā anta bi-muʾminin lanā wa-law kunnā ṣādiqīn*. Our source gives the sense in two directions: al-Bayḍāwī reads it as their admission that they could not convince him because he thought badly of them; and the same line may be taken as their tactical excuse for the absence of witnesses. Both are present in the sentence's grammar, and the second is the more instructive: a *pre-emptive* claim of disbelief inoculates the hearer against his own judgment. Whatever he concludes, they have an answer — if he accepts the report they are exonerated, if he rejects it he has confirmed his distrust of them, so his suspicion becomes the proof of his unfairness. This is why the tradition's ethicists treat it as a sign of a corrupt heart: the liar arranges the conversation so that being caught is impossible rather than so that lying is unnecessary.

The verb chosen for "believe" is, however, the verb of faith — *āmanta bi-*, "to credit, to accept as true" — and the sūrah's own closing line repeats the construction with a universality that should not be missed: *"and most of mankind, however eagerly you desire it, are not believers (bi-muʾminīn)"* (12:103). The same failure of crediting is present in both sentences, at two scales. Al-Rāzī's and Ibn Kathīr's observations converge on the lesson: credibility is a trust (amāna) that a person owns in the eyes of others, and like a trust it can be lost through conduct; a man who has spent it finds that even the truth is inadmissible in his mouth. Islamic law formalises this in the procedure of *al-jarḥ wa-l-taʿdīl* — the scrutiny of witnesses for uprightness (*ʿadālah*) before their speech is accepted as evidence — a science whose entire existence presupposes the verse's anthropology: testimony is not a string of words but a property of a person, and a person who has lied once has altered the evidentiary weight of everything he will ever say again.

**What Yaʿqūb Does Not Do**

Finally, the verse is worth reading for its silences. He does not accuse them of murder; he does not demand the shirt; he does not curse them or report them to a ruler; he does not interrogate them further. His verdict on their claim is the sentence in verse 18 — "rather, your own selves have embellished a matter for you" — and then the two-fold discipline of "beautiful patience, and God is the one whose help is sought." The commentators (our source, al-Qurṭubī on this verse) note that Jacob arrives at the correct conclusion about motive while lacking any admissible evidence: an unrent garment he has not yet seen, a rumour of a flock unharmed, and twenty years of knowledge about his sons. A judge could have done nothing with his suspicion; a father could. The sūrah is thus also a lesson on the ethics of suspicion: *ẓann* is forbidden as a basis for accusation (49:12 — *"avoid much assumption, for some assumption is sin"*), yet it is the ordinary and often the correct working assumption of a person who knows the actors, and the difference between the two lies in what one does with it — Yaʿqūb does not punish on it, does not expose them on it, and does not act as though he were certain; he hands the matter to God and continues to be their father. That is the model of patience the verse will be named for in the next line, and the reason the sūrah's readers have taken "beautiful patience" from this house rather than from any other.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:18

> **18** *And they brought forth his shirt with false blood. He said, “Nay, your souls have seduced you in this matter. Beautiful patience! And God is the One Whose help is sought against that which you describe.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Word That Verdicts a Scene**

*Wa-jāʾū bi-qamīṣihi bi-dam[in] kadhib*. The shirt is mentioned before the blood; the blood before the lie; and the lie is not narrated but attached as an adjective to the blood. Arabic rhetoricians treat this as a case of *majāz* in the idāfa construction, a transferred epithet: the blood was real goat's or lamb's blood (the reports in our source say they slaughtered an animal for the purpose, and compare Genesis 37:31), and what lied was the statement made about it. To call the blood itself "lying" compresses the whole indictment into a single word and, as al-Zamakhsharī observes in his treatment of such transfers, does so without overstating anything — for the blood is nothing but its function in their claim; the object exists here only as evidence, and the evidence is fraudulent, so the object is named by its fraud.

The *qamīṣ* itself deserves attention: the shirt is the sūrah's great instrument, and it appears three times as a decisive document. Here it is forged evidence, and the father sees through it; at 12:26-27 it is torn and decides a capital question in a magistrate's house; at 12:93 and 12:96 it is the medicine of an old man's blindness and the token of a dream's completion. The word for it (*qamīṣ*) is a borrowing into Arabic from a language of the great empires (Middle Persian *kamīs*, cognate with Greek *khitōn*), a fact noted in the classical lexicons (Lisān al-ʿArab; Tāj al-ʿArūs), and it is not incidental that in a story set in Egypt and told in the vocabulary of Canaanite and Egyptian household life the object that carries the truth has a foreign name.

**The Evidence Jacob Cites: a Garment Without Claw-Marks**

Our source preserves the reasoning of the commentators: Yaʿqūb asked them why, if a wolf had devoured his son, the shirt bore no rent corresponding to a struggle — an animal that kills tears; a child taken from a pit does not. The sūrah itself does not spell out the argument, because the verse that follows is doing a different work: the father is not cross-examining, he is concluding. But the argument is Qur'anic in its logic, and is used elsewhere in the chapter at a far greater stake: at 12:26-28 a torn shirt decides between the accusation of assault and the defence of flight, and the torn front-versus-back test is called by the exegetes the *ḥukm bi-l-qarīna*, adjudication by indication — the standard method of the Islamic judge when direct proof is absent. This verse is thus the sūrah's first lesson in the law of evidence: a claim is examined against the physical world, and a lie usually fails at some small point of fit.

**"Rather, Your Own Selves Have Embellished a Matter for You"**

*Bal sawwalat lakum anfusukum amrā*. The verb *sawwala*, from *sawl* (ease, smoothness), means to make a thing appear smooth, manageable, and therefore acceptable; the subject is not Satan but *anfus*, the selfs. The phrase occurs twice in this sūrah, here and at 12:83, and both times it is Yaʿqūb speaking about his sons — a repetition that our source marks, and it is the chapter's own summary of the psychology of wrongdoing. The brothers do not experience their act as a crime, because the self first re-describes it; the crime is not chosen but *embellished into* choice.

This is the sūrah's doctrine in one sentence, and it is the ground of its famous later verse, 12:53: *"truly the self is commandingly evil (ammārah bi-l-sūʾ)"* — the same anthropology stated from the inside by the innocent party rather than from the outside by the father. Between the two occurrences the chapter uses three different words for the sources of a wrongful act, and the classification is exact: the self's *tasyīl* (the brothers, 12:18, 12:83), the woman's *kayd* (12:28, 12:33: "their plot"), and the *nafs*'s inner command (12:53). Each is met with a different remedy in the sūrah's vocabulary: patience for what the self has smoothed, remembrance and refuge for what another plots, and God's turning-away of evil for what the self commands (12:24: "thus We turn away from him evil and indecency").
