The classical ethicists draw the practical conclusion that a person should not trust his own sense that a plan is "reasonable" without asking who has been made to disappear from it, since *tasyīl* is precisely the feeling of smoothness about a decision which harms a person whose presence would otherwise obstruct it. The sūrah's placement of the word here — the first father in the story naming the mechanism, and his own son later confirming it from the other side of the same family's conduct (12:83, "rather, your selves have embellished an affair for you") — is the reason it became a technical term in Muslim moral psychology: al-Ghazālī in *Iḥyāʾ* book of the *nafs*, and Ibn al-Qayyim in *Madārij al-Sālikīn*, both use this verse as the proof that self-justification precedes action, and that its detection is the first work of conscience.

**"Beautiful Patience" — a Technical Term of Grief**

*Fa-ṣabrun jamīl*. Our source gives the definition used across the commentaries: patience that does not complain to anyone but God — with some reading "my patience is (or will be) beautiful," i.e. an ellipsis of the verb. Al-Ṭabarī's phrasing and al-Zamakhsharī's agree on the operative element: the *jamāl* of the patience lies in its not disclosing the wound to those who cannot heal it, and in its not using the wound as a weapon against anyone. Al-Junayd's much-quoted definition in al-Qushayrī's *Laṭāʾif al-Ishārāt* is in the same register: patience is to swallow what is bitter without announcing it. The sūrah uses the phrase three times on Yaʿqūb's lips (12:18, 12:83, and 12:86 with "I complain only to God"), which makes it a refrain — and refrains in this chapter are structural: the first utterance is a lie believed, the second a second loss reported, and the third a despair refused; the same words measure how much the man has been asked to carry, and how he carries it the second and third time exactly as he said he would the first.

The Prophet's ﷺ own definition of the quality, delivered on a quite different occasion, is the sharpest commentary available:

> *"Patience is at the first strike (al-ṣabru ʿinda al-ṣadma al-ūlā)."* (Bukhārī 1283; Muslim 926, from Anas)

That is, patience is not a resource one draws on later; it is the quality of the first instant, and anyone can be patient after the shock has spent itself. Yaʿqūb is shown performing it at the moment the shirt is put in front of him, which is the sūrah's verdict on the sincerity of what he had said years earlier about the wolf ("patience is beautiful," he had not said then — he said it now).

**"And God Is the One Whose Help Is Sought Against What You Describe"**

*Wa-Allāhu al-mustaʿānu ʿalā mā taṣifūn*. Two details of the Arabic do real work. The form *mustaʿān* is a passive participle with the sense of "the one whose help is sought," so the sentence makes God the destination of the request rather than the recipient of a complaint: help is *asked of* Him *against* (ʿalā) the description they have offered. And the verb is *taṣifūn*, "you describe," not "you lie" — Yaʿqūb refuses, in the very sentence of his accusation, to name the crime; he names the speech-act. The courtesy is not weakness. In the Qur'an, the same verb-cluster is used of those who "describe" God with what He is not (37:159: *"glorified is God above what they describe"*), so that *waṣf/taṣīf* carries in this Book a flavour of overreach: speech that passes beyond what the speaker is entitled to say. Al-Zamakhsharī's and al-Bayḍāwī's notes both mark it, and the near-identical phrase at 21:112 — the closing verse of Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ, where the command is: *"Say: My Lord, judge with the truth; and our Lord is the Most-Merciful, the One whose help is sought against what you describe"* — shows the idiom used across the Book for a case handed to God because it cannot be handed to a court.

The practical form of the sentence — a statement of what has happened, an attribution of motive to the selfs involved, a refusal to accuse further, and a transfer of the case to God — is what the jurists later cite when discussing the conduct of a plaintiff who lacks proof: he may say what he knows and no more, and he may not convert probability into verdict. That is why the preachers and the writers of adab have treated the household of Yaʿqūb as the paradigm of forbearance in dispute, and why "beautiful patience" became, in Muslim usage, not stoicism but a form of testimony: the man who is patient in the described way is making a claim about God's knowledge of the case, and the sūrah rewards it by reopening the case at 12:86-100 in his favour.

**The Verse's Place in the Whole**

Verse 18 is the hinge of the sūrah's first act. The plot has been completed (9-15), the report delivered (16-17), and the evidence forged (18); and the response to it establishes the chapter's moral centre. Nothing is done to the brothers; they are not punished, disowned, or investigated. Instead, the father states the psychology (verses 8 and 18 both describe an interior operation), performs the ethic, and the narrative moves — with a speed the sūrah has not used before — to the caravan and the well (12:19). The reader is left with the chapter's first fully formed virtue, and with the knowledge that God, not the household, is handling the case: the same knowledge that will make Yūsuf, twenty years later and holding the lives of the whole family in his hands, say "God has been gracious to us, truly whoever is mindful and patient — God does not lose the wage of the beautiful-doers" (12:90), and cancel it with "no reproach upon you today" (12:92). The father's beautiful patience is the seed of the son's beautiful forgiveness, and the sūrah has planted it in a sentence of three clauses about a shirt.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:19

> **19** *And then a caravan came along and sent their water carrier, and he let down his bucket. He said, “Good news! Here is a boy!” So they hid him as merchandise. And God knew well what they were doing.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Caravan, a Bucket, and the Rejection of Chance**

*Wa-jāʾat sayyāratun fa-arasalū wāridahum fa-adlā dalwah*. The *sayyārah* is a company of travellers (the pattern *faʿālah* in Arabic often denotes a group engaged in an activity — *qāflah*, *rukbān*, *sayyārah*), and the *wārid* is the one whose job it is to reach the water first, from *wurūd*, "coming to the watering place." Our source records the tradition's details — that the boy had been in the well three days, that the caravan was going from Midian toward Egypt, and that it had lost its way and come upon the well by chance (Ibn Kathīr's and al-Baghawī's reports) — and then adds nothing about chance itself, because the last clause of the verse does the theological work: *wa-Allāhu ʿalīmun bi-mā yaʿmalūn*, "and God is knowing concerning what they do." That clause brackets the whole incident, and the bracket is the sūrah's standing answer to luck: events in this chapter arrive fortuitously for the actors and by knowledge for God. Compare 12:21's "and We established Yūsuf in the land," where the same caravan's commerce turns out to have been a placement.

The verb *adlā* ("he let down [the bucket]") is used in Arabic precisely for lowering a vessel into a well on a rope, and the sūrah's camera stays on the rope: Yūsuf's exit from the pit is achieved by the ordinary equipment of a water-drawer. This is the chapter's constant method — the elevation of Yūsuf never requires a miracle, only the availability of a means. The well was hidden (*ghayābat*, 12:15); the rescue is a bucket.

**"O Good News! This Is a Boy!"**

*Qāla yā bushrā hādhā ghulām*. *Bushrā* is glad tidings, and its cognate *bashīr* is the title the sūrah will reserve for the news that restores Yaʿqūb's sight: "when the *bashīr* came, he placed it [the shirt] upon his face" (12:96), and earlier "they came to your father with *bashīr*" — the sūrah's word for the good report that the brothers' lie destroyed. The cry of a water-drawer finding a child is thus spoken in the same vocabulary the chapter uses for revelation and for the return of a son, and the reader who has followed verses 4-15 will hear an echo of verse 3: *nuqīṣṣu* — God's *bishārah* to a grieving old man is being prepared by an incidental shout at a well.

The word for the child is *ghulām*, "a boy," and the word's later history in Arabic, from "a youth" to "a servant, a retainer," shadows the fate the verse records: within two lines the boy is merchandise. There is also a lexical note worth recording because the Qur'an's usage is deliberate: *ghulām* in the Qur'an is used of young men generally, and in a striking phrase of this sūrah (12:23, 12:25, 12:30, 12:33, 12:51) the women of Egypt call Yūsuf "her *ghulām*" — her boy-slave — even though he has by then been educated in the house for years and later becomes *al-ʿAzīz*; the social label sticks, and the sūrah preserves it.

**"They Concealed Him as Merchandise"**

*Wa-asarrūhu biḍāʿah*. The verb is *sarra*, to hide, and the noun *biḍāʿah* is goods held for trade. Our source gives two construals: that they concealed him, treating him as part of their cargo so no one would claim him, and that they said of him "this is merchandise" in order to make his presence in the caravan look lawful. Al-Ṭabarī's and al-Zamakhsharī's readings both turn on a single moral fact: to make a person saleable one must first make him invisible as a person, which is why the brothers hid him in a hole and the caravan hides him inside a category. The sentence's structure performs that reduction: there is no verb of asking, no "who are you," no "what is your name" — a boy is declared a thing.

This is also the point at which the sūrah's earlier law of *luqṭah* (see the note on verse 10) closes: a found object of value is to be publicly announced, and a found child, in the law's own reasoning, must be freed and maintained, never sold. The Prophet ﷺ made the sale of a free person one of three transactions that God Himself opposes:

> *"God says: Three I will be the opponent of on the Day of Resurrection: a man who gives in My name and then betrays; a man who sells a free person and consumes his price; and a man who hires a worker, takes full measure from him, and does not give him his wage."* (Bukhārī 2227, from Abū Hurayrah)

**"And God Is Knowing Concerning What They Do" — the Sūrah's Signature Clause**

Our source's commentary on the sūrah's closing note here is worth generalising, because the phrase recurs at moments of decision in this chapter (12:20 "they valued him not — and God knew"; 12:25 "then he would have inclined… but he saw the proof of his Lord"; 12:83 "God is Knowing, Wise"). The clause is not a threat; it is the sūrah's statement of the frame. Every act in the well, the concealment, and the sale is being witnessed by a knowledge that will convert it, in the end, into testimony that the actors themselves will hear from the boy's mouth (12:15's promise, fulfilled at 12:89-90). The commentators of *al-tawḥīd* use this class of verse for a specific doctrinal point: God's knowledge (*ʿilm*) encompasses human acts before and after they are done, which is the ground on which the sūrah can both condemn the action and guarantee its disclosure. Al-Ashʿarī's and al-Māturīdī's readings of the verse converge on the practical discipline the chapter teaches: a person who believes he is unobserved will plan, and a person who believes he is observed will act — and everything in this sūrah happens to people who forgot the second belief.

**What the Verse Omits**

Two absences should be marked. First, there is no account here of Yūsuf's interior state in the well; the sūrah gives us one line of revelation (12:15) and no description of fear, thirst, or deliverance — a restraint which the tradition's preachers used to argue that God sometimes relieves a person by not making the ordeal narratable at all, and which the exegetes supply from the reports our source records (the water bitter then sweet, an amulet containing a shirt from Paradise brought to Abraham at the time of the fire, and Gabriel placing it on the boy). Those narratives are popular and imaginative; they are not Qur'anic, and the note of 12:15's *ilhaām* — God speaking to the boy and promising disclosure rather than rescue — is the sūrah's own, and enough.

Second, the brothers are not mentioned. This is the last we hear of them for forty verses; they have got exactly what they asked for in verse 9 ("cast him out into some land") and the text declines to say "be careful what you wish." It will say it later, in their own mouths, when the man they sold asks for a brother, and the whole family is brought to a granary to be measured out grain by the person they disposed of (12:59-66). The verse's real subject is therefore not the caravan at all, but the sūrah's method: a plot closes, and a provision (*rizq*) opens at the same bucket, in the same word, with no transition except "God is knowing what they do."
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:20

> **20** *And they sold him for a low price, a number of dirhams, for they valued him not.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Price That Is Also an Indictment**

*Wa-shatarūhu bi-thamanin bakhṣin dārahima maʿdūdat*. *Bakhṣ* is not merely "low." It is the word for a price that defrauds — to give less than a thing is worth — and its verb occurs in the Qur'an in the law's own vocabulary of the market:

> *"…and do not defraud (lā tabkhasū) people of their goods."* (Qur'an 11:85, the sermon of Shuʿayb)

and in the chapter on weights: *"Woe to those who give short measure, who when they take the measure from the people exact it in full, and when they measure or weigh for them give less (yakhṣirūn)"* (83:1-3). The sūrah has thus priced Yūsuf with the exact verb the Qur'an elsewhere forbids, and the reader is meant to notice that the sin in this verse is not "selling a slave" as an institution but *bakhṣ* — the suppression of a person's due. Who does the suppressing? Our source notes the point recorded by al-Rāzī: that a low price is a mark of a free person taken unlawfully, since no honest market could rate a kidnapped child at pocket-change; the price is itself the confession that the transaction was theft. Al-Ṭabarī preserves a second nuance: any price paid for him was *bakhṣ*, because the thing sold was a soul.

**"Counted Dirhams" — the Grammar of Contempt**

*Darāhima maʿdūdat*. In the economies of the ancient Near East, significant sums moved by weight of metal; *maʿdūda*, "counted out," is how one pays in coins, by number, for small and ordinary purchases. The phrase therefore does double duty: it makes the sum trivial (a handful of coins) and it makes the payment grudging — counted, not weighed; the seller keeping an eye on the pile. The transmitted reports give the figure as twenty, twenty-two, or forty dirhams (numbers supplied by the exegetes from earlier traditions, not by the text), and some of them explain the brothers' share of it. None of that is Qur'anic; the verse's own information is the rhetoric: a human life, valued by the market at what could be counted on the fingers.

The sūrah's audience in Makkah knew the price of a slave, and this is part of the point: the chapter is willing to record an exact social fact about the valuation of persons. The man who was sold for counted coins will sit, in the same sūrah, in judgement over a nation's grain, insisting that the measure be filled and that short weight be returned to its owners (12:59, 12:88). The chapter thus builds one of its quietest arguments: a person's worth is not what the market says, and the market's verdict is exactly the thing a righteous administrator exists to correct.

**"And They Were in Him of the Disinclined"**

*Wa-kānū fīhi mina al-zāhidīn*. The root *z-h-d* yields in ordinary Arabic the sense of not wanting a thing, and here the meaning is contemptuous indifference: they did not want him, did not care for him, thought little of him. Our source records two construals of the clause: either that they set no store by him, treating him as scavenged goods, or that they did not wish to keep him — fearing, as al-Rāzī suggests, that he would be claimed as a stolen free person. Both are present in the sentence's plain sense, and both are damning: a person's fate in a market is decided by whether the buyers want him, and the sūrah tells us that the verdict was "not much."

The word carries, of course, a technical history in Islamic ethics: *zuhd* became the name for the discipline of detachment from the world, and *zāhid* the ascetic. The sūrah's use here is pre-technical and, for that reason, instructive: *zuhd* in the Qur'an's own diction can mean either a virtuous indifference to what is not worth having or a wicked indifference to a person one is obliged to value. The chapter keeps both senses in play about its hero: the men who were *zāhidūn* in Yūsuf sell him cheaply, and Yūsuf himself proves to be the real ascetic in the story — refusing the offered body (12:23), refusing release until the truth about him is public (12:50), and finally asking not for the palace but for a death in submission (12:101: *"take me in submission, and let me die as a Muslim"*). Ibn Kathīr and al-Qushayrī both make this the sūrah's answer to verse 20: a soul that the market valued at a few dirhams was valued by God at prophethood, and the disparity between the two valuations is the chapter's whole argument about worth.

**They Bought the World with the Hereafter**

The verb used for the transaction is *sharā*, "to buy," and the Qur'an's great idiom for misplaced valuation is the same verb:

> *"Those are the ones who bought (staraw) the life of this world for the price of the Hereafter; their punishment will not be lightened, and they will not be helped."* (Qur'an 2:86; cf. 4:74)

Read with 12:20, the idiom exposes the brothers' side of the bargain, which the verse never mentions. They were not the sellers in the sentence's grammar — the caravan sold — but they produced the merchandise, and what they received was: a father's unhealed grief, a lost brother, twenty-some years of a man they could not recognise in a royal court, and a family obliged to beg for rations. In the language of the Book, they sold the Hereafter for a few dirhams of emotional relief, and lost both. This is the practical meaning of the *āyāt* promised to the inquiring in verse 7: the chapter shows what a bad trade is, and it uses the vocabulary of trade all the way through.

**"And God Is Knowing Concerning What They Do" — the Clause That Ends the Scene**

The closing line of verse 19 hangs over this verse as well: the transaction is concluded in the market and recorded in knowledge. Al-Rāzī's discussion of the pair of verses isolates the sūrah's use of the passive of *ʿamila* — "what they *do*" — rather than "what they did": the divine record is not a report of a closed event but a present tense about the actors, and the chapter's later disclosure (12:89-90) is the opening of that record. The reader is thus given a rule: the price at which a person is sold in this world is not the price at which his case is finally valued; the market sets a figure, knowledge sets the account, and the account comes due — in this sūrah, not in punishment but in forgiveness, which is the more terrifying of the two for the men who counted the coins.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:21

> **21** *The man from Egypt who bought him said unto his wife, “Give him honorable accommodation. It may be that he will bring us some benefit, or that we may take him as a son.” And thus did We establish Joseph in the land, that We might teach him the interpretation of events. And God prevails over His affair, but most of mankind know not.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Al-ʿAzīz: A Title, Not a Name**

*Al-ʿazīz* is a rank, not a person's name — the word denotes one who is mighty, cherished, or held in a high position, and our source explains that the sūrah uses it of whoever occupies the office (see 12:30, 51, 78, 88, where the same title is given also to Yūsuf himself after his appointment). The exegetes identify the buyer with the biblical Potiphar (Genesis 39:1), variously as *Qiṭfīr* or *Iṭfīr*, and as "chief of the guard," while other reports make him keeper of the treasury (a reading our source notes may be inferred from Yūsuf's later office over "the storehouses of the land," 12:55, 12:78, 12:88). Two observations of the sūrah's precision belong here. The first is that it does not call the ruler *Firʿawn*: in this chapter he is "the king" (*al-malik*, 12:43, 50, 54, 76), while in the Moses narrative the ruler is always Pharaoh. Modern readers have seen in this a point of historical accuracy, since the title "Pharaoh" (*per-aa*, "great house") attaches to the throne only in the later New Kingdom; the classical commentators, who had no such chronology to work with, were content to gloss *malik* as king and *ʿazīz* as a high official, and Ibn Kathīr notes simply that the sūrah's usage fits the period it depicts. The second is the woman's naming: the Qur'an never names her. It calls her "the woman in whose house he was" (*allatī hiya fī baytihi*, 12:23) and "his wife" — the name Zulaykhā (or Rāʿīl) belongs to the transmitted reports and to the great Persian romance of Jāmī, not to the text. The sūrah's restraint is a discipline, not an omission: it defines her by her position in the household and by what she does, refusing to convert her into a legend's heroine.

**"Make His Lodging Honourable" — the Ethics of a Household with a Servant in It**

*Akrimī mathwāhu*. The *mathwā* is a lodging, a place of settling; and "honour the lodging" is not a euphemism for comfortable furniture but a rule of treatment, the same idiom as the Qur'an's language of hospitality, in which a guest's standing is measured by how he is seated and served (cf. 51:26-27, where Abraham's guests are honoured with a roasted calf and a "do not fear" before anything is asked; and 11:69-70 with Lot's "this is my daughter, she is purer for you," and 12:31 where the women are given a repast and knives — the sūrah's hospitality scenes are always also tests). Al-Qurṭubī and al-Zamakhsharī both note the instruction's domestic reach: the word is addressed to the wife, which means that in this household the duty of kind treatment is a joint obligation, and the master of the house discharges his moral responsibility by commanding his wife to honour the person he has bought. The instruction also, as the commentators observe, contains a rebuke in advance: it will be the wife who fails to honour the lodging given to her, and the sūrah has her husband state the obligation before she breaks it (12:23-25). Nothing in the text is more quietly damning than a recorded instruction that is not followed.

**"That He May Be of Use to Us, or That We May Adopt Him as a Son"**

*ʿAsā an yanfaʿanā aw nattakhidhahu waladā*. Our source flags that the sentence is verbatim the words of Pharaoh's wife about the infant Moses (28:9) — the same household of Egypt, the same hope, the same two options, and in both cases a child bought or rescued from a death that the powerful intended for him: "and We inspired to the mother of Moses: 'nurse him; and when you fear for him, cast him into the river, and fear not, grieve not; and We will return him to you and make him of the messengers'" (28:7). The verbal echo is one of the Qur'an's clearest instances of two narratives illuminating one another, and the differences are as pointed as the sameness: in Moses' case the household is the persecutor of the child's people and the adoption will end in its overthrow; in Yūsuf's the household is merely a household, the adoption is proposed and not carried out, and the benefit is real — he becomes, in this same verse's second half, the means by which the region is fed, and the master's wife is exposed.

The proposal of adoption is worth a note of its own, since it is one of the places where the Qur'an's narrative and the later law are read together. The Arabic for adoption here is *takhādhuhu waladan*, "to take him as a child," and Islam legislated a decisive distinction between the fact of raising a child and the fiction of transferring his lineage:

> *"And call them [your wards] by the names of their fathers; that is more just with God. And if you know not their fathers, then they are your brothers in religion and your protégés (mawālīkum)."* (Qur'an 33:5; and 33:4: "He has not made your claimed sons your sons")

The sūrah is not abrogating anything — it is reporting a 2nd-millennium household's hope — but the reader who knows the later law sees its shape already present in the verse: Potiphar offers a *position*, "as a son," and the text keeps it in the conditional. The boy will in fact be treated better than a son, and the chapter's own witness to it is Yūsuf's sentence in prison: *"that is of what my Lord (rabbī) has taught me"* (12:37) — the same word he used in the house, in the crisis of the accusation, for the man who had bought him: *"God be my refuge! Truly he is my lord (rabbī), he has made my lodging honourable"* (12:23). The commentators debate which lord is meant at 12:23 (our source records both readings: the ʿazīz, or God Himself), and the double usage is not confusion but hierarchy — the one who housed him and the One who taught him, named by the same word because both acts are, in Yūsuf's speech, kindnesses that oblige him.

**"And Thus We Established Yūsuf in the Land" — Position as Curriculum**

*Kadhālika makannā li-Yūsuf fi al-arḍ li-nuʿallimahu min ta'wīl al-aḥādīth*. *Makkannā* is from *makān*, "place": We gave him a place, settled him, made a room in the world for him — the verb the Qur'an uses when it speaks of a people's being located in a land for a test (7:165: *"and He made you successors upon the earth and raised some of you above others in degrees, so that He might try you in what He gave you"*). The crucial word is the purpose clause: the establishment is *in order that* We teach him. Position is not the reward here but the classroom; the office of a great house is the syllabus for a man who will one day judge Egypt's famine. This reverses a reading of the story as rise-and-fortune: in the sūrah's own grammar, the house of the ʿazīz, the marketplace, and later the prison (12:39: "in the houses you eat in") are all *taʿlīm*, and the teaching named is *ta'wīl al-aḥādīth* — the interpretation of events, promised in verse 6 and delivered across the chapter's four dream-scenes (12:36-41, 12:43-49). Al-Rāzī's remark in our source is exactly on point: this gift was the means of his ascendancy; his rise is a function of what he was taught, not the other way about.

**"God Prevails over His Affair, but Most of Mankind Know Not"**

*Wa-Allāhu ghālibun ʿalā amrihi*. The sentence is the sūrah's own summary of everything from the well to the palace, and it has been read by the commentators as a general maxim about how God's purposes are executed. Two construals are recorded: that God is dominant over what He wills in His affair, so that nothing frustrates it; and (the sense al-Zamakhsharī favours) that He is *ghālib* in the sense of "prevailing gently," overcoming opposition by means the opponents do not perceive — the *lutf* of 12:100. Both are in the verse: the brothers willed the pit, the caravan willed a cargo, Potiphar willed a servant, and what was achieved was the placement of a prophet in a granary, which none of them willed or could have planned. The clause is close in function to the Book's recurring statement on the same theme:

> *"They wish to extinguish the light of God with their mouths, but God refuses except to complete His light, though the disbelievers dislike it."* (Qur'an 61:8; cf. 9:32)

The tail — *wa-lākinna akthar al-nās lā yaʿlamūn* — is one of the sūrah's repeated refrains (also at 12:40 and 12:68; and in the Qur'an at 7:187, 30:6-7, 40:57), and its sense here is specific: the majority of people do not know that history's operative will is God's, because they judge by the visible intentions of the visible powerful. Of the brothers, of the caravan, of the women of the city, and of the ʿazīz's household, each acts on what he or she sees; and the verse's epistemology explains the sūrah's otherwise surprising gentleness toward them: they are not villains in the sense of rivals to God, they are *those who do not know* — which is also the condition from which the chapter's ending rescues the brothers, when the man they sold says "no reproach upon you today" (12:92).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:22

> **22** *When he reached his maturity, We gave him wisdom and knowledge. Thus do We recompense the virtuous.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Functions as a Chapter Break**

*Wa-lammā balagha ashuddahu ātaynāhu ḥukman wa-ʿilmā*. Structurally, verse 22 ends the story of the well and the purchase and turns the sūrah toward the crisis of the house; content-wise, it is one of the Qur'an's most concentrated statements about qualification. Everything between Yūsuf's arrival and his maturity is compressed into a single temporal clause — "when he reached his *ashudd*" — and the reader is given no scene, no years, no training. The compression is itself a teaching: a preparation that no one records is still preparation, and the sūrah's indifference to the interval is a rebuke to the taste that wants the story of the making, not the making.

**"Ashudd" — the Vocabulary of Full Strength**

*Ashudd* is the plural of *shiddah*, "hardness, firmness," and denotes the fullness of bodily and mental powers — the age at which a person's strength and judgement have both arrived. The Qur'an's uses make the double condition explicit. In al-Aḥqāf the same term is joined to a further threshold of discernment:

> *"And We have enjoined upon man, concerning his parents, kindness… until, when he reaches his full strength (balagha ashuddahu) and reaches forty years, he says: 'My Lord, enable me to be grateful for Your favour… and reform for me in my offspring; indeed I have turned to You in repentance, and indeed I am of the Muslims.'"* (Qur'an 46:15)

And in al-Anʿām the maturity of an orphan is defined not by strength alone but by the ability to manage property: *"And do not approach the orphan's property except in a way that is best, until he reaches the age of marriage (bulūgh); so if you perceive in them sound judgement (rushd), release their property to them"* (4:6; cf. 6:152). This is one of the roots of the Islamic legal doctrine of *bulūgh* and *rushd*: majority is physical and intellectual together, and the transfer of responsibility is made to depend on demonstrated capacity rather than on a birthday. Our source's note (Aj) glosses the gift given here as "prophethood, or the ability to judge between people with justice," and the two are not alternatives: the sūrah is describing a man in whom the capacity for governance and the capacity for revelation have both arrived, which is precisely the profile that verse 55 will present when Yūsuf asks for the treasury.

**"Wisdom and Knowledge" — What Is Given and Why**

*Ḥukman wa-ʿilmā*. *Ḥukm* is judgement: the ability to decide, from *ḥakama*, "to govern with restraint" — the word the Arabic uses for a judge's ruling and for the bit that stops a horse; and *ʿilm* is knowledge of what is. The pair is a technical combination in the Qur'an, used of Yūsuf here, of Moses at 28:14 ("when he reached his full strength and attained maturity, We gave him judgement and knowledge; and thus do We recompense the beautiful-doers"), and — most tellingly — in the Qur'an's own catalogue of prophets at 6:84, where Yūsuf is named by name among David, Solomon, Job, Moses and Aaron, and the list is closed by the identical clause of our verse: *"…and Whom We preferred over the worlds… and thus do We recompense the beautiful-doers (wa-kadhālika najzī al-muḥsinīn)"* (6:84, with 6:86-87). The recurrence is not repetitive but definitional: the Qur'an's account of leadership qualification is always the same two nouns, and always followed by "thus do We recompense the muḥsinūn."

The point of the third element — the general law — is to move the verse from biography to doctrine: Yūsuf's wisdom is not an individual favour, it is the standing rate of exchange in God's economy. Al-Rāzī draws the corollary that God's gifts of competence are given to the moral, and not to the clever: the two words cover the moral-intellectual authority to decide (*ḥukm*) and the factual knowledge of the case (*ʿilm*), and the sūrah will demonstrate them in sequence — first the refusal of the house (the *ḥukm* that decides), then the interpretations in the prison and the court (the *ʿilm* that knows), then the administration of the famine (both in office).

**"Thus Do We Recompense the Beautiful-Doers" — Iḥsān Defined**

*Wa-kadhālika najzī al-muḥsinīn*. *Iḥsān* is the noun of doing what is beautiful (from *al-ḥusn*, the opposite of *al-sūʾ*), and the Prophet ﷺ gave it the definition the sūrah here assumes:

> *"Iḥsān is that you worship God as though you see Him, for if you do not see Him, He sees you."* (Bukhārī 50; Muslim 8, from ʿUmar's report of the ḥadīth of Jibrīl)

Our source lists the sūrah's uses of the word: Yūsuf described by the two prisoners as "among the beautiful-doers" (12:36: "we see you as being among the muḥsinīn"), the promise "our Lord is the best of the teachers" and the reward of iḥsān at 12:56 ("indeed whoever fears and is patient — God does not waste the wage of the beautiful-doers"), the brothers' plea "we see you as being among the muḥsinīn" at 12:78, and Yūsuf's own verdict at 12:90, where he applies to himself the very clause of verse 22: *"whoever is mindful and patient — truly God does not lose the wage of the beautiful-doers."* The two verses — 12:22 and 12:90 — are thus the chapter's opening axiom and its closing proof: the same law, tested at the beginning by a youth in a household and at the end by a minister with the power to take revenge and choosing not to.

It follows that in this sūrah *iḥsān* is not sentimental niceness but the specific virtue of doing well at the point where it would be easier to do otherwise: at 12:22 it is the maturity to use strength well, at 12:23-24 the refusal of an unlocked door, at 12:56 the administration of a country, at 12:90-92 the cancellation of a grievance. Al-Qurṭubī, in the sentence our source records, connects the "wisdom and knowledge" given here with "the mysteries and the proper etiquette of servanthood toward God" — and that is the sūrah's own ordering: competence is given to one whose servanthood has already been trained, which is why the Qur'an can say of the same man, decades later, that God "turned away from him evil and indecency" (12:24) and also that he is "knowing" (ʿalīm) enough to be trusted with the storehouses (12:55).

**Why the Sūrah Names a Law Rather Than a Miracle**

The final clause generalises (*kadhālika najzī*), and the generalisation is the practical content for the reader: no miracle marks Yūsuf's maturity, no fire from heaven, no staff turned to a serpent. What the sūrah records instead is a cause-and-effect in God's governance — moral excellence, then competence granted. The commentators of *al-asbāb* observe that this is why the chapter has been used in Islamic education as a manual of formation rather than of wonder: the boy who is fed, housed, taught, and then entrusted with judgement is the model of the *murabban wa-l-muta'allim*, the educated man fit to rule, and 3:79's description of the *rabbānī* — the person formed by God — "because you used to teach the Book and because you used to study it" (*bimā kuntum tuʿallimūna al-kitāba wa-bimā kuntum tadrusūn*) — is the same idea stated as a duty on the learner. A modern reader should notice the order: strength first, then wisdom and knowledge, then office. Any claim to leadership that skips the second item is not, on this sūrah's scale, disqualified by a rule but simply shown to be unprepared — a point the chapter proves twice more, when the man with *ḥukm* is able to see through a stratagem with the cup (12:76-79) that defeats everyone else in the room.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:23

> **23** *But she in whose house he was staying sought to lure him from himself. She locked the doors and said, “Come, thou!” He said, “God be my refuge! Truly He is my lord, and has made beautiful my accommodation. Verily the wrongdoers will not prosper!”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"She Sought Him from Himself" — the Grammar of Seduction**

*Wa-rāwadat-hu allatī hiya fī baytihi ʿan nafsihi*. The verb *rāwada* means to seek, to demand, to court persistently, and its construction here is the thing to notice: she sought him *ʿan nafsihi*, "away from his own self." Arabic idiom treats the seduced person as one being displaced from himself, and the sūrah's later verses keep the same construction — "the wife of the ʿazīz sought him away from himself" (12:30) and "what is the matter with you when you sought Yūsuf away from himself?" (12:51). The root *r-w-d* occurs in the Qur'an only in this episode and, once outside it, in the story of Lot — *"they sought to lure him from himself concerning his guests (rāwadūhu ʿan ḍayfihī), and We blinded their eyes"* (54:37) — so the idiom is reserved in the Book for these two scenes of a household besieged over a guest: in the Book's diction, the danger is not that a person will obtain something but that he will be moved outside his own self, and the phrase is the sūrah's own definition of temptation as self-alienation. The theological psychology that follows is exact: a man who "wants" in this way has already stopped being the one who decides.

The woman is identified only by her position: *allatī hiya fī baytihi*, "she in whose house he was" — a phrase with no name and no honorific, and it does two things at once. It states her power (the house is hers, and Yūsuf is in it, which is the whole asymmetry of the scene) and it declines to grant her the dignity of a name in a chapter which does not even name Yaʿqūb. The reports give her as Zulaykhā or Rāʿīl (our source notes both), and Persian literature — most consequentially Jāmī's *Yūsuf wa Zulaykhā* — turned her into one of the great figures of mystical romance; but the sūrah keeps her where it put her, as a position of authority in a house, and lets her conduct carry everything.

**The One Question the Sūrah Does Not Answer: How Handsome Was He?**

Our source's note on this verse records the report that "Yūsuf was given half of beauty," which is transmitted in Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim:

> *"Yūsuf was given half of beauty."* (Muslim 162, from Anas)

It is worth pausing on what the sūrah does with that material, because the chapter is being used here as an argument about desire and the *burhān*, and it never once describes his face. The only data the text supplies are the reactions of others — the women who cut their hands and call him an angel (12:31), and the confession of the man who bought him that he was struck by the boy's inner beauty, according to the transmitted gloss recorded in our source. Genesis 39:6 says plainly "Joseph was handsome in form and in appearance"; the Qur'an omits the description and keeps the consequences, which is a deliberate rhetorical choice: the reader must imagine a beauty strong enough to make a room full of women forget what they were holding, and the strength of the temptation in the story is thereby made the reader's construction rather than the text's assertion. The "half of beauty" report is cited by the commentators (al-Qurṭubī; Ibn Kathīr, who adds the popular continuation that his beauty was halved at the moment of the trial — a story with no sound chain) for the plain purpose of explaining verse 23's *rawāda*: temptation here was not a trivial invitation but an assault, and the man who resisted it is called, in the same breath, one of God's purified servants. A reader should hold both facts at once — the beauty that the ḥadīth affirms, and the ugliness of what was being proposed — because the sūrah's moral force lies in the distance between them.

**"She Locked the Doors" — the Room as a Court of No Appeal**

*Wa-ghalaqaq al-abwāba*. The doors are locked, and the plural is taken by the rhetoricians to indicate more than one barrier — the reports in the commentaries say seven, and the sūrah does not. What matters is what the plural does to the situation: the room is not merely private but sealed, and sealing it is the decisive act, because it removes the possibility of an appeal. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī both observe that the verse's economy is exactly this: she has arranged a place where no third party exists — and God, who is not a third party but the witness, is the only presence Yūsuf then invokes. The sūrah has already prepared the theology for this: the "signs" (12:24, 12:35) and the "proof of his Lord" appear in the very next verses, and the chapter's claim is that a closed room is never closed in the sense that matters (cf. 58:1: *"God has indeed heard the speech of the woman who pleads with you concerning her husband, and [listens to] your complaint to God; and God is Hearing, Seeing"* — the same pair of divine names, *Samīʿ ʿAlīm*, appears at 12:27 and 12:34).

The detail is also the chapter's first piece of evidence against her, and it is the reason the later interrogation is so brief: the one who locked the doors had decided in advance what would be done with a witness. When at 12:51 she says "I sought to lure him from himself and he refused," the locked doors are what make her confession credible — an innocent woman does not lock.

**"Come!" — a Word the Sūrah Does Not Translate**

*Wa-qālat hayta laka*. The expression is a summons of urgency and desire, and the grammarians and exegetes of the earliest generations record that it is not standard Arabic: al-Ṭabarī transmits "it is Syriac (al-sūrānīyya), and its sense is 'hasten' (aqla)"; other early reports say it is the speech of the Egyptians, and some lexicographers treat it as a loan-word used in Ḥijāzī Arabic for the language of seduction. Ibn ʿAṭiyyah and later stylists note the effect: the sūrah, whose diction is otherwise Arabic in the most exact sense, lets her speak in a foreign word. That is a piece of literary realism and a piece of argument: the woman's summons is alien to the Book's own tongue, and it is quoted untranslated rather than absorbed, while Yūsuf's answer is pure prophetic idiom — "*māʿādhu billāh*." The verse sets a foreign cry against an Arabic refuge.

**"God Be My Refuge" — the Grammar of Flight**

*Qāla māʿādhu billāh*. The word is *maʿād*: a refuge, a place or state of return to which one flees for safety (the root ʿ-w-d means "to return"; the lexicons distinguish *maʿād*, the place of return, from *mawʿid*, a promise appointed). The verb used is *iʿtadha*, to betake oneself to protection, and the form of Yūsuf's reply is the point: he does not say "I do not wish" but "I take refuge in God" — he locates the safety outside himself, in a place the locked doors do not reach. It is the formula the Prophet ﷺ used in the night prayer:

> *"I seek refuge in Your pleasure from Your anger, and in Your pardon from your punishment, and I seek refuge in You from You; I cannot enumerate praise of You; You are as You have praised Yourself."* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, Book of Prayer; also Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī in their chapters on the night prayer. The phrase's strangeness — "and I seek refuge in You from You" — is precisely the logic of Yūsuf's sentence: what is fled from is not an independent power but what God permits, so the only refuge is toward Him)

The sūrah's sentence then gives two reasons and one maxim. *Innahu rabbī aḥsana mathwāya* — "truly he is my lord, he has made my lodging beautiful." Our source records the two classical referents: Potiphar, on a reading it attributes among others to al-Qurṭubī, so that Yūsuf reasons "he honoured me and I will not betray him" (cf. Genesis 39:8-9, "he is not greater in this house than you are… how then could I do this great wickedness and sin against God?"); or God, on the reading of al-Baghawī and al-Qushayrī, so that "my true Lord is the One who brought me out of the well and put me in this honourable lodging, so it is not fitting that I should disobey Him." Ibn ʿĀshūr and al-Zamakhsharī both prefer that both senses are intended, and the double meaning is the sūrah's moral grammar: fidelity to a benefactor and fidelity to God are not two reasons but one, because the lodging's beauty is from God through the man. The final clause universalises the reason into a law: *inna-hu lā yufliḥu al-ẓālimūn* — "the wrongdoers do not prosper." *Falāḥ* is agricultural in origin (to split the soil, hence to succeed as a crop succeeds), and the sūrah's claim is that wrongdoing does not yield: a man who gains a body and loses a self has not harvested anything.

**The Scene's Place in the Prophet's Own Teaching**

That a young man, alone with a woman of rank and beauty, says "I fear God," is not for the sūrah alone; it is the situation described in one of the most cited of the seven categories in the Prophet's ﷺ ḥadīth on the shade of the Day when there is no shade:

> *"Seven whom God will shade in His shade on the day when there is no shade but His: … and a man whom a woman of high position and beauty invites herself to him, and he says: 'I fear God.'"* (Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031, from Abū Hurayrah)

The ḥadīth does not name Yūsuf, but the wording is his situation, and the commentators (al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr) cite it here for the reason that the sūrah's verse states as a general law: *kadhālika* — "thus" — God turns away evil from the one who flees to Him (12:24). The practical teaching that the tradition's preachers have drawn from verse 23 is a rule of three moves, each visible in the verse: recognise that the situation has been engineered (the locked doors), do not negotiate with it (no "let us talk"), and leave — which is why 12:25 says only *istābaqā al-bāb*, "the two of them raced to the door," and the sūrah records no argument, no haggling, and no explanation from Yūsuf. Refusal in the Qur'an is not delivered as a debate; it is delivered as flight.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:24

> **24** *She indeed inclined toward him, and he would have inclined toward her, had he not seen the proof of his Lord. Thus it was, that We might turn him away from evil and indecency. Truly he was among Our sincere servants.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Debated Sentence in the Sūrah**

*Wa-laqaḍ hāmmat bihi wa-hamma bihi law allā an rā burhāna rabbihi*. The clause *hāmmat bihi* — "she certainly intended him" — is straightforward; the difficulty is the second half, and the whole history of interpretation of this verse turns on how the words *wa-hamma bihi law allā an rā* are joined.

The reading favoured by the great majority of the exegetes, and the one the grammar of the sentence most plainly supports, takes *law* as the conditional particle "if / but for," with *amma* dependent on the negation it implies: "and he would have inclined toward her, had he not seen the proof of his Lord" — i.e. on the condition that he had not seen it. On this reading Yūsuf never intended the act at all; the verse states what would have happened in the counterfactual, in order to show how narrow the escape was. Al-Ṭabarī's discussion, and Ibn Kathīr's after him, prefer this, and the theological reason is decisive for them: the prophets are preserved in the matter of the message, and a sin that would have required divine *ṣarf* (turning away, in the verse's own next clause) is not something one attributes to one whom God names "among Our sincere servants."

A second reading, transmitted in some chains from Ibn ʿAbbās and favoured by certain early ascetics, takes the two verbs in parallel as both real: "she intended him and he intended her" — and then the "proof of his Lord" is precisely what interrupted the intention before it became an act. Al-Rāzī, who reports both, and al-Qurṭubī both note that on this reading the inclination was a momentary passing of the heart which he did not pursue (our source records exactly this gloss as the majority view), and that it would not be a sin, since the Prophet ﷺ taught:

> *"God passes over for my community what is whispered to by their own selves, so long as they do not act upon it or speak of it."* (Bukhārī 5269; Muslim 134, from Abū Hurayrah)

The difference between the two readings is the difference between a man who never wavered and a man who wavered and was caught before the fall; and the sūrah's own vocabulary makes the second unobjectionable and the first more honourable. The reason the verse is left syntactically open, al-Biqāʾī suggests, is that its function is not to grade Yūsuf but to state a doctrine: what follows is not "so he resisted" but "thus We turn away from him evil and indecency" — the verse's point is God's action, not the man's, and the open syntax keeps the moral credit with God in either reading.

**"The Proof of His Lord" — Burhān**

*Burhān* is the word for demonstrative, compelling proof — in the Qur'an it is used of the evidence given to the messengers (*"O mankind, there has come to you a proof (burhān) from your Lord, and We have sent down to you a clear light"* — 4:174), and of Moses and Aaron sent *"with Our signs and a clear authority (sulṭān mubīn)"* (23:45); here it is something Yūsuf *sees*. The word's ordinary logic is exact: a *burhān* is not a feeling but a piece of evidence that ends the argument, and the sūrah does not say what it was — the verse gives the category, not the content, and the commentaries supply a range:

Our source records the widely circulated narrative: that she had veiled an idol in the room, explaining that she was ashamed before it, and Yūsuf reasoned — how can you be ashamed before a statue that neither sees nor hears, and not before God, the Hearing, the Seeing? on which realization he "saw the proof" (al-Ṭabarī via Q and others). It also records that others took it as an appearance of Gabriel (al-Rāzī), and the wider literature reports that he saw his father Yaʿqūb's figure or the name of God. All of these are *akhbār* supplied to fill a Qur'anic silence, and Ibn Kathīr's treatment — transmit, mark the weak ones, decline to bind the verse to any of them — is the correct procedure. The point of the verse is that the perception of God's presence is a *burhān* in the strict sense: it is not a mood but a demonstration, and what it demonstrates is that the room has an occupant the door cannot lock out (see the note on 12:23 and on 58:1).

**"Thus, That We Might Turn Away from Him Evil and Indecency"**

*Kadhālika li-naṣrifa ʿanhu al-sūʾa wa-l-faḥshāʾ*. The verb *ṣarafa* is the same used for turning aside the wind or the course of events, and the form "We turn aside from him" states the sūrah's doctrine of moral prevention: the preservation here is not attributed to Yūsuf's strength but to God's turning — a point the tradition's theologians press, since 12:23 showed the man fleeing, and this verse shows God turning. The pairing *al-sūʾ wa-l-faḥshāʾ* is glossed by the exegetes as evil in general and indecency in particular: *sūʾ* is the harm that damages the self and its religion, *faḥshāʾ* (from *faḥish*, that which exceeds due measure and would be shameful if seen) the specific act. The two words together describe the whole field of the wrong: the private loss and the public transgression.

Then the verse's ground: *innahu min ʿibādinā mukhlaṣīn*. The reading that most of the reciters give here — passive participle *mukhlaṣ* — means "made pure, selected," i.e. purified by God; the alternative active reading *mukhliṣ* means "one who purifies his religion for God." Both are in the reports and the sūrah does not force a choice (see 15:40 and 38:82-83, where Iblīs says: *"except Your servants among them, mukhlaṣīn"*). The verse thus teaches the reciprocal structure of sincerity: God purifies whom He wills, and the purified are those whose devotion He has taken over. Ibn al-Qayyim, in *al-Fawā'id* and *Madārij al-Sālikīn*, builds on this verse a much-repeated observation — that the *ikhlāṣ* of a servant is the reason God's protective turning (*ṣarf*) operates, and that sexual transgression is specifically the sin in which *ikhlāṣ* is tested, because it is the act most secret and therefore the one that best reveals whether a person believes he is alone. Yūsuf's *ta'wīl* was given him in the same breath as his election (12:6, 12:21), and the sūrah keeps the order: purity first, then knowledge, then office.

**The Fasting Man and the Glass of Rosewater**

Our source records Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī's analogy for the state described in the verse — a man fasting in the heat of summer who is handed iced rosewater: the inclination to drink is not a sin; the fast is precisely the fact that he does not drink. Al-Rāzī's comparison is used in the *Mafātīḥ al-Ghayb* to answer an objection of the philosophers: if Yūsuf felt nothing, his refusal would be no virtue, and if he felt and refused, why does the sūrah say "We turned away evil from him"? His answer runs on the sūrah's own distinction between inclination and intention: the heart's movement is *ḥads*, a shock, and what God removes from the sincere servant is the *ʿazm*, the resolve. The practical teaching, drawn by al-Ghazālī in the *Iḥyāʾ* from this passage, is for anyone fighting a desire: do not count the first movement of the heart as your sin, and do not count it as your innocence either — it is the occasion on which the *burhān* is either seen or not seen.

**How the Verse Closes Its Question**

The sūrah gives no verdict of reward here, no "and We praised him," because the verse is doing something more careful: it explains why nothing happened. The sequence of 12:23-24 is the chapter's answer to the reader's implicit question — could a man in that room really refuse? — and the answer is: yes, but not by himself. And in case the reader is inclined to conclude that Yūsuf was therefore merely lucky in his own psychology, the same clause turns the luck into election: he is *min ʿibādinā mukhlaṣīn*, one of the servants God has taken for Himself. The chapter will keep this pattern to the end: flight at the door (12:25), the false accusation (12:25), the torn shirt as proof (12:26-27), and years in prison for a word mislaid (12:42) — with the *ṣarf* of God, in every one of them, functioning as the sūrah's real plot.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:25

> **25** *And they raced to the door, while she tore his shirt from behind. And they encountered her master at the door. She said, “What is the recompense for one who desires ill toward thy wife, save that he be imprisoned, or a painful punishment?”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Race**

*Istawabaqā al-bāba wa-ttāqat qamīṣahu min dubur*. The verb *istābaqā* is the mutual form of "to race," and the reader has met it eight verses earlier in the brothers' mouths: "we went racing (natāsābāqu) with one another" (12:17). The sūrah's two races are the hinge of its first two acts, and the parallel is a moral one. In the first, a group of sons race in play and leave a child with their baggage, and the outcome is a shirt with forged blood which fails to convince; in the second, a woman and a man race to a door and the outcome is a shirt torn by her hand, which convinces everyone who sees it. Both scenes turn on a garment, both turn on the same verb, and in both the deceiver is undone by an object. The chapter, which began by calling itself a Book of "signs" (12:1), is teaching the reader how evidence works: a claim is worth nothing against a physical fact, and liars in this sūrah consistently produce the fact that convicts them.

She tore the shirt "from behind" — *min dubur* — because he was fleeing and she was pulling from behind; the direction of the tear is the whole case, as verse 26 will make explicit. Al-Zamakhsharī's note on the phrase is that the sūrah says *iṭṭāqat*, "it tore," rather than *qaddat* or *shaddat*, because the verb implies the tearing of something whole and the loss of what is torn — a shirt cannot be torn and kept. There is also a lexical point worth recording, since the word *qamīṣ* here is the same as at 12:18 and 12:93: the sūrah's one garment performs three functions across the chapter — false evidence, true evidence, and finally the medicine of an old man's sight. Muslim preachers have long used this triple life of a shirt as the chapter's argument that God makes ordinary objects carry His verdicts: the same class of thing that was used to wound Yūsuf's father is used to vindicate Yūsuf himself and then to heal his father.

**"They Encountered Her Master at the Door" — an Iltiqāʾ that Ends the Affair**

*Wa-ltaqā sayyiduhā ʿinda al-bāb*. Two details matter. The first is the word for the husband: *sayyid*, "her master," the language of a great household, in which the wife's dependence on her husband's authority is written into the noun (the sūrah uses *zawj* elsewhere for "spouse," and here, at the moment when her honour will be pleaded in front of a third party, uses the word of mastery). It also continues the sūrah's vocabulary of *rubūbiyya*, since Yūsuf had called the same man *rabbī* ("he is my lord," 12:23): lord to the servant, master to the wife, and in either case a human authority that the chapter will show to be limited — Potiphar decides nothing in the end except a cover-up (12:29), and it is the king who will decide everything (12:50, 12:54).

The second detail is the door. She had locked *al-abwāb* (12:23), plural, the many barriers of the sealed room; the encounter happens at *al-bāb*, singular, the one door she had counted on being useless to him because he could not pass her. The sūrah's spatial precision is one of its most admired features: the locked doors do not stop the flight, and the open door does not save the woman. Al-Rāzī and others make of it a general rule of the chapter: whoever builds an enclosure around a sin has not secured its outcome, only its location; the outcome is decided elsewhere.

**Her Question, Which Is a Verdict**

*Mā jazāʾu man arāda bi-ahlika sūʾan illā an yusjana aw ʿadhābun alīm*. This is the most studied sentence in the episode, and its structure rewards patience. She does not accuse Yūsuf in her own person; she asks a legal question — "what is the recompense of one who intends evil with your household?" — which (1) presupposes the guilt as the content of an impersonal relative clause ("whoever intends…"), so that the charge appears as a category rather than an allegation; (2) uses *ahla-ka*, "your wife/your household," not "me," thereby transferring the case from her body to his honour and enlisting his jealousy as the prosecutor (our source records this reading: she sought to rouse his *ghayrah* so that he would be avenged on him); (3) frames the alternatives with *illā*, "nothing but," as though the range of possible penalties were a settled matter of law rather than something to be investigated; and (4) offers two options — prison or a painful punishment — as though she were being magnanimous in proposing the lesser. The glosses our source records for this clause mark the fourth move: her question is in form a request for a ruling and in fact a sentence already written. The tradition's ethicists add the point our source makes about her love: she claims to be undone by passion for him, and in the same breath asks that he be beaten or jailed; the sūrah thereby lets the reader see what possession calls itself, and what a "great love" becomes the moment it is refused. (The exegetes' discussion of her love's progression, referred to in our source at 12:51, belongs to that verse's commentary.)

**"Prison" — the Word the Sūrah Turns Inside Out**

She chooses *an yusjana*, "that he be imprisoned," and the sūrah will spend the next dozen verses showing that the penalty she proposed was, in God's economy, the deliverance Yūsuf himself had asked for: *"prison is dearer to me than that to which you call me"* (12:33). The wordplay is not only situational. The noun *sijn* in the Qur'an belongs to two registers: the prison of this world (here; and "so he was in prison (sijn) for a while," 12:42), and the *sijjīn* of the next — "and what will convey to you what is *sijjīn*? A book, inscribed" (83:7-9), the name given in the earliest Arabic usage to the sealed record of the wicked (the lexicons relate it to a Syriac *sigin*, a seal, and the phrase *sijjīn* in the sūrah of the Defrauders is the counterpart of *illiyyūn*, "the book exalted," 83:18-21). Yūsuf is sent by a woman of Egypt to the place he prefers, and the sūrah has already named, in the same diction, the prison of the soul from which no Potiphar can release.

**The Verse's Quiet Claim About Recompense**

The last thing to notice is a word: *jazāʾ*, recompense. This sūrah uses the term on both sides of its moral ledger, and the two occurrences form a pair the reader should keep. Here the woman asks, "what is the *jazāʾ* of one who intends evil?" and at 12:70 the sūrah answers in its own voice, after the cup has been planted in the brother's bag: *"and thus do We recompense the wrongdoers (kadhālika najzī al-ẓālimīn)"* — the identical formula to the one used of Yūsuf in the house at 12:22, "and thus do We recompense the beautiful-doers (al-muḥsinīn)." The chapter therefore sets two clauses side by side, one spoken in anger by a powerful woman and one spoken by God, and arranges them so that each is applied exactly once to each party: Yūsuf, wronged in a house, receives *ḥukm* and *ʿilm*; the brothers, who used the vocabulary of legal penalty against an innocent man, are made to receive a penalty invented in the very scene we are reading. That is the sūrah's view of justice: not a theory, but a structure of consequences in which a person's own word is the instrument.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:26

> **26** *He said, “It was she who sought to lure me from myself.” And a witness from her own people testified, “If his shirt is torn from the front, then she has spoken the truth and he is among the liars.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Sentence of Defence**

*Qāla hiya rāwadatnī ʿan nafsi*. Yūsuf's entire defence in the household's tribunal is six words, and the sūrah gives him nothing more: no account of the locked doors, no mention of the room, no plea of character, no appeal to the years of service. The verb he uses is hers, exactly as in verse 23 and as the women will repeat in verse 30 — *rāwadaniy* — which the rhetoricians take to be deliberate: the innocent man does not manufacture new language, he describes what happened in the words the accuser had already used, and thus makes her own vocabulary the record. Al-Zamakhsharī's comment is the useful one: a false accusation is met here not with elaboration but with the bare reversal of the subject and object; she sought *him*, and the sentence that establishes it has no adjectives in it.

There is a discipline in this for the reader. In a world of scandal, the Qur'an shows the wronged party making one factual statement and then leaving the case, and the sūrah will keep to that pattern: at 12:50-51 Yūsuf, now in a position to demand a public investigation, asks for exactly one thing — that the women be asked what their matter was — and does not pursue the woman herself. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī both note that the vindication he obtains is legal and not emotional; he is cleared, not avenged, and the sūrah's silence about his feelings at the moment of acquittal is part of its ethics of restraint.

**"A Witness from Her Own People" — the Testimony of an Insider**

*Wa-shahida shāhidun min ahlihā*. The witness is introduced by the verb *shahida*, "testified," and the phrase *min ahlihā* locates him inside the woman's own kin — a person whose interest would ordinarily be to support her, and whose evidence against her therefore carries the force of a confession extracted from the hostile side. The classical exegetes, as our source records (al-Rāzī), identify him as a paternal cousin of hers, described as a man of judgement; another strand, transmitted in the commentaries and supported by a sound ḥadīth, holds that he was an infant speaking in the cradle:

> *"None spoke in the cradle except three: ʿĪsā ibn Maryam, the witness of Yūsuf, and a man in the time of Zakariyyā."* (Bukhārī 3436, from Abū Hurayrah)

Al-Ṭabarī transmits the cradle story among the reports, and the majority of the juristic exegetes are content with a wise kinsman, since nothing in the verse requires a miracle and a miracle is not what the verse is about. The important thing is what the sūrah says about the man: he "testified" (*shahida*), a verb which in the Qur'an belongs as much to God as to people — "God testifies to what He has revealed to you" (4:166), "sufficient is God as a witness" (48:28; 13:43) — and the placement is not accidental: the innocent party in a house of power has no advocate, so the text provides one, and calls his intervention a *shahāda*. The law of testimony in the Qur'an (2:282; 24:1-13) is built on exactly this problem — the need for a second voice — and this verse is one of the places the commentators cite for the principle that God supplies evidence when evidence is suppressed.

**"If His Shirt Is Torn from the Front…" — a Test That Could Have Gone Against the Victim**

*Law kāna qamīṣuhu qudda min qubulin fa-ṣadaqat wa-huwa mina al-kādhibīn*. The witness proposes a criterion before looking: front means she is truthful, back means she is lying. Four features of that construction deserve notice.

It is falsifiable. The criterion is not "he is innocent if he swears," nor "she is lying because she desired a man"; it is a physical state of an object that could refute the man's claim as easily as confirm it. It is offered by her own family, which is why the verdict binds: the party with the most to lose by the test chose the test. It is stated as a conditional, i.e. as a rule of inference and not as a judgment about persons — the witness never says "he is innocent," only what the shirt will show. And it comes first in the verse, before the observation ("so when he saw his shirt torn from behind," 12:28), which is the sequence of honest reasoning: hypothesis, then inspection — not inspection after a conclusion has been reached. This is why the pericope has been cited in the Islamic tradition's literature of *al-ḥukm bayna al-nās*, adjudication between people, as a model: the sūrah allows a verdict to rest on a *qarīna* (indicative evidence) when direct testimony is unavailable, provided the indicator is one that both parties can see and that could have gone either way.

**The Lexicon of the Torn Garment**

*Qadd* is a rending that runs lengthwise; *qubul* and *dubur* are front and back, and in Arabic the same pair does duty for the affairs of a person — what faces you and what is behind you, the manifest and the concealed. The sūrah's use of the words in this paired form is thus also a statement about the case: the woman's affair had a *qubul* (a face shown to the household, the master's instruction to honour his lodging, 12:21) and a *dubur* (the locked doors, 12:23), and the shirt has become the object on which the two are read. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the tearing was *from behind*, so the garment was pulled at by a hand on the man's back as he ran: an act of restraint, not of struggle with an assailant; the direction of the tear is the direction of the will. And the physical reasoning is not merely plausible but the plainest available: a man who attacks has his front to the resisting hand of the one he attacks; a man who flees presents his back. The exegetes record that the same test was understood to have settled the matter without any need for interrogation of the parties, which is why 12:27-28 are so short.

**The Verse's Theology of Vindication, Delayed**

The final thing this half-verse says concerns timing. Yūsuf's standing is not restored by the household's finding: within a verse or two he is in prison (12:35), and the case is not publicly and finally closed until the women are interrogated years later, when the woman herself says *"now the truth has become clear: I sought to lure him from myself, and he is of the truthful"* (12:51). The vindication of the innocent in this sūrah is therefore not an acquittal that returns a life; it is a record being built. The witness testifies in a corridor; the women of the city talk in the market (12:30); and 12:27's function is to establish in front of a household a fact that no one will act upon for two decades. The verse's lesson for the wronged reader is the sūrah's constant one: the truth of what happened to you is already on the record and it has a fixed appointment (12:92; 12:100), so a person may decline to manufacture his own justice while he waits — and may go on doing good in the meantime, which is precisely what Yūsuf does in the prison that the verdict in this verse failed to prevent (12:36-41).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:27

> **27** *But if his shirt is torn from behind, then she has lied and he is among the truthful.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Half of a Fair Test**

*Wa-in kāna qamīṣuhu qudda min duburin fa-kadhabat wa-huwa mina al-ṣādiqīn*. Verse 27 is the mirror of verse 26, and the mirroring is the point. The witness states both possible results before either party looks at the garment, so that no one can be accused of fitting the evidence to a conclusion already reached. The rhetorical tradition calls this *taʿlīq bi-l-sharṭayn*, suspension on both conditions, and treats it as the mark of a judgment that is a method rather than a preference. The sequence across 12:26-28 is therefore: a criterion proposed, a second criterion proposed, and then — only then — an inspection (12:28: *fa-lammā ra'ā*). Al-Shāṭibī's account of the purposes of the law and the jurists' chapter on *al-bayyināt* both cite this class of verse for the rule that an indicator may decide a case where witnesses cannot, provided the indicator was specified in advance.

Notice, too, what is and is not decided. The test can establish that a woman lied about an event; it cannot make Yūsuf righteous, and it does not try. The verdict is a verdict on speech: "she has lied," not "she is a liar," and "he is of the truthful," not "he is innocent in every respect." This economy — the willingness of a Qur'anic adjudication to decide the specific charged act and no more — is one reason the sūrah's legal readers treat verses 26-28 as a model for the judge's restraint: he rules on the matter put before him and leaves the persons to God.

**"Among the Truthful" — Classes of Speakers**

*Huwa mina al-ṣādiqīn*, "he is of the truthful," the partitive idiom that places a person in a class rather than describing him exhaustively. The Qur'an uses the same construction of the prophetic company: *"and whoever obeys God and the Messenger — those are with those whom God has blessed of the prophets, the truthful (al-ṣiddīqīn), the martyrs, and the righteous; and what an excellent companion they are"* (4:69), and the command *"be with those who are truthful"* (9:119). Truthfulness (*ṣidq*) in the Islamic tradition is not merely accuracy of statement but the alignment of the inner with the outer, and it is a habit before it is a virtue. The Prophet ﷺ described its accumulation in a way that explains how a household servant in Egypt gets the label:

> *"Truthfulness leads to righteousness, and righteousness leads to the Garden, and a man keeps telling the truth and training himself in truthfulness until he is written with God as a ṣiddīq (one of the truthful). And falsehood leads to wickedness, and wickedness leads to the Fire, and a man keeps lying and training himself in lying until he is written with God as a kadhdhāb (a habitual liar)."* (Bukhārī 6094; Muslim 2607, from Ibn Masʿūd)

Yūsuf is called *ṣiddīq* twice in this sūrah — here by a witness who has never met him, and there by the cup-bearer in prison: *"Yā Yūsufu, ayyuha al-ṣiddīq"* (12:46) — and the second is the more remarkable, because it is spoken by a man whose profession is attendance at a king's table, in a cell, on the strength of years of observed conduct rather than of any miracle. The sūrah's psychology of reputation is thus explicit at this juncture: a person's class-membership among the truthful is established by repetition in the small, and it is what gets him trusted by strangers, in a household and in a prison.

**A Verdict No One Acts Upon**

The verse's last contribution is to its own timing. The second conditional is fulfilled before the reader's eyes in the very next line, and yet nothing changes: the man is "of the truthful," and in three verses he is in prison (12:35). Al-Qurṭubī and Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī both observe that the chapter deliberately separates being right from being released, so that the reader cannot mistake righteousness for a technique of advancement. The verse also prepares the chapter's later confession at 12:51, where the woman herself, years on, says *"I sought to lure him from myself, and he is of the truthful (wa-innahu mina al-ṣādiqīn)"* — the identical phrase, now from the mouth of the one with the most to lose by saying it, at the moment when the man who has become the ruler of Egypt's granary could have taken anything he wanted and asked instead for these words. Between 12:27 and 12:51 the sūrah has shown that a public correction of the record is sometimes worth more, and certainly costs more, than compensation; and 12:52-53 show what Yūsuf does with the victory: he refuses to claim his own purity (*"and I do not declare myself innocent; truly the self is commandingly evil, except as my Lord shows mercy"*), so that the verse's declaration "he is of the truthful" is not converted by its subject into a claim of sinlessness. The chapter's ethics of vindication are complete at that point: evidence is for establishing what happened, not for establishing who deserves to be trusted forever.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:28

> **28** *So when he saw that his shirt was torn from behind, he said, “Verily this is among the schemes of you women—your scheming is great indeed!*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"When He Saw" — the End of Litigation and the Beginning of Cover-Up**

*Fa-lammā ra'ā qamīṣahu qudda min duburin qāla innahu min kaydakinna*. The verse is built around a single act of seeing, and the sūrah's legal realism is in the grammar: there is no re-argument, no further witnesses, no interrogation of the two principals — "when he saw." The torn shirt is an object the master of the house can hold, and once he has, the case is over in the only sense a case is ever over in a household where one party has all the power. Al-Ṭabarī's note is apt: the verdict is reached by the plainest means available to a man who is neither a prophet nor a judge, and the chapter does not pretend otherwise.

But look at what follows the verdict, and the verse becomes a study in the limits of evidence. The finding exonerates Yūsuf, and Potiphar draws from it not a duty to punish his wife nor a duty to restore the servant but two instructions: "turn away from this" (12:29) and "ask forgiveness for your sin" (12:29). The truth is established and then immediately ordered to be suppressed, because the man has understood the situation correctly in every respect except the moral one: an acknowledged fact will not save him from scandal, so he attempts to save the household's face. The sūrah reports his conduct without endorsing it and, in verse 30, shows the suppression failing: "the women of the city began to say…" — the sentence "he sought to conceal it, and the concealment did not profit him" is never written, because the plot is written instead.

**"This Is of Your Stratagem" — What Potiphar Concludes**

He draws a general conclusion from a particular case: *inna kaydakinna ʿaẓīm*. Our source records the two possible referents of "he": the master of the house, or the wise witness (al-Qurṭubī); and if it is Potiphar, then the words are a direct response to the framing of his wife's question in verse 25, where she had turned herself into the victim and demanded his jealousy on her behalf. The remark is a man's attempt to convert a personal catastrophe into an observation about a class of persons — "you women," the second-person plural — which has an obvious psychological function: it is easier to live with a household in which one has been deceived if the deception is attributed to the nature of the deceiver than to one's own failure to see. The Qur'an, characteristically, neither approves nor editorialises; it lets the sentence stand in the mouth of a pagan official and lets the chapter's own treatment of women answer it.

That answer is worth following, because the sūrah is more complicated than the accusation. It reports the "greatness" of women's *kayd* here; it shows that *kayd* operating in 12:31-33 — the invitations, the knives, the ultimatum; and it lets the women themselves be interrogated by the king and give their testimony in 12:50-51. But against the generalisation the chapter sets a set of particulars: the same women who admire Yūsuf and cut their hands are the women who, in the same sentence, acknowledge that what they are looking at exceeds the human ("this is no human being; this is nothing but a noble angel," 12:31); the woman who plotted is the one who finally tells the truth and names her own sin (12:51); and Yūsuf's reply to the whole business, at 12:53, declines even to claim credit for his own chastity. Nowhere in the chapter is a moral quality distributed by sex. The Prophetic norm for a man's speech about a class of people belongs here as well, since the community was given a general rule of treatment that excludes contempt:

> *"Be kind to women, for they are a support for you, and you have taken them as a trust from God."* (the wording of the Farewell Sermon reports, in Muslim's *Ḥajj* chapter and Aḥmad's *Musnad*; the element "be kind to women" is sound in Bukhārī and Muslim)

Ibn Kathīr's gloss on 12:28 is a fair summary of the adab of the verse: Potiphar's sentence is a saying of the Arabs and of men under provocation, not a text to be legislated from — and the sūrah's own relocation of the source of evil, in Yūsuf's later words, is from a class of people to a structure of selfhood: *"the self is commandingly evil (ammārah bi-l-sūʾ)"* (12:53). That is the chapter's correction of verse 28 without any polemic: the danger is not women; the danger is the human self left to its own management, and the remedy is not suspicion of a class but mercy from God.

**The Sūrah's Word "Kayd" — Whose Stratagem Wins**

The verse uses *kayd*, and this sūrah reserves the word for three agents, which makes its distribution a piece of the chapter's theology. It is used of the brothers' plotting against Yūsuf (12:5, "lest they devise against you a scheme"), of the women's plotting (12:28; 12:33, "if You turn not their scheme away from me, I shall incline to them"), and — remarkably — of God's own devising on Yūsuf's behalf: *"thus We devise (nikīdu) for Yūsuf; it is not fitting that he should take his brother under the king's law, except that God wills"* (12:76). The word's moral valence in the Qur'an is fixed by its agent: human *kayd* is concealment and harm; divine *kayd* is the arrangement of circumstances by which a plan is quietly outflanked, and it is repeatedly declared as such (3:54: *"they devised, and God devised, and God is the best of devisers"*).
