The sūrah's structure makes the comparison unavoidable, because verse 28's "your scheme is great" is spoken by a man who believes he is managing an affair, while 12:76 will be spoken about a man who has spent two decades being managed by God. Al-Rāzī's question at 12:76 — how can God be called a *kāʾid*, when deception is base? — receives the tradition's standard answer: God's *kayd* is not the concealment of truth from one entitled to it, but the exposure of the schemer's purpose by letting the scheme run its course into the consequences it was designed to avoid. Verse 28 is a specimen of the base kind; the chapter will spend the next fifty verses showing which kind actually works.

**"Great Indeed" — the Word That Runs the Whole Chapter**

*ʿAẓīm* is the sūrah's own measure of things: the shirt's tear is called "great," but the chapter's uses of the word keep re-rating what deserves it. The brothers say "our father is in manifest error" (12:8) and the affair they "embellished" is not great; the women's *kayd* is great (12:28) and it cannot keep Yūsuf from a prison that will make him the ruler of a nation's bread; Yūsuf calls the dream's interpretation "one of the matters my Lord has taught me" (12:37) and the forgiveness he grants his brothers "no reproach upon you today" (12:92); and the sūrah's last word on greatness belongs to God: *"My Lord is Latīf over whom He wills"* (12:100), and *"God has been gracious (tafaddala) to me"* — the phrase in verse 100 whose comparative the sūrah keeps using for the rise from the well to the palace (12:6, 12:21, 12:56). Potiphar's "great" is a man's estimate in a corridor; the chapter's subject, from its first verse's "signs of the clear Book" to its last "a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe," is the measurement of a life by God. The reader is meant to notice the difference in scale between a verdict that ends a household's quarrel and a patience that ends a family's exile.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:29

> **29** *Joseph, turn away from this. And you, seek forgiveness for your sin. Truly you were among those at fault.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Commands, Wholly Unequal**

*Qāla yā Yūsufu aʿriḍ ʿan hādhā, wa-staghfirī dhanbak, innakunti mina al-khāṭiʾīn*. The verse contains one address to the man who was nearly destroyed and one to the woman who destroyed him, and the disproportion is the sūrah's indictment. To Yūsuf: turn away from this, i.e. keep it to yourself, do not speak of it, do not press the advantage you have — the same verb *aʿraḍa* that the Qur'an uses elsewhere for a merciful overlooking (7:199: *"take to forgiveness, command the right, and turn away from the ignorant"*). To her: *istaghfirī dhanbak* — ask forgiveness for your sin. The finding of the court has been accepted, and the master of the house, having accepted it, does the two things a man of his position can do: he protects his honour by silence and he acknowledges that a wrong requires God's pardon.

Our source records that the speaker may be the wise kinsman rather than the husband (al-Qurṭubī allows both), but in either case the sentence's structure is the same, and it is remarkable. A pagan official pronounces the two most important verbs of the Islamic moral vocabulary — *iʿriḍ* and *istaghfir* — and the sūrah puts them in his mouth with no correction. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr both dwell on this: the recognition that a sin must be *asked about* — that it is not merely an exposure to be managed — is treated as a piece of *fiṭrah*, of the moral knowledge that survives inside an unguided household. The chapter's theology of conscience is thus at work before its theology of prophethood: even in a polytheist court, the torn shirt produces the words "ask forgiveness."

**"Turn Away From This" — the Ethics of Not Pressing a Victory**

Consider what Yūsuf is being offered, and what he later declines. Verse 29 hands him, in effect, a settlement: you are cleared, the matter is closed, say nothing. He is a servant in the house, with no standing to prosecute his master's wife; the household's verdict has already come; to press it would end his position. The chapter's later narrative shows exactly how he behaves when he *can* press it: he does not. When the king's envoys come for him after the dream is interpreted (12:50), Yūsuf refuses to leave prison until the women's case is publicly retried, and when the woman confesses (12:51) he says: *"that was so that he may know that I did not betray him in secret, and that God does not guide the scheme of the treacherous"* (12:52) — and immediately declines to claim his own purity (12:53). Ibn Kathīr notes on 12:52 that Yūsuf's demand for a public hearing was not revenge but the clearing of a record that had been suppressed by verse 29's instruction: silence, in a house like this, is a verdict in the accuser's favour.

The practical rule that the commentators derive from verse 29 for the wronged party is a hard one: you may be advised to "turn away" from what was done to you by people who have no intention of punishing the doer, and the choice in front of you is between bitterness and patience. The sūrah shows Yūsuf taking neither of the two obvious options: he does not litigate and he does not conceal his innocence when concealment is all that power wants. Verse 29's "turn away from this" is obeyed in the letter (no more words about the room) and refused in the spirit (he does not accept that the matter is finished for God, who is "Hearing, Knowing," 12:34).

**"Ask Forgiveness for Your Sin" — Istighfār Without Tawbah**

The command to her is remarkable for what it is not. He tells her to seek forgiveness; the sūrah does not record that she repented at that moment. The distinction between *istighfār* (asking to be shielded) and *tawbah* (the turning that includes ceasing, regret, and restitution) is a technical one in Islamic ethics, and the Qur'an's own pairing of the two makes it visible: *"and ask forgiveness of your Lord and turn to Him (wa-tūbū ilayhi)"* (11:3; 11:90). What the master orders is the first half; the second half takes the rest of the chapter, and on the classical reading it is completed only much later — the reports transmitted in the tafsīr literature (our source's notes on 12:51 and 12:85) about her eventual acknowledgment and, in the popular strands, about her marriage to Yūsuf after Potiphar's death, are *akbār* of no established rank, and the sūrah's own testimony stops at 12:51: she tells the truth about herself in front of the women and the king. That is what the verse 29 command produces, in a text that is not interested in romance: the guilty party eventually says, plainly, "I sought him from himself, and he is of the truthful."

The Prophetic teaching on the wider power of *istighfār* is cited by the preachers on this verse, and the sound texts are sufficient for it: the Qur'an itself, on Noah's tongue, joins forgiveness to provision — *"so I said: ask forgiveness of your Lord, for He is ever forgiving — He will send the sky upon you in abundance, and give you increase in wealth and children, and provide for you gardens and provide for you rivers"* (71:10-12). Yūsuf's own entry into the chapter's most consequential sentence is an *istighfār* on his father's lips (12:98: *"he will forgive us"*), which is the sūrah's final comment on verse 29's command: the word that a pagan official uses to close a scandal becomes, at the end of the story, the word by which a whole family is dissolved of its guilt.

**"You Have Been of the Sinful" — a Verdict That Is Not Punishment**

*Innakunti mina al-khāṭiʾīn*: the participle *khāṭiʾ* is from *khaṭiʾa*, to err, to commit a wrong act, and the construction places her in a class rather than branding her person — the same idiom, in the negative, that verse 27 had used of Yūsuf ("he is *of the truthful*"). The sūrah's fairness is visible even in the husband's speech: the household's finding convicts an act and locates the woman among the sinful by what she did, and no further penalty is imposed. It is a small thing and an instructive one: in a chapter about a young man destroyed by an accusation and a woman preserved by a confession, the text never lets either be described in terms that their acts do not support.

Finally, the reader should mark what verse 29 does not say. There is no exposure of her in the city, no divorce, no punishment of Yūsuf, and no statement from Yūsuf at all — his silence here is the sūrah's first sign that the vindication he cares about is not being negotiated in this house. The verse closes the private phase of the affair and hands it to the public phase that begins in the next line, and the transition is itself the moral: whatever is suppressed by the powerful is not resolved, only relocated — "and the women of the city said…" (12:30).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:30

> **30** *Some women of the city said, “The viceroy’s wife sought to lure her slave boy from himself! He has filled her with ardent love. Truly we consider her to be in manifest error.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Private Case Becomes a Public One**

*Wa-qāla niswatun fī al-madīnati*. *Niswah* is a collective noun for a group of women, and *al-madīnah* here is the first time in the Qur'an's narrative of Egypt that the word "the city" is used as an actor. The household's verdict (12:26-29) suppressed the matter; the city now pronounces on it. This is one of the sūrah's structural laws, and it deserves to be stated explicitly because the whole chapter is built by it: every act of concealment in Yūsuf's story enlarges the audience. The brothers hide him in a pit whose name means "hiddenness" (12:15), and the caravan carries him into a market; Potiphar orders silence (12:29), and "the women of the city" begin to speak; she invites the women into her house to hide her guilt (12:31), and they produce the public testimony that will eventually be needed at the king's court (12:50-51). Al-Qurṭubī's summary of the sequence is exact: God exposes what the powerful try to cover, not by miracle, but by the ordinary machinery of talk.

The verse also shows what a public does when it obtains facts without responsibility. The women of the city have the correct information — she did seek him, and he did refuse — and they draw from it an entertainment rather than a verdict. Their speech is precisely true and wholly unhelpful to the man it names, and the sūrah gives him no voice in it: he is "her *ghulām*," her boy-slave, in their mouths, and the chapter will not dignify their label by correcting it. When the king finally summons him, the address is "Yā Yūsufu ayyuha al-ṣiddīq" — "O Yūsuf, O truthful one" (12:46) — and the sūrah thereby places the city's vocabulary and the truth's vocabulary side by side, letting the reader observe that a scandal names a person by his weakest relation and an inquiry names him by his firmest quality.

**"She Circled Him" — the Verb of Insistent Pursuit**

*Rāwadat ʿabādā ʿan nafsihi*. The verb *ḍāwwara* (in some recensions read with the emphatic *ḍād*, from *ḍ-w-r*, "to go round, to keep returning to") means to importune, to circle a thing repeatedly without leaving it — and it is used in the Qur'an only here, in this one clause, where the women report what the household had suppressed. Its force is the force of the sūrah's whole idiom of seduction (see the note on *rāwada* at 12:23): the same construction *ʿan nafsihi*, "away from himself," is preserved, so that even in second-hand report the moral definition of the act does not change — she was trying to move him out of his own self. The addition of the possessive *ʿabd*/*ghulām* ("her slave-boy," which our source's translators render as "her slave boy") gives the sentence its edge of power: what she is said to have done was possible because he was, in law, hers. The sūrah does not soften this. It is a chapter that repeatedly states a person's vulnerability as a legal and economic fact — a boy sold for debased coin (12:20), a servant who cannot refuse the master's wife (12:23), an accused man whose exoneration changes nothing about his employment (12:29) — because its doctrine of God's protection is only credible if the danger is described accurately.

**"Love Has Pierced Her"**

*Qad shaghafahā ḥubb*. The verb *shaghafa* means to split, to pierce to the inmost part; the lexicons (Taj al-ʿArūs; Lisān al-ʿArab) transmit two glosses from the early Arab authorities: that the *shaghāf* is the thin membrane covering the heart, so that love has reached that covering; or that it is the heart itself, so that the love has pierced it through. Whichever is intended, the image is of an arrow's entry, and the sūrah uses a hapax for it: this is the only place in the Book where *shaghafa* appears, and it is here applied — by gossips — to a woman in love. The chapter's realism about desire is remarkable: passion is not described as an abstraction but as a wound, and the wound is what makes her conduct intelligible to the observers even as they condemn it.

The sūrah's other uses of the same field of words are worth setting beside it, since they constitute a Qur'anic psychology of love. The verb *ḥabba* is used here of Yaʿqūb's love for his sons ("Yūsuf and his brother are more beloved to our father than we," 12:8), of the love the brothers will claim for Banyāmīn (12:64, 12:77, 12:83), of Yūsuf's preference for prison over sin (*"al-sijnu aḥabbu ilayya"*, "prison is dearer to me than that to which you call me," 12:33), and of the love of this world as a general human condition (3:14: *"beautified for people is the love of desires (shahawāt) — women, sons, heaped treasures…"*). Read as a set, the sūrah does not treat love as a feeling one has; it treats it as an allegiance one is moved toward, whose objects compete, and whose danger is the same in the bed, the marketplace, and the palace. Al-Rāzī's long discussion of *ḥubb* at this verse — in which he distinguishes love that is obedience to desire from love that is obedience to the Beloved's command — is the classical answer to a question this chapter keeps asking, and it is why the mystical readers (our source's introduction quotes al-Maybudī: "the lover ought to receive news of the pain of love and the burning desire of lovers") found in Zulaykhā's *shaghaf* the figure of the soul's wound, while the jurists found in it a cautionary diagnosis.

**"We See Her in Manifest Error" — the Sūrah's Refrain, Misapplied**

*Innā lanarāhā fī ḍalālin mubīn*. The sentence is the third occurrence of a formula that this sūrah uses to mark a certain kind of blindness, and its history here is instructive. In 12:8 the brothers said of their father: *"truly our father is in manifest error"* (*ḍalālin mubīn*), because he loved Yūsuf more than the rest; in 12:28 the husband said of his wife's sex: *"your plot is great"*; now the women say of the wife: *"we see her in manifest error."* The echo with 12:8 is recorded in our source from al-Rāzī and it is precise: twice the phrase describes a person whose love has exceeded the bounds of what observers consider reasonable, and in both cases the observers who diagnose the error are themselves inside a worse one. Yaʿqūb's love was rightly excessive, and he was not wrong; this woman's love is wrongly excessive, and her error is real — but the speakers who see it are, as we have just noticed, gossiping about the beautiful servant rather than investigating the master's wife's power, so their "we see" is not insight. That is the sūrah's standing point about the term *baṣar* and its cognates, which it will state as a thesis at 12:105 ("how many a sign they pass by, and they are turning away") and 12:83 ("O my sons, go and inquire"). The verse is thus not only a piece of plot; it is another exhibit in the chapter's argument that the ability to detect someone else's *ḍalāl* is no evidence whatever that one has found the way oneself.

**What the Verse Achieves in the Story**

Structurally, 12:30 does three things in one line. It ends the household's fiction of privacy; it puts Yūsuf's innocence into the city's speech before it is anywhere near a court; and it creates the social pressure that produces the scene of the knives. Al-Ṭūsī's and al-Zamakhsharī's notes record that the women's talk is called *kayḍ* (plot) in the next verse precisely because their purpose was not to judge her but to see him, and the mechanism is fully intelligible to any reader who has watched a scandal become an invitation: the accused's disgrace, told in the right company, becomes an occasion for the very thing the teller was supposed to be denouncing. Verse 30 therefore belongs in this commentary not as a report about a woman's passion but as the sūrah's analysis of a public: a crowd that correctly identifies another person's error, names its object by his legal inferiority, and uses the truth it has got hold of as a route to what it wants to look at.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:31

> **31** *So when she heard of their plotting, she sent for them, and prepared a repast for them, and gave each of them a knife. And she said [to Joseph], “Come out before them!” Then when they saw him, they so admired him that they cut their hands and said, “God be praised! This is no human being. This is naught but a noble angel!”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Demonstration Designed as an Alibi**

*Fa-lammā samiʿat bikaydihinna arsalat ilayhinna wa-aʿtadatahunna muttabaʾan wa-ātat kulla wāḥidatin minhunna sikkinan wa-qālat ukhruj ʿalayhinna*. The verse's economy is severe: five verbs, no adjectives, and an entire scene of social manoeuvring. *Kayd* — our source's note records that the women's talk is called plotting because it was conducted in secret and because it was aimed at a result — is the right word: she has been told what the city is saying, and she does not deny it, deny the women, or appeal to her husband; she manufactures a situation in which the truth of her case will be proved on their own bodies. The logic of the demonstration is airtight in the world it inhabits: if the women, seeing him once, are undone, then a woman who lives with him daily cannot be blamed (our source's gloss from al-Ṭabarsī). She has converted her accusers into expert witnesses for her defence, and she has done it by arranging a meal.

*Muttabaʾ* is the word for the reclining-place or spread prepared for guests, and the reports add that the fruit they were set to cut was citrons or something similarly requiring a knife (our source cites al-Qurṭubī and others for the repast and the knives; the detail of the fruit's type is from the transmitted accounts, not the text). The knives are what turn a banquet into a test: hospitality gives a woman's house its lawful reason to assemble guests, and a knife gives each of them something to do with their hands when their attention is taken. Al-Zamakhsharī's note on such scenes in this sūrah — that the plot is always described by its equipment — applies here: the verse lists the sending, the spread, the knives, and the order to come out, and the reader is made to see a trap assembled item by item.

**"Come Out to Them" — the Body That Is Displayed**

*Wa-qālat ukhruj ʿalayhinna*. She commands him, as the household's property commands its dependants, to appear before the women; and the sūrah grants not one word from him at this moment. That silence is part of the chapter's design: Yūsuf has already said everything he intends to say about this affair (12:23, and in prayer at 12:33), and the text will not give the woman the satisfaction of a dialogue. When he appears, the verse changes grammar — the verbs of seeing, magnifying, cutting, and speaking all belong to the women, and Yūsuf is grammatically only an object. It is a subtle piece of honesty about what beauty does to a room: the sūrah records the effect, not the cause, and leaves the reader to notice that the man who is the most discussed person in Egypt is, in this scene, the least active.

*"Fa-lammā raʼaynahu ʿaẓẓamnahu wa-qaṭṭaʿna aydīyahunna"* — "when they saw him, they magnified him and cut their hands." *Taʿẓīm* is the verb of finding something great (the same root as Potiphar's "your *kayd* is *ʿaẓīm*," 12:28, and as the sūrah's "the *ʿaẓīm*" news of 78:2 in other contexts); and the cutting of the hands is stated flatly, without the comment — supplied by the transmitted reports, our source notes (My) — that they did not feel it. The Qur'an's own emphasis is elsewhere: the women cut the hands *instead of* the fruit, which is to say that an object of such beauty displaces the most ordinary skill of the body. Al-Qushayrī's gloss in *Laṭāʾif al-Ishārāt* on the sentence is the classical spiritual reading: a heart occupied with the seen no longer notices what it is doing with the hand.

**"This Is No Human Being" — the Aesthetic Argument**

*Ḥāshā li-Allāh mā hādhā basharan, in hādhā illā malakun karīm*. *Ḥāshā* is an exclamation of remote avoidance — "far from it, God forbid" — and the women's first reaction to the sight of Yūsuf is not desire but glorification. Our source's note makes the point exactly: the exclamation is an expression of amazement at God's creative power, so that the beauty they see sends them to the One who made it. The sūrah thus presents a completed argument about the beautiful: the *āyah* (verse 1's word) is a thing that points beyond itself, and here the pointing happens involuntarily, on the tongues of a room full of women who had come to judge an adulteress and end up confessing a Creator. Al-Rāzī's addition in our source is that their likening him to an angel was also a legal verdict in his favour — an angel does not commit fornication; the testimony they offer without being asked is the sūrah's own acquittal of him in public.

The comparison is not idle, because the Qur'an's other use of the sūrah's own verb of seduction is the story of Lot: *"and they sought to lure him from himself concerning his guests (rāwadūhu ʿan ḍayfihī), and We blinded their eyes"* (54:37). Both scenes are about a household, a guest, a shut door, and a crowd pressing toward a sexual violation; in Lot's case the assailants are struck blind and the guest is carried off ("so We saved him and his family," 27:17); in Yūsuf's case there is no blindness and no rescue, only a torn shirt, a prisoner's cell, and a man who declines to negotiate. The sūrah never draws the parallel; the verb draws it for the reader, and the point that emerges is the chapter's own: God protects His servants in this world by whatever means the case requires, and the means sometimes look, from inside the story, like the loss of everything.

**"A Noble Angel": the Hadith of Nobility**

The word *karīm* applied to Yūsuf here is worth pairing with a sound saying of the Prophet ﷺ, because it shows how the earliest community heard this chapter's praise of him:

> *"The noble one (al-karīm), son of the noble one, son of the noble one, son of the noble one: Yūsuf ibn Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm."* (Bukhārī 3382, from Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ; in *Fatḥ al-Bārī* Ibn Ḥajar transmits the same fourfold nobility from the Prophet ﷺ and discusses why these four are so named)

The women use *karīm* of an angel's beauty; the Prophet ﷺ uses it of a lineage of prophets. The sūrah's own placement of the word between the two is its quiet correction of the scene: the four in that chain are named in this very chapter (vv. 6, 38, 91: "the religion of your fathers Abraham, Isaac and Jacob"), and Yūsuf's line of nobility is exactly the line God is shown completing His blessing upon — while the women, admiring a face, have no idea that the man they glorify is the grandson of a man saved from a fire and the great-grandson of a man commanded to sacrifice a son. In a sūrah whose subject is the reversal of appearance by outcome, *karīm* is the word that carries both readings at once: what the room sees and what the record contains.

**The Verse as a Study in Reversal**

Read as a unit, 12:29-31 is a sequence in which every attempt to manage a story defeats its own purpose. Potiphar's "turn away from this" (12:29) produces the women of the city (12:30); the women of the city produce the banquet (12:31); and the banquet, designed to excuse, produces the public record of his innocence that the household had tried to bury. Verse 31's specific contribution is that the women who came to condemn her are the ones who fall, and that their fall becomes his vindication, since they cannot afterwards maintain the story that a man like that would have to be taken by force by anyone — they have seen what merely looking at him did to them. The sūrah is teaching its doctrine of *kayd* again: human scheming has a direction, and the direction is always toward the exposure of the schemer; the *kayd* of God (12:76) has the direction of the pit's exit, of the shirt's tearing, and of a room in which the innocent man says nothing and is declared greater than a human being.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:32

> **32** *She said, “This is the one on whose account you blamed me. I indeed sought to lure him from himself, but he remained chaste. And if he does not do as I command, he shall surely be imprisoned; and he shall be among those humbled.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict Turned Back on the Jury**

*Qālat dhālika alladhī lumtumni fīhi wa-laqaḍ rāwadtuhu ʿan nafsihi fa-staʿṣama*. She points at him and says, in effect: this is the man about whom you blamed me — and you have just cut your own hands over him. The argument is an appeal to the jury's conduct, and the sūrah's audience is meant to see both its force and its baseness. Its force: if a single glance unseats their self-command, the charge of unnatural cruelty against her is unreasonable; their bodies have testified to her case. Its baseness: she produces no regret in the sentence, and no word for Yūsuf except as the object she tried to obtain; and the very women she enlists to share her excuse will, in the next clause, be enlisted to share her threat. Al-Zamakhsharī's phrase for this is *ḥujjah al-khaṣm ʿalā nafsihi*, and the sūrah lets the guilty party win a point of fact while losing the case of her soul.

**"He Remained Chaste" — the Verb of Withholding**

*Fa-staʿṣama*. The verb is the intensive-reflexive of *ʿaṣama*, "to withhold, to restrain," and the *istifʿāl* form (*istaʿṣama*) means "he held himself back, he made himself chaste" — the sūrah's own verdict, put in the mouth of the one who tried to have him. Its noun, *ʿifāf* (from *ʿaṣama*, "to be chaste" in the classical usage), becomes the technical term of Islamic ethics for sexual purity, and it is one of the virtues the Prophet ﷺ tied to God's help rather than to human effort:

> *"Whoever asks [of people], do not give him; and whoever is self-sufficient, God will keep him self-sufficient (wa-man yastaghnī yughnihī Allāh); and whoever strives for chastity, God will make him chaste (wa-man yastaʿaffif yuʿiffihi Allāh)."* (Bukhārī 1469; Muslim 1053, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, in the context of a prohibition on persistent asking)

The two clauses of that ḥadīth belong together in this scene, because both describe the same discipline: *istighnāʾ* (making oneself independent of what is not owed to one) and *taʿāffuf* (striving toward chastity), and the ḥadīth's promise is that the effort is met with divine enablement. That is the sūrah's exact theology at this point: Yūsuf's *fiʿāl* is not a triumph of willpower, since verse 24 said "thus We turn away from him evil" and verse 33 will say "if You do not turn their plot away from me"; and the woman's word *istaʿṣama*, "he held himself back," is the chapter's report of what the help of God looks like from outside.

**The Threat, and Its Odd Phrasing**

*Wa-in lam yafʿal mā āmurhu bihi layusbananna fī al-sijni wa-yakuna mina al-ṣaghīrīn*. Three things about the sentence. First, it is the third time in nine verses that a penalty is discussed in this household, and the shift of voice is a plot-point in itself: in 12:25 she asked her husband what the recompense should be, in 12:29 he pronounced his own (concealment), and now she states it herself, in the impersonal passive with the double nunun of the oath: "he will surely be imprisoned." She has stopped persuading and started executing, which is the ordinary trajectory of an insulted passion. Second, the prison is named with the definite article, *al-sijni*, as a place already available — the house of the ʿazīz has a prison in it, and the sūrah's realism about the architecture of power is quietly damning: the same building contains the spread and the knives (12:31) and the cell. Third, and most revealing, is her second consequence: *wa-yakūna mina al-ṣaghīrīn*, "and he will be among the small ones." She does not threaten death; she threatens a reduction of rank. In a world where a free-born prophet's son has been bought for counted dirhams (12:20), the worst thing that can be done to a person is to make him small, and her sentence says what the chapter's whole first act has demonstrated: the great and the small are decided, in this life, by whoever holds the keys.

The sūrah's answer to that claim is structural rather than verbal. The chapter will spend eighty verses on the reversal of *ṣughar* and *ʿiẓẓām*: the one she intends to make small is established "in the land" (12:21), given judgement and knowledge (12:22), and finally addressed as "O Yūsuf, O truthful one" by a king's household that cannot manage its own famine without him (12:46, 12:54-55); while the title of *al-ʿazīz*, "the mighty," which was hers by marriage, passes to him (12:23 "the woman in whose house he was, the wife of the ʿazīz" versus 12:26, 12:30, 12:51, 12:78, 12:88, where the title is applied to Yūsuf). *Ṣaghīr* and *kabīr* in this sūrah are not descriptions but things that happen to people; and the record keeps them all — "it leaves nothing small or great but it counts it" (18:49) — which is the chapter's permanent answer to every threat of the kind she makes.

**The Verse's Place in the Chapter's Argument about Desire**

The sūrah has so far described three kinds of wanting, and this verse states the third. The brothers wanted a father's attention and engineered a disappearance (12:8-15). The woman wanted a body and engineered a trap (12:23-31). Yūsuf wanted — the sūrah has been careful to give him wants, in the form of an expressed preference: "prison is dearer to me than that to which you call me" (12:33) — and instead of engineering he prays. Verse 32 is the hinge between the two styles: it presents desire as policy (she has a plan, a threat, a penalty, and a jury) and shows for the first time that policy does not work, because the man she is about to imprison will, within two dozen verses, be the one whose word the king takes on the survival of the country. Al-Rāzī's summary of the passage is that the sūrah is drawing a line between *ḥubb* that seeks to possess and *ḥubb* that seeks to be obeyed: she says "if he does not do what I command" — the grammar of ownership — and the chapter's hero says "my Lord" (12:33) — the grammar of servanthood. The rest of the sūrah, including the famous prison sermons about *al-dān* and the "stratagem of God" on the cup, is a demonstration that only the second kind of love produces the freedom the first kind demands.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:33

> **33** *He said, “My Lord! Prison is dearer to me than that to which they call me. If Thou dost not turn their scheming away from me, I shall incline toward them and be among the ignorant.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"My Lord" — the Address That Does the Work**

*Qāla rabbi*. The two words of Yūsuf's reply are the entire equipment the sūrah gives him for the last stand in the house of the ʿazīz: no argument, no appeal to Potiphar, no reference to his own years of service, no claim of strength. He calls, and he calls with the name of nurture — *rabb* — which the chapter has already loaded (12:6 "your Lord will choose you and teach you"; 12:23 "he is my lord" (about Potiphar or God, according to the two readings); 12:37 "that is of what my Lord has taught me"; 12:100 "my Lord has been gracious to me"). Al-Rāzī's note that Yūsuf "turned to God in humility and asked for His help, knowing that he could only be saved by God and not by his own effort" is exactly the point of the form: the sūrah has staged the man's competence in everything else (governing a household, judging dreams, administering a country) so that this single sentence can state the limit of it. The hadith's rule is the same: the person who asks to be spared dependence on himself is the one to whom enablement is promised — *"wa-man yastaʿaffif yuʿiffihi Allāh"* (Bukhārī 1469; Muslim 1053, quoted at verse 32 above) — and the Prophet ﷺ himself sought the same protection in the words the chapter's commentators put beside this verse:

> *"O Turner of hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion."* (al-Tirmidhī 2140; Aḥmad — graded ḥasan by al-Tirmidhī; the wording is also in Ibn Mājah)

If the one whose heart is preserved by revelation still prays for firmness, a young man in an unlocked room may be excused for praying not to be left to himself for the blink of an eye.

**"Prison Is Dearer to Me" — a Preference Stated in the Language of Love**

*Al-sijnu aḥabbu ilayya mimma adʿūnanī ilayhi*. The comparative of *ḥubb* is used for a prison, and the word has been used in this sūrah for the father's love, the brothers' claim to wish well, and the woman's pierced heart (12:8, 11, 30). The chapter is teaching that *ḥubb* is a direction of the will and not a pleasure: Yūsuf "loves" a cell — he prefers it — because the alternative is a kind of happiness he does not want. Al-Qushayrī, in the gloss our source records, puts it as the choice of the next life over this one: compliance would be comfort in the house and punishment beyond it; prison is discomfort in the house and felicity beyond it. That reading is the standard one and needs no embellishment, but its sharper edge is that the sūrah does not present him as someone indifferent to what he is refusing. He knows exactly what is on the table — beauty, rank, and a room already prepared — and the verse makes him say the preference in the verb of desire rather than of duty, which is the Qur'an's way of describing a virtue that has become appetite.

The choice of prison is also a legal-moral datum: the jurists' doctrine of *sadd al-dharāʾiʿ*, closing the means to harm (the principle developed by Mālik and by Aḥmadī and later theorised at length by Ibn al-Qayyim in *Iʿlām al-Muwaqqiʿīn*), rests on cases in which a lawful thing must be abandoned because of what it leads to. Yūsuf is given as the exemplar by the preachers: he does not negotiate with the doorway — he had already fled once and it produced only an accusation (12:25) — so he asks that the inclination itself be removed. The two-stage structure of his conduct is instructive: flight first (the natural means), then prayer (the real one), and the sūrah endorses both.

**"If You Do Not Turn Their Plot Away from Me"**

*Wa-in lam taṣrif ʿannī kaydahunna aṣbu ilayhinna wa-akun mina al-jāhilīn*. The conditional is the most quoted phrase in the passage for what it says about the human self, and the grammar of sin's approach in this sūrah is graduated with precision. Three verbs have marked the stages: *rawāda*, the outward pursuit and importuning (12:23, 30, 51); *ḥamma*, the pressing inward intention (12:24); and here *yaṣbu*, "I would incline" — from *ṣ-b-w*, the verb of the heart's leaning, used in Arabic of the inclination of a branch or of a young man toward what he fancies. The sūrah thus distinguishes being sought, intending, and inclining, and Yūsuf's prayer asks God to intervene before the third stage, which is where the second becomes the first.

The claim "I would incline" is not, on the majority reading, a statement that he had already inclined; it is a confession that inclination is possible. Ibn Kathīr, in his treatment of this verse, stresses that a servant who is confident of his own immunity has forfeited his protection, and the classical ethicists make the same point from the Prophet's ﷺ own rule about the direction of the will:

> *"None of you believes until his desire follows what I have brought."* (al-Nawawī's Forty Ḥadīth, no. 41, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr; transmitted in Aḥmad and by others — the ḥadīth masters differ on its chain, some grading it ḥasan through its supporting routes)

> *"All the sons of Adam are sinners, and the best of the sinners are those who repent."* (al-Tirmidhī 2493; Ibn Mājah 4251, from Anas — graded ḥasan)

And the "ignorance" of which he seeks refuge — *wa-akūna mina al-jāhilīn* — is the sūrah's own epistemology applied to sin: to act on desire is, in this Book's usage, not weakness of will but a failure of knowledge, a mis-weighing of what is present against what is promised (compare 12:22's *ḥukm wa-ʿilm*, "judgement and knowledge," which is precisely the equipment for weighing; and Lot's people, "do you approach desires while you see?" 27:54). The *jāhilīya* is not an age of stupidity but of knowing the worse to be worse and doing it.

**The Verse as a Model of the Believer's Speech Under Pressure**

Set beside the woman's sentence in verse 32, Yūsuf's is a study in contrast. She says: *this is the one about whom you blamed me; I sought him; if he will not obey he will be jailed and made small* — three clauses, all about other people's conduct, opinions and fates. He says: *my Lord; prison is dearer to me; if You do not turn this away, I shall incline* — three clauses, all about God and about himself, with no word about her, no accusation, no exposure, and no claim about his own character. The tradition's preachers have drawn from this the etiquette of a person under attack: speak to God about the danger rather than to people about the danger's author. That discipline pays off in the sūrah's own arithmetic of consequences: within fifty verses she is confessing in the king's court, and he is the man whose interpretation the court cannot do without — not because he conducted a campaign to expose her, which he never did, but because inclination was turned away from him and he was left, in the meantime, in prison, doing good (12:36: "we see you as being among the beautiful-doers").
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:34

> **34** *So his Lord answered him, and turned their scheming away from him. Truly He is the Hearing, the Knowing.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"His Lord Answered Him" — the Verse That Answers a Theology**

*Fastajābā lahu rabbuhu*. The verb *istijāba* is not "to hear" but "to respond," and it is used transitively with the preposition *li-*: God "responded to him." The sūrah, having quoted a two-line prayer, reports its acceptance without narrating the moment of relief — the reader is not told what Yūsuf felt or what happened next, only that the answer came. This is the Book's standard model of supplication: a request, a response, and a silence about the mechanism. The general rule the Qur'an states is explicit and unconditional:

> *"And your Lord says: Call upon Me; I will respond to you."* (Qur'an 40:60)

> *"And when My servants ask you concerning Me — indeed I am near. I respond to the invocation of the one who calls when he calls upon Me; so let them respond to Me and believe in Me, that they may be rightly guided."* (Qur'an 2:186)

The second of these two verses is placed in the Qur'an's own commentary tradition as a companion to Yūsuf's prayer, and its context in al-Baqarah is instructive for this one: it comes in the middle of the verses about pilgrimage and turning the face toward the Sacred House, and the early exegetes (al-Ṭabarī, and Ibn Kathīr citing the report about a man who asked the Prophet ﷺ "is our Lord near so that we whisper to Him, or far so that we raise our voices?" — transmitted in ʿAbd al-Razzāq and Aḥmad, and adduced by the commentators on 2:186 as its occasion) took it as a statement that the address needs no distance. Yūsuf, in a house where the doors were locked and the powerful were listening, is told here that the address needs no room.

**"He Turned Their Scheming Away from Him"**

*Fa-ṣarafa ʿanhu kaydahunna*. The verb *ṣarafa* is the sūrah's own technical term for what happened in the room: 12:24 had already read *"thus We turn away from him (li-naṣrifa ʿanhu) evil and indecency,"* and here the same root returns, now attached to *kayd*, the plot. The repetition tells the reader that verses 24 and 34 describe one continuous divine action at two moments: prevention from the act, and deflection of the plan. This matters for the theology of prayer in this chapter, because the *ṣarf* here is not the removal of the situation but the removal of its power over him. Yūsuf asked, in verse 33, not to be released but not to incline; the answer he is given is exactly what he asked for. He is not rescued from the house; he is preserved within it — and then he goes to prison, which the sūrah narrates in the very next verse (12:35).

The commentators have drawn a hard and useful rule from this: God's answer to a prayer is measured by what was asked and by what is good, not by what the petitioner expected. Al-Qurṭubī's discussion of verse 34 makes the connection to the *samīʿ ʿalīm* formula at the end: He heard the request and He knows its outcome, so the answer is given in the form of knowledge. This is why the sūrah can state an answer and a prison in consecutive verses without embarrassment: the answer was real, the prison was real, and the chapter's whole argument is that a life preserved from a sin is a larger deliverance than a body removed from a building. Yūsuf's own retrospective verdict, at 12:100, is exactly this: *"my Lord has been most subtle (Laṭīf) to me"* — the *lutf* named as such by a man looking back over a well, a cell, and fourteen years.

**"Truly He Is the Hearing, the Knowing"**

*Innahu huwa al-Samīʿu al-ʿAlīm*. The two names are joined, as they are in the verse about Hājar and Ishmael (14:40: *"our Lord, You know what we conceal and what we reveal"*) and in the prayer of Abraham at the House (2:127: *"our Lord, accept from us; truly You are the Hearing, the Knowing"*). Their pairing here fits the situation precisely. *Al-Samīʿ* is the answer to the room: a whispered *rabbī* in a sealed house is a speech-act with a listener, which is why the sūrah uses the name of hearing rather than of power — the point being not that God is strong enough but that He attends. *Al-ʿAlīm* is the answer to the future: Yūsuf could not know whether inclining would cost him everything or whether prison would be the road to a granary, so the verse ends with the name of knowledge, which is the sūrah's standing reassurance from 12:6 onward (*"your Lord is Knowing, Wise"*) and again at 12:83 and 12:86 ("I know from God what you do not know").

Two other uses of the same pair in the Qur'an complete the doctrine. In al-Mujādala, God's vindication of a woman whose complaint could not reach any court is stated with the identical formula: *"God has indeed heard the words of the woman who pleads with you concerning her husband and [complains] to God. And God hears your mutual debate; indeed God is Hearing, Seeing"* (58:1). And the Prophet ﷺ taught his community to seek refuge in a way that mirrors Yūsuf's sentence structure — the petition against being left to oneself:

> *"O God, Your mercy I hope for, so do not leave me to myself for the blink of an eye, and set right for me my whole affair; there is no god but You."* (Aḥmad's *Musnad* and Abū Dāwūd's *Sunan*; al-Ḥākim graded it ṣaḥīḥ on the condition of the two Shaykhs, and al-Dhahabī concurred — and the phrase "do not leave me to myself for the blink of an eye" is the exact shape of 12:33's conditional)

**The Verse as the Chapter's Answer to Its Own Question**

Read against the opening of the story, verse 34 is the sūrah's proof of the promise made in verse 6 — that God would choose him, teach him the interpretation of events, and complete His blessing. The promise was about a future the boy could not verify, and the chapter has now shown the first two clauses working: the choice (elected, preserved) and the teaching (the *ta'wīl* that will appear in the next two dozen verses, from the prisoners to the king). What the verse adds is the mode in which such promises are kept in this book: not by making the world easy, but by turning away from a person the one thing that would ruin him, even while everything else proceeds. That is why the sūrah can pair "He answered him" with a prison sentence in the next breath, and why the preachers of this chapter — al-Ghazālī in the *Iḥyāʾ*'s book of trust, Ibn al-Jawzī in *Ṣayd al-Khāṭir* — keep returning to verse 34 as the definition of answered prayer: the answer is the *ṣarf*, and the *ṣarf* is often invisible until verse 100.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:35

> **35** *Then it occurred to them, after they had seen the signs, that they should imprison him for a time.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"After They Had Seen the Signs" — the Verse's Whole Weight Is in the Order**

*Thumma badā lahum min baʿdi mā raʾawu al-āyāti la-yasjunannahu ḥatta ḥīn*. The sequence of the sentence is its argument: first the seeing, then the decision. The evidence — the torn shirt, the direction of the rent, the witness's conditional test — had been examined; the man was known to be innocent; and the conclusion reached was to lock him up. The sūrah says all of that in four words, *min baʿdi mā raʾawu al-āyāt*, and the reader is left to supply the motive, which the sūrah's own context makes plain: a household cannot prosecute its mistress, a servant cannot remain in a house where his presence is a daily rebuke, and an innocent man who is visible is an accusation that walks. Verse 29 ("turn away from this") had already attempted concealment; verse 35 is the next step of the same policy, and the sūrah's realism about institutions is that it calls it by its shape without editorialising.

The verb *badā lahum* carries the double sense the rhetoricians note: "a new course appeared to them," and idiomatically "they changed their opinion" (*badā lahu fī raʾyihi*). So the verse records a reversal: they had one view (the finding of 12:26-28) and took another. Al-Zamakhsharī's gloss is that the "them" is the household and those consulted in the case (our source's note records both: the family of Potiphar, or his counsellors), and the plurality is important — the decision is not one man's anger but a committee's judgement, which is the sūrah's most sober sentence about how the innocent are handled: not by a villain, but by a group that has thought about it.

**"The Signs" — Āyāt in a Corridor**

The word *āyāt* is used here, as our source records, for "the indications of Yūsuf's innocence". That choice links the household's corridor to the sūrah's opening claim — *tilka āyātu al-kitābi al-mubīn* (12:1) — and to its own thesis verse, "in Yūsuf and his brothers there are signs for the inquiring" (12:7). The chapter has been telling its reader all along that evidence is the medium in which it wants to be read; here the same word names the evidence the actors had and refused to follow. It is the sūrah's demonstration of its own premise: *āyāt* profit only the asking (12:7) and are passed over by the turning-away (12:105), and a household that sees signs and acts on its convenience is the type of the people who see signs in the heavens and the earth and go on. The Qur'an's general wording on this is exact:

> *"And if they see a sign (āyah), they say: 'We will not believe until we are given the like of what God's messengers were given.'"* (Qur'an 6:124; cf. 54:2: *"and if they see a sign, they turn away and say: 'passing magic'"*)

**"For a Time" — Ḥattā Ḥīn, and the Arithmetic of a Short Sentence**

*Ḥatta ḥīn*: "until a while." The sūrah's vagueness is not negligence; the next thing the reader learns is that the imprisonment meant to last "a while" became several years, because one sentence went unspoken (12:42: "But Satan caused him to forget to make mention to his lord. So he remained in prison for several years"). Our source records that the total was seven years according to most commentators, while other transmitted estimates are longer, and the sūrah itself never fixes a number — the phrase *biḍʿa sinīn* means "a number of years" (*biḍʿ* being the plural of *biḍʿah*, the indefinite small numeral). The text leaves the count open, because its interest is not duration but the disproportion: a household's temporary expedient, a man's whole youth.

The word *ḥīn* in Arabic is a stretch of time of indeterminate length, and its lexical field includes a mother's period of weaning (cf. 31:14: "his weaning (*fiṣāluhu*) is in two years"), so the sūrah's language here encloses an ordinary life-cycle. Genesis 39:20-23, which our source cites for comparison, says Potiphar took him and put him in the king's prison, and that "whatever he did, God was with him" — the Qur'an's version omits the theological gloss at this point and says something harder: they imprisoned him *after they had seen the signs*. The vindication is deferred to the king's court (12:50-51), and God's presence in the cell is shown, in the sūrah's manner, by what Yūsuf does there (12:36-41), not asserted about it.

**The Sūrah's Doctrine of Institutional Injustice**

Taken together, 12:25-35 form one of the Book's fullest accounts of how a person is destroyed by a process that produced the right finding. Nothing illegal was done: the evidence was examined, a witness was heard, a verdict of not-guilty was reached — and the man was jailed anyway, "for a time," because that was the tidy outcome. This is why Muslim ethicists, from al-Māwardī's *Adab al-Dunyā wa-l-Dīn* to Ibn Khaldūn's remarks on the politics of offices, treat this passage as a case in the sociology of power rather than in the biography of a prophet: the innocent party is not punished for a crime but for the inconvenience of his innocence. The chapter, however, does not end there. Its next move is to put Yūsuf, inside the cell that this verdict created, in charge of the two men whose profession was to be near the king (12:36), and to make the prison the only room in Egypt where the truth about dreams was available (12:43-44). The sūrah's answer to institutional injustice is not that justice will be done to him in the same currency, but that the currency itself will be his to set: "you will sow for seven years… and set aside what you harvest" (12:47), and finally "no reproach upon you today" (12:92) — a phrase that ends the matter without a trial.

**Two Practical Sentences the Verse Leaves With Its Reader**

First: the verse is the Qur'an's warning against "temporary" concessions to convenience, because *ḥattā ḥīn* is how every durable injustice begins — as a short measure that will be explained to the conscience as prudent. Second, and more quietly, the sūrah's placement of 12:35 immediately after 12:34 ("his Lord answered him, and turned their scheming away from him; truly He is the Hearing, the Knowing") is a piece of argument about the answer to prayer: God's *ṣarf* of the women's *kayd* from him is stated in one verse, and in the next he is imprisoned. The two verses are not in tension but in sequence: the plot that was turned away was the plot against his chastity, not the plot against his liberty. Whoever prays for preservation from a sin may still be exposed to the consequences of other people's fear — and the sūrah's own later verdict on that experience is Yūsuf's: *"my Lord is Latīf over whom He wills"* (12:100). The reader is entitled to ask which of the two he asked for, and the verse answers that he asked for the right one.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:36

> **36** *Now two young men entered the prison with him. One of them said, “Truly I see myself [in a dream] pressing wine.” The other said, “Truly I see myself [in a dream] carrying bread atop my head, from which the birds eat.” “Inform us of its interpretation. Truly we see you as being among the virtuous.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Entered the Prison With Him" — Companionship as the Sūrah's Category**

*Wa-dakhala maʿahu al-sijna fatayān*. The verse's first word about the two men is that they came in *with him*, and the sūrah keeps them at his side in the address: *yā ṣāḥibay al-sijni*, "O my two prison-companions" (12:39). The word *ṣāḥib* — companion, the one whose company is a claim upon you — is the same the Qur'an uses for the Prophet ﷺ in the cave: *"when he said to his companion (li-ṣāḥibihi): do not grieve, for God is with us"* (9:40). The sūrah's choice is small and deliberate: Yūsuf does not say "my two fellow-prisoners" but "my two companions," and the difference is the whole of his conduct in the cell — he does not treat his situation as a degradation to be endured among inferiors, but as a relationship with duties. The Prophet ﷺ made the same ethic a rule of conduct:

> *"The best of companions with God is the best of you toward his companion."* (Bukhārī 6057, from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ)

The lesson of 12:36's *maʿahu* is that a righteous man's prison becomes a madrasa precisely because he refuses the hierarchy the world has imposed on him.

**The Two Dreams and the Grammar of the First Person**

*Innī arānī aʿṣiru khamran / innī ḥāmilin fawqa raʾsī khubzan taʾkulu al-ṭayru minhu*. Each man speaks of himself in a construction the sūrah preserves twice: "I see myself pressing" — the imperfect of seeing with the accusative of the one seen — which is the classical way in Arabic of reporting a vision in which the dreamer is present to himself as acting. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the speech is *kināya* (oblique expression): the cup-bearer does not say "I saw grapes being pressed" but "I see myself pressing wine," and the baker does not say "my head was exposed" but "I carry bread from which the birds eat." Both dreams are, in the tradition's language, *ramz* (cipher) rather than *naṣṣ* (plain statement): a sign of honour (the cup in the king's hand) and a sign of disgrace (scavengers above the head) — and the detail that the bread is "above" the head rather than in his hands is what makes the second dream's shame unmistakable to any listener.

Genesis 40:5-7 gives the same two dreams, with the cup in Pharaoh's hand and the baskets above the baker's head, and adds that the two men asked why their dreams were "not alike" — a detail the Qur'an drops in favour of one thing only: they come to Yūsuf. Our source records the background the commentators supply: they were the king's cup-bearer and baker, the baker having plotted to poison the king and the cup-bearer being implicated by association (al-Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī), and the reason they ask him is that they had seen him interpreting other dreams, and had judged his character (al-Baghawī). The second of these is the sūrah's own emphasis, because the verse ends not with "you are clever" but with "we see you as being among the beautiful-doers."

**"We See You as Being Among the Muḥsinūn" — Competence Trusted by the Wicked**

*Innā narāka mina al-muḥsinīn*. The word *iḥsān* has already been used twice in this sūrah in the same register — 12:22's "thus do We recompense the beautiful-doers," and Potiphar's instruction "make his lodging beautiful" (*akrimī mathwāhu*, 12:21; the sūrah's Yūsuf will call the same kindness "he made my lodging beautiful," 12:23) — and the prisoners' sentence is a verdict of long observation, not flattery. The point matters for the sūrah's theory of qualification: Yūsuf's standing in a jail is earned by conduct (their "we see you," *narāka*, is a claim about what is visible), and the chapter has insisted throughout that God's gifts arrive through conduct, not instead of it (12:22: maturity, then *ḥukm* and *ʿilm*, then the law "thus do We recompense"). Al-Rāzī, in the note our source records, adds that they trusted his interpretation because they trusted his religion: a dream-reading from a man known for God-consciousness is more likely to be from an angelic source than from the self or the devil (see 12:6's classification of dreams).

There is also a sociological observation here that the sūrah makes no effort to hide. The men are the king's servants and the king's failed security; the only person in Egypt capable of reading the king's mind is in a cell, and the reason is a household's convenience (12:35). The chapter's irony about institutions is now structural: Egypt's future depends on a man the state is keeping in the dark, and the state's own employees are the ones who know it.

**A Dream and a Law: The Pressing of Wine**

One detail of the first dream is used in the exegetical literature for a question of legal theory: the cup-bearer's employment is the production and serving of *khamr*, and the sūrah mentions it without censure. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn ʿAbbās's reports in al-Ṭabarī both handle this with a principle: the law of a previous dispensation is not our law, and what God permitted to a people is not an argument for its permissibility after the Qur'an's prohibition — *"O you who believe, intoxicants… are an abomination of Satan's handiwork; avoid it"* (5:90). The sūrah is not silent on the matter, only uninterested; and it is a technique of the Book to record the customs of earlier societies without endorsing them, which is why the *uṣūl* rule "our sharīʿa abrogates theirs" (al-Zarkashī, *al-Baḥr al-Muḥīṭ*; al-Shāṭibī, *al-Muwāfaqāt*, on the changeability of divine law across nations) is developed partly out of verses of this kind. The practical import for the reader of 12:36 is that a dream about an ordinary trade is treated by the sūrah as data, not as a moral lesson: the chapter's theology is that God can speak through the neutral furniture of a man's working life.

**Why the Dreams Are Reported Before Their Meaning**

Finally, notice the narrative economy: verses 36-41 keep the interpretation until verse 41, and insert in between a sermon on *tawḥīd* (12:37-40). That is the sūrah's own ordering of priorities — the interpretation of two dreams is not the important event in the cell; the declaration of God's oneness to two pagan functionaries is. Our source's note on 12:37-40 observes that Yūsuf made mention of his God-given knowledge and his faith as a means to invite the young men to believe in God's Oneness, taking the opportunity of their trust to call them to God before answering their question; and the sūrah's later summary of his method — *"say: this is my way; I call to God upon insight (ʿalā baṣīrah), I and whoever follows me"* (12:108) — is the chapter's own generalisation of what happens in these six verses. The reader is thus given, in a prison, a model of teaching: competence earns the audience; the audience is used for what matters; and the service is then performed generously and well.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:37

> **37** *He said, “No food with which you are provided will come unto you, save that I shall inform you of its interpretation before it comes. This is among the things my Lord has taught me. I forsake the creed of a people who believe not in God, and they who are disbelievers in the Hereafter.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Claim Made Before the Question Is Answered**

*Qāla lā yaʾtīkumā ṭaʿāmun turzaqānīhi illā nabaʾtukumā bi-taʾwīlihi qabla an yaʾtiyakumā*. The two men have asked for an interpretation of dreams already seen; Yūsuf replies with a statement about all future ones — nothing will be provided to you that I do not tell you the end of before it arrives. Our source records the classical gloss: the *ṭaʿām* here is "the food with which you are provided," i.e. the substance of the dreams that come in sleep, and the assurance is that his gift is not a single lucky reading but a standing capacity. Our source records that the phrase alludes to the dreams the two men might have in their sleep, which Yūsuf assures them he will be able to interpret before they become actual events in their waking state (cf. Genesis 40:8); it records too another construal, that God taught him these things *because* he forsook the creed of a people who do not believe, and a third, that he mentioned his God-given knowledge and his faith together as a means of inviting the young men to believe in God's Oneness. Either way the structure is: Yūsuf first establishes that he speaks from a source, and only then delivers the reading (12:41).

The rhetorical reason for this order is that a prediction which is fulfilled after the fact is worthless to a hearer, since anyone can guess; the value of prophecy is that it announces itself before the event. Genesis 40:8 has the two men say "we have dreams and there is no one to interpret them," and Joseph answers "do not interpretations belong to God? tell me them"; the Qur'an's version goes further, having Yūsuf explicitly locate the capacity in divine teaching and then demonstrate it with a second claim he can be checked against: the future arrival of food. That is also how the Qur'an elsewhere describes such knowledge, and the limit it places on it, in the same breath:

> *"Knower of the unseen, and He does not disclose His unseen to anyone, except a messenger whom He has approved — for He sends forth before him and behind him watchers."* (Qur'an 72:26-27)

Yūsuf's sentence is, in the sūrah's own terms, an instance of 72:27 rather than an exception to 72:26: what he knows, he has been given; and this is the point of the second clause.

**"That Is of What My Lord Has Taught Me" — the Etiquette of a Gift**

*Dhālikumā mimmā ʿallamanī rabbī*. The attribution is the verse's centre of gravity, and the sūrah has already established the pair *taʿlīm*–*rabb* twice for him: *"thus will your Lord choose you and teach you the interpretation of events"* (12:6) and *"We established him… that We might teach him"* (12:21). What a prophet says in prison is what the chapter said in Canaan, and the reader is meant to notice that the gift has been kept exactly as promised, twenty years and two trials later.

Against that, the Book sets the opposite sentence, spoken by another man at the peak of worldly success:

> *"Qārūn said: 'I was only given it because of knowledge I have.'"* (Qur'an 28:78)

The two statements are a matched pair in the Qur'an's moral psychology: the same kind of excellence — a capacity God has given — claimed as an accomplishment, and claimed as a favour. Qārūn's line is about wealth; Yūsuf's is about knowledge, and the sūrah's point is that the second temptation is the subtler one, because a man who says "God taught me" about his own insight has to mean it, and cannot then use the insight to build a self. The classical ethicists — al-Muḥāsibī in *al-Riʿāya ḥuqūq Allāh*, al-Ghazālī in the book of gratitude in the *Iḥyāʾ* — treat 12:37 as the verse that defines the *ʿālim*'s conduct: the attribution of his own competence to God is not piety added on to the knowledge, it is the knowledge's condition — because the moment a man owns the gift, he begins to sell it.

**"I Have Left the Creed of a People Who Do Not Believe in God"**

*Innī taraktu millata qawmin lā yuʾminūna bi-llāhi wa-hum bi-l-ākhirati hum kāfirūn*. The word *milla* is not *din*: it denotes a traced-out way of life inherited across generations (from *malla*, to fill a waterskin — the sense of something poured out and followed), and the Qur'an uses it of the religion of Abraham specifically (2:132, 2:135, 3:65-67, 22:78, 43:22-23). Two consequences follow from Yūsuf's word choice. First, the break he announces is not primarily about gods but about an inherited culture — the *millat* of the Egyptians' court religion, which is precisely what a household servant would be expected to absorb; and he says of it "taraktu," "I have left," in the perfect: this is a completed act, not an intention. Second, the negation comes first and the affirmation only in the next verse (12:38: "and I follow the creed of my fathers"), which is the structure of the *kalima* itself — *lā ilāha*, then *illā Allāh*. The two verses read as a lesson in the order of tawḥīd: a religion is entered by leaving one, and the leaving must be named before the following is claimed.

That the *millat* he rejects is defined by two failures — not believing in God, and denying the Hereafter — is the sūrah's own diagnosis of the religion of power. Potiphar's house, the women of the city, and the king's court all act as though nothing follows this life; and Yūsuf, in a cell, names the two premises that make their conduct coherent. The pairing recurs as the Qur'an's standard definition of unbelief (cf. 75:20-25: *"but you love the fleeting world and leave the Hereafter"*), and it explains why the prison sermon is not an add-on to the dream-interpretation but its condition: a man who believes in a Hereafter can afford to be free in a room where everyone else is a slave to consequence.

**What the Verse Teaches About Preaching**

The sūrah gives the theory of this scene at its end: *"say: this is my way; I call to God, and whoever follows me, upon insight (ʿalā baṣīrah)"* (12:108). Yūsuf's conduct in verses 36-41 is the theory in practice. He does not refuse to serve the two men; he uses their trust, and the order of his speech is instructive: he begins with the gift and its source (so that they know where the answer comes from), then states his own position (so that they know the price of accepting it from him), then — only in 12:41 — tells them what they came for. The method is also an ethic: he makes his message cost them something (association with a convicted foreigner who despises their gods) before he makes it useful to them; and he never once insults their gods in these verses, but describes the men — "a people who do not believe in God" — and lets the argument of 12:39-40 do the work on the gods themselves. The verse's practical lesson for any teacher, in any room, is in its first clause and its last: competence is attributed away, and conviction is stated before the service is performed.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:38

> **38** *And I follow the creed of my fathers, Abraham, and Isaac, and Jacob. It is not for us to ascribe any partners unto God. That is from the bounty God has bestowed upon us and upon mankind, but most of mankind do not give thanks.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Affirmation, and Its Three Names**

*Wa-ttabaʿtu millata ābāʾī Ibrāhīma wa-Isḥāqa wa-Yaʿqūba*. Where verse 37 named what was left, this verse names what is followed, and it does so with a genealogy rather than a creed: not "the religion of tawḥīd" but "the way of my fathers." The three names are the sūrah's own link back to verse 6, where the blessing was promised to be completed "upon you and upon the family of Jacob, as He completed it upon your forefathers (ābāʾika) Abraham and Isaac." The chapter has thus built a bracket: the promise of a completed blessing on a prophetic house, and, twenty years later, the man in a cell claiming that house as the reason for his refusal of Egyptian religion. The two verses tell the reader that the promise is not a feeling but a tradition — a transmitted *milla* which a man can point to, name, and be held to.

The word *ābāʾ* — "fathers" — in Arabic covers forefathers generally, and Isaac was Yūsuf's grandfather; the sūrah is not erring but using the Semitic idiom of lineage, in which a grandson is a "son" and an ancestor a "father" (the same usage in 2:133, "what will you worship after me," and in the Bible's "the God of your fathers"). What is striking is the selection: Ishmael, Abraham's other son, is not among the three, because the line being named here is Yūsuf's own, and the promise attached to a line in the Qur'an is not a claim of inherited salvation. The chapter makes that explicit at 11:45-46, where Noah's own son is drowned and Noah is reproached for asking, and at 66:10, where Pharaoh's wife is a model for believers while Pharaoh is not. The *milla* of Abraham is a way to be followed, not a blood-right, and Yūsuf's sentence is written as an act of choosing: *tabiʿtu* — "I followed."

**"It Is Not for Us to Ascribe Partners to God"**

*Mā yakūnu lanā an nushrika bi-llāhi min shayʾ*. The construction — *mā yakūnu li-* plus *an* — is not the language of prohibition ("it is not permitted"); it is the language of what does not and cannot be the case for such a person: "it is not for us," "it would not become us." The same idiom marks a thing as beneath the dignity of the one described rather than merely forbidden: in the Qur'an's dialogue with Jesus, *"he will say: glory be to You! It is not for me to say of You what is not true (mā yakūnu lī an aquola mā laysa lī bi-ḥaqq)"* (5:116), and of God himself: *"it does not become (mā yanbaghī) God to take a son"* (19:92). The idiom marks a stage in moral formation: the person who has arrived at virtue no longer experiences its opposite as a temptation he resists, but as a description of himself he cannot accept. The Prophet ﷺ stated the same principle about the believer's character in the strongest possible terms:

> *"The fornicator does not fornicate while he is a believer; and the drinker does not drink while he is a believer; and the thief does not steal while he is a believer."* (Bukhārī 2475; Muslim 57, from Abū Hurayrah)

The ḥadīth is read by the commentators not as a sentence of expulsion but as a description of a condition: that a faith actually present and a sin actually chosen cannot fully coexist at the moment of the act. Yūsuf's "it is not for us" is the same claim at the level of a creed: shirk is not a sin he is strong enough to avoid, it is a description of a person he is not.

It follows that the sūrah has placed the strongest sentence of its theology of *tawḥīd* in the mouth of a prisoner, in a room, to two functionaries of a pagan state, with no platform, no protection, and no prospect of converting anyone. That placement is the chapter's answer to the question of why the sermon precedes the dream-interpretation: Yūsuf's position gives him nothing to lose, which is the condition of saying "it is not for us" at all. The verse is thus the practical counterpart of 12:53's *"the self is commandingly evil"* — the same anthropology inverted: what a person becomes when God shows mercy is not a being without desire but a being for whom certain options have ceased to be possible.

**"That Is of God's Bounty Upon Us and Upon Mankind"**

*dhālika min faḍli llāhi ʿalaynā wa-ʿalā al-nāsi*. The clause is the sūrah's theology of grace in miniature, and its two objects — "upon us and upon mankind" — are what make it remarkable. The blessing of prophetic monotheism is first a favour to a family (the same family named in 12:6), and then a favour to all humanity, though Yūsuf says the second half with no trace of entitlement. Al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī both gloss it as: the guidance we have is not our achievement, and its benefit extends beyond us. The Qur'an's general statement of the same idea is:

> *"And whatever of blessing (niʿma) you have, it is from God; then when harm touches you, to Him you cry for help."* (Qur'an 16:53; cf. 2:243 on the ungrateful many, 10:60, 27:73, 40:61, all cited in our source's note)

The verse's last words give the chapter's recurring sadness: *wa-lākinna akthara al-nāsi lā yashkurūn* — "but most people do not thank." Here for the first time in the sūrah the "most people" clause is attached not to knowledge but to gratitude, and the difference is instructive: they are not blamed for failing to know, but for failing to acknowledge what they have been given. This is why the sūrah's refrain, "and most people do not know" (12:21, 40, 68) and here "do not thank," forms a single diagnosis: ignorance and ingratitude are one condition, and gratitude is a form of knowledge. Yūsuf's own life is the counter-example; the sūrah will end with him attributing his elevation to *faḍl* (12:100: "my Lord has been gracious (Laṭīf) to me" and 12:101, his dying prayer) and then with the chapter itself closing on the words *"a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe"* (12:111) — the two words which describe a favour, and a people who receive it.

**The Discipline of Thanksgiving**

That a man who has lost a family, a name, and twenty years should attribute his state to *faḍl* and not to his own endurance is the verse's practical teaching, and the Sunnah's rule about how thanks is paid belongs to it:

> *"Whoever is shown a kindness and says to the one who did it: 'May God reward you with good (jazākā Allāhu khayran),' has indeed expressed his gratitude excellently."* (al-Tirmidhī 2035, from Usāma ibn Zayd; graded ḥasan by al-Tirmidhī)

The Prophetic wording is instructive in its direction: gratitude for a benefit is not paid back to the benefactor's ego but referred to God, who is its source. That is exactly the movement of 12:38's sentence, where the favour on a family is traced beyond the family, and the thanks which "most people do not give" is the one thing a believer cannot be prevented from giving, in a prison, by anyone.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:39

> **39** *O my fellow prisoners! Are diverse lords better, or God, the One, the Paramount?*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"O My Two Companions of the Prison"**

*Yā ṣāḥibay al-sijni*. He calls them companions — the two of the prison — and not "prisoners," "slaves," or "servants." The sūrah's word choice at the head of the sermon is a piece of its ethics of address: Yūsuf begins by standing with them, not over them. Ibn Kathīr notes on 12:36-39 that this is the conduct of a man who could have used their dependence on him to acquire a patron, and instead used it to speak about God: he calls them "my companions" first and then asks his question. It is the chapter's answer to the question of how to talk to people who need something from you: the need is honoured, the address is equal, and the claim made on the hearer is only about what they already believe.

**The Question's Shape — a Comparison, Not a Refutation**

*A-arbābun muntaṣirūna khayrun am Allāhu al-Wāḥidu al-Qahhār*. The form is an interrogative of choice ("is this better, or that?"), and the logic is deliberately minimal. Yūsuf does not begin by denying the existence of the gods of Egypt, nor by attacking the king's religion, nor by asking them to abandon their names; he puts before them a comparison they can conduct from inside their own experience: many dispersed lords, or One who is paramount — which is better? *Muntaṣir* is from *n-sh-r*, to spread, to scatter, and the participle means "dispersed in different directions," each going his own way. So the contrast is not "many versus one" arithmetically but "many, and therefore divided" versus "one, and therefore whole." The two men, who live at the mercy of a court where a dozen functionaries can each destroy them and none can save them, know this in their bodies; Yūsuf's question starts from the data of their servitude.

The logic is the sūrah's own, and it is stated elsewhere in the Book as an argument about order rather than about power:

> *"If there had been in them [the heavens and the earth] gods other than God, they would have been ruined (la-fasadata). So glory be to God, Lord of the Throne, above what they describe."* (Qur'an 21:22)

> *"God strikes a parable: a slave owned by quarrelling partners (ʿabdan fīhi shurakāʾu mutafāriqūna), and a slave belonging exclusively to one man (rajulin wāḥidin): are they equal?"* (Qur'an 39:29)

The second of these is the Qur'an's own gloss on our verse, and its image is exact for two royal cup-bakers: divided ownership makes a person the object of incompatible demands, and what makes life unlivable in a court is not that there are lords but that they disagree. Al-Rāzī, in the note our source records on this verse, draws the cosmological conclusion: if there were more than one god the order of the world would show fissures; where the sūrah's two prisoners stand, the fissures are visible in their own case. Monotheism in this chapter is not first a metaphysical thesis but a diagnosis of why polytheism cannot deliver what it promises — an argument about *kayd* (12:28, 12:33) as well as about being: many plotters means many failures.

**"The One, the Paramount" — Two Names Chosen for the Hour of Judgement**

*Al-Wāḥidu al-Qahhār*. The pair occurs in the Qur'an in settings of ultimate exposure: *"and they will all come forth to God — and the weak will say to the proud… "* and *"the Dominion this day belongs to God, the One, the Paramount"* (40:16); *"and they will stand before God, the One, the Paramount"* (14:48); *"Say: He is God, the One; God, the Eternal Refuge (al-Ṣamad)"* (112:1-2); and, in Ṣād, *"Say: I am only a warner, and there is no god but God, the One, the Paramount, Lord of the heavens and the earth and what is between them, the Mighty, the Forgiving"* (38:65-66). The name *Qahhār* — from *qahara*, to compel, to subdue so that the thing subdued cannot resist — is thus used in the Book not for raw force but for the sovereignty that remains when every other claim has been dissolved, and it is paired (38:66) with *Ghaffār*. The sūrah's own balance is the same: the God who is *Qahhār* in the cell is *Ghafūr Raḥīm* at the end of the affair (12:53; 12:92; 12:98). Yūsuf's hearers, who live in fear of a compulsion that punishes, are told about a compulsion that forgives — and it is precisely at that hinge that the sermon's next verse will say "He commands that you worship none but Him."

**Why the Sermon Comes First**

The two men had asked for a reading of their dreams (12:36), and Yūsuf spends five verses on God before he gives it (12:37-41). The order is the sūrah's point about the priority of teaching. Our source records that verse 39's function, after verse 38's claim of prophecy, is to establish "the basis of prophecy, namely its divine origin," and that the demonstration of God's oneness is the ground on which the interpretation can be trusted: a man who reads the future is either told by the One who made it or is guessing. The chapter's later self-description generalises the conduct into a rule for the community: *"Say: this is my way; I call to God upon insight, I and whoever follows me, and glory be to God, and I am not of those who associate partners"* (12:108) — the last clause of 12:108 being the exact echo of the prison sermon's "it is not for us to ascribe partners" (12:38). The sūrah thus frames its own biography: the man who would be the ruler of Egypt begins, in a cell, by doing the thing the chapter later defines as the messenger's work, and the two passages are worded to rhyme.

**What the Verse Asks of Its Reader**

The question in 12:39 is left open in the text because the answer was obvious to the two men and should remain so to us: Yūsuf never records their reply, and the sūrah does not need it. The interrogation is addressed, in the first instance, to anyone who has ever arranged his life around several competing authorities whose demands cannot all be met — a market, a family, an office, a reputation — and who has noticed that serving many masters produces not freedom but collisions. The chapter's claim is that "the One, the Paramount" is not a restriction on the world's government but its simplification: one address for the whole of a life, which is why Yūsuf can be simultaneously a servant in the house, a prisoner in a cell, and a minister in a court, without changing his allegiance. That is the practical content of *tawḥīd* in this sūrah, and it is why it is preached, in these verses, to two frightened palace employees.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:40
