> **40** *You serve not, apart from Him, except names which you have named, you and your fathers — God has sent down no authority for them. Judgement belongs to none but God. He commands that you serve none but Him. That is the True Religion, but most of mankind have no knowledge.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Names You Have Named" — Idolatry as an Act of Naming**

*Mā taʿbudūna min dūnihi illā asmāʾan sammaytumūhā antum wa-ābāʾukum*. The sentence's grammar is an exclusive negation: what you worship, apart from Him, is nothing except names. Yūsuf does not say "you worship idols," nor "you worship demons," nor "you worship things that have no existence"; he says that the objects of their cult have only one mode of being — they are labelled. To *summa* a thing into existence, to name it as a god, and to inherit that naming from one's fathers, is the whole history of the practice. The verse's polemical force is that it reduces the gods of Egypt to an act of the worshippers: the name is theirs, and therefore no claim upon them, and the accusation does not reach anything outside the human will. The same argument, with the same words, is made to the Meccans:

> *"Have you considered al-Lāt and al-ʿUzzā, and Manāt, the third, the other? Do you have the male and He the female? That then is an unfair division. They are nothing but names which you have named, you and your fathers; God has sent down for them no authority (mā anzala llāhu bihā min sulṭān). They follow nothing but conjecture and what the self desires, though the guidance has already come to them from their Lord."* (Qur'an 53:19-23; cf. 7:71, both cited in our source's note)

The rhetoricians note that 12:40's *antum wa-ābāʾukum* ("you and your fathers") is the sūrah's own answer to the appeal to ancestry. The inheritance of a name is the mechanism by which an empty sign becomes a religion; and in 12:38 Yūsuf has already contrasted his own inheritance — "the creed of my fathers Abraham, Isaac and Jacob" — where what is inherited is a command that was sent down, not a label that was coined. The two verses must be read together, and they define the test the chapter applies to every tradition: does it trace back to revelation or to a decision of the ancestors (cf. 2:170; 5:104; 31:21; 43:22-23).

**"God Has Sent Down for Them No Authority" — the Epistemology of Belief**

*Mā anzala llāhu bihā min sulṭān*. The *sulṭān* is a warrant that constrains — from *s-l-ṭ*, power that makes a case binding — and in the sūrah's own vocabulary it is a close cousin of the *burhān* that came to Yūsuf when he refused (12:24). The standard the verse sets is thus not "feel free to believe what you like" but its opposite: belief requires an authority that has been *sent down*, and the absence of one is not an invitation to fill the gap but a verdict that there is nothing to believe. The commentators gloss *sulṭān* here as a demonstrative proof or a revealed authority, and the classical kalām literature (from al-Ashʿarī's *Ibṭāl al-istiḥsān* to al-Ghazālī's *Faṣl al-maqāl*) treats this as one of the verses that make *burhān*, not *taqlīd*, the condition of assent. A reader of this sūrah will also note the honesty of its own epistemic register: the chapter has told us that God gave Yūsuf *ḥukm* and *ʿilm* (12:22), and Yūsuf in this verse states his authority as something given, not claimed (12:37: "that is of what my Lord taught me"); nothing in the prison sermon asks to be believed on the strength of the speaker's charisma alone.

**"Judgement Belongs to None but God"**

*In al-ḥukma illā lillāh*. The clause is the sūrah's most quoted, and it has carried a heavy political afterlife: it was the battle-cry of the Khārijites against ʿAlī after the arbitration at Ṣiffīn, and his answer — "a word of truth intended for falsehood (kalimat ḥaqqin yurādu bihā bāṭil)," a reply transmitted in the ḥadīth collections in the reports about the Khawārij — is the classical warning about the verse's misuse. What the verse says in its own context is narrower and more radical: the *ḥukm* that binds conscience belongs to God, so that no human tribunal — "diverse lords" (12:39), the king, the ʿazīz, the mob of the city (12:30) — has the last word. In the sūrah's plot the point is immediate: Yūsuf, who is being held by men who have seen the evidence of his innocence, tells them that the judgment that matters is elsewhere; and the chapter then shows him *judging* — "each man was returned his merchandise" (12:76), and his rulings on the grain, on the measure, on the accusation of theft (12:75-76) — with the sūrah's own gloss that above every one possessed of knowledge is the All-Knowing (12:76). The reconciliation of the two is given in the Book's general rule for judges and rulers:

> *"O David, We made you a vicegerent upon the earth, so judge between the people with truth (fa-ḥkum bayna al-nāsi bi-l-ḥaqqi), and do not follow desire, for it will lead you astray from God's path."* (Qur'an 38:26; cf. 4:105: "judge between the people by what God has shown you")

So 12:40 denies neither courts nor administration; it denies their autonomy. Human judgment is legitimate when it is exercised *bi-l-ḥaqqi*, under a warrant it did not issue to itself — which is why Yūsuf can hold office and say, in prison, that judgment is God's.

**"That Is the True Religion, but Most People Do Not Know"**

*dhālika al-dīnu al-qayyimu wa-lākinna akthara al-nāsi lā yaʿlamūn*. *Al-Qayyim* is the intensive active participle of *qāma*: what stands as it ought to, and by standing keeps others upright; used of the religion in 30:30 (cited in our source), 9:36, and 9:11. The Qur'an's phrase means neither "the most convenient" nor "the most spiritually refined," but the creed that holds its footing in reality: worship of the One who actually exists, on the basis of an authority actually sent down. Against that, the verse's ending is the sūrah's most persistent qualifier: "but most people do not know" — the third occurrence of the formula in this chapter (12:21; 12:40; 12:68) and its permanent protection against reading prophecy as sociology. The truth of a religion is not a function of its adoption, and the chapter, which has just shown an entire city wrong about one man, is entitled to say so.

**The Verse as a Criterion**

The practical gain of 12:40 for a reader who is not in an Egyptian prison is a test with three parts, in the verse's own order: what am I devoted to that has only been *named*; what authority — sent down or merely inherited — stands behind it; and whose *ḥukm* am I treating as final? Yūsuf's sermon is addressed to two men whose entire lives were organised by the first kind of thing: titles, rites, and the fears their names carried. He dismantles none of the machinery; he points out that it is a name. The chapter then shows the two men behaving exactly as the analysis predicts — one of them forgetting the man who saved his life (12:42) — and the sūrah leaves the reader to judge whether a system that produces such gratitude deserves the reverence it receives.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:41

> **41** *He said, “O my fellow prisoners! As for one of you, he shall give his lord wine to drink; but as for the other, he shall be crucified, and the birds shall eat from his head. The matter concerning which you seek my verdict is decreed.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer, at Last, and in the Register of Decree**

Only now, after five verses on God, does Yūsuf answer: *qaḍiya al-amru alladhī fīhi tastiftiyān* — "the matter about which you two are asking for a ruling has been cut through." The sūrah's word for their request is *istiftāʾ*, the seeking of a *fatwā* (root *f-t-y*, to make clear; *iftāʾ* is answering a legal question with clarification), which means the chapter formally treats a dream-reading as a ruling. That is not a loose metaphor; it sets the standard by which Yūsuf's answer is offered. He uses no *rubbering* terms: no "perhaps," no "it seems," no "I think it portends." He says it has been *qaḍā* — from *q-ḍ-y*, whose core sense is to cut, to decide definitively — as in "your Lord has decreed (wa-qāḍā rabbuka) that you serve none but Him" (17:23), a verse whose content 12:40 has just quoted in Yūsuf's sermon. The rhetoricians note the elegance of a muftī who uses the verb of *qaḍāʾ* rather than *iftāʾ*: he does not give an opinion that may be acted on; he reports a sentence already passed.

This is the sūrah's ethic of telling hard things. The manner is candour rather than tact: neither man is left to infer his own fate from the other's face. A man about to be executed deserves an honest sentence more than a kind ambiguity, and the sūrah presents the prophet giving exactly that. The same virtue will appear at the other end of the chapter: the cup-bearer, recounting the affair to the king, calls him *al-ṣiddīq*, "the truth-veracious" (12:46), because his word and his reading matched what came to pass.

**"He Shall Give His Lord Wine to Drink" — One Act of Service as a Whole Career**

The first man's dream was that he saw himself *pressing* wine (12:36); the interpretation is that he will *pour* it for the king. The change from pressing to giving-to-drink is the point: the press is labour, the cup at the royal hand is office. Our source records that the wine-carrier would be saved and restored to his former position, as a wine-server to the king of Egypt (cf. Genesis 40:12-13), while the baker would be crucified for his crime and, after his death, the birds would eat from his head (cf. Genesis 40:18-19). What the sūrah preserves is only the sentence "he shall give his lord wine to drink," and its terseness is instructive: a whole life's reversal — prison back to palace in three days — is described by a single return to an ordinary duty. It is the same compression the chapter uses for elevation itself (12:56 "thus did We give him a place in the land") and for being forgotten (12:42 "so he stayed in prison for some years"): in the Qur'an's moral vocabulary, promotion and ruin are both just the reassignment of tasks.

The detail that the restored man will serve *his lord* — *rabbahu*, "his lord," in the sense of master — is worth a note on the sūrah's diction, because it is one place where Yūsuf himself uses *rabb* of a human. The chapter's vocabulary of authority is graduated: *rabb* of God throughout (12:6; 12:33; 12:37; 12:101), *rabb* of Potiphar in Yūsuf's own refusal (12:23: "truly He is my lord (*rabbī*) and has made beautiful my accommodation"), *rabb* of the king here and in 12:42, *al-ʿazīz*, "the mighty one," for the minister (12:30; and the brothers' address in 12:72, 78, 88), and *al-malik* for the sovereign, whom Yūsuf addresses directly in 12:43 and who summons him in 12:50 and 12:54. The words of power in this sūrah are used without embarrassment, and the distinction between them is exactly the distinction Yūsuf came to teach: "diverse lords (arbāb), or God the One, the Paramount?" (12:39). To serve wine to a *rabb* is a job; to worship one is the error.

**"He Shall Be Crucified, and the Birds Shall Eat from His Head"**

*wa-amma al-ākharu fa-yuṣlabu fa-taʾkulu al-ṭayru min raʾsihi*. The word *yuṣlabu* is from *ṣ-l-b*, and our source renders the verb simply as "crucified," and notes that the birds eat from his head after his death; exposure, not the killing alone, is what the word denotes. The sūrah uses the same verb of Pharaoh's threat to the magicians: *"I will surely crucify you on the trunks of palm trees"* (20:71; 26:49), and it belongs, in the Book's penal language, to exposure after death rather than to the act of killing — which is why the jurists, treating the penalty of *ḥirāba* in 5:33 ("or that they be crucified"), debated whether the *ṣalb* there means execution by impalement or the exposure, for a set period, of a body already killed; the schools differ on both points, as the chapters on *ḥirāba* in the fiqh manuals and al-Qurṭubī's commentary on 5:33 show. Genesis 40:19 has Pharaoh "hang the chief baker on a tree, and the birds shall eat your flesh from off it," a wording closer to hanging than to impalement; the sūrah's *ṣalb* and the exposure of "his head" describe the Egyptian practice the chapter expects its audience to recognise.

The birds eating from the head, rather than from the body, is the detail the sūrah keeps from the dream (12:36 "carrying bread on his head, and the birds were eating from it"): the head is the seat of rank and of the crown, and so the punishment is inscribed on the very part that held office. That is the coherence of the two dreams as the chapter presents them: a hand raised to the king's mouth, and a head left open to the sky.

**"The Matter Is Decreed" — and the Second Execution the Sūrah Does Not Narrate**

The sūrah records the interpretation of both dreams and never tells us that the baker was put to death. After verse 42 it goes straight to the years in prison, the king's own dream, and the search for an interpreter (12:43-45). This is one of the most striking instances of the chapter's restraint: everything the two men asked for has been answered, and the execution, which the interpretation announced, is left offstage — the text does not feed its audience a killing it has already made inevitable. The rhetoricians of the Qur'an call this *iqtṣār*, cutting short (Sibawayh's *al-Kitāb*; ʿAbd al-Qāhir al-Jurjānī's *Dalāʾil al-iʿjāz* on omitted clauses), and in a narrative the technique has an ethical result: the sūrah is not the teller of the fate of the condemned; it is the reporter of a word of knowledge.

What fills the space instead is the third man's fate, and it belongs to the same economy: the cup-bearer lives, is restored, and forgets; and his one omitted sentence — "your lord has an innocent man in his prison" — costs a prophet years (12:42). The chapter's grim arithmetic about small human failures in powerful positions is thus established by the very men Yūsuf has just judged: the innocent are not only destroyed by malice, but forgotten by the grateful.

**A Ruling the Reader Is Meant to Test**

The last clause of verse 41, *qaḍiya al-amru alladhī fīhi tastiftiyān*, is deliberately verifiable: two careers, one restored, one ended, within three days (Genesis 40:20-22; the sūrah does not need to state it). Dreams in the Qur'an are not privileged as private gnosis but offered as public evidence: the chapter's own summary of how such knowledge works is that it is taught (12:37), given (12:6, 22), and then fulfilled in front of people. This is the practical standard for every claim to inspired knowledge in Islamic thought: what is announced beforehand should be recognisable afterwards, and a teacher who cannot bear to be checked has not the *sulṭān* of which 12:40 speaks. Yūsuf's answer in this verse is a promise, a warning, and a demonstration, in one sentence each, and the sūrah lets the events audit it.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:42

> **42** *And he said to the one of them whom he knew would be saved, “Mention me to your lord.” But Satan caused him to forget to make mention to his lord. So he remained in prison for several years.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The One of Them Whom He Knew Would Be Saved"**

*wa-qāla lilladhī ẓanna annahu nājin minhuma*. The received translation renders the verb as "knew," and it is entitled to, since in the Qur'an *ẓann* frequently denotes a conviction firm enough to act upon; Yūsuf had read the dream and pronounced the matter decreed (12:41). Still, the word the sūrah chose is *ẓann*, not *yaqīn* or *ʿilm*, and the choice is worth respecting: the chapter reserves unqualified knowledge to God — "above every possessor of knowledge is a knower" (12:76), "God has sent down no authority" for anyone else's claims (12:40) — and lets even the best human reading of the future stand in the grammar of expectation. The verse thus models a discipline of speech: Yūsuf acts decisively on what he has been given, without describing the given as a possession. It is the same courtesy the chapter has already put in his mouth twice (12:6, 12:21, 12:37): the interpretation is a *teaching*, and a teaching has a Teacher.

There is a further quiet dignity in the wording: Yūsuf addresses only the one he expects to live. He does not ask either man for a favour, and he says nothing at all to the condemned. The sūrah has him perform one small act of life-seeking with the survivor and none with the dying — an economy of feeling that the chapter practises everywhere: the innocent man in the cell does not spend his remaining hours on the man about to be executed, and the text offers no comment on the mercy or the hardness of that.

**"Mention Me Before Your Lord" — Asking to Be Remembered**

*udhkur-nī ʿinda rabbika*. The request is not "get me out," "speak in my defence," or "explain that I am innocent." Our source records the plain fact — Joseph told the wine-carrier to mention him to the king of Egypt (cf. Genesis 40:14-15) — and what the sūrah declines to add is a plea: *udhkur-nī*, "remember me aloud," a single sentence in the hearing of a ruler who had forgotten a prisoner existed, which was enough, since the case itself (12:26-28) was unanswerable once opened. The sūrah has already taught that vindication is not the innocent man's own project (12:29: "Joseph, turn away from this. And you, seek forgiveness for your sin. Truly you were among those at fault"), and it now shows him behaving accordingly: he asks to be brought to mind, not to be cleared, and leaves the verdict to the process.

**Satan in This Sūrah: The Sower of Rift, Not of Desire**

*fa-anṣāhu al-shayṭānu dhikra rabbihī*. The sūrah names Satan three times in all, and this is the middle of the three: Yaʿqūb's warning that "Satan is a manifest enemy to man" before the well (12:5), the devil's work here in the prison, and Yūsuf's own verdict on the whole affair at its end:

> *"My Lord has been gracious to me… and indeed Satan was a cause of discord (nuzugh) between me and my brothers."* (Qur'an 12:100)

That distribution is the point. In this chapter the devil is never the tempter to a sin — the seduction scene of 12:23-24 mentions no Satan at all, because the temptation there is named as the self's desire and answered by a *burhān* from his Lord. Satan appears only as the one who gets *between* people: between the brothers and their brother (12:8-10, 12:16, 12:100), and between Yūsuf and his deliverer (12:42). Enmity to man (12:5) is, in this sūrah's depiction, enmity to a family's bond. Our source records the commentators' second reading of the clause — "Satan caused him to forget the remembrance of *his* Lord," i.e. God (cf. the alternative translation in 12:42's note): at the moment Yūsuf spoke, the young man's God-consciousness slipped, and a soul that has lost its vertical anchor loses its duties too. The same construction appears in the story of Mūsā and his servant, where the loss of a fish is traced to the same cause:

> *"He said: do you see? When we took refuge at the rock, I forgot the fish — and none but Satan made me forget to mention it (wa-mā ansānīhu illā al-shayṭānu an adhkurahu)."* (Qur'an 18:63)

The parallel is instructive because in both cases the forgetting is small, unheroic, and consequential — nobody sins; nobody is even tempted, in the ordinary sense; a sentence simply goes unspoken, and a prophet stays in the dark. That is the sūrah's theology of *nisyān*: the mechanism by which the devil does most of his work in a decent person's life is not the injection of an evil desire but the deletion of a duty from the mind at the moment it was due. Genesis 40:23 states the same fact without the theology: "the chief cupbearer did not remember Joseph, but forgot him."

**"So He Remained in Prison for a Number of Years"**

*fa-labitha fī al-sijni biḍʿa sinīn*. The conjunction is not *fa-li-ajli dhālika* but a plain sequence — "he forgot, and he stayed" — and the difference matters: the verse juxtaposes without formally assigning the delay as a punishment. It is the transmitted reports that draw the moral, and our source preserves the criticism in a prophetic report:

> *"God have mercy on Joseph. Had it not been for a statement he made — 'Mention me to your lord' — he would not have remained in the prison as long as he did."* (a prophetic report transmitted in al-Ṭabarī's Tafsīr, as our source records)

Our source also records, on the authority of Ibn ʿAbbās, that had Yūsuf remembered God when he told the wine-carrier to mention him, God would have released him earlier; the same source notes that the verse's second reading — "Satan caused him to forget *remembering his Lord*" — is what makes the report coherent, since the fault it supposes is a lapse of reliance, not a wrong done to anyone. The chapter itself records no rebuke, and the request was for a lawful right by lawful means; the delay is best read, with the commentators' judgment, as the fruit of hanging a need on a forgettable man rather than on the One who "answers the call of the one who calls" (2:186). Both halves are needed. What the sūrah says is that Yūsuf asked, and that the asking did not work; what the reports add is that the asking was, in a sense, the reason it did not work — because a word addressed to a courtier is a word at the mercy of a courtier's memory, while the matter belonged to the One whose judgment is His alone (12:40: "Judgement belongs to God alone"). That is exactly the lesson the chapter has prepared in the prison sermon, and it is not stated there.

The arithmetic of the delay is left open on purpose: *biḍʿ* is the plural of *biḍʿah*, the Arabic indefinite numeral for the range between three and nine or ten, so "a number of years" (the grammarians' rule, Sībawayhi's *al-Kitāb*). Our source records that the whole stay amounted to seven years according to most commentators, and the note on 12:45 in our source takes that verse's phrase "remembering after a while" — literally *baʿda umma*, "after a long while" — to mean the same seven years — that is, the length of Yūsuf's imprisonment, so that the forgetting and the delay are measured by one and the same number. Genesis 41:1 says two full years passed between the forgetting and the king's dream. The sūrah's own vagueness is not ignorance but proportion: the years are not the point, the unspoken sentence is.

**The Forgetting and the Remembering — the Sūrah's Economy of Gratitude**

The verse ends in a cell and the next scene is a palace, and between them the sūrah puts nothing at all; the memory returns late and under pressure (12:45: "the one who had been saved among the two said, remembering after a while: 'I shall inform you of its interpretation, so send me forth'") — and our source notes that he persuaded the king by pointing to his own verified experience of Yūsuf's reading, which is to say: he remembered the dream that had come true, and only then remembered the man whose mouth it came from. The chapter's ethic of gratitude is explicit elsewhere — "so remember Me, I will remember you" (2:152) — but here it is enacted as a warning to both parties: a favour remembered only when it is needed is the currency of courts, and the Prophet ﷺ set against it a different standard:

> *"He who does not thank people does not thank God (man lam yashkuri al-nāsa lam yashkuri llāh)."* (from Abū Hurayrah; transmitted by al-Tirmidhī and Abū Dāwūd in the chapters on gratitude, and graded *ḥasan* by al-Tirmidhī)

> *"Whoever forgets a prayer or sleeps through it, let him pray it when he remembers it; there is no expiation for it except that."* (Bukhārī 597; Muslim 684)

The second ḥadīth is the sūrah's own remedy stated as law: a duty dropped is not voided by the dropping, and the moment of remembering is the moment of performance. The cup-bearer remembers after years and immediately speaks (12:45-46), and because he does, Yūsuf comes out — and the chapter will have the saved man's late sentence be the hinge of a famine's relief for a region (12:47-49). A forgotten mention cost a man his freedom; a remembered one cost the forgotten man nothing and saved Egypt. That is the sūrah's verdict on the smallest of duties.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:43

> **43** *And the king said, “Verily I see [in a dream] seven fat cows being eaten by seven lean ones; and seven green heads of grain, and others dry. O notables! Give me your opinion on my vision, if you can interpret visions.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The King Said" — a Title the Sūrah Chooses with Care**

*wa-qāla al-maliku*. The ruler of Yūsuf's Egypt is never called *Firʿawn* in this chapter; he is *al-malik*, "the king," as at 12:50, 12:54, and in Yūsuf's own phrase "the law of the king" (*dīn al-malik*, 12:76). The Qur'an's usage elsewhere is the reverse: the oppressor of Mūsā is *Firʿawn*, the name by which Egypt's New Kingdom rulers were addressed in the Book's Arabic (from the Egyptian *per-aa*, "great house," which became a title of the person rather than the palace). The distinction between the two Egyptian courts is one of the sūrah's quiet pieces of precision: the reader of Genesis 41 finds "Pharaoh" in the Yūsuf story, and the Qur'an has no word for the earlier dynasty's sovereign except *malik*. Our source's commentary nowhere has to point this out for the observation to matter: the sūrah is not paraphrasing a received narrative but using the vocabulary of a court whose law — not whose cult — is what constrains Yūsuf (12:76).

**Two Visions in One Sentence**

The dream is double: seven fat cows devoured by seven lean, and seven green ears beside seven withered. Our source's note on 12:46 records that the two images were read by the commentators as one sign told twice, and in the sūrah's own register that is the mark of a confirmed matter: Yūsuf's father had warned about the vision that would set the plot in motion (12:4-5), and the sūrah closes the whole affair with "O my father, this is the interpretation of my vision of old; my Lord made it true" (12:100) — an event whose *taʾwīl* arrives from far off and lands exactly. The doubling is not ornament. It fixes the same seven years in two media, animals and grain, so that the reader cannot mistake the dream for anything other than an agricultural forecast: cattle are Egypt's movable wealth, and the ear of grain its standing one; the lean eating the fat and the dry withering the green is the same sentence in two dialects of famine.

The animals' behaviour is what makes the vision terrible: the lean cows do not merely outlive the fat, they *devour* them (*yaʾkuluhunna*), and the withered ears are placed *beside* the green (*wa-ukhara jādidāt*, "and others dry"), so that hope and ruin are seen together. The note on 12:47 in our source makes the same point about the vision's order: it does not show the years of scarcity destroying the years of plenty by subtraction but by *consumption*: what was produced in the fertile years will be eaten up in the lean ones — and hence the instruction to store.

**"ʿAbara" — Interpretation as a Crossing**

*in kuntum fī al-ruʾyā taʾburūn*. The verb *ʿabara* (root *ʿ-b-r*, to cross over) is used for reading a dream, and our source records the lexicographer's point: interpretation "connotes the notion of moving from the imaginal and symbolic forms in dreams to the actual meanings they symbolize." It is a crossing, and a crossing requires a ford — the interpreter must know the terrain on both banks. This is why the same verb is used in the Qur'an of passing over a difficulty and of the "crossing" of the sea: *ʿabara* is a passage with a destination. So the king's conditional sentence, "if you can cross," is a piece of court realism: he is not asking for opinions, but for someone who can get from the image to the event, and he admits that the capacity is not guaranteed by rank.

The word *ruʾyā* itself is the Qur'an's term for a veridical vision (as our source's note on 12:4 records), as distinct from *ḥulm*, which in the Prophet's ﷺ usage covers the ordinary and the satanic dream:

> *"A true vision (al-ruʾyā al-ṣāliḥa) is from God, and a dream (al-ḥulm) is from Satan."* (Bukhārī, Kitāb Taʿbīr al-ruʾyā; Muslim, Kitāb al-Salām)

The sūrah's usage fits this discipline precisely: the king says "I see" in the language of vision, the notables reply with *aḥlām*, "dreams" in the plural of *ḥulm* (12:44) — their downgrade of his word is the first thing they do, and the sūrah marks it by the nouns.

**"O Notables" — the Office and the Empty Shelf**

*Yā ayyuha al-malaʾu aftūnī fī ruʾyāya*. The *malaʾ* are the notables, the prince's council (our source glosses: "those in his entourage and in high offices in his kingdom"); and the verb of asking is *aftū* — "give me a ruling," the technical term of juridical response. The scene is thus framed as a consultation of experts whose discipline has failed them at the exact moment the state needs it: the court's function is to answer, and it cannot. Genesis 41:8 has Pharaoh's "magicians and all the wise men of Egypt" unable to interpret, and then adds the detail the Qur'an omits — that there was "none that could interpret" — because the sūrah's interest is not the spectacle of failure but its cause: *ḥuḍūr* of office without *ʿilm* of the thing asked. The sūrah then puts its hand on the mechanism in 12:44: they do not merely say "we cannot"; they say the dream is nothing, which is the standard move of a competence that protects its reputation by denying the reality of what it cannot read.

The whole episode is the chapter's most direct commentary on how institutions lose their future. Yūsuf, who can cross from symbol to meaning, is in a cell because of a word the *malaʾ* themselves heard (12:30), and the court that could have released him is the court now forced to ask a prisoner's help through a servant (12:45). The sūrah's economics of talent are unsentimental: men capable of saving a region from famine are lost for years to a household's embarrassment, and the recovery is arranged by chance memory in a corridor. Its ethics are equally plain: Yūsuf interprets anyway — the famine's relief is not conditioned on his own vindication, though he will use the opening to ask for one thing, a retrial of the women (12:50) — and the chapter makes that sequence, help first and justice in the same breath, the measure of the man.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:44

> **44** *They said, “What confused dreams! And we are not experts in the interpretation of dreams!”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Halves of an Honest Sentence**

*Qālū aḍghāṯu aḥlāmin wa-mā naḥnu bi-taʾwīl al-aḥlāmi bi-ʿālimīn*. The reply has two limbs, and the sūrah's judgment falls on the first, not the second. "We do not know how to interpret dreams" is, in the Qur'an's own ethics of speech, a virtuous sentence — it is the refusal to pronounce without knowledge, which the Book makes a duty:

> *"And do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge; surely the hearing, the sight and the heart — of all these you will be questioned."* (Qur'an 17:36)

The second limb — *aḍghāṯu aḥlām*, "jumbled dreams" — is the failure: it is a diagnosis of the patient by the physician who cannot treat him. Had they stopped at their own ignorance, the story would not have needed a prisoner; they add a dismissal of the dream's reality, and this is what the sūrah exposes. The rhetoricians note that the plural *aḥlām* is used here where the king had said *ruʾyā*: the council answers his word "vision" with the word for ordinary dreams, and by that single substitution declares that nothing has been revealed to him at all. Our source's note is exact: *aḥlām* here is a synonym in their mouths of the *ruʾyā* of 12:43, and they are placing the king's vision in the category of "mixed-up dreams" that correspond to nothing in reality.

**"Aḍghāṯ" — What the Word Means**

*Aḍghāṯ* is the plural of *ḍighth*, which the lexicographers explain from the sight of a flock returning from different pastures, each animal mingled with others, the trails confused (the root *ḍ-gh-ṯ* signifies intermixture and the confusion of one thing with another, so that the word is used of a herd in which several flocks have been driven together and can no longer be told apart). Applied to dreams, it is the standard term for a sleep in which the mind's daytime residue has been stirred without order. The classical psychology of dreams, which our source records in its note on 12:4 and 12:43, sorts visions into what comes from God, what comes from the self's own talk (*ḥadīth nafs*), and what comes from Satan; the notables are asserting, without any warrant, that the king's dream falls into the second class. Their competence ends where the claim begins: they can name a category, and the naming stands in for knowledge.

**Taʾwīl: Returning a Thing to Its Origin**

*taʾwīl al-aḥlām*. Our source's note on this verse sets out the word's history with care: *taʾwīl* etymologically means "bringing something back to its origin" (its root *أ-و-ل*, to return), and in the Qur'an it is used of the meaning to which multivalent verses return (3:7; cf. 10:39), of the outcome to which events return (18:87), and here of the reality to which a dream returns. The note adds that in later Islamic usage *taʾwīl* came to signify more particularly the spiritual or esoteric reading of a verse, opposed to the outward sense of *tafsīr* — and that the distinction is not rigid, since the title of al-Ṭabarī's encyclopedic commentary uses *taʾwīl*, not *tafsīr*, in its title (*Jāmiʿ al-bayān ʿan taʾwīl āy al-Qurʾān*). The reader of this sūrah has the word's history in miniature: Yaʿqūb says "I know from God what you do not know" when he sends the sons (12:86), and Yūsuf says "this is the *taʾwīl* of my *ruʾyā*" at 12:100, so the term runs from the dreams of 12:4 through to their fulfilment in 12:100, and the sūrah's own final verse makes the whole of its telling an act of *taʾwīl* in the etymological sense: a return of the story to its origin.

That the notables disclaim *ʿilm* of *taʾwīl* is thus not a small modesty: they are declaring that they have no access to origins — the meaning of things is not in their hands. The chapter's theology of knowledge (12:6, 22, 37, 76) says the same in a positive register: interpretation is *taught* (12:37), and every knower has one above him who knows (12:76).

**The Council's Bluff, and What It Costs Egypt**

The exchange is one of the Qur'an's compressed studies of an elite under pressure. Asked for a ruling, they produce a classification: "confused dreams," which relieves the king of his anxiety and them of their office. The sūrah does not report whether the king accepted it; it simply turns, in the next verse, to the man in the back row who remembers (12:45). This is the chapter's realism about the after-life of such answers: they do not end the matter, they only delay who speaks it. And the delay is the whole of the plot's tragedy: seven years of the famine's approach were available for preparation at the moment the dream was first told.

The etiquette of dreams in the prophetic reports belongs to the same discipline, and it is collected in the manuals under precisely this heading: a dream is to be related only to one who is learned or well-wishing; the man who sees what he dislikes seeks refuge with God, spits dryly to his left three times, turns over from his side, and does not spread it, since spreading it is what may make it land on him (Bukhārī, Kitāb Taʿbīr al-ruʾyā; Muslim, Kitāb al-Salām). None of this is superstition in the Book's sense; it is the recognition that an interpretation is an act with consequences. A dream read badly can manufacture hope or kill it, which is why Yūsuf's two readings in this sūrah are delivered in the order they are: first the sermon and the attribution of knowledge to God (12:36-40), then the verdict (12:41); first the years of sowing and the years of famine (12:47-49), then the year "in which people will be given help."

**The Verse as a Mirror for Any Enquiry**

Readers have taken *mā naḥnu bi-taʾwīl al-aḥlāmi bi-ʿālimīn* as the sūrah's type of the professional who is honest about his limits and dishonest about the subject. There is a whole ethics of the sentence in the combination: the first half is obligatory (a muftī who does not know must say so), and the second half is the sin (he must not then tell the questioner that the question is nonsense). Al-Shāṭibī's rule about the one who speaks without competence in *al-Muwāfaqāt* and the famous dictum transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās — that it is enough of knowledge for a man to say, when asked what he does not know, "I do not know" — are the tradition's own gloss on this verse's first half. Its second half is the warning in every quarter: an institution's readiness to dismiss a signal as "noise" is the last thing it does before the signal becomes a famine.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:45

> **45** *Now the one who had been saved among the two said, remembering after a while, “I shall inform you of its interpretation, so send me forth.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Named Only by What Was Done for Him**

*wa-ammā alladhī najā min al-rajulayni*. The sūrah does not call him the cup-bearer here, or the wine-server, or the king's officer; he is "the one who was saved." The verb *najā* (root *n-j-w*) is the word for reaching high ground, for getting clear, and the Qur'an uses it of those carried clear of the flood: *"and Noah, when he called out before that, and We answered him and rescued him and his family from the great distress"* (21:76), and of Lot: *"We gave him judgement and knowledge, and We rescued him from the town that practised the foul things"* (21:74). Cf. 10:103: *"thus it is an obligation upon Us to rescue the believers."* Applied to a man restored to a job after three days in a cell, the word is a gentle irony — the sūrah grants him the vocabulary of deliverance, because from Yūsuf's point of view that is exactly what it was: his reading, and not the court's, is what saved him. But it is also a piece of the chapter's ethics of memory: the only title that matters on the day of crisis is the one he received from a prisoner's interpretation, and the sūrah wears it for him in perpetuity.

The rescue is also, in the sūrah's terms, a *demonstration*. Yūsuf had said "the matter about which you inquired has been decreed" (12:41), and when one of the two is found alive, the whole city's debt to the man in the cell becomes a fact of public record rather than a private memory.

**"Remembering After a While" — Baʿda Umma**

*wa-d-dakara baʿda umma*. The verb is the intensive-reflexive of *dhikr*, *idhdhakkara*, "he recalled after having forgotten," and the noun of duration is remarkable: *umma*, in the lexicons a long stretch of time or a generation, is the same word as the community. The sūrah thus says, in one phrase, that he remembered "after a community" — a people's worth of time. Our source records that the commentators measure it as seven years, the length of Yūsuf's stay (al-Baghawī), which means that the sentence is built to make the reader feel the proportion: seven years for one sentence.

What the verse then does theologically is more important than the count. Between 12:42 and 12:45 the actor is not memory but its recovery. In 12:42 "Satan caused him to forget" (*anṣāhu*), and here he "recalled" (*idhdhakara*) — the sūrah's own account of how a duty returns: it is not summoned by good intention, it is sent back at the moment it is needed, when the notables have failed and the king has asked. The Qur'an's general statement of the mechanism is the verse that makes forgetting a consequence of forgetting God:

> *"And do not be like those who forgot God, so He made them forget themselves; those are the defiantly disobedient."* (Qur'an 59:19; cf. "so remember Me, I will remember you", 2:152, and 20:124)

The cup-bearer is not a villain in this sūrah; he is its illustration of the small, ordinary, expensive kind of failure — a man who did not lie, did not scheme, merely did not think of someone for a few years. That is the reason the sūrah places at the end of a long narrative of enmity and betrayal, as if to say that the ruin of the innocent is usually carried out by the inattentive.

**"I Shall Inform You" — Self-Recommendation in a Time of Need**

*anā unabbiʾukum bi-taʾwīlihi fa-ar silūn*. He volunteers himself, and the wording is modest in a way the Arabic makes audible: he does not claim to know the *taʾwīl* of the king's dream, but to be able to bring word of it — *anbaʾa* is to convey news, and the noun he uses for the content, *taʾwīl*, keeps the distance between "I know" and "there is a man who knows." Our source records how the speech worked on the king: he persuaded him by pointing to his own case, that when they were in prison together Yūsuf had interpreted his dream and it came to pass exactly as he said (cf. Genesis 41:9-13, where the man's confession is longer: "I remember my faults this day… there was with us a young man… and it happened just as he interpreted"). The sūrah compresses the apology to a single act of reference: the verification is the argument, and the argument is enough. Genesis's fuller speech, which the Qur'an drops, contains the man's admission of sin; the sūrah leaves the moral work implicit, because it has already shown what a forgotten word costs.

The last two words are the verse's practical lesson in manners: *fa-ar silūni*, "so send me" — plural, addressed to the council, from a servant who asks to be dispatched rather than commanding an audience. He cannot simply summon the prisoner, and he does not ask the king to release him; he asks to be sent. A society that has imprisoned its best interpreter now has to go to him; the sūrah lets the indignity of that fall on the institution, not on Yūsuf, who is the one being asked.

**The Verse's Place in the Chapter's Architecture**

12:45 is the hinge on which the second half of the sūrah turns. Everything before it has been the story of concealment: the well that was made to look like a wolf's killing (12:13-18), the shirt torn from behind (12:26-28), the doors locked (12:23), the household's decision to bury the finding under a few years of imprisonment (12:35). Everything after it is revelation: the truth comes out at the king's request (12:50-51), the women confess (12:51), and Yūsuf's own name for what happened to him is that God "made the vision true" (12:100). The mechanism of the turn is one sentence spoken by a man who had failed to speak it for seven years — which is the sūrah's view of how Providence uses the tardy. It also shows the chapter's realism about testimony: the court does not act because a prisoner's innocence is argued, but because a survivor's verified experience is offered by the only man who had it. In a society, the truth travels by way of creditors; here the debt is a life, and its repayment is one line in a corridor.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:46

> **46** *“Joseph, O truthful one! Give us thine opinion concerning seven fat cows being eaten by seven lean ones, and seven green heads of grain, and others dry, that haply I may return to the people, that they might know.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Double Vocative, and the Title That Replaces the Cell**

*Yā Yūsufa ayyuha al-ṣiddīq*. The address has two vocatives, which is unusual enough for the grammarians to have a rule for it: *ayyuha* placed after a proper name serves to lengthen the pause before the request, to add weight to the epithet, and — in the rhetoricians' analysis of deferential speech — to signal the speaker's need. The man who asks is a courtier asking a prisoner, and the sūrah gives him the grammar of a superior's politeness: he does not say "the prisoner," or "the Hebrew," or "Yūsuf, interpret"; he says the name and then the title, and the title is the thing that authorises the ask. Our source records the title's double reference, as an acknowledgement earned in the cell: it can refer to the truthfulness of his interpretation, or to the fact that in all their time together they had never seen him tell a lie.

That second meaning is the more extraordinary. A man who has been in prison for years, under an accusation of attempted rape, is given the sūrah's highest available word for integrity by two of the kingdom's employees, and the sūrah never lets the court apologise for the mistake. The chapter's verdict on his reputation is delivered in a compound of praise inside a request.

**"Ṣiddīq" — a Rank, Not an Adjective**

The word is the intensive form of *ṣādiq* (root *ṣ-d-q*), one whose truthfulness is habitual in word, in deed, and in belief, and the Qur'an uses it as a category of the saved:

> *"And whoever obeys God and the Messenger — those will be with those upon whom God has bestowed favour: of the prophets, the truthful ones (al-ṣiddīqīn), the martyrs, and the righteous; and what excellent companions!"* (Qur'an 4:69)

> *"And those who believe in God and His messengers — those are the truthful ones (al-ṣiddīqūna) and the witnesses, with their Lord; they have their reward and their light."* (Qur'an 57:19; cf. 39:33)

The rank of the *ṣiddīq* is thus set beside prophecy and the martyr in the Book's own lists, and the phrase of 57:19 — "with their Lord" — places it in the company of those whose standing is not visible. That is the sūrah's irony in this verse: Yūsuf holds the second-highest rank named in the Qur'an while sitting in a jail, and the title is first spoken aloud by a servant in a corridor. It is also the reason the epithet survives into Muslim usage as a personal name of honour — Abū Bakr is called *al-ṣiddīq* in the Islamic memory for the immediacy of his assent — and the reason a whole literature of *ṣidq* exists, from al-Muḥāsibī's treatises on sincerity to al-Ghazālī's paired chapters on truthfulness and sincerity in the *Iḥyāʾ*: the two words *ṣidq* and *ikhlāṣ* (the latter used of the servants of God at 12:24 and 15:40) name, in that literature, the same single act of making one's speech and one's self correspond.

**The Dream Repeated Verbatim — the Qur'an's Method of Verification**

The seven cows and the seven ears are recited again in wording almost identical to the king's (12:43), and the repetition has a function the sūrah's audience would have felt: the court's failure is on the record, and Yūsuf is asked the same question that the *malaʾ* could not answer, without any new information being added. The technique is documentary — the two accounts must match word for word for the reader to see that the difficulty was not the dream but the interpreters. Genesis 41:16 has Joseph answer "It is not in me; God will give Pharaoh an answer of peace," and the Qur'an's version has already said the same thing at greater length in the prison (12:37, 40) and will say it again in the answer (12:47: "you will sow for seven years in the usual way").

The other difference is instructive: the king asked "give me your opinion… *if you can* interpret visions" (12:43), a conditional addressed to experts; the cup-bearer says simply *aftinā*, "give us our ruling," without the condition. The sūrah's final clause makes the change the point — the request is now made by a man who knows the answer will come.

**"That Haply I May Return to the People, That They Might Know"**

*laʿallī arjiʿu ilā al-nāsi laʿallahum yaʿlamūn*. Our source records the commentators' two readings of the purpose clause: the notables and the king might learn the interpretation, or might learn Yūsuf's true rank and knowledge and so release him (with the note that the envoy hopes the court will come to know of Joseph's standing). Both are present in the sentence, and the sūrah does not separate them — which is how the chapter handles mixed motives throughout. The man wants Egypt saved and his own reputation restored; he wants the dream answered and the years of silence atoned.

Our source notes that when the wine-carrier related the dream to him, Yūsuf saw at once an opportunity to impress the king and so be set free, and an opportunity to help him, since part of the dream signalled an agricultural crisis for the people of Egypt (cf. 12:48); and the sūrah approves both motives without comment. That is the chapter's answer to an asceticism which holds that public service must be undertaken with purely unselfish intention: Yūsuf's famine plan (12:47-49) is his means of vindication, and his vindication is Egypt's rescue. The same realism structures the last of his speech: he asks for the women to be called and questioned (12:50) *after* he has solved the crisis, so that the truth should not be bought with the state's hunger. The sequence is the ethics: serve first, clear your name second, and never in the other order.

**"O Truthful One" and the Burden of the Title**

The address also fixes the terms on which the answer must be given. To be called *al-ṣiddīq* by a king's messenger is to be told that the audience will disbelieve anything convenient, and Yūsuf's reply does not flatter: the seven lean years will eat what was stored, and the only remedy is fifteen years of disciplined sowing and rationing (12:47-49). The chapter keeps this pairing — the title and the hard sentence — in view until the end, where the man called *ṣiddīq* forgives his brothers with the words "no reproach upon you today" (12:92) and dies asking for death in submission (12:101). Truthfulness in this sūrah is not a quality of accurate reporting only; it is the refusal to make a thing pleasant in the telling, whether the thing is a famine or a father's grief (12:86-87). A reader who is called honest before being asked a question should expect the same duty.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:47

> **47** *He said, “You will sow diligently for seven years. Then whatever you harvest, leave in its ear, save a little that you eat.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Interpretation That Issues in Work**

*Qāla taḥṭibūna sabʿa sinīna dāʾaban*. Yūsuf's answer does not stop at decoding the image — he never tells the king that the cows are years and the ears are harvests — but goes straight to what must be done, in the imperative of a plan. This is the sūrah's own criterion for distinguishing prophetic knowledge from divination: a true reading of a sign changes conduct, and the interpreter whose gift does not produce a command is not reading anything. Al-Rāzī's note, recorded in our source, is that the seven fat years were to be met by sowing "diligently," and the lean ones would devour the surplus; the interpretation is therefore half forecast and half instruction.

The verb the sūrah uses for the instruction is startling in a verse about grain: *taḥṭibūna*, from *al-iḥṭāb*, the cutting and gathering of firewood (the commentators gloss it as "labouring and earning," from the hard, unglamorous work of bringing in fuel, and the base translation's "sow diligently" preserves the effort rather than the agriculture). The choice of word puts the emphasis on sustained labour in the years of plenty, not on enjoying them. And the sūrah's other use of the same root, in the last chapter of the Book, is its comment: *"and his wife too, the carrier of firewood (ḥammālat al-ḥaṭab)"* (111:4) — the word for the woman who gathered kindling to feed malice. In 12:47 the root is used of gathering to store; in 111:4 of gathering to burn. The two verses together give the Qur'an's whole economics of accumulation: what are you collecting, and is it for the winter or for the fire?

**"Leave It in Its Ear" — the Agronomy of the Verse**

*fa-mā ḥaṭabtum fa-tarukūhu fī sunbulihi illā qalīlan minhu taʾkulūn*. The command not to thresh is the verse's most concrete piece of wisdom, and the exegetes are unanimous on the reason: grain left in the head keeps — the chaff shields it from weevils, from damp, and from the temptation to eat it. The instruction is thus not a ritual but storage technology, and the sūrah, which nowhere else in the chapter gives a technical direction, gives exactly one here. Al-Ṭabarī reports the gloss "leave it unthreshed, for that is more preserving," and the practical jurists of later centuries treated the verse as the Qur'anic basis for *ibdāʾ al-ghalāʾ* — the state's duty to compel the hoarded grain to market at a fair price when a famine bites, a rule developed in the *ḥisba* manuals from this episode (Ibn ʿUkhuwwa's *Maʿālim al-qurba*; the Mālikī chapters on *ibdāʾ al-ghalāʾ*). The sūrah's reserve is not buried treasure; the little that is eaten each year is the working portion, and the rest stays in the husk until it is needed, which is the definition of a strategic stockpile.

The exception clause, "save a little that you eat," also disciplines the reader's imagination about abundance. The seven good years are not to be lived out as seven good years: the same years must be a period of net saving, and the sūrah fixes the allowance at "a little." The Qur'an's own general rule for consumption is here embodied in policy: *"and eat and drink and do not be extravagant (wa-lā tusrifū)"* (7:31), and *"those who, when they spend, are neither extravagant nor stingy, but stand between that as a middle course"* (25:67). Yūsuf's Egypt is given the middle course as a fifteen-year programme.

**"Dāʾaban" — Persistence as the Unit of the Plan**

*Dāʾaban* — "continuously, as a habit," from the root that gives *daʾb*, a person's customary way of acting (the root gives *daʾb*, a people's or a person's customary way of acting, and the commentators gloss *dāʾaban* as "uninterruptedly, as a matter of routine"). The single adverb carries the verse's hardest requirement: seven years of diligence, not a year of heroic response. Famines are survived by routines, and Yūsuf's reading of the dream is a routine. The same insistence structures his father's advice at the other end of the sūrah: *"so patience is beautiful (fa-ṣabrun jamīl)"* (12:18; 12:83; 12:86; 12:90 — Yaʿqūb says God alone is to be asked for help against what you describe, and then models patience as an active, spoken discipline). The chapter's two sages, father and son, each prescribe a long practice rather than a single act: Yaʿqūb waits with speech, Yūsuf works with a schedule.

**The Plan as Trust, Not as Doubt in God**

Muslim readers have found in 12:47-49 a standing answer to the caricature that reliance on God (*tawakkul*) means passivity: the prophet with a dream from God prescribes fifteen years of ploughing, storing, and counting. The Prophet's ﷺ own proverb on the subject is transmitted in this sense:

> *"A man said: O Messenger of God, should I tie my mount and trust in God, or leave it free and trust? He said: 'Tie her and trust (iʿqilhā wa-tawakkal).'"* (al-Tirmidhī 2517, from Anas ibn Mālik; graded *ḥasan*)

The plan is the trust's proof, and the verse's next words will show why proof is needed: the lean years "devour what you advance" (12:48), so the only question the dream actually poses to a society is whether it will spend its fertility on itself. Modern readers, with their emergency funds, their grain silos, their seed banks and their pandemic stockpiles, are living inside 12:47's sentence structure — seven years of ordinary work, an unglamorous reserve held in a form nobody wants to look at, and a small allowance for living — and the sūrah's judgment on such prudence is implicit in the fact that it is put in a prophet's mouth as the interpretation of a vision sent by God.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:48

> **48** *Then after this will come seven hard [years] which will devour that which you have saved in advance for them, save a little of that which you have stored.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Years That Eat: Personification as the Sūrah's Style**

*Thumma yaʾtī min baʿdihī sabʿun shidādun yaʾkulna mā qaddamtum lahunna*. Seven "hard" years, and they *eat*. The verb is the same one that carried the dream: the lean cows "were eating" the fat ones (12:43: *yaʾkuluhunna*), and now the years themselves are the animals. The sūrah keeps the image's grammar intact instead of explaining it away, and the effect is that the famine is not a condition but an appetite — something with a mouth, that will consume what was put in front of it. The adjective *shidād* (plural of *shadīd*, hard, severe; from *shidda*, tightness, as used of a binding or a grip) belongs to the same field: the years are "tight," constricting, and the image of scarcity in the sūrah is not thirst or dust but pressure.

Such personification is one of the Qur'an's methods of moral arithmetic. In this sūrah the years eat, the shirt torn from behind testifies in a corridor (12:27), and a cup that belongs to the ruler is planted in a brother's sack (12:72) — the non-human world is drafted into the moral story because, in the sūrah's view, it was always in it. Objects and periods act; the sentence's agency is distributed among things that humans ordinarily treat as passive. That is the chapter's *ʿaqīdah* in miniature: nothing in the world is inert, because everything in it is being used.

**"What You Advanced" — the Economics of the Word Qaddama**

*Mā qaddamtum lahunna*. The verb *qaddama* (to send ahead, to advance) is the Qur'an's habitual word, not for grain, but for deeds stored for the next life: *"and whatever good you advance for yourselves (wa-mā tuqaddimū li-anfusikum min khayrin) you will find with God"* (2:270; cf. 73:20 — the idiom is pervasive). Its use here, in an instruction about wheat, is a small act of the sūrah's theology of time: what a household puts by for a famine and what a believer puts by for the Hour occupy the same grammatical position, and both are described as "advance." That is why the verse's exception is worded as an audit rather than a ration: *illā qalīlan mimmā tuḥṣūn* — "save a little of what you count." The root *ḥ-s-b* here (to enumerate, to compute, to restrict — the root of *iḥṣāʾ*, tallying, which the Qur'an uses of the record that survives every human advance: *"every thing We have tallied in a book (aḥṣaynāhu kitāban)"* (78:29), and *"We give life to the dead, and We write down what they have sent ahead (mā qaddamū) and their footprints"* (36:12) converts the store into a ledger. The seven hard years will eat everything except the amount that was deliberately kept track of, and the sūrah's phrasing implies that an uncounted reserve is no reserve.

The same pairing of "advance" and "reckoning" is made of the life of this world in general: *"know that the life of this world is play, diversion, adornment, boasting among you, and rivalry in increase of possessions"* (57:20), and the promise attached to the other kind of advance: *"whatever good you advance for yourselves you will find with God, and He is bestower of reward"* (2:270). The famine, in 12:48, is the eschaton in miniature: it consumes what was not advanced, and only the counted little survives.

**The Ethics of the Famine's Supply**

The verse's exception clause has had consequences in Islamic law far beyond Egypt. Once the sūrah establishes that the community must hold a store and that the store will be eaten down to "a little," the jurists ask the question the verse does not: what happens when the little is in private hands and the public is hungry? The answer, developed across the schools and enforced by the market inspectors, is the rule of *ibdāʾ al-ghalāʾ* — compelling those who hold staple food to bring it to market at a just price — with the prophetic prohibition of hoarding as its basis:

> *"No one hoards except a sinner."* (Muslim, Kitāb al-Siqāya; the wording "لَا يَحْتَكِرُ إِلَّا خَاطِئٌ")

> *"None shall sell in our market unless he has understood the ruling, otherwise he will have eaten usury whether he wishes or not."* (ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb's report on the Medinan market, transmitted in the *ḥisba* literature and in ʿAbd al-Razzāq's *Muṣannaf*)

Neither of these texts is about famine as such; both are about the arithmetic of the reserve. What the sūrah's "save a little" makes unavoidable is the question of distribution rather than of production: the store is a public trust, and the law of the granary belongs, in the Muslim tradition, to the same chapter of knowledge as the law of weights, measures and honest weights at 83:1-3. The sūrah's own sequel confirms it: Yūsuf is put over the storehouses (12:55), and the chapter's later dispute with his brothers turns on a measure, a planted cup, and a refusal to take an innocent man for a guilty one: *"We would be wronging indeed (innā idhan la-ẓālimūn) if we took anyone but the one at whose place we found our goods"* (12:79). That is, the whole second half of the sūrah is about the integrity of a granary's arithmetic, in a family as in a state.

**A Hard Year Is Not an Impossibility**

*"and whatever good you advance for yourselves, that is better for you"* (64:16) — the pairing of advance and reward is the Book's constant. The plan does not abolish the hunger; it bounds it. The seven fat years cannot be banked against everything — the lean devour all of it "save a little" — and yet the little is decisive: it is what stands between a famine and a collapse (the sūrah's own Egypt has grain when Canaan has none, and that asymmetry is what brings the brothers to the minister's door, 12:58). The advice to a reader, whether managing a household budget or a country's reserves, is in the verse's structure rather than its content: prepare for the worst with the assumption that it will consume the plan, and keep the counted remainder in a form that does not rot — the grain in the ear, the good deed in the record, the reputation for truthfulness that the sūrah will spend at 12:46 and repay at 12:100.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:49

> **49** *Then after this will come a year wherein people will be granted succor, and wherein they will press [wine and oil].”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fifteen Years, and the One That Is Named Last**

*Thumma yaʾtī min baʿdi dhālika ʿāmun*. The interpretation ends with a single year, and its indefinite article is the rhetoricians' clue: fourteen years of sowing and of famine are measured out in sevens, and the year that follows is simply "a year" — unnamed in length, unnamed in quantity, but described in two acts. Our source records al-Rāzī's gloss that this is "the last year of the cycle, a blessed year of much bounty for the people of Egypt," and that the pressing of oil and wine indicates that abundance has returned. The verse's brevity is the point: after fourteen years, the year that matters is the one in which people stop counting.

The structure of the divine answer to the dream also completes the chapter's own arc of numbers. Yaʿqūb said "I know from God what you do not know" (12:86); Yūsuf's speech in prison had already announced that God "teaches" the interpretation of events (12:6, 12:37); and here the gift of knowledge is shown to consist not in foreknowledge alone but in the sequence it makes possible — first the labour, then the hunger, then the year. The sūrah's theology of history is agricultural: a cycle, not a catastrophe.

**"People Will Be Granted Succour" — the Passive Voice of Grace**

*Fīhi yughāthu al-nāsu*. The verb is passive, and the agent is left unspoken because it is God: the people are given help. The root *gh-w-th* gives *al-ghayth*, rain — the word that names the thing a famine-struck country asks for — and the sūrah uses the same root for the relief sent at Badr, when the Muslims cried for help and were answered with water:

> *"Remember when you cried for help (tastaghīthūna) from your Lord, and He answered you: I will aid you with a thousand angels following one another."* (Qur'an 8:9)
The same passage names the water sent down in that hour for exactly the purpose the root *gh-w-th* carries: *"and He sends down upon you water from the sky to purify you with it, and to take away from you the stain of Satan, and to bind fast your hearts and to set your feet firm with it"* (8:11).

The verse's wording therefore says two things at once: the year is a year of rain, and the year is a year in which prayer is answered. It is the sūrah's final reply to the prison prayer of 12:33-34 ("my Lord, the prison is more beloved to me… and if You do not turn their scheming away from me…"), which the chapter recorded as answered: *wa-istajāba lahu rabbuhu*. God's answer preserved Yūsuf then; the year of *ghawth* preserves a nation now, and the sūrah's arrangement lets the reader see that the same name, *al-Mujīb*, governs a cell and a climate. The indefinite plural *al-nās* — "the people" — rather than "Egypt" or "the king's subjects" extends the benefit past the borders of the state: it is the reason Canaan's family comes to buy grain (12:58) and the reason a famine in one valley becomes a reunion in another.

**"And in It They Press" — the Return of the Verb of a Dream**

*wa-fīhi yaʿiṣirūn*. Pressing — grapes, olives, dates — is the *ʿaṣr* (root *ʿ-ṣ-r*) of the cup-bearer's dream: "I see myself pressing wine" (12:36). The sūrah closes its fifteen-year forecast with the same verb that opened the prison scene, and the echo rewards a reader who has held the two together: the man whose own pressing was the sign that a prophet could read what God chose to disclose is present, in the last word of the last verse, as the nation's surplus. The rhetoricians call this kind of verbal return *rad al-ʿajūz ʿalā al-ṣadr*; in a chapter whose theme is that events return to their origin (the root of *taʾwīl*, 12:44), the recurrence is an argument: the end is a *taʾwīl* of the beginning.

Two features of the wording deserve notice. First, the repetition of *fīhi* ("and in it they press"), fronting the prepositional phrase twice, marks the year out by its two properties, as if to say: in this year, and in no other, are people helped and do they press. Second, the shift from the passive (they are given help) to the active (they press) is the sūrah's whole doctrine of providence in six words: what descends is given, what rises is done. Rain is not threshed; the same root that produced "I see myself pressing" now belongs to an entire people, and the verse's implicit instruction is that a blessing is only realised in the work that presses it out — as the Book says elsewhere:

> *"It is He who made the earth tame for you, so walk among its slopes and eat of His provision — and to Him is the resurrection."* (Qur'an 67:15)

**A Year Measured by Its Surplus, Not Its Subsistence**

It is worth observing what the sūrah chooses as the index of recovery: not bread, but the pressing of oil and wine. A year is not good because people are fed — that is merely the absence of famine — but because there is enough to convert fruit into its stored and tradeable form. The commentators' gloss, recorded in our source, is precisely this: the pressing indicates "that there would be food in abundance once again." In a household's life, as in a country's, the return to surplus is the return of the discretionary act: the oil that can be pressed is the oil nobody needed for the emergency. This is why the year of *ghawth* is a year of the press and not a year of the storehouse — the storehouse belongs to the previous two phases.

The sūrah's sequel makes the same point ethically. Yūsuf's policy in the lean years is the return of the price to the brothers' saddlebags when they come again (12:62: "put their merchandise back into their bags"), so that the state's granary is not the instrument by which it buys their loyalty; a government that has fed a people is not entitled to own them. The year of pressing, in which men give thanks for the ordinary luxury of oil, is thus the sūrah's answer to the temptation of perpetual emergency — the temptation of a ruler to keep the famine as a tool, and of a person to keep his hardship as an identity.

**The Verse and the Believer's Arithmetic of Hope**

Fourteen years of a righteous man's life, and one verse at the end of them: this is how the chapter tells its reader that outcomes are not proportionate to duration. The verse is read in Muslim devotion alongside its own Qur'anic twin, the assurance revealed to the Prophet ﷺ under pressure:

> *"For with the hardship there is ease; surely with the hardship there is ease; so when you are free, stand up, and to your Lord turn with longing."* (Qur'an 94:5-7)

The exegetes note the definiteness of *al-ʿusr* (the hardship, one) and the indefiniteness of *yusr* (ease, twice), and the Prophet ﷺ's comment on it is transmitted in the books of refinement of the heart: "one hardship will never overcome two eases" (from Ibn Masʿūd, in Bukhārī's *Kitāb al-Riqāq*). Yūsuf's Egypt is the sūrah's demonstration of the arithmetic: seven years of plenty and seven of dearth are answered, not by a reversal of the weather, but by the work of a man who told the truth about the dream before he was asked to. The last year of the fifteen belongs to people who were saved by words they could not have produced themselves — and the sūrah does not record the king thanking anyone for them.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:50

> **50** *The king said, “Bring him to me!” So when the messenger came to him he said, “Return to your lord and ask him, ‘What of the women who cut their hands? Surely my Lord knows well their scheming!’”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Bring Him to Me" — and the Prisoner Who Is Not Brought**

*Qāla al-malikuʾtūnī bihi*. The king, who had been unable to get an interpretation from his notables, now commands that the interpreter be fetched; and the man who refuses to come is the same man who has just saved Egypt from a famine it had fourteen years to prepare for. Our source records the sequence plainly: when the wine-carrier returned and told the king the interpretation, the king asked for Joseph to be brought; but when the messenger came to release him, Yūsuf asked, "What of the women who cut their hands?" — a request that the women be confronted by the king, which the king then did.

The chapter's timing is its sharpest piece of political wisdom. Yūsuf refuses the honour at the moment it is most easily granted, and he does so before he is in any danger of losing it: the king's summons is not a trap, the offer is genuine, and the man who declines it is not being coy. The principle he acts on is that a public appointment made to fill a vacancy is no substitute for the public correction of a public wrong. He could have walked out with a title and left the record as it was — an innocent servant convicted on a mistress's word, a household that had seen the evidence and jailed him anyway (12:35). He will not. But he also will not refuse the storehouses once the truth is out: three verses later he asks to be put in charge of them (12:55), so the verse is neither a refusal of office nor a blackmail of the state. It is a condition about the record.

**"Return to Your Lord and Ask Him" — the Question in Place of the Freedom**

*Irjiʿa ilā rabbika fa-sʾalhu mā bālu al-niswati allātī qaṭṭaʿna aidiyahunn*. He addresses the messenger with the honorific the prisoners used of the king ("your lord," 12:41, 42) and asks for one thing: a question. Not "ask for my release," not "tell him I am innocent," but *fa-sʾalhu* — put it to him — and the question is worded as an inquiry into a state of affairs: *mā bālu*, "what was the *bāl* of…," the idiom for the truth of a matter, its weight and its outcome. The sūrah uses the cognate phrase of another ruler's interrogation: *"he said: what is your matter (fa-mā khaṭbuka), O Samiri?"* (20:95), where Moses demands an account of an act of deceptive craft; here the king will ask *mā khaṭbunna* (12:51) of the women's craft. Both are scenes of power calling in a plot for audit, and Yūsuf's initiative is to make the audit happen.

There is a further subtlety in what he asks: not "what did they say about me?" but "what became of the women who cut their hands?" He names them by the miracle they were given — the moment when, struck by what they saw, they cut themselves and lost the distinction between a man and an angel (12:31-32). His defence, in other words, is conducted through the witnesses whose own experience had broken their categories. He does not appeal to Potiphar's grief, to the public's pity, or to the king's gratitude, but to the only people in the city who knew what they had seen.

**"Surely My Lord Knows Well Their Scheming" — the Verdict Left Where It Belongs**

*Inna rabbī yaʿlamu kaydahunn*. The verse's last clause is the sūrah's own theology of *kayd* spoken aloud by its victim: God knows the women's plot, and the sentence therefore requires no further prosecution from him. He repeats the wording of his prayer in the cell — "if You do not turn their scheming (*kaydahunna*) away from me, I shall yield to them" (12:33) — and the answer had come: *"so his Lord heard his prayer and turned their scheming away from him"* (12:34). The imprisoned man's theory of judgement, which he had preached to his two companions ("judgement belongs to none but God," 12:40), is now the maxim on which he stakes his own freedom; and the risk of that is visible: he has turned down the king's summons on the strength of a divine knowledge he cannot display. The sūrah then lets the human court do the rest of the work — the king's question (12:51), the women's absolution, and the wife's confession — without pretending that God's knowledge needed the trial.

Our source records a transmitted detail on the sequel: when the messenger went back to the king, the matter of the women was not put, and the delay in Yūsuf's release continued until the king asked of his own accord; the reports preserved in al-Ṭabarī mention a short additional stay in the prison — three days, seven days, or more — before the inquiry was held. The sūrah itself records only the king's question, and its own silence about the intermediate days is characteristic: it does not need to make the innocent man's patience costly, since it has already made the cost of the household's convenience plain.

**What the Verse Teaches About Being Honoured**

The scene is the chapter's test of every case in which a wronged person is offered compensation in the form of advancement: a settlement instead of an admission, a title instead of a correction, "let's move on" from people who were never told they were wrong. Yūsuf's conduct sets three rules. He does not refuse help that is needed by others: he gives the interpretation first and asks for nothing with it. He does not accept restoration that leaves the false record standing, because a record is a public thing and his name is not his alone to spend. And he asks only that a question be put — the minimum possible request that would let the truth arrive — and then leaves the outcome to God's knowledge. In practice this is the opposite of a campaign for vindication: there is no letter to the press, no retelling of the offence, no demand for the women's punishment. When at last he has the floor, he will use it to say not "punish them" but "my Lord knows their scheming" (12:50) and, in the verse that follows, "I do not declare myself free of fault" (12:53) — a vindication that refuses to be complete, and a sūrah that treats that refusal as the mark of a man who had really been taught by his Lord (12:6, 22, 37).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:51

> **51** *He said, “What was your purpose when you sought to lure Joseph from himself?” They said, “God be praised! We know no evil against him.” The viceroy’s wife said, “Now the truth has come to light. It was I who sought to lure him from himself, and verily he is among the truthful.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The King Puts the Question in the Sūrah's Own Words**

*Qāla mā khaṭbunna idh rāwadttunna Yūsufa ʿan nafsihi*. The ruler's first act in the retrial is to ask what the chapter has been asking all along, and he asks it with the chapter's verb: *rāwada ʿan nafsihi*, "to seek to lure someone from himself," the phrase used of her at 12:23 and of the whole affair at 12:51's confession. The coincidence of wording is the sūrah's technique for making a courtroom and a text describe one event: the king's question is not a legal formula but the chapter's own language of self-alienation (12:23c), so the trial becomes a reading of the story. *Mā khaṭbunna* — "what was your *khaṭb*" — is the idiom for a weighty matter demanding an account, and the Book uses the same frame when Moses calls the Samiri to answer for the calf: *"what is your affair (fa-mā khaṭbuka), O Samiri?"* (20:95). In both scenes a man of God has been wronged by a device, and the one who is asked is not the deceiver but the deceived.

The question is put, too, to the plurality that condemned him: "the women" (12:50-51), the same company that spread the story ("the women of the city said," 12:30) and the same company that sat at the banquet with the knives (12:31). The chapter's accusation was always collective, and its correction is collective: the group that enjoyed the scandal is asked, in public, whether they knew any evil in him. This is the sūrah's answer to the question of how a rumour is undone — not by disproof but by recall.

**"God Be Praised! We Know No Evil Against Him"**

*Qulna subḥāna llāhi mā ʿalimnā ʿalayhi min sūʾ*. The women's reply begins with the same act of glorification they had performed at the banquet: "God be glorified (ḥāshā li-llāh)! This is no human being, but a noble angel" (12:31), and — as at 12:31 — the *tanzīh* is uttered at the moment when a creature has been wrongly described. The Qur'an's paradigm of that usage is the angels' answer to the charge that God takes sons:

> *"Glory be to You (subḥānaka)! There is no knowledge for us except what You have taught us."* (Qur'an 2:32; cf. 21:16-17, 16:1)

Note the exactness of their denial: "we know no *sūʾ* against him" — the word is the sūrah's own for the offence with which he was charged ("what is the penalty for one who intends *evil* with your wife?" 12:25; and the purpose of God's *burhān*: "thus do We turn away from him evil and indecency (*al-sūʾa wa-l-faḥshāʾ*)" 12:24). The women do not recant their descriptions of his beauty, do not apologise for the banquet, and do not mention what they had said about being afflicted by it. They withdraw the charge, and the sūrah reports the withdrawal as the fact. A society's retraction is usually partial, and 12:51 is the Book's honest record of what an honest retraction can be: "we knew no evil in him" — the minimum, spoken publicly, which is exactly what the case required.

**"Now the Truth Has Come to Light"**

*Qālati-l-marʾatu al-āna ḥaṣṣa al-ḥaqq*. The received translation's "the viceroy's wife" supplies the referent; the sūrah says simply "the woman," as if the whole affair had, by now, only one. And the verb is one the Qur'an uses nowhere else: *ḥaṣṣa*, in the reduplicated form which Arabic employs for full, repeated clarity — the truth pressed out of its hiding, as fruit is pressed out of its skin (the root carries the sense of being clear and particular, and the same field lies behind *al-ḥāṣṣ*, the specially singled-out). The rhetoricians hear in the reduplication the idea that the truth has been examined, sieved, and found standing. She had already said "I lured him and he refused" to the women (12:32) — a private admission before a private jury; the sūrah records both, and the difference between them is the verse's subject: what is true in a room and what is true in a court.

**Zulaykhā's Progression — Love That Has Ceased to Be Self-Interest**

Our source's note on these verses is one of the sūrah's finest pieces of moral psychology.
