Before, she had blamed him for a sin he did not commit, because her own desire was more important than his well-being (see 12:25); then she admitted his innocence before the women, chiefly to deflect their scorn (see 12:32); and at this point her love for him had grown so strong that it surpassed her self-interest, and so she spoke the truth about him publicly. The progression of degrees of love in her soul is at the same time a progression of a spiritual journey: her first attraction was purely sensual, but here she has overcome her lower self, and her infatuation with his physical beauty has been transformed into a spiritual love in which she is able to see his inward beauty — much as Potiphar did when he first saw him in the marketplace (12:21-22) — and the catalyst was his inner virtue, marked by truthfulness, which she recognises when she calls him "among the truthful."

That last phrase, *wa-innahu mina al-ṣādiqīn*, is the sūrah's irony closing around her mouth. The words belong first to the brothers, who came to their father with a forged shirt and said: *"and you would not believe us, even if we were truthful (wa-law kunnā ṣādiqīn)"* (12:17) — the protest of liars about their own honesty. The very phrase now comes from the mouth of the woman who invented a rape. The sūrah's moral vocabulary is thus put back on its feet by the person who had abused it: to be "among the truthful" is not a claim one makes for oneself (12:53: "I do not declare myself free of fault"), and 12:46's prisoner's title — "O truthful one" — is conferred, in this verse, by the accuser.

**"Now" — and the Verse That Distinguishes Two Late Confessions**

The adverb *al-āna*, "now," occurs in the Qur'an at a moment of decisive exposure, and its most terrible instance is the drowning man's belief:

> *"Until, when drowning overtook him, he said: I believe that there is no god but He in whom the Israelites believe, and I am of those who submit. Now? (a-l-āna) — and before this you were rebellious, and you were of the corrupters!"* (Qur'an 10:90-91; cf. 4:18; 6:158)

The verse's logic is that a belief compelled by the visible end of options does not count; and the reader of 12:51 is meant to ask why the woman's "now" is accepted and Pharaoh's is not. The answer the sūrah supplies is that hers costs her everything and is spoken before the punishment falls: she names herself the sinner in a court where naming him was still permitted, and where her husband's honour — and her own — would be destroyed by the sentence. Pharaoh's "I believe" is an attempt to buy his way out of what he is already suffering; hers is a confession that makes his suffering unnecessary. The chapter's *ʿaqīdah* of repentance is entirely in that contrast: truth late is still truth when it is spoken against the speaker's interest, and it is not truth when it is only a plea.

Egypt's king, having asked, does nothing further: he does not punish the women, does not depose the household, does not record an apology. He goes on, in the next verse, to summon Yūsuf for the work that must be done (12:54). This too is the sūrah's realism about institutional justice: the correction of a record is cheap, and the man who insisted on it then went and ran the country's granaries.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:52

> **52** *“This is so, that he may know that I betrayed him not in his absence. Truly God guides not the scheming of the treacherous.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Whose Words These Are**

The sūrah does not say who speaks 12:52, and our source records the division of the commentators precisely: the majority of the commentators — our source's list for this reading includes al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī among others — take the words as Yūsuf's, a continuation of his statement in the previous verse, while others, again including al-Rāzī, take them as the woman's. The two readings are grammatically equally smooth, and each gives the verse a different moral shape.

If the speaker is Yūsuf, "that he may know that I betrayed him not in his absence" is addressed to the husband: the point of the whole retrial, on this reading, is not the punishment of the woman but the restoration of a trust. Yūsuf had been bought, housed, educated and entrusted (12:21-22); his innocence in the one matter where a master's trust is everything is the thing he insists be publicly established, and the sūrah's sequel shows why — the same office will hand him the nation's storehouses, and the office requires that men know what kind of man he is (12:54-55). If the speaker is the woman, the sentence is her own last and best act: she too wants her husband to know that she did not betray him *bi-l-ghayb*, in his absence — an astonishing thing for an adulteress in a story like this to be concerned about, and consistent with the progression our source records, in which her love has come to surpass her self-interest (12:51c). The ambiguity is the sūrah's, and it makes husband, wife and servant capable of saying the same line: a chapter that has spent thirty verses on a triangle of betrayal ends by letting all three speak with one syntax of fidelity.

**"In His Absence" — Integrity Defined as Conduct Under No Witness**

*an lam akhunnahu bi-l-ghayb*. The *ghayb* here is the husband's absence, the locked room, the unobserved hour (12:23: the doors were locked, and yet "God be my refuge"). The verse thus defines betrayal and its opposite by the same test: what a person does where nobody is watching. The Qur'an's general form of this is the verse that makes the unseen the only truly public place:

> *"Say: whether you conceal what is in your breasts or reveal it, God knows what is in the heavens and what is in the earth. And He is powerful over all things."* (Qur'an 3:29; cf. 67:13-14: "and whether you speak aloud or whisper — He knows what is in the breasts. Does He not know, He who created? And He is the Latīf, the Aware")

The last of these pairs God's knowledge in the unseen with the name *al-Laṭīf*, and that same name is the sūrah's own summary of how Yūsuf's life was managed: *"my Lord is Latīf over whom He wills"* (12:100). 12:52 sits at the centre of that vocabulary. The man who says "God does not guide the plot of the treacherous" is the man who, in the only moment when treachery was available and costless, refused it because a knowledge that is subtle, intimate and total was present to him. The sūrah has been careful, all along, not to say that his strength held him; it said the *burhān* of his Lord came to him (12:24) and that his Lord turned the women's *kayd* away from him (12:34). Here he states the doctrine in the first person, without claiming credit for it.

**"God Guides Not the Scheming of the Treacherous" — a Law, Not a Threat**

*wa-anna llāha lā yahdī kayda al-khāʾinīn*. Two words carry the clause. *Kayd* is the plot that works by indirection, and the sūrah has used it three times already: her "this is of your *kayd*" (12:28), Yūsuf's prayer "turn away from me their *kayd*" (12:33), God's answering it ("*ṣarafa ʿanhu kaydahunna*," 12:34). *Khayāna* is the specific failure of trust — not open enmity but the misuse of a confidence. The verse's assertion is not that treachery will be punished at some future point, but that *kayd* built on *khayāna* is not guided: it has no *hudā*, no direction to a destination, because the traitor's own instrument is the trust he is consuming. The sentence is thus a description of a mechanism: a plot that must first destroy the very relationship it lives in cannot be steered.

The general law is stated elsewhere in the Book in the same terms — *"and they plot, and God plots, and God is the best of planners"* (3:54; 12:76: "thus do We devise for Yūsuf") — and this sūrah's own demonstration is structural: the brothers' plot to remove Yūsuf puts him in the house whose wife plots against him, whose trial puts him in a prison whose inmates put him in the king's court, so that every *kayd* in the chapter is over-planned by a planning that is not a plot. When the sūrah says "God guides not the scheming of the treacherous," it is not promising that betrayal will be punished; it is reporting what fifteen years have shown Yūsuf, that treachery never arrives anywhere.

**The Sūrah's Ethics of Trust and Its Verse of Office**

12:52's word *khiyāna* belongs to a vocabulary the sūrah has been building since the well. Yaʿqūb's accusation against his sons is phrased as a breach of entrustment: *"shall I entrust you with him as I entrusted you with his brother before?"* (12:64), and their answer, when they plead for Benyamin, is that God is "the best of guardians (khayrun ḥāfiẓan)" (12:64). At the peak of his own career, Yūsuf asks for the treasury in the words that answer 12:52 exactly: *"set me over the storehouses of the land; I am a skilled keeper, knowing"* (12:55: *innī ḥafīẓun ʿalīm*). The chapter's qualification for public office, then, is not competence alone but proven conduct in *ghayb* — a man's record where no one was watching is the only credential that the state can rely on, because a treasury is a room of locked doors. That is why the vindication had to be public (12:50): the office he wanted required the record, and the record he wanted did not require the office. The Prophet's ﷺ saying on the same subject makes the traitor's mark a matter of character rather than creed:

> *"The signs of the hypocrite are three: when he speaks he lies, when he promises he breaks it, and when he is entrusted he betrays (wa-idhā ʾutimina khāna)."* (Bukhārī 33; Muslim 59, from Abū Hurayrah)

The sūrah's innocent man, who was called a liar in a household and acquitted in a court, ends the episode by refusing to enjoy the acquittal: he names the mechanism by which betrayal fails, and asks nothing further. That, the chapter means, is what a *ḥāfiẓ* sounds like.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:53

> **53** *But I absolve not my own soul. Surely the soul commands to evil, save whom my Lord may show mercy. Truly my Lord is Forgiving, Merciful.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Acquitted Man Declines to Acquit Himself**

*Wa-mā ubarriʾu nafsī*. The verb *barraʾa* is the language of a court: to find a person free of a charge, to pronounce the *barāʾa*. The sūrah has spent twenty-eight verses on the machinery of such a finding — the shirt, the witness from her household, the conditional test, the evidence that was seen and then buried under a few years of imprisonment (12:26-35), the women's denial and the woman's confession (12:51). And now, with the verdict in hand, the man who asked for the retrial refuses the last move the verdict permits: he will not declare himself free of fault. The distinction he draws is the chapter's own: he is truthful (*ṣādiq*, 12:51) and he will be called veracious (12:46), but truthfulness is not the same as purity, and an acquittal is not an absolution of the soul.

Our source records that the majority of the commentators take these words as Yūsuf's, continuing the statement of verse 52, and that they are said to be spoken out of humility, and because he wished to draw attention to the fact that it was God who protected him from falling into sin — a gloss that points back to 12:24, where God's intervention was described as turning away from him "evil and indecency." Others, our source notes, assign the sentence to Zulaykhā (among them Ibn Kathīr and al-Rāzī), which fits her with equal ease: she, who had blamed another for a sin she committed, finally refuses to declare herself free. The ambiguity is another of the sūrah's quiet devices: the two people most entitled to say "I am innocent" in this story, and the only two words that keep it from being a story about the vindicated.

**"The Soul Commands to Evil" — the Verse That Founded a Psychology**

*Inna al-nafsa la-ammāratun bi-l-sūʾ*. *Ammāra* is the intensive active participle of *amara*, to command, and the *fāʿʿāl* pattern marks habitual, unremitting action: the soul is not occasionally inclined but continually issuing orders. It is the self behaving as a tyrant in its own house. The evil it commands, *sūʾ*, is the sūrah's word throughout for the thing Yūsuf was preserved from (12:24: "thus do We turn away from him evil and indecency"; 12:25: "whoever intends evil with your household"; 12:51: "we knew no evil against him"). So the verse's sentence can be read as Yūsuf's own commentary on his case: the very evil he did not commit is what every soul spends its life commanding; his innocence is not evidence of a stronger nature but of a mercy.

Our source's note on this verse sets out, with unusual compactness, the scheme by which Muslim thought has organised the human self:

> *"Islamic sources identify three levels of the soul on the basis of Qur'anic references. The first level is the 'soul that commands to evil' (al-nafs al-ammāra bi-l-sūʾ, based on the present verse); this is the soul that calls a person to submit to his or her base desires, and to those thoughts and actions that lead to forgetfulness of God and go against His Command. Next is the blaming soul (al-nafs al-lawwāma; 75:2), which reproaches a person for his or her evil states and forgetfulness of God, while actively attempting to effect change within that person for the better. Finally, the highest kind of soul is the soul at peace (al-nafs al-muṭmaʾinna; 89:27), which has conquered the lower self and resides in peace in the remembrance of God, returning to God content and contenting."*

The two other members of the triad are oath-passages, and the Qur'an's manner of introducing them is itself a lesson in the psychology. God swears "by the day of resurrection" and then "by the blaming soul" (75:1-2), setting conscience alongside the Hour as evidence that the self is not transparent to itself but is being addressed and judged; and at the end of al-Fajr He turns to the soul that has attained stillness:

> *"O soul at peace (yā ayyatuha al-nafsu al-muṭmaʾinna), return to your Lord, well-pleased and pleasing; enter among My servants, and enter My Garden."* (Qur'an 89:27-30)

The ladder is not a theory of three kinds of people but of three states in one person, and this sūrah dramatises the ascent: the command (12:23-24, the *hamm* that was answered by a *burhān*), the blame and self-reproach of the years in prison (12:29-35, when a man is not believed by the city that saw the evidence), and at the end the settled prayer of a old age: *"You are my Patron in this world and the Hereafter; cause me to die in submission (aslimlī) and let me enter among Your righteous servants"* (12:101). Al-Ghazālī's treatment of the soul's discipline in the *Iḥyāʾ*, and Ibn al-Qayyim's in *Madārij al-sālikīn*, both organise their manuals around these three names, and the practical literature of *muḥāsaba* (self-reckoning, from the verse "let every soul look to what it has sent ahead," 59:18) is the technique for moving upward from the first to the second.

**"Save Whom My Lord May Show Mercy" — the Exception That Is the Whole Doctrine**

*Illā mā raḥima rabbī*. The exception is grammatically from the generality of "the soul," and theologically it is the clause on which the sūrah's *ʿaqīdah* of prophethood balances. The chapter has twice said that God acted to preserve Yūsuf (12:24, 12:34); here Yūsuf himself makes preservation an instance of mercy rather than of merit. That is why the verse has been quoted on both sides of the classical argument about the prophets' *ʿiṣma*: those who argued that the messengers were protected from sin in a strong, divinely guaranteed sense pointed to 12:24 and 12:34 and read this verse as humility — the language of a servant, not a confession of fault; those who insisted that no human being may claim purity of self read it as a denial of exactly that. The Shīʿī imami exegetes, who hold the prophets sinless in the technical sense, treat the sentence as Yūsuf's *tawāḍuʿ* before God. Neither reading requires the verse to say less than it does: no claim of self-sufficiency survives it.

The Book's most instructive parallel is in Sūrah al-Nūr, in the passage that deals with the great slander against the Prophet's household — a case of an innocent person accused of sexual misconduct in a house, an accusation spread by the community, a vindication delayed, and a rule revealed about how the community should have behaved:

> *"Were it not for God's bounty upon you and His mercy, not one of you would ever be pure (mā zakā minkum min aḥadin abadan); but God purifies whom He wills, and God is the Hearing, the Knowing."* (Qur'an 24:21)

The two verses are the same sentence in different keys: 24:21 speaks to a community, 12:53 to a court, and both refuse purity as a human possession. The sūrah's own sequel makes the point social rather than interior: three verses later the minister of the granaries is asked to be forgiven by his brothers (12:97-98), and the father replies with the name the acquitted man had just used — *"my Lord is the Forgiving, the Merciful"* — as if the whole story of the shirt and the prison exists to teach Egypt's court and Canaan's house that a verdict is not a purity.

**"My Lord Is Forgiving, Merciful" — Where the Verse Ends the Argument**

*Inna rabbī ghafūrun raḥīm*. The pairing is the sūrah's closing formula for the affair: God's forgiveness is stated, and the women are not punished; the king is not reproached in the text; and the men who threw a boy in a well will hear, "no blame upon you today; God will forgive you" (12:92). Yūsuf's reason for declining self-vindication is therefore not scrupulosity about his record but a judgment about where records end: the One who forgives is the One whose verdict matters, and a man who has been acquitted by a court that had jailed him has no need to add to the acquittal — he has, however, every reason not to convert it into an estimate of his own soul. The ethics of the verse, for a reader who has been wronged, are contained in that order of clauses: state the fact (12:50-52), refuse the moral inflation (12:53), and leave the verdict to the Mercy (12:53b).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:54

> **54** *And the king said, “Bring him to me, that I might reserve him exclusively for myself.” Then when he had spoken with him, he said, “Truly this day thou shalt be of high rank and trusted in our presence.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Summons, Two Motives**

*Wa-qāla al-malikuʾtūnī bihi*. The king's command of 12:50 was issued for a practical reason — the famine forecast had to be verified and the man who made it consulted — and this repetition of the same words ("bring him to me") carries a different motive, which the sūrah states: *li-astajriyahu li-nafsi*, "that I may reserve him exclusively for myself." Our source records the sequence the commentators give the interview: after summoning Yūsuf and speaking with him, the king perceives his intelligence, wisdom and virtue, and so expresses the wish to keep his abilities for himself alone, before setting him over the storehouses of the land (al-Qurṭubī; al-Ṭabarī; cf. Genesis 41:37-44). The verse's own ordering is careful: the intention to reserve him comes before the conversation, and the verdict of 12:54 comes after it. The king wants the man; the interview is what turns possession into respect.

The phrase *li-astajriyahu li-nafsi* is a ruler's language and the sūrah does not disguise it: *istijrāʾ*, to draw to oneself, to take exclusively — the vocabulary of appropriation. Egypt's sovereign treats even the innocent as a resource. The chapter has already said, in the household's own idiom, "make his lodging beautiful… perhaps he may be useful to us" (*ʿasā an yanfaʿanā*, 12:21): both masters, Potiphar and the king, speak of Yūsuf in the grammar of benefit. What changes is that the king, having seen the evidence of verse 51 and heard the interview, abandons the language of use for the language of standing: "you are with us *makkīn* and *amīn*."

**"Makkīn": the Word Used Twice in the Qur'an, and Where Else**

*Innaka l-yawma ladaynā makkīnun amīn*. *Makkīn* is the intensive active participle of *makkana*, to be firm in place, established, secure in rank, and it occurs in the Qur'an only one other time:

> *"But the God-fearing are in gardens and rivers, in a seat of truth, in the presence of a Mighty King who establishes them (ʿinda malīkin makkīn)."* (Qur'an 54:54-55)

That is the only place in the Book where the same word describes a station conferred by a king, and its context is Paradise. The verbal echo is too exact to be accidental: the court of Egypt grants Yūsuf the noun the Qur'an otherwise reserves for the nearness of the saved to God. Two consequences follow. First, the sūrah's implicit measure of worldly rank — the highest thing a pharaonic state can say about a man is, in the Qur'an's vocabulary, a shadow of the only real establishment. Second, the pairing with *amīn* places trust beside station, which the verse's second half requires: rank without trust would be exactly the combination that had put an innocent man in a cell for years.

The other verse that uses the pair is Sūrah al-Takwīr's description of the angelic messenger, and it makes the same association: *"a noble messenger, trustworthy (rasūlin karīmin amīn)"* (81:19-21, where the words *makkīn* and *amīn* both occur of the one "endowed with power in the presence of the Lord of the Throne, obeyed there, trustworthy"). The Qur'an's lexical field for authority, in other words, is organised around the two words Egypt's king spends on Yūsuf — which is how the sūrah tells its reader that the minister's real office is a trust, not an honour.

**"This Day" — a Ruler's Time and God's**

*Innaka l-yawma*. A king's "today" is the whole of his reach. The sūrah's own theology of the word is stated in a verse that belongs to the chapter's sermon-material: God says of the Day of judgement *"the Dominion today belongs to God, the One, the Paramount"* (40:16) — the one "today" in which every *makān* and every *malik* has dissolved. Yūsuf's career in Egypt is framed by the same contrast: he will hold this "today" for the rest of the famine and die asking to be taken in submission (12:101), and the sūrah never lets the reader mistake a minister's rank for the thing it resembles. Yaʿqūb's own line in this chapter has already prepared the reading: "God is best of guardians and the most merciful of the merciful" (12:64), said about the son now standing before the king's throne.

**"When He Had Spoken with Him" — the Interview the Sūrah Refuses to Report**

The verse contains a whole court scene in six words: *fa-lammā kālamahu al-maliku qāla…*. Everything the reader is given is the verdict, not the exchange. This is the sūrah's habit at its moments of greatest drama: the throwing into the well is dispatched in a clause (12:15), the baker's execution is announced but never narrated (12:41-42), and a father's twenty years of grief arrive in a sentence ("my soul has a grief from which the like of this cannot be consoled," 12:84 in effect — the actual wording is 12:86: "I complain of my grief and my sorrow only to God, and I know from God what you do not know"). The verse here has the same specific purpose. If the interview were quoted, the sūrah would be offering the reader a portrait of the wise minister's statecraft — a manual, in effect. Instead it reports what the meeting changed: a man's legal status and the state's estimate of him. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī both fill the silence with the usual matters — the king asking about the interpretation, Yūsuf expounding God's oneness, the king's conversion reported in some traditions — but the text itself, whose restraint the source's notes repeatedly admire, leaves the room closed. The reader's conclusion is the one the sūrah wants: the worth of the interview is visible in the sentence it produced, "trusted in our presence."

**What a State Gets When It Finally Listens**

Egypt's rescue in this narrative is not engineered by a hero who forces his way out but by a ruler who, when the evidence and the advice both arrive, does the minimum competent thing: brings the man, speaks to him, and gives him authority. The sūrah marks this with a realism unusual in prophetic stories — the king never repents, never mentions the years in prison, and never apologises; he simply promotes the innocent man because he needs the plan. It is a lesson for institutions in a very practical register: states are often saved not by conversion but by the deployment of competence they had wasted, and the credit the text gives for this is thin and exact. What the chapter gives instead of an apology is a verdict on Yūsuf's character, spoken by a pagan in a court whose word the sūrah lets stand as the measure: high rank, and trustworthy. The reader is left to notice that the man who has been through 12:23, 12:35 and 12:50 can be described in two words and cannot be described in any honours.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:55

> **55** *He said, “Set me over the storehouses of the land. Truly I am a skilled keeper.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**What the Vindicated Man Asks For**

*Ijʿalnī ʿalā khazāʾini al-arḍi innī ḥafīẓun ʿalīm*. The verse is the sūrah's most economical portrait of a character: Yūsuf has just been summoned, honoured, and offered a place at the king's elbow — and he asks for the granaries. Not the throne, not the treasury of the realm's gold, not the governorship of the nome, not revenge, not even his own household's restoration; the storehouses. The word *khazāʾin* (plural of *khizāna*, a store, the locked room where grain, goods and valuables are kept) is one of the terms the Qur'an borrows from the administrative vocabulary of the great empires whose subjects its first audience had been (the chapter on non-Arabic words in al-Suyūṭī's *al-Muzhir* lists this family among the Persian administrative loanwords, alongside *istabraq* and *sundus* for the fabrics of Paradise). Whether or not the etymology is pressed, the word's register is exact: this is the language of a state's accounting, and the sūrah lets a Hebrew shepherd's son speak it without embarrassment. The verse's first word, however, is the real evidence: *ijʿalnī*, "appoint me" — a request for an office, made by a man who has just learned from his own career what an appointment without grounds can do.

**"A Skilled Keeper, Knowing" — the Two Qualifications of Office**

*Innī ḥafīẓun ʿalīm*. The pair is deliberate, and the sūrah has prepared both words. *Ḥafīẓ* — one who guards, who prevents loss, waste, theft and tampering — is the attribute Yaʿqūb had given to God alone when he refused to surrender Benjamin: *"God is the best of guardians (khayrun ḥāfiẓan), and He is the most merciful of the merciful"* (12:64); the father, asked to trust, had said that only God keeps. Here the son, asked to be trusted, claims the same quality in the only sense a human can claim it: not as power over outcomes, but as fidelity in what is handed to him. *ʿAlīm* — knowing — is the sūrah's own qualification for the job: the interpretation of events (12:6, 21), the measure of the years (12:47-49), the audit of a famine. The two words together are the Qur'an's definition of the competent official: *amāna* and *ʿilm*, integrity and expertise, and 12:55 is the answer to 12:52's *khiyāna*: "I did not betray him in the unseen," therefore "appoint me; I am a keeper."

It is worth noting that he states his own credentials. Arabic's emphatic construction (*innī ḥafīẓun ʿalīm*) is not a modesty's formulation, and the sūrah does not make him pretend otherwise. The tradition reads this as the difference between boasting and testimony: to declare fitness when one knows it and when the office requires it is, in the jurists' discussion, a duty rather than a vanity, whereas to seek office for its honours is the forbidden thing.

**Seeking Office: the Verse That Had to Be Reconciled with a Ḥadīth**

The verse poses a real problem for Muslim law, because the Prophet ﷺ said, in terms that appear to close the door:

> *"We do not appoint over this work anyone who asks for it or who is eager for it."* (Bukhārī, Kitāb al-Aḥkām; Muslim, Kitāb al-Imāra)

> *"O ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Samura, do not ask for authority; if it is given to you by asking, you will be left to it, and if it is given to you without asking, you will be helped by it."* (Bukhārī, *loc. cit.*; Muslim, Kitāb al-Imāra)

The commentaries on both ḥadīths (Ibn Ḥajar's *Fatḥ al-Bārī* in the book of judgements; al-Nawawī on Muslim) handle Yūsuf's request with a set of distinctions which has become standard in the *siyāsa* literature: the prohibition concerns seeking *wilāya* for the sake of status and pay, not offering oneself to prevent harm or secure a right; Yūsuf asked at a time of famine when the people's food would otherwise have been mismanaged; he was under a non-Muslim sovereign's law rather than in the Islamic polity where the *imāma* is a trust of the whole community; and the ḥadīth's clause "you will be left to it" describes the danger precisely — the man who gets office by asking is abandoned to his own competence, which is why the sūrah pairs the request with a claim of knowledge rather than of right. Al-Māwardī's *al-Aḥkām al-sulṭāniyya*, in the chapter on the appointment of the minister of execution (*wazīr al-tanfīdh*), treats the whole episode as the Qur'anic warrant for the principle that office is to be given to the competent who are willing, and that a qualified man who stays silent while the unqualified are in charge shares in the loss.

**Genesis 41 and the Sūrah's Refusal of Regalia**

The parallel account of the elevation is instructive by contrast. In Genesis 41:39-43 Pharaoh says that there is none as discreet and wise as Joseph, takes his signet ring from his hand and puts it on Joseph's hand, clothes him in fine linen and puts a gold chain about his neck, and has him ride in the second chariot with the proclamation "bow the knee." The Qur'an reports none of it: no ring, no robes, no chain, no chariot, no second-in-command formula — only the request for the storehouses and the king's two words (12:54: *makkīn*, *amīn*). This is not a forgetful abridgement; the sūrah has been removing spectacle from the story since 12:15, where the well itself is unnamed and the brothers' act is reported in a clause. What it wants the reader to see is what Yūsuf did with the authority, not what the authority looked like. The only "investment" the chapter records is functional: "thus did We give Yūsuf a place in the land, to settle where he wished in it" (12:56), and even this is attributed to God, not to Pharaoh's favour.

**The Granary and the Household**

One further detail of the verse's wording carries the rest of the sūrah. He asks to be set over *khazāʾin al-arḍ*, "the storehouses of the land" — the whole country's reserve — and the chapter's later scenes will keep returning to the same institution: the measure given full and the vessel returned to the bags (12:62, 65), the king's cup placed in a brother's bag (12:70), and the ruling that taking an innocent man for a guilty one would itself be wrongdoing (12:79). Yūsuf's administration is thus narrated almost entirely as a question of weights and restitution, and it is through the granary's arithmetic that his brothers are finally brought to tell the truth about what they once did with a shirt. The sūrah's political theology is in that choice: the state's real business is the measure, and a man's character is legible in how he handles what is not his. The Prophet's ﷺ words on the same subject turn office itself into a *khizāna* that must be given back: *"it is a trust (amāna), and on the Day of Resurrection it is shame and regret, except for the one who takes it by its right and discharges what is owed in it"* (Muslim, Kitāb al-Imāra, from Abū Dharr). Yūsuf, who had been a trust in Potiphar's house and had not been betrayed by his own hand (12:52), asked for the one office where the same discipline could be exercised for a nation.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:56

> **56** *Thus did We establish Joseph in the land, that there he might settle wheresoever he will. We cause Our Mercy to fall upon whomsoever We will, and We neglect not the reward of the virtuous.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Thus Did We Establish Him" — the Same Sentence as the First Blessing**

*Wa-kadhālika makkannā li-Yūsufa fī al-arḍ*. The sūrah has said this before, about the same country and the same man, at the moment of his purchase: "and the one of Egypt who bought him said to his wife: make his lodging beautiful; perhaps he will be useful to us, or we may adopt him as a son… thus did We give Yūsuf a place in the land (*makkannā*), that We might teach him of the interpretation of events" (12:21). Our source's note brings the two verses together and draws the distinction that matters: the purpose of the first establishing was that he be *taught*; the purpose of the second is that he *settle where he will*. Between the two lies the whole of the sūrah — the seduction, the shirt, the prison, the two dreams, the famine's forecast, the retrial — and the chapter's commentary announces that the sequence is one act. The promise made to a child ("your Lord will choose you and teach you," 12:6) is described, when it is finished, as a *tamkīn*: a giving of place, a firmness in the land.

The verb's field is spatial and political at once. *Makkana* is to make a thing firm in its place; *tamkīn* is what a state does to a person when it installs him where he can act. The sūrah uses the cognate noun of the believers' station in Paradise — "in the presence of a Mighty King who establishes them (*makkīn*), in a seat of truth" (54:54-55) — and here the same root describes Egypt's court. The echo is the sūrah's measure of worldly power: what a pharaoh can confer is a *makān*; what God confers is a place that does not need a pharaoh to keep it.

**"To Settle Wherever He Wills" — Freedom as the Fruit, Not the Reward**

*Yatabawwaʾu minhā ḥaythu yashāʾ*. The verb *tabawwaʾa* (from *abwāʾ*, the settled place to which a person returns; used of taking up a dwelling, and — in the Qur'an's idiom — of the choice of a home: "and it was said to them: dwell (uskunū) in the land" — but the *bawwāʾ* form is more deliberate than dwelling: it is choosing where to put one's tent). The verse gives Yūsuf, at thirty-something, what the chapter has been tracking from the beginning: the right to locate himself. As a boy he was thrown where they threw him (12:10, 15), sold into a country he did not choose, housed at another's pleasure (12:21: "make his lodging beautiful"), jailed at a household's convenience (12:35), and kept there by a forgetful man's silence (12:42). "Wherever he will" is the sūrah's definition of the reversal — not wealth, not honours, but the disposal of one's own *manzil*, the same noun that named Potiphar's household's first duty to him.

That the verse puts this freedom as God's act ("thus did We establish") rather than as the king's gift is its theological point; the sūrah never says "the king appointed him and made him free." The court is the instrument, and even the instrument's good faith is a mercy: *"We cause Our mercy (raḥmatanā) to fall upon whom We will."*

**"We Cause Our Mercy to Fall upon Whom We Will" — the Verse's Hard Clause**

The clause is the chapter's answer to any reader tempted to treat success as a verdict. The verb *nūlī*, with its object left indefinite and its recipients governed by a divine will no one instructs, is the Qur'an's own phrasing of grace's unaccountability. Moses's prayer in al-Aʿrāf states the same rule and then writes mercy into a covenant for conduct: *"My mercy embraces all things, so I will write it for those who fear God and give the zakāt and believe in Our signs"* (7:156). In its context here, the sentence does two things at once. It refuses to let Yūsuf's elevation be read as a payment he was owed by history, a just-world arithmetic in which the good get offices; and it refuses the opposite reading, that the court's favour was the real cause of anything. Both are refused, and in their place stands a mercy distributed by a will no one instructs.

The same name, *raḥma*, was what the prison prayer was about: "if You do not turn their scheming away from me, I shall be one of the ignorant," answered at once — "his Lord heard his prayer and turned their scheming away from him" (12:33-34). The sūrah's rule about the recipients of that mercy is stated twice more, before and after this verse: the election and teaching of 12:6, and Yūsuf's own verdict on his brothers, "whoever fears and is patient — God does not waste the reward of the beautiful-doers" (12:90). Verse 56 is thus the middle term of an equation the chapter keeps restating: a gift that is unearned, a practice that is not optional, a reward that is not wasted. Election in this sūrah never excuses conduct, and conduct never obliges election.

**"We Do Not Waste the Reward of the Beautiful-Doers"**

*Wa-lā nuḍīʿu ajra l-muḥsinīn*. The formula belongs to the sūrah from 12:22 ("thus do We recompense the beautiful-doers"), and it will close the family's story at 12:90 in the words Yūsuf uses about his brothers: *"whoever fears and is patient — God does not waste the reward of those who do beautiful things"* — so that the sentence which here explains an elevation will there excuse a forgiveness. Outside the chapter, the same clause is a fixed expression of the Book's optimism: *"We do not waste the reward of one who does good"* (18:30); *"and God does not waste the reward of the beautiful-doers"* (3:171; 11:115: "and be patient, for God does not waste the reward of those who do beautiful things"). The word *ajr*, reward, is deliberately not *ujra*, wage: the reward of the muḥsin is not a bargain struck but a gift guaranteed, and the sūrah's own definition of *iḥsān* in this chapter has never been only moral — Potiphar's wife was told to make his *mathwā* beautiful (12:21), the prisoners saw him as a *muḥsin* (12:36), and the king's court called the minister *amīn* (12:54). Excellence in a cell, in a market, and in a storehouse is one thing in the sūrah's eyes, and the verse's promise is that none of it is lost, which is a claim about the ledger of the world that can only be verified, the chapter insists, from the other side of death (12:57).

**The Verse as a Comment on Career and Providence**

Readers who have been promoted late, or restored after an injustice, tend to feel that the delay has been repaid; 12:56 corrects the feeling in three moves. It attributes the elevation to God's establishing rather than to anyone's change of mind, so that gratitude does not curdle into dependency on patrons. It locates the real gain in the ability to choose one's own place — the thing the years of imprisonment had actually taken — rather than in rank. And it declares, in the middle of the sentence about mercy, that the reward of excellence is not wasted, which is a promise about the future rather than an explanation of the past. The sūrah's own summary of the man's life, spoken at its end, will be: "he was gracious to me (laṭīfan bī) when He brought me out of the prison and brought you from the desert" (12:100) — and even the vindication is folded, in his mouth, into a courtesy he did not arrange.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:57

> **57** *And the reward of the Hereafter is better for those who believe and are reverent.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Price Tag on the Palace**

*Wa-la-ajru l-ākhirati khayrun lilladhīna āmanū wa-kānū yattaqūn*. The sūrah has just told the reader that Yūsuf was established in the land, free to settle wherever he wished, and that God's mercy is given to whom God wills. In the next breath it re-prices that elevation. This is the Book's way of handling success inside a prophetic story: it does not deny the reality of the good thing (the establishment is real, the freedom is real, the king's "you are established and trusted with us" is real), and it refuses to let the good thing be the verdict. The classical rhetoricians call such a sentence an *iʿtirāḍ* — an interposed clause that breaks the narrative to state a rule — and in this chapter the device has now been used twice in the same place: "God guides not the scheming of the treacherous" (12:52) and here. Between them lies the elevation itself, and the chapter's ordering says that a man's vindication and his promotion are both episodes inside a law.

The comparative *khayr* is the sūrah's own word for the scale on which it has been asking the reader to judge. In the cell, Yūsuf had used the grammar of preference to refuse a temptation: "prison is more beloved to me (*aḥabbu ilayya*) than what they invite me to" (12:33). Here God uses the grammar of value to finish the sentence: the reward of the next life is *better*. The verse does not say that the prison was better than the palace; it says that what the prisoner was promised outweighs what the minister received. The two statements together give the chapter's theory of choice: a man declines a lesser evil for the sake of a greater good, and a God who takes note pays in a currency the choice was made in.

**"For Those Who Believe and Were Reverent" — the Condition Restated**

*Lilladhīna āmanū wa-kānū yattaqūn*. The pairing is the Qur'an's standard for the reward that is not wasted: faith as the root and *taqwā* as the habit. The past tense with an imperfect — *kānū yattaqūn*, "they kept being God-conscious" — is the idiom for persistence rather than for a single act, the same construction as *"they who were steadfast in their prayers"* (23:9; 70:23), and it is exactly what this sūrah has been narrating: not a moment of refusal but twenty-odd years of it. Yūsuf's own formulation of the rule, twenty verses on, will be the sūrah's most quoted: *"whoever fears God and is patient — God does not waste the reward of those who do beautiful things"* (12:90), where the same three terms the chapter has run through its plot — *taqwā*, *ṣabr*, *iḥsān* — are bound into one condition.

Our source's note on 12:56-57 supplies the wider frame for the comparative "better." It cites, first, the verse about the reward of the next life being greater: *"and the abode of the Hereafter is certainly greater (akbar), if they only knew"* — and the verse it has in mind is the one that speaks of those who emigrated after being wronged:

> *"And those who emigrated for God's sake, after they were wronged, We will settle them in the world as a good thing (sanubawwiʾuhum fī al-dunyā ḥasanatan), and the reward of the Hereafter is greater, if they only knew."* (Qur'an 16:41)

The wording of 16:41 — *nubawwiʾ*, from the same root as the sūrah's *yatabawwaʾu* in 12:56, "to settle them in a good lodging" — makes it a near-commentary on our verses: a good settlement in this world, and a greater reward in the next, granted to a man wronged and exiled. This is the sūrah's own exegesis of Yūsuf's career, in the Qur'an's general law about the wronged. The note adds the two further texts the chapter's audience would have known: *"the life of this world is nothing but play and diversion, and the abode of the Hereafter — that is the real life (al-ḥayawān)"* (29:64), and, to the Prophet ﷺ himself, *"the next life is better for you than the first"* (93:4).

**"Abqā": the Verse's Unspoken Contrast with a Dwelling-Place**

One lexical detail makes the verse's point more exactly. The sūrah has just said that Yūsuf was given the land to "take as a dwelling wherever he wished" (*yatabawwaʾ*, 12:56), a verb of choosing a settled home. The Qur'an's comparative for the next life in the same field is *"and the Hereafter is better and more lasting (abqā)"* (87:17), from *b-q-w*, to remain — the very root that gives *baqāʾ*, permanence; and the verse that pairs the two in a single sentence is worth quoting whole, for it also supplies the sūrah's missing term — patience: *"what is with you runs out, and what is with God endures; and We will certainly reward (wa-la-najziyanna) those who were patient with the best of what they used to do"* (Qur'an 16:96). Egypt's granaries, the most successful institution in this story, are described in a verb of dwelling that the Qur'an elsewhere reserves for what does not last. The verse's theology is not an argument against prosperity but a statement about its duration: a man may be given the whole land to settle in, and the reward of the next life is better — not because the land is evil, but because the reward outlives it.

**What the Verse Denies Its Reader**

Two common readings of the sūrah are closed off by 12:57, and the chapter's editors have put them in the least comfortable order possible. The first is the reading of the elevation as vindication's completion, as though prison ended because the story needed a happy ending; the verse says the payment for the years of injustice is elsewhere, and its recipients are the believing and God-conscious, not the successful. The second is the ascetic reading, as though the chapter were telling us that the righteous man is punished by the world and rewarded by the next; the verses before this one describe a real mercy, a real freedom of movement, a real statecraft that saved a region from famine. What 12:57 denies is the assumption that either of these is the point: the measure of a life is not its outcome in the visible world in either direction, and the sūrah's own opening line about its narrative — "there are signs (āyāt) for the inquiring" (12:7) — keeps the reader's attention on the asking rather than the acquiring. The reward is guaranteed, and the reward is deferred; the chapter's ethics live in the interval.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:58

> **58** *The brothers of Joseph came and entered upon him, and he recognized them, while they knew him not.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"The Brothers of Joseph Came" — the Family Reassembles under a Famine**

*Wa-jāʾa ikhwatu Yūsufa*. After two verses of divine commentary on his establishment in the land, the sūrah returns to the plot with the sentence that begins its second half, and it begins it with a relational noun: they are "the brothers of Yūsuf," a phrase the chapter has not used for them since they stood over the well (12:8-10, 12:16-18). The naming is the verse's first piece of irony: the men who made his name disappear are introduced by it. Our source records why they came: the famine that had afflicted Egypt had reached Canaan too, and they sought to buy the provisions stored during the seven good years (cf. Genesis 42:1-8; our source links the arrival explicitly with the plan of 12:47-49). The chapter's Providence is here entirely economic: a family is reunited because a state has grain and a valley does not, and the sūrah is content to let the weather and the ledger do the work that the brothers' malice began.

The verb *fa-darakhū* — "they came in upon him" — belongs to the root *d-r-k*, to arrive at, to come upon in time, the root of the Arabic for pursuit and overtaking (*darak*, *adraka*), and of the "darak" used for a guest who arrives unasked. So the sentence contains, in a single rare verb, the two things the scene is: an arrival at a destination and an unexpected coming upon someone. They reached him; and what they did not know, when they reached him, was who he was.

**"He Recognised Them, While They Knew Him Not"**

*Fa-ʿarafahum wa-hum lahu munkirūn*. The asymmetry is expressed by two verbs from two different roots of knowledge — *maʿrifa* and *inkār* — and the sūrah's choice of the negative is the point. It does not say "they did not know him (lam yaʿrifūhu)," which would describe a failure of recognition; it says *munkirūn*, from the root of *nakāra*, to find something strange, to refuse what one is shown. The same word-family is used in the Qur'an of the people of Lot, who answered the angels' visit with "strange things" (*munkar*, 7:80; 29:28-29), and of those who deny revelation by finding it unrecognisable: the brothers' non-recognition is not an accident of memory but a continuation of the moral condition the chapter first named in their father's house — "you have made Yūsuf and his brother more beloved to our father than us; our father is clearly in error (*ḍalāl*)" (12:8). The verse thus states, in one word, that they cannot see what is there.

Our source records the reasons the commentators give for the physical side of it: the length of time since they had seen him, the Egyptian dress and rank that made him another kind of person, and the awe that his station inspired in them (they assumed he had perished years before). A man does not expect to find the person he destroyed in the place of power, and the commentators' sympathy with that plain fact is what makes their explanations so short. But the sūrah itself does not explain, and its silence is deliberate: it needs the disguise to be complete, and it needs Yūsuf to know and say nothing, because everything the next twenty-five verses will do — the demand for the eleventh brother (12:59), the price returned in the saddlebags (12:62), the cup planted and the theft charged (12:70-76), the plea to take one brother in place of another (12:78) — is possible only while he is a stranger.

**Why He Does Not Tell Them**

The chapter never says why Yūsuf conceals his identity, and the commentators' answers fall into two classes. One takes it as strategy: he wanted to see whether they had changed, and so arranged the tests (the demand for Benjamin, the returned money, the planted cup) to observe their conduct with a brother they had no reason to protect. This reading is supported by the sūrah's own vocabulary: "thus did We devise a scheme for Yūsuf" (12:76), which attributes the whole plan to God's making and makes the concealment an instrument, not a revenge. The other takes it as prudence: the brothers had once killed a shirt with blood and lies; a man who revealed himself to them before they were confronted with what they had done would have been, at best, an embarrassment they would have managed.

Both readings are right about one thing: Yūsuf's aim is not to punish. When he finally speaks the fact, he says it privately and tenderly to Benjamin — "I am your brother; do not grieve at what they used to do" (12:69) — and when he announces himself to the rest, he does it in a verse that asks about *their* act, not *his* suffering: "do you know what you did with Yūsuf and his brother, when you were ignorant?" (12:89). The withheld recognition is the sūrah's method of producing that question in them. He could have obtained an apology; he chose instead to give them thirty verses in which they behaved, unknowingly, differently.

**The Prostration That Is Not Here**

One detail of the verse's silence is worth naming for readers who know the parallel. Genesis 42:6 records that the brothers came and "bowed themselves to him with their faces to the earth," and Genesis 42:9 makes Joseph remember his dream at the sight and treat them harshly because of it. The Qur'an's version of the arrival mentions no prostration at all. It has a reason: the chapter began with a dream in which eleven stars, the sun and the moon *prostrated* to him (12:4), and its father refused to recount it precisely because the brothers would then plot the fulfilment (12:5). If the brothers bowed here, the dream would be satisfied by accident, in a grain office, before anyone had repented. The sūrah therefore withholds the prostration until its last act, when "he raised his parents upon the throne, and they fell, overwhelmed, prostrate before him" (12:100) — a family's voluntary *sujūd*, after the brothers' own confession (12:90), and in the same breath as "this is the interpretation of my dream of old; my Lord made it true" (12:100). The chapter's art is in the refusal to let the sign be spent early.

**The Verse as an Experience**

Muslim readers have taken *ʿarafahum wa-hum lahu munkirūn* as the sūrah's type of the reversal in which the wronged party is recognised and the wrongdoers are not: the meeting at a market, an office, a hospital corridor, in which the one who was thrown into a well arrives as the person you must ask. Two lessons the verse models are worth stating plainly. The first belongs to Yūsuf: he does not use the advantage of being the only one who knows. He says nothing about the shirt, the well, or the years; the sūrah records no word of reproach from his mouth until the matter is finished (and then only a question, 12:89, and then the pardon, "no blame upon you today," 12:92). The second belongs to the brothers: not knowing who a person is does not exempt them from what they are doing, and the chapter's moral arithmetic keeps a separate account of the two — which is why the sūrah, immediately after this verse, makes them pay for grain with a pledge and a promise (12:59-66), and why the ledger is the place where they are finally met.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:59

> **59** *And when he had arranged for their provisions, he said, “Bring me a brother of yours from your father. Do you not see that I give full measure, and that I am the best of hosts?*

**Expanded Commentary**

**He Feeds Them First, Then Makes His Demand**

*Wa-lammā jahhazahum bi-jihāzihim qālaʾtūnī bi-akhin lakum min abīkum*. The sequence in the first clause is the whole of the verse's ethics. The brothers arrive, hungry and suspicious, and Yūsuf does what his office requires before anything else: he equips them with their *jihāz*, their travel provision — the gear, saddles and food a caravan needs to get home (the verb *jahhaza* is the technical word for fitting out an expedition). Only once they have what they came for does he state the condition for the next visit. He does not hold the grain hostage to get the brother; he measures out the full ration, and then says: bring him. The distinction is between leverage and terms. A ruler who withholds food to extract a person is a tyrant; a minister who feeds the hungry and then sets the price of future trade is a statesman, and the sūrah is fastidious about showing which of the two Yūsuf is.

Our source records the mechanics the commentators supply: the eleven brothers had brought eleven camel-loads' worth of payment and asked for provisions for eleven dependents, of whom one — Benjamin — had been left at home; Yūsuf's demand is thus the verification of a claim they had made about their household (al-Qurṭubī), and his refusal to grant the eleventh load without the eleventh brother is "a subtle way of denying the brothers their request without accusing them of wrongdoing" (al-Rāzī). The chapter keeps the point in the wording: he does not say "you are lying," he says "bring me him."

**"A Brother of Yours from Your Father" — the Sentence That Tells Them Too Much**

*Bi-akhin lakum min abīkum*. Of the eleven, all were sons of Yaʿqūb, and so the qualification "from your father" would be meaningless unless it were doing a specific work: the commentators take it as Yūsuf's way of asking for a full brother, one of the same father and mother, and Benjamin alone answers to that among the sons of Yūsuf's own lineage. The effect on the brothers is another matter entirely. A foreign minister who names the family's internal structure knows things no customs officer needs to know, and the sūrah lets the reader feel the chill without stating it: they will spend the rest of the chapter unable to tell whether they are being tested, robbed, or recognised. Genesis 42:15 makes the demand explicit and adds an oath ("by the life of Pharaoh, you shall not leave here unless your youngest brother comes here"); the Qur'an has no oath, no threat, and no accusation — one clause, and it is phrased as a request about a person, not a demand for submission.

Yūsuf's own words, moreover, do not say "my brother." The chapter has him speak from inside the official role completely: he has recognised them (12:58) and will not use the knowledge. The verse's pathos is in that restraint — the man asking for the only full brother he has left does so in the grammar of a requisition, and the sūrah grants him no more than the pronoun "your father," which is the closest he comes to saying *my father* (a phrase he will use when he speaks at last to Benjamin: "I am your brother," 12:69).

**"Do You Not See That I Give Full Measure, and I Am the Best of Hosts?"**

*A-lā tarawn annī ūfī al-kayla wa-anā khayru al-munzilīn*. The appeal is to a public record, and the two nouns are precise. *Ūfī al-kayl* — "I fulfil the measure" — is the prophets' commercial vocabulary; the command to "give full measure and weight" is the repeated charge of the Qur'an's moral economy (6:152; 11:85; 17:35; 26:181-183: *"give full measure and do not be of those who lose"*), and Yūsuf's self-description places him inside that tradition of prophetic business ethics rather than outside it as a statesman. When the sūrah wants to establish that he is a man whose word can be relied on, it does not cite his miracles or his rank, but his scales.

The second half is stranger and warmer: *khayru al-munzilīn*, "the best of those who give lodging." The word *inzāl* is not "hospitality" in the abstract but the act of settling a guest in a place, and the sūrah has been using the same root since Potiphar's first order: "make his lodging beautiful" (*akrimī mathwāhu*, 12:21 — the household's word for what they did for a bought boy), and Yūsuf's own famous answer to temptation: "he has made my lodging beautiful" (*aḥsana mathwāya*, 12:23). The chapter's ethic of fidelity is built out of this vocabulary of the guest-room: the man who was given a beautiful lodging has become, in his own account of himself, the best of those who provide one. His claim is not "I am powerful," but "I am good at this: the measure, the room, the welcome." It is also why the demand is addressed to the brothers as guests rather than as suspects — the state's granary, in this sūrah, is run on the etiquette of a house.

**The Verse's Argument from Conduct**

Read as a piece of rhetoric, the sentence shows how Yūsuf wins cases: by pointing at the record. He has no authority over Yaʿqūb, no leverage except that Egypt has food, and no willingness to accuse; so he argues from what they can verify themselves — "do you not see?" (*a-lā tarawn*) is an appeal to their own observation, and it is the same appeal the sūrah makes of the women's testimony, of the shirt, and of the dream that came true ("I shall inform you of its interpretation," 12:45, where the cup-bearer argues from what he saw). The chapter's epistemology is empirical all the way down, and even its prophet argues by evidence rather than by status. This is also why the brothers' negotiation continues: they do not flee or protest the demand as an outrage, they go home and lobby their father (12:61), because the man who set the terms has behaved in a way that makes terms negotiable. That, the sūrah suggests, is the practical fruit of *iḥsān*: it buys credibility before it buys reward.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:60

> **60** *But if you bring him not unto me, you shall have no measure from me, nor shall you come nigh unto me.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Terms, Stated Once, in the Negative**

*Fa-in lam taʾtūnī bihi fa-lā kayla lakum ʿindī wa-lā turishūn*. The conditional is bare: no threat of violence, no imprisonment, no seizure of the goods they have just received, no appeal to their fear of the state. Two consequences follow from not bringing the brother — the measure is not given to you, and you are not admitted. The first is an economic sanction, the second an exclusion from access; both are the kinds of things an official may legitimately decide about a trading relationship. The sūrah is careful, in the midst of a story full of household injustice, to keep Yūsuf's own exercise of power inside the limits of a bargain: he withholds a favour, he does not inflict a punishment. The chapter has just recorded a court that "saw the signs" and jailed an innocent man anyway (12:35), and its portrait of the minister who now has the same power pointed in the opposite direction is part of its argument about what office should look like.

Our source records how the commentators read the second clause: the brothers' failure to produce Benjamin would expose them as liars about the size of their household, and so they would neither be given grain nor be permitted even to enter the city (al-Baghawī; cf. Genesis 42:9-20, where the demand arrives with an oath and an accusation of spying). Genesis's version is adversarial — Pharaoh's governor suspects espionage and holds Simeon as hostage (Genesis 42:17-19, 42:24); the Qur'an has no accusation, no chains, and no hostage, only a condition. The sūrah's restraint here is deliberate: nothing is allowed to interfere with what the chapter is actually about, which is whether these men can be trusted with a brother.

**"No Measure for You With Me" — the Granary as an Instrument of Moral Evidence**

The word *kayl* — the measured portion of grain — has been the sūrah's term for Yūsuf's justice since the previous verse ("I fulfil the measure," 12:59), and it will carry the whole later plot: the measure that is withheld and given, the vessel of the king placed in a bag, the brothers' insistence that they "came not to work corruption in the land" and are not thieves (12:73). Everything the chapter will test is conducted through the distribution of a measured good, because a measure is exactly the sort of thing that reveals a person: it can be shorted, faked, hoarded, shared, or given full. The brothers' honesty about their family, their willingness to risk their father's grief for the sake of the grain, and finally their offer to surrender themselves in Benjamin's place (12:78) are all produced by the pressure of a ration.

There is also a political realism in the sentence's opening: in a famine, the measure is the only language a state and its subjects share. Yūsuf knows he cannot command Canaan to release a boy; he can only say what Egypt will and will not do. His method is to let consequences do the persuading, which is why the next scene takes place not in a courtroom but in a household discussion (12:61-67).

**"Nor Shall You Come Nigh Unto Me" — Access as the Second Currency**

The last clause is directed at the men's standing rather than at their stomachs, and the sūrah has trained its reader to notice that access is what power really distributes. Potiphar's household denied Yūsuf nothing but freedom and got a prison instead (12:35); the king's court had no access to interpretation until a servant volunteered to go and fetch it (12:45); and now the minister says: if you will not meet the condition, you will not enter. It is a sanction with a purpose beyond punishment. What Yūsuf needs is not obedience but the brother's presence, and exclusion from the granary is the only lever that reaches an old man in Canaan who has one son left of the two he loves. The clause is also a piece of courtesy: it is addressed to the brothers as men of business, not as criminals; the sūrah's wording does not once, in these verses, say "I will punish you."

**A Verse About the Limits of a Good Man's Leverage**

Finally, notice the arithmetic of the demand from Yūsuf's side. He has waited twenty years and does not seize the moment: no announcement, no summons of the family, no demand for the father's appearance. He asks for one person, in a form a minister could ask, and he leaves the rest to the years that follow. The chapter's larger point about leverage is in that restraint — a man who could have used a famine to punish his brothers uses it only to arrange a meeting. When the meeting comes, he will tell his brother privately, "I am your brother; do not grieve at what they used to do" (12:69), and the public part of the story will continue for another twenty verses as a test of the ten. That is the sūrah's theory of the useful threat: it should be no stronger than the good you are trying to obtain, and it should never be aimed at the person but at the situation. The brothers hear the conditions, and go home to persuade — the verse's sequel (12:61) shows exactly what Yūsuf's limit-keeping produced: not terror, but a plan.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:61

> **61** *They said, “We shall seek to lure him from his father; that we shall surely do.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Verb of Predation, Put in the Brothers' Mouths**

*Qālū sanurāwiduhu ʿan abīhi wa-innā fāʿilūn*. The verb is the chapter's own. The root *r-w-d* has so far belonged to a single drama: the woman of the house "sought to lure him from himself" (12:23), the women of the city were told how she "lured him and he refused" (12:32), and at the retrial she confesses it again ("I, I sought to lure him from myself," 12:51); outside this sūrah the same root occurs only of Lot's people, who "sought to lure his guests from themselves" (54:37). Now the brothers use it for lobbying their father: *sanurāwiduhu*, "we shall work on him, we shall turn him from what he wills." The sūrah does not comment on the choice, and does not need to. Its moral vocabulary treats a family's persuasion of a reluctant parent as a species of the same act — the attempt to wrench a will from its own purpose — and the reader who has just watched what *murāwada* did to a household is told, in one verb, what it will do to a father.

The effect is compounded by the object: *ʿan abīhi*, "from his father" — the same prepositional frame as "ʿan nafsihi," "from himself." Yūsuf was sought *away from himself*; Yaʿqūb will be sought *away from his own judgment*. The sūrah's psychology of pressure is that the two are continuous: to persuade hard is to try to make a person act against the thing he sees, and whether that is violence or love is decided only by what the persuaders are trying to protect. In this case they are trying to feed a family and to keep a promise they have already made to a minister; the chapter is not accusing them of a crime here, only recording the register in which they speak.

**"We Shall Surely Do" — the Brothers' Third Self-Guarantee**

*Wa-innā fāʿilūn*. The clause is an emphatic affirmation of their own capacity to act, and it is the third of the sūrah's self-certifying sentences. They said to their father, when they came with the bloodied shirt: "you would not believe us, even if we were truthful" (12:17, the phrase of liars protesting); they had said when asking for Yūsuf, "we shall be sincere advisers of him" (12:11: *wa-innā la-nāṣiḥūn*); and here: "we shall surely do it." The sūrah's pattern is a study in the ethics of promising: the men are not liars in 12:61 — they intend what they say — but they are speaking about tomorrow without an exception, and the Book's rule about that is explicit:

> *"And never say of a thing 'I will surely do it tomorrow,' except: 'if God wills' (illā an yashāʾ Allāh)."* (Qur'an 18:23-24)

The verse of al-Kahf is the sūrah's own gloss on the brothers' clause. The *illa* of exception is not a formula of piety bolted onto speech; it is an admission about the real author of the next hour. The chapter will demonstrate the point in its own plot: the brothers do "work on" their father, they extract from him the strongest possible pledge (12:66), they set out under his conditions (12:67-68), and they still lose Benjamin — not by treachery, not by negligence in any simple sense, but because the keeping was never theirs ("God is the best of guardians," 12:64). "We shall surely do it" is the sentence a sūrah about providence exists to qualify.

**The Future Tense, the Pledge, and the Arithmetic of Pressure**

*Sanurāwiduhu* — the near future with *sa-*, which Arabic usage treats as the more immediate of the two futures (the grammarians distinguish it from *sawfa* as the nearer and more confident) — is the tense of a plan already agreed within the group. The brothers have decided before they speak; the father has not been consulted, only addressed. The sūrah shows the mechanics of a family's coalition: the sons arrive with a settled proposal, an argument drawn from evidence (the returned merchandise, 12:65), and the language of competence ("we shall guard him," 12:63). The verse reports no rebuttal, only the father's answer in the next verse (12:64), and the answer is not refusal but a question — "shall I entrust him to you…?" — which converts the negotiation into a moral examination of their history.

There is a practical observation here that the sūrah does not spell out but clearly intends. People under pressure (a famine, an empty store, a state that has made a demand) reliably reach for the leverage they have, and the leverage of adult children is their parent's dependence. Yūsuf's granary has made Yaʿqūb needy, and need has turned the brothers into lobbyists. Nothing in the verse is illegal; nothing in it is innocent either. The chapter's ethic is that a proposal to an elderly parent should be judged by whether it says "we will guard him" or "God will guard him" — and the difference between the two is what the rest of the sūrah exists to show.

**The Sentence the Sūrah Leaves Unanswered**

Notice finally what is *not* said: no apology to the father for the past, no mention of Yūsuf, no acknowledgment that the boy they now propose to lose was once a boy they lost. The brothers' speech in 12:61 is entirely forward-looking, and the sūrah lets the silence stand as the indictment. Yaʿqūb, who knows what he knows about their capacity for the plausible sentence, answers not with anger but with memory (12:64) and with a condition (12:66) — the two resources left to a father who has already lost one son to a story he believed. The sūrah's realism about families is at its fullest in this exchange: the same people who wronged you are now the only ones who can help you, and they are sincere, and they are asking for the thing you cannot give.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:62

> **62** *He said to his servant boys, “Put their merchandise in their saddlebags. Perchance they will recognize it when they have gone back to their people; perchance they will return.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command to the Servants, and the Grammar of an Unseen Kindness**

*Wa-qāla li-ghilmānihi jiʿalū silʿatahum fī riḥālihim*. The verse is a stage-direction: the minister, having given the measure and set the condition, turns to his *ghilmān* — the young men in his service — and tells them to put the goods back where they found them. The sūrah rarely shows Yūsuf issuing an order to subordinates, and when it does, the order is to give something away. This is the chapter's way of characterising a man at the height of his powers: not the speech he makes to the powerful (12:50, 12:55), but the instruction he gives to those who cannot repay him. The word *ghulām* (pl. *ghilmān*) is the Qur'an's ordinary term for a young male attendant or servant — the same word used, in the Book's other register, of the attendants of Paradise: *"circulating among them, boys, as though they were pearls laid out"* (52:24; cf. 56:17-18) — and it belongs here to the household of Egypt's granary, where a boy bought as a slave (12:19) has grown into the man with boys at his command. He uses the authority, in the first scene where we see it exercised, to return money.

The word for what is returned, *silʿa*, is the trader's term for merchandise, the goods a caravan carries to market; and our source records the commentators' two readings of what was put back — the goods the brothers had presented in exchange (al-Baghawī; al-Zamakhsharī), or the price they had paid for the food (several of the later commentators), and the same ambiguity affects 12:65, where the brothers' exclamation is "our merchandise returned to us!" Either way the act is a refusal to take payment for grain at a time when grain is the only thing anyone has.

**Two Purposes in One Act: the Doubling of *Laʿalla***

*Laʿallahum yaʿrifūnahā idh inqalabū ilā ahlihim, laʿallahum yarjiʿūn*. The verse states its aim twice, with the same particle of hopeful expectation. As a matter of style, the sūrah's own commentators note that two *laʿalla* clauses attached to one action mark two distinct purposes intended by a single deed — a technique of compression the chapter uses at 12:8 as well, and in the very next verse-pair of this sequence (12:65: recognition and the argument for return). Here the two purposes are ordered with pedagogical precision: first they will *recognise* (yaʿrifūnahā), then they will *return* (yarjiʿūn). Kindness is not assumed to work; it is described as a sequence with a stage of comprehension before a stage of action. The verse's realism is in that ordering — a person must first notice what was done for him before the deed can change his conduct.

The verb of the first stage deserves the weight the sūrah gives it: *yaʿrifūnahā*, "they will recognise it." The root *ʿ-r-f* has just been used for the failure of recognition in the same household: Yūsuf "recognised them, while they did not recognise him" (*munkirūn*, 12:58). The returned goods will be recognised by the very men who could not know their own brother — and the sūrah's point is not an irony but a diagnosis: they are capable of perceiving an honest act, because perceiving an honest act is easier than perceiving a person. It is only later, when the measure is withheld and the cup is found, that they will be forced into the harder recognition; and the chapter has here planted the instrument of it, in their own sacks.

**Trust Built by a Transaction, Not by a Demand**

Look at the pair of verses this one completes. In 12:60 the minister withheld: no grain, no access, if the condition is not met. In 12:62 he gives: the price back, the burden of the next journey made easier. The combination is the sūrah's complete picture of legitimate pressure — a sanction that stays inside the terms of the deal, and a generosity that the recipient did not bargain for. Yūsuf needs something from these men, and he does not use their need to take from them. Our source's note puts the intention plainly: by returning the whole of their merchandise, he sought to win their confidence and their reliance on him for future dealings, so that they would come back with Benjamin (cf. Genesis 42:25-26).

The plan works, and the sūrah shows exactly how. When the brothers reach home, it is this act they use as their argument — "what more do we seek? this is our merchandise returned to us" (12:65) — and it is the father's own recollection of their earlier dishonesty that he sets against it (12:64). Yūsuf has, by returning money, bought the family's permission to risk a son. The verse is thus the chapter's quiet treatise on the political power of commercial probity: a single clean transaction can do what an army cannot, namely, make people trust a stranger with their most vulnerable possession.

**"Put It in Their Saddlebags" — the Sacks That Will Carry Both Things**

The word *riḥāl* (the loads and gear of the mounts; from the root of *raḥla*, a journey and the packing for it) will recur at the sequence's crisis, when the search is made: "he began with their sacks before the sack of his brother, and then he brought the vessel out of his brother's sack" (12:76). The same bags, in other words, hold the returned kindness and the planted cup. That is not a flaw in the plan but its shape: Yūsuf's method with these men is to give them grounds for trust and then to test whether the trust holds when the test costs them something. The chapter's moral logic is severe and fair — it gives before it asks, and it asks only what it can verify. The Prophet's ﷺ rule about a hand that holds another's property is the general form of the verse's first clause:

> *"The hand is responsible for what it has taken until it returns it."* (a ruling of the Prophet ﷺ on deposits, transmitted by Abū Dāwūd and al-Tirmidhī)

Yūsuf, who had been himself a deposit in Potiphar's house and had refused to convert that trust into an advantage (12:52: "I did not betray him in the unseen"), returns the price of grain to men who had once sold him. The sūrah does not call this forgiveness. It calls it *iḥsān* — and it has already told the reader what becomes of that: "We do not waste the reward of the beautiful-doers" (12:56).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:63

> **63** *So when they returned to their father, they said, “O father! The measure has been withheld from us. So send our brother with us that we might obtain the measure. We shall surely be his keepers.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence with No Author**

*Yā abāna muniʿa minnā al-kayl*. The brothers' report begins in the passive: "the measure was withheld from us." They do not name the minister, and the sūrah does not name him either, so that the first fact the family hears is an impersonal refusal — grain stopped coming. Arabic's passive is the grammar of a decision already made somewhere above the speaker, and the brothers are speaking to a man who has no power over it; their syntax is the syntax of pressure. There is a second, unintended, accuracy too: the chapter has been telling its reader all along who withholds and who gives ("We give the measure to whom We will," in effect the theology of 12:56 and 12:64), and Yaʿqūb, whose own answer in the next verse is that only God keeps, will not be fooled by a sentence that leaves the agent out.

The report is brief and transactional, and its brevity is part of the sūrah's characterisation. Nothing is said of the eleven loads received, of the minister's manners, of the demand's odd phrasing. What the brothers bring home is the shortage, because the shortage is the argument.

**The Rhetoric of a Family's Risk**

*Fa-ar sil maʿanā akhāna naktal lak wa-naḥnu lahu ḥāfiẓūn*. Three moves, each careful. First, the request is framed as a service to the father: "send him with us *and we will measure for you* (naktal *lak*)" — the lām makes the errand his, not theirs; the grain is for his house, his household, his table. Second, the boy is called "our brother," not "your son." The sūrah has just put the sharper phrase in the minister's mouth — "a brother of yours from your father" (12:59) — and the brothers drop the qualification, which is exactly what an evasive sentence does: it keeps the truth and removes the emphasis. "From your father" was a reminder that one of them shares a mother with the son Yaʿqūb believes dead; "our brother" is a word that spreads the responsibility over eleven. Third, a guarantee, in the strongest form Arabic has: *innā la-hu ḥāfiẓūn* — "we shall surely, certainly, be his keepers."

That last word is the sūrah's hinge. *Ḥāfiẓ* is the term Yūsuf used of his own fitness for office ("I am a skilled keeper," 12:55), and it is the term Yaʿqūb will use of God in the very next verse ("God is the best of keepers," 12:64). The chapter thus arranges three claims of guardianship in ten verses, one true of a minister's conduct, one false of ten men's intentions, and one true of God alone. The middle one is not a lie — the brothers intend to guard the boy — but it is a claim about the future they cannot keep, and the sūrah's judgment on such claims was already stated at 18:23-24: do not say "I will do it" without "if God wills." What makes 12:63 a studied piece of family rhetoric is that it says "we shall keep him" where the father's answer says "God shall keep him"; the entire tragedy of the next twenty verses is conducted between those two verbs.

**The Echo of the First Request**

Readers who remember the beginning of the sūrah will hear in this verse the sentence the brothers spoke twenty years earlier, when they asked to take Yūsuf out: *"O our father, why do you not trust us with Yūsuf?
