We shall surely be sincere advisers to him"* (12:11: *wa-innā lahu la-nāṣiḥūn*). The two passages are composed as a pair: a son is asked for, a guarantee is offered, the father refuses — and in each case the guaranteeing brothers are the same men, and in each case the object of the guarantee is the child they do not actually love as much as they claim. The sūrah's ring-composition makes the echo a verdict: whatever good intentions the brothers now have, the history is the history, and Yaʿqūb's answer in 12:64 will name the earlier case in the very terms of the present one ("shall I entrust you with him as I entrusted you with his brother before?"). The second request is therefore not a fresh negotiation but the reopening of an old wound, and the sūrah's refusal to let anyone mention the wound out loud is part of what makes these verses so taut.

**The Famine as the Argument That Beats Precaution**

It is worth noticing what has changed between 12:11 and 12:63, because the change is the sūrah's sociology. Then, the family had food and the father had no reason to bend. Now a state has withheld grain, and the returned money in their sacks (12:62) has supplied a piece of evidence the father cannot argue with (12:65: "what more do we seek? our merchandise returned!"). The brothers are not manipulating him so much as presenting a case that hunger has made unanswerable: the risk to one son must be set against the starvation of a household. Yaʿqūb's subsequent concessions — the pledge (12:66), the instruction to enter by separate gates (12:67) — are the measures of a man who has been forced to say yes and knows what a yes is worth. The verse's enduring practical lesson is about that moment in every family's life when caution must give way to necessity, and the language of assurance ("we'll be careful," "we'll watch him") is asked to do the work that only circumstances can do. The sūrah's answer is in the father's sentence: entrust it to God, and take every precaution besides — and then be ready for the loss anyway, because, as he will say when the news comes, patience in this matter is beautiful and God alone is asked for help against what is described (12:83-86).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:64

> **64** *He said, “Should I entrust him to you as I entrusted his brother to you aforetime? But God is the best of keepers, and He is the most Merciful of the merciful.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Is Not a Refusal**

*Qāla hal āmanukum ʿalayhi illā kamā amintukum ʿalā akhīhi min qablu*. Yaʿqūb's answer is a rhetorical question with a condition attached, and the sūrah's readers have argued about its force ever since: is he refusing ("I will not entrust him to you, since I entrusted his brother to you once and lost him") or conceding with a warning ("I shall entrust him no differently than before — with God, not you, as the guardian")? The context settles it: he does send the boy (12:66-68), having extracted a pledge. So the sentence is not a veto but the expression of a man who cannot say yes without first stating what a yes costs. It is the speech of a parent who has already been wronged by his own trust, and the sūrah gives him the sharpest form the language allows: an interrogation that is also a memory.

The grammar reinforces the double sense. *Hal āmanukum… illā kamā* — "shall I entrust you with him except in the manner that I entrusted you…" — leaves the comparison open: "as I did before" may mean "under the same conditions, with the same reliance on God" or "with the same result, which you know." The chapter is not being obscure; it is reporting how such sentences work in a family where trust has been broken. A man who has lost one son to a guarantee cannot grant another without saying, first, what the guarantee has cost him.

**"His Brother" — the Name the Father Will Not Say**

*ʿalā akhīhi min qablu*. Our source records the commentators' gloss: the brother referred to is Yūsuf (cf. 12:15's sequel), and the phrasing is the verse's most moving feature. Yaʿqūb does not say "my son," and he does not say "Yūsuf." He says "his brother," and the sūrah is not merely avoiding an anachronism about Benjamin's family tree — the circumlocution is the behaviour of grief. The lost son has become, in the father's own speech, a relational term: the one who makes the other boy a brother. Twenty years of mourning have turned a name into a pronoun, and the sūrah lets the sentence stand without comment on it. It is also, in its way, an act of the father's knowledge: as if saying the name in front of these men, who once heard a story about a wolf, would make the wound part of the negotiation.

The words *min qablu*, "aforetime," "before," are the sūrah's own marker of the past acting in the present; it is the same phrase Yūsuf will use at the very end, when the family is reunited and the eleven bow: "this is the interpretation of my dream of old (*min qablu*); my Lord has made it true" (12:100). Father and son, at the two ends of this arc, both use the phrase to lay a past event beside a present one — Yaʿqūb to remember a loss, Yūsuf to recognise a promise. The chapter is, among other things, about the difference between those two uses of memory.

**"God Is the Best of Keepers" — a Theology in the Comparative**

*Fa-llāhu khayrun ḥāfiẓan*. The syntax is precise: the comparative *khayrun* is followed by the accusative of specification (*ḥāfiẓan*, "in respect of guardianship"), which is how Arabic marks "better as a keeper" rather than "a better keeper than you are, but still competent." The verse does not accuse the sons of malice; it relocates the office. The brothers had claimed *ḥifẓ* ("we shall surely be his keepers," 12:63); Yūsuf had claimed it for himself before the king ("I am a skilled keeper," 12:55); now the father assigns it, in the comparative degree, to God. The sūrah's whole narrative of trust — the lost shirt, the entrusted boy, the treasury of a nation — is held together by this small chain of one root.

What the phrase does not say is as important as what it does. It does not promise that Benjamin will not be lost. Yaʿqūb, who has been told for twenty years that his son is dead, is not in the business of expecting safety, and the sūrah has been explicit that God's *ḥifẓ* does not mean a life without the well: Yūsuf was "kept" through a slavery, an accusation and a prison, and "kept" in the sense that nothing of him was wasted and nothing of him was lost to God. The verse's theology is that the best of keepers may keep a person by ways that look, from Canaan, exactly like losing him. That is the reading 12:100 will confirm, and it is the reading the sūrah is training its audience in when it puts this sentence in a father's mouth at the moment he consents to the second loss.

**"The Most Merciful of the Merciful" — the Father's Two Sentences**

*Wa-huwa arḥamu al-rāḥimīn*. The superlative is the Book's idiom of the afflicted: Mūsā says it after the golden calf, asking forgiveness for himself and his brother ("my Lord, forgive me and my brother and admit us into Your mercy; You are the most merciful of the merciful," 7:151); Ayyūb says it in the middle of his loss ("affliction has touched me, and You are the most merciful of the merciful," 21:83); and in this sūrah it occurs exactly twice — here, at the beginning of the second journey to Egypt, and on Yūsuf's lips at the moment he absolves his brothers: *"no reproach upon you today; may God forgive you, and He is the most merciful of the merciful"* (12:92). The two occurrences frame the whole Benjamin sequence: a father invokes God's mercy as he hands his son over to men he cannot trust, and a son invokes the same mercy as he releases the men who destroyed him. The sūrah's design makes the second the answer to the first — the father's prayer was answered not by keeping Benjamin safe in Canaan but by making him the means by which the family that had broken was reassembled, and the words Yūsuf speaks when it is done are his father's words.

It is also worth adding that Yaʿqūb's other theological sentence, at the end of this same story, is in the same register: when the sons ask forgiveness of him, he says *"I shall ask forgiveness for you from my Lord; He is the Forgiving, the Merciful"* (12:98). Between 12:64 and 12:98 the father's language moves from *arḥamu al-rāḥimīn* to *al-ghafūru al-raḥīm*, from mercy asked to forgiveness promised — and the sūrah, which has spent its whole second half on the mechanics of a reconciliation, marks the change with nothing but the names of God.

**What the Verse Teaches About Entrusting**

Practically, 12:64 is the chapter's model of the conditional consent. Yaʿqūb does not forbid (he cannot: a household must eat), does not pretend the past is unimportant (he names it in a clause), does not accept a verbal assurance (he demands a pledge "in God's name," 12:66), and takes precautions of his own (the separate gates, 12:67) while saying out loud that the precaution is not the security ("I can avail you nothing against God," 12:67). The combination is the same as the Prophet's ﷺ rule "tie her and trust" — precautions and reliance in one hand — and the sūrah shows both parts working: the camel is tied, and what happens next happens anyway. That is the verse's hardest and most honest lesson: entrusting to God does not prevent the loss; it prevents the loss from having the last word.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:65

> **65** *And when they opened their belongings, they found their merchandise had been returned to them. They said, “O father! What [more] do we seek? This is our merchandise returned unto us! Now we can provide for our family, and keep watch over our brother, while getting another camel-load—these are meager rations!”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Discovery, and the Argument from Evidence**

*Wa-lammā fataḥū miʿatahum wajadū silʿatahum ruddat ilayhim*. The verse's verbs are those of opening and finding, and the sūrah has already prepared them: Yūsuf had said "put their goods in their saddles, *perhaps they will recognise it* (laʿallahum yaʿrifūnahā) when they return to their people" (12:62), and here they find, and recognise. The sequence is the chapter's small demonstration of how trust is manufactured: not by a speech but by an object, found at home, in one's own bag, when it is least expected. Ibn Kathīr's note, in the form our source preserves it, is that the brothers appealed to this gesture of goodwill in order to persuade their father to release Benjamin (al-Baghawī), for his presence was now necessary if the family was to be fed again.

Their speech that follows is an argument entirely from that evidence: "what more do we seek? (mā nabtaghī)" — an idiom of impatience: what are we still hunting for, what is left to prove? The returned merchandise is, in their reasoning, a certificate on the minister: a man who gives back money in a famine will return a boy. The sūrah lets the logic stand, because it is not false; what it lacks is not evidence but foresight. The chapter's reader knows something the brothers have not reckoned with: that the honest minister is also the man who has engineered the whole situation (12:59-60, 12:62), and whose next demand will be enforced by an accusation (12:70-76). Good faith in a transaction is not a guarantee about the design.

**"We Will Bring Provision for Our Family, Guard Our Brother, and Gain an Extra Camel-Load"**

*Wa-namīru ahlanā wa-naḥfaẓu akhāna wa-nazīdu kayla baʿīrin*. Their pitch has three parts and they are in the right order — first the household, then the brother, then the profit — and the sūrah has the brothers say all three, because it is honest about the motives that carry a family through a famine. The verb *namīru* (to bring *mīra*, provisions; the noun is the ordinary word for a caravan's cargo of food, and the economic vocabulary of early Islam — the *mīrāt* of a town, the official who oversees provisioning — is derived from it) is the verb of procurement; *naḥfaẓu* is the verb of guardianship they have already used (12:63) and that their father has just reassigned to God (12:64).

The third clause is the sharpest: "an extra camel-load's measure." *Baʿīr* is the load-bearing camel, the unit of a caravan's capacity, and the brothers are negotiating a bonus — one more sack — as though the measure were the point of the errand and not the boy. The sūrah's realism about hunger is that it shows the family making a business case while discussing the safety of a child, and then closes the sentence with the words that judge the whole negotiation: *dhālika kaylu yasyīrun*, "this is a scant measure" — the present ration is small. The phrase is translated sometimes as "an easy measure" (the root *y-s-r* supplies both senses, "little" and "easy"; cf. "We will make things easy for him," 92:5-7, and "ease" as the counter-word to hardship in 94:5-6). Their meaning is: what we have now is barely enough, so accept the risk. The sūrah's double meaning is that a "small measure" is precisely what an easy life is made of, if only the family survives.

**What Genesis Sees in the Same Money**

The parallel moment in Genesis 42:27-28 is instructive by contrast. There, when the brothers find their money returned in their sacks, their hearts fail them and they say to one another: *"What is this that God has done to our souls?"* (Genesis 42:28) — the money is read as a divine act, and the family is frightened. The Qur'an's version keeps the fact and reverses the reaction: the brothers read the returned money as a commercial argument and become, if anything, more confident. That is not a contradiction between the accounts but a difference of subject. Genesis is telling a story in which God's hand is visible to the actors; the sūrah is telling a story in which God's hand is visible to the reader only, while the actors reason as people do — from evidence to advantage. The chapter's theology is undamaged: it has just said that the one who established Yūsuf was God (12:56), that judgment belongs to God (12:67), and that He is the best of guardians (12:64). What it declines is to make its characters into interpreters of their own lives. Yaʿqūb alone, in this scene, declines the brothers' reading, and he asks not for evidence but for a bond in God's name (12:66).

**The Verse's Lesson in the Limits of Reassurance**

Read as advice, 12:65 is the moment in every negotiation when one party offers the other a piece of past good conduct as security for a future risk, and the two are not the same thing. The sūrah's answer is neither to trust the evidence nor to dismiss it: the father weighs it, and then makes the transaction carry the maximum possible guarantee (an oath "from God," 12:66), and adds his own precautions (12:67). The chapter never says he was wrong to be satisfied by the returned money, and never says he was safe. In the end he is neither: the pledge is given, the gates are avoided, and Benjamin is lost to an accusation — at which point the sūrah's verdict on the whole episode will not be "you trusted the wrong men," but "the affair was ours" in effect: "God is best at keeping" (12:64) — which is the sentence the brothers' evidence was never going to supply, and which is why they needed to hear it from a father rather than derive it from a sack.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:66

> **66** *He said, “I will not send him forth with you till you give me a solemn pledge before God that you will surely bring him back to me, unless you are surrounded.” So when they gave their solemn pledge, he said, “God is Guardian over what we say.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Pledge "From God" — the Word the Prophets Are Bound By**

*Qāla lan urisilahu maʿakum ḥatta tuʾtūnī bi-mawthāqin mina llāhi la-taʾtunannahu ilayya illā an yuḥāṭa bikum*. The word for the guarantee, *mawthāq*, is from the root of a knot tied tight (*waṯq*; *mawthūq* is what is firmly bound, and the "mīthāq" is a solemn, binding covenant), and the Qur'an uses the same term of the covenant God took from the prophets: *"We took from the prophets their covenant, and from you, from Noah, Abraham, Moses and Jesus son of Mary; and We took from them a solemn covenant (mīthāqan ghalīẓā)"* (33:7; cf. 2:63, 2:27, 3:81). The sūrah's choice is extraordinary in a sentence about a father and his sons at the door of a famine: the register of prophecy's compact is used for a family contract about a boy's safe return. Yaʿqūb, who has already been betrayed by the word of his sons (12:11, 17), will not accept a promise; he requires an oath in God's name, and the sūrah gives his demand the vocabulary of revelation's own bonds. There is a theology in that: an oath "from God" is not a magical reinforcement but the acknowledgement that only the One who hears all speech can make a promise stick, and the verse's last clause says exactly this when the father hands the whole transaction to God as *wakīl*.

The construction *lan… ḥatta* ("I will not send him until…") is the grammar of an absolute condition, and the sūrah's realism is that the condition, once met, does not make the father any safer. The pledge is given; the loss happens anyway. What the verse is showing, then, is not a clever negotiation but a man doing everything a man can do — and the sūrah honours him for it while making it useless as a guarantee.

**"Unless You Are Surrounded" — the Excuse of Impossibility in a Single Clause**

*Illā an yuḥāṭa bikum*. The exception is the verse's jurisprudence: from *ḥ-w-ṭ*, to be encompassed, seized from every side, overpowered. The base translation's "unless you are surrounded" keeps the image, and our source records the commentators' gloss: a general condition meaning "unless you fall into a situation in which you cannot keep your oath." So the father's contract contains, from the beginning, a doctrine of impossibility: no bond binds beyond capacity, and an oath is not an obligation to do what has become physically impossible. That single clause is one of the Qur'anic roots of the legal maxim that hardship lifts obligation (*"God does not burden a soul beyond its scope,"* 2:286; *"and He has not placed for you any difficulty in the religion,"* 22:78), and it is also the mark of the sort of man Yaʿqūb is here: he is not trying to trap his sons into a perjury, he is trying to convert their intention into the strongest instrument available, while leaving an exit for the case where the world overpowers them. The sūrah will, in fact, use that exit: when Benjamin cannot be returned, the brothers do not flee Egypt but stand their ground and beg (12:78-80), and the father's own verdict on such cases is stated in advance at 12:83: "O my sons, go and inquire about Yūsuf and his brother, and do not despair of God's *rawḥ*."

**"God Is Guardian over What We Say"**

*Qāla llāhu ʿalā mā naqūlu wakīl*. When the pledge is given, the father does not count it as security; he names God as the one who stands between the two parties' words and their truth. *Wakīl* is the one entrusted with an affair — the agent, the guarantor, the one on whom a matter is laid — and the Qur'an uses it both of human agency (a guarantor of a debt) and, in the Book's most repeated form, of God alone: *"and put your trust in God (tawakkal ʿalā llāh), and God is sufficient as a Guardian"* (33:3; cf. 4:81; 39:62, "He is the Disposer of all things"). The verse's phrasing is a *dhikr* formula that the Prophet ﷺ's own words made proverbial in Islamic memory: *"God is sufficient for us, and the best Guardian (ḥasbunā llāhu wa-niʿma al-wakīl)"* — the words of Ibrāhīm when he was thrown into the fire, and of Muḥammad ﷺ when the people gathered against him, as Bukhārī records in the commentary on *"Those to whom people said: 'the men have gathered against you, so fear them' — and it increased them in faith, and they said: 'God is sufficient for us, and the best Guardian'"* (3:173). The parallel is more than decorative: in both cases the sentence is spoken at the end of a negotiation with powerful people, by someone who has just extracted the maximum assurance and then declines to rely on it.

There is also a small structural rhyme in the sūrah. The father asks his sons for a *mawthāq* and installs God as *wakīl* over the words (12:66); the herald at the granary, a few verses later, offers a *kafīl* — a human guarantor — for a camel-load of grain, "I shall be its guarantor (wa-anā bihi kafīl)" (12:72). The two guarantees stand in the same sequence, one given to a father about a person and one given by an official about a vessel, and the sūrah's point is that only the first kind of bond is ultimately honoured, because only there did someone invoke the One who hears it.

**The Verse and the Ethics of Guarantees**

Islamic legal thought built a good part of its law of oaths, surety and *ḍamān* out of verses like this one, and the ethic it extracts is not simply "make people swear." The father's conduct models three things a promise should do: it should define the obligation exactly ("you will bring him back to me"), it should state the excusing condition ("unless you are surrounded"), and it should place the ultimate reliance somewhere other than the promisor's sincerity ("God is Guardian over what we say"). The first two are the craft of a contract; the third is the craft of a believer. Yaʿqūb has learned from the loss of one son that the strength of a guarantee is not in the man who gives it, and the sūrah approves his lesson without granting him protection from its consequences: the pledged journey ends in an accusation of theft, and the father's own words about God's guardianship are proved right in the only way this chapter ever proves anything — by the long sequence in which nothing is lost that matters.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:67

> **67** *And he said, “O my sons! Enter not by one gate, but enter by separate gates. Yet, I cannot avail you aught against God. Judgment belongs to God alone. I trust in Him; and let those who trust, trust in Him.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Father's Precaution, in the Language of a Command**

*Wa-qāla yā banīya lā tadkhulū min bābin wāḥidin wadkhulū min abwābin mutafarriqīn*. The sentence is addressed as an imperative and framed as a command from a prophet-father, and yet the next verse will say of it that it "availed them nothing against God." That tension — an order that changes nothing, given by a man who knows it will change nothing — is the verse's subject. Yaʿqūb is not prescribing a law; he is doing what a parent does when he cannot prevent a journey: he edits its details. The sūrah's treatment of the advice is entirely respectful. It records the instruction, records that they followed it (12:68: "and when they entered from where their father commanded them"), and states the effect in a clause that is one of the Qur'an's most humane observations: it was not a protection, "but it was a need in the soul of Yaʿqūb that was fulfilled" (12:68). A father's anxiety, taken seriously and acted upon, has real value even when it is causally inert; the sūrah knows the difference between what protects a family and what holds a family together.

The instruction itself is a piece of practical wisdom about conspicuousness: eleven well-built young men arriving together at a city gate during a famine is a single fact that a crowd can notice, remember and resent. *Abwāb* — gates — is the plural of *bāb*, and Egypt's cities, in the commentators' imagination, have several; *mutafarriqīn* (scattered, dispersed) marks the difference between a procession and a queue. The sūrah's own moral vocabulary elsewhere uses *tafarruq* of the fragmentation of a community ("hold fast, all of you, to God's rope and do not split (wa-lā tafarraqū)," 3:103; 42:14), and there is a bitter appropriateness in a father who must send his sons apart to keep them safe: the house that could not stay undivided in affection (12:8: "Yūsuf and his brother are more beloved to our father than we") now cannot walk through a gate together.

**"Lest the Eye": the Commentators' Two Explanations**

Our source records the classical reading that Yaʿqūb was guarding them from the evil eye (*al-ʿayn*), since they were strikingly handsome and the eye of envy attaches to what is conspicuous; and the same note records that other commentators decline the supernatural reading altogether and take the verse as simple security advice, the brothers by now recognised in Egypt and liable to attract hostility or jealousy if they entered as one group. The two readings are not far apart, because the tradition's understanding of the evil eye is precisely a social phenomenon given metaphysical weight: envy looks, and looking harms. The prophetic texts on the subject are unambiguous that the harm is real — "the eye is real (al-ʿaynu ḥaqq)," and the remedy taught in the Sunnah is not defensive magic but the Qur'an's own words of refuge and the blessing spoken on what is admired:

> *"Say: I seek refuge with the Lord of the daybreak, from the evil of what He has created, and from the evil of the darkness when it overspreads, and from the evil of the blowers in the knots, and from the evil of an envier when he envies."* (Qur'an 113:1-5)

The Prophet ﷺ used to seek refuge for al-Ḥasan and al-Ḥusayn with these and similar words, "and from the evil of every striking eye (ʿaynin lāmma)," as Bukhārī records in the book of the prophets; and the legal tradition's general counsel — to conceal what is blessed until it is thanked for, to speak well of what one admires, and to avoid describing one's blessings before the envious — is built on such texts. What is striking about 12:67, from the reader's point of view, is that a prophet's son is warned about the eye of envy by his own father, in a household whose entire calamity began with envy (12:8-9, "kill Yūsuf or cast him into a land"). Yaʿqūb knows his family's disease; the verse is his attempt at quarantine.

**"I Cannot Avail You Anything against God" — and the Verse the Son Preached in Prison**

*Wa-mā ughnu ʿankum mina llāhi min shayʾ*. The verb is from *ghinā*, self-sufficiency: "I am of no use to you as against God" — the disclaimer a believer must attach to every precaution, stated by the Prophet's ﷺ own commission: *"Say: I possess no benefit or harm for myself, except as God wills; and if I knew the unseen, I would have multiplied the good, and no evil would have touched me"* (7:188; cf. Hud's confidence: *"there is no living creature but He grasps it by the forelock,"* 11:56). Then follows the sentence that gives the verse its place in the chapter's architecture:

> *"Judgement belongs to none but God. On Him I place my trust; and let those who place their trust place their trust on Him alone (ʿalayhi tawakkaltu wa-ʿalayhi falyatawakkali-l-mutawakkilūn)."* (Qur'an 12:67)

The phrase *in al-ḥukmu illā lillāh* has occurred once before in this sūrah, in the mouth of Yūsuf, addressing two prisoners about the religion of Egypt (12:40). The father and the son, separated by twenty years and by a border, speak the same sentence in the same words. That is not a coincidence of diction but the sūrah's way of showing that the household of Abraham has one theology across its distance: Yaʿqūb says it as he releases a son into a famine, Yūsuf says it as he rejects a city's gods, and the chapter's narrator has already said that both of them were taught (12:6, 12:68: "he possessed knowledge because of what We taught him"). The repetition is also the verse's answer to any reader who thinks the sermon in prison was a display of Yūsuf's superior spirituality: his father preaches exactly the same thing, in the middle of an ordinary family negotiation, and does so with the same mixture of action and resignation — "enter separately" and "judgement is God's."

**Trust as a Verb with an Object**

The last clause shifts from first person to an imperative addressed to everyone: *wa-ʿalayhi falyatawakkal al-mutawakkilūn* — and the rhetoricians hear in the fronted preposition and the repeated name an exclusivity: "on Him — and on Him alone — let those who trust trust." The verse is thus one of the Qur'an's definition-texts on *tawakkul*, read alongside "and whoever trusts in God, He is sufficient for him" (65:3) and the Prophet's ﷺ saying that if people "trusted in God with true trust (law tawakkalūna ʿalā llāhi ḥaqqa tawakkulihi), He would provide for them as He provides for the bird that goes out at dawn hungry and comes back full" (al-Tirmidhī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr). The sūrah's context shows what the trust is not: it is not the absence of planning. Yaʿqūb takes every precaution in the chapter — the gates, the pledge, the condition of force majeure (12:66) — and then locates his confidence entirely outside them. The verse's practical form, in Muslim ethics, is the two-handed rule "tie her and trust" (used above at 12:47): the precaution is the obedience, the trust is the theology, and confusing them is the error of both the fatalist and the control freak. The chapter will prove the point in its own way: the sons enter by separate gates, and lose Benjamin anyway; and the father, told of it, says that patience is beautiful and God is the one asked for help against what is described (12:83).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:68

> **68** *And when they entered whence their father had commanded them, it did not avail them aught against God, but it fulfilled a need in Jacob’s soul. Truly he was possessed of knowledge because of that which We taught him, but most of mankind know not.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Precaution That Was Obeyed, and Did Not Work**

*Wa-lammā darakhū min ḥaythu amarahum abūhum mā kāna yughnī ʿankum mina llāhi min shayʾ*. The verse records compliance and inefficacy in a single breath: they entered exactly as their father ordered — scattered through separate gates — and it availed them nothing against God. The sūrah has already stated the theory in the father's own words (12:67: "I cannot avail you anything against God"), and now it performs the demonstration, before the reader, of the same proposition. Nothing in the chapter is more careful than this about the status of means: taking precautions is obedience, and precautions do not bind the One who decrees. The classical theologians built their discussion of *asbāb* (causes) on verses like this one — the cause is real, willed, and to be pursued, and yet "you do not will except that God wills" (76:30; cf. 81:29) — and the practical form the discussion takes is exactly Yaʿqūb's: enter by separate gates, and know that the gates will not save you.

Note the sūrah's verb: *mā kāna yughnī*, "it was not of a use" — from *ghinā*, self-sufficiency, the same root as the father's disclaimer in 12:67. The means "avail" only in the sense that they make a person not needful of blaming himself. They have no power over the outcome; the sūrah declines to say even that they pleased God, and says instead what they did: they satisfied a need in a heart.

**"But It Was a Need in the Soul of Yaʿqūb That Was Fulfilled"**

*Wa-lākinna ḥājatan fī nafsi Yaʿqūba quḍiyat*. This is one of the Book's tenderest clauses. The precaution's function was psychological and relational: a father's anxious requirement, met by his sons, and thereby settled — "a need in the soul," not a change in the decree. Our source records al-Rāzī's gloss that the advice and the sons' acceptance of it served to assuage Yaʿqūb's fear of losing his children. The word for fulfilment is *quḍiyat*, from *qaḍāʾ*, the same verb used of a divine decree ("your Lord has decreed," 17:23) and of Yūsuf's verdict on the two prisoners' dreams ("the matter you asked about is decreed," 12:41). The sūrah's diction is doing a quiet work here: a father's small need is "settled" with the verb reserved for what God executes. That is not flattery of the patriarch; it is the chapter's theology of ordinary love — a man's worry about his sons, when it is honoured by them, is an affair God completes, even though it accomplishes nothing.

Read against 12:68's sequel, the clause also excuses the father in advance. When Benjamin is lost, the brothers will come back and the family will face the question of whose fault it was; the sūrah has already told the reader that the father's instructions were obeyed and that the gates were never the protection. It is the chapter's way of refusing to let the story turn into a blame contest, in a household whose whole history is one.

**"He Had Knowledge Because We Taught Him" — the Father as a Taught Man**

*Wa-innahu lidhū ʿilmin mā ʿallamnāhu*. This is the verse's theological pivot and the reason the sūrah interrupts its own plot to say it. Yaʿqūb's conduct in this episode — his refusal of easy assurance, his demand for a pledge, his precautions, his transfer of the affair to God, and his refusal to despair (12:83, 12:87) — is not temperament or cleverness but *ʿilm*, and the ʿilm is given: "that which We taught him." The sūrah's verb of teaching has run through the whole chapter as the mark of the elect: God teaches Yūsuf the interpretation of events (12:6, 12:21) and the prisoners' dreams (12:37: "that is of what my Lord taught me"), and here the same verb is turned on the old man in Canaan. The sūrah's doctrine of prophetic knowledge, in other words, covers the father as well as the son, and it is taught in both cases. Al-Zamakhsharī's comment on the clause, in the register of the chapter's other notes, is that it removes any suggestion that Yaʿqūb acted on mere conjecture about the gates: he knew, by what he had been given, both that means must be taken and that means do not save.

The same teaching is the ground of the father's uncanny accuracy about Yūsuf: "I know from God what you do not know" (12:86), said when everyone else in the family has conceded the boy is dead. A sūrah that has insisted so heavily on *taʿlīm* as the source of the prophet's competence (against Qārūn's "I was given this because of knowledge I have," 28:78) ends by attributing to God the knowledge that made a father do the right thing at the wrong time.

**"But Most People Do Not Know" — the Chapter's Refrain**

*Wa-lākinna akthara al-nāsi lā yaʿlamūn*. The fourth occurrence of the formula in this sūrah (12:21, 40, 68, 103), and the one most pointedly placed: right after the verse that says the precaution did not work. Its sense is that most people cannot hold the two halves together. Either they trust the means and then conclude that God is absent when the means fail — which is the reasoning of the brothers, who protest their innocence to the criers (12:73) and, when the cup is found, explain the loss by invoking an old family history: "if he has stolen, a brother of his stole before" (12:77) — or they despise the means as useless and so make themselves careless, which is not a form of trust but of negligence. The people who know, in the sūrah's vocabulary, are the ones who take the precaution and locate the outcome elsewhere: "and whoever trusts in God, He is sufficient for him" (65:3).

The final practical lesson is in the verse's sequence, which a reader in any anxious situation can use as an order of operations: advise what is in your power (12:67), accept that obedience from those you send, do not imagine it will save them (12:68a), let the giving-way of your own fear be enough (12:68b), and remember that the knowledge which ordered the whole thing is a taught thing, not a native wit (12:68c). Yaʿqūb will be allowed one more act of this kind of knowledge: when the shirt of Benjamin is brought back stained with false blood, he will say, in the sūrah's most quoted line about grief, "your souls have embellished an affair for you; so patience is beautiful (fa-ṣabrun jamīl) and God is the one asked for help against what you describe" (12:18) — and then, twenty verses later, at the news of the second loss, the same words with one addition: "perhaps God will bring them all to me" (12:83). The precaution is finished; the hope is not.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:69

> **69** *And when they entered upon Joseph, he drew his brother close to himself and said, “Truly I am your brother; so be not distressed on account of that which they used to do.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verb of Shelter, Not of Recognition**

*Wa-lammā dakhala ʿalā Yūsufa āwā ilayhi akhāhu*. The sūrah's verb is *āwā*, from the root that gives *maʾwā*, a refuge or sheltering place (the noun *maʾwā* is the Book's word for a destination that receives people: "their lodging is Hell" (4:97), "the Fire is the lodging" (79:39), and, for the one who feared his Lord, "the Garden is the lodging" (79:41); the verb's ordinary sense is "to take in, to give lodging"). The word's field has been the sūrah's moral vocabulary from the start: Potiphar orders his wife "make his lodging beautiful" (*akrimī mathwāhu*, 12:21), Yūsuf names his chastity by it ("he has made my lodging beautiful," 12:23), and he will describe his whole career to the king by it ("I give full measure and I am the best of hosts," 12:59). Now the first thing the exalted man does with his own full brother, after twenty years and in front of ten strangers, is to give him lodging: *āwā ilayhi*, "he took him into his own shelter." The recognition is not described as an embrace or a tear; it is described as an act of quartering, which is how this chapter has been teaching what love consists in — a place, made, for a person.

Note also the shift of verb between 12:58, 12:68 and this verse. The brothers "came upon" (*darakhū*) the minister; here they "entered upon" (*dakhala ʿalā*) Yūsuf. The sūrah uses *d-r-k* for arrival and *d-kh-l* for the audience; the change marks the moment the family stops being a trading party and becomes a household again, if only for one private sentence.

**"I Am Your Brother" — the Half of the Family That Is Reunited First**

*Qāla innī akhūka*. Two words, and the sūrah says nothing about how the boy received them. Benjamin is the only brother Yūsuf ever speaks to as a brother; the other ten will be addressed throughout as a group, "you" plural, and will not be told who he is for twenty-one more verses. Our source records the tradition's account of the interview: Yūsuf informed him privately that he was his brother (Ibn Kathīr) and explained to him all that had happened (Ibn Kathīr), consoled him with the words of this verse, noting that these events were in the past and according to God's will (al-Baghawī), and asked him not to mention the meeting to the others (Ibn Kathīr). Our source also records the disagreement about the plan that follows: Ibn Kathīr states that Yūsuf and Benjamin between them devised the stratagem to keep Benjamin with him, while al-Ṭabarī says the plan was not known to Benjamin. The difference matters for the reading of 12:70-76: on the first, the two brothers are accomplices in a fiction; on the second, Benjamin's horror at the cup found in his bag is genuine, and the sūrah's silence about his feelings during the search is a protection rather than an omission.

The verse's disclosure of the secret to one man, and not to ten, is the chapter's answer to the question of what to do with a family that has harmed you. He does not stage a scene. He takes aside the one who was not part of it (Genesis 43:29-31 has him weep on Benjamin's neck and then speak kindly to all of them; the sūrah gives the tears to no one, and the words to one) and tells him the fact, and nothing about the brothers except that they are to be grieved at no longer.

**"Do Not Be Distressed at What They Used to Do"**

*Fa-lā tabtahish bimā kānū yaʿmalūn*. The verb *ibtahasha* is rare and specific: to be confounded, struck senseless by distress — the state of a person who cannot assemble a reaction. It names the state of a person who cannot assemble a reaction, and it is exactly the condition of this family: the father whose eyes went white with sorrow (12:84), the father whose eyes went white with grief (12:84), the brothers who arrive at the gate with an oath on their lips ("by God, you certainly know that we came not to work corruption in the land," 12:73). Yūsuf's command to Benjamin is a command not to be stunned: do not be confounded by their conduct.

Three things are compressed in the clause. First, the act is placed in the past and made habitual: *bimā kānū yaʿmalūn*, "at what they used to do" — the sūrah's own past-habit construction, which frames the crime not as the brothers' nature but as their previous conduct, a thing that had a course and is over. Second, the wording never blames Benjamin for anything, and it does not require him to forgive, to reconcile, or to forget; it only releases him from the shock. Third, Yūsuf declines to make the boy a party to a grievance. A brother who was a child at the time is not handed the family's account of itself; he is told that what happened was what "they used to do," and that it is not his business to be destroyed by it. The same restraint will be the sūrah's final word on the matter: when the ten have confessed and God has gathered the family, Yūsuf will not recount the years but cite the providence: "my Lord has been gracious to me when He brought me out of the prison and brought you from the desert" (12:100), and the evil that followed is attributed to Satan, "a prompter of discord between me and my brothers" (12:100) — a verdict of the kind Benjamin is being told to accept now, twenty verses early.

**The Ethics of Private Truth in a Public Reconciliation**

The verse has been read in the commentaries as the sūrah's warrant for two rules that Muslim ethics takes seriously. The first is that a wrong done to a family should not be narrated to everyone at once: Yūsuf keeps the secret, arranges the conditions in which the ten can be tested and can choose to be different (12:59-76), and tells the truth only to the one who can bear it usefully. This is the same wisdom as the father's decision at the beginning of the sūrah not to recount the dream, "lest you devise a scheme against your brother" (12:5) — Yaʿqūb suppressed a future-truth to protect a boy; his son suppresses a present-truth to protect a man. The second rule is the verse's explicit content: a person who has been made a witness of wickedness is not obliged to be disturbed by it. "Do not be confounded" is not an instruction to minimise an evil; it is a refusal to let the evil have the last psychological word, which is why the sūrah's own summary of this disposition is *ṣabr* rather than numbness — Yaʿqūb's "patience is beautiful" (12:18, 83, 86), Yūsuf's "whoever fears and is patient, God does not waste the reward of the beautiful-doers" (12:90). The chapter's reunion begins, accordingly, not with a speech to the ten but with one sentence, in private, to the frightened youngest: you have a brother, and you need not be stunned.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:70

> **70** *And when he had made ready their provisions, he put the drinking cup into his brother’s saddlebag. Then a herald cried out, “O you men of the caravan! Truly you are thieves!”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Repeats an Earlier One, and Changes It**

*Wa-lammā jahhazahum bi-jihāzihim jaʿala al-siqāyata fī raḥli akhīhi*. The opening clause is word-for-word the beginning of 12:59 ("and when he had equipped them with their equipment"), and the sūrah's repetition is the reader's cue. Twice the minister fits the brothers out for the road; the first time he adds a request ("bring me a brother of yours from your father"), and the second time he adds a thing placed in a bag. Everything the chapter will do with the cup is prepared by that symmetry: Yūsuf's method is to give, and then to give again — with a difference that obliges. The reader who remembers 12:62, where the goods were put *back into their saddles* as a kindness, now finds another object put into a saddle for a purpose that is not kindness, and the sūrah wants the two acts compared: the same hand, the same sacks, one returning a debt and one planting an evidence.

The object is named *al-siqāya* — the vessel from which water is drawn, from *s-q-y*, the root of the cup-bearer (*sāqī*, 12:42) and of irrigation. It is the thing that gives drink in a land whose wealth was its river and whose famine was thirst; and in the next verse the crier's party will call it *ṣawʿ al-malik*, "the king's bowl," a word from the root of measuring and pouring out. Our source records that the commentators gloss it as the king's goblet, used "not only for drinking, but also for measuring food," and that it was said to be gold studded with jewels. Two names, then, for one object — a drinking-cup and a measuring-bowl — and the double naming is a piece of the chapter's design: the king's instrument of rationing is the instrument by which a family is detained. Provision and power are the same object in Yūsuf's Egypt, and the sūrah lets the vocabulary carry that.

**"Into His Brother's Saddlebag" — the Syntax of an Ambiguous Agent**

The verb *jaʿala* has no stated subject in the verse, and Arabic's readers have taken it two ways. The received translation supplies Yūsuf ("he put the drinking cup"), which the context requires, since it was he who equipped them; and the sūrah's own sequel supplies God: "thus did We devise a scheme for Yūsuf" (12:76). That is not an inconsistency but the chapter's way of stating the double agency it has asserted since 12:13, when the brothers' plot was over-written by God's plan for a patient boy. In the sūrah's world a prophet's ruse is also a providence, and the syntax of the sentence enacts the claim. Our source records the note's theological edge: the placing of the cup "was based on divine revelation, because the ultimate goal of the plot was for God to show His solicitude toward Joseph's brothers." The device, in other words, is not Yūsuf's revenge but the chapter's last act of the *kayd* whose earlier instalments were all divine too: "We turned away from him their scheming" (12:34) and "We devised for Yūsuf" (12:76).

As for *akhīhi* — "his brother," not "Benjamin," whose name the sūrah never utters — the phrasing keeps the reader's sympathy where the plot needs it. The object is put with *his brother*, the one Yūsuf took into his shelter one verse earlier, and the sūrah does not comment on the unfairness of that: Benjamin will be frightened (12:69: "do not be confounded"), and the ordeal will end with him kept close and the family reunited. The chapter's willingness to let an innocent man be briefly terrified in order to bring eleven to a moment of honesty is one of its hardest features, and its logic is the logic of 12:76's verdict: God raises whom He wills in degrees, "and above every possessor of knowledge is a knower."

**"Then a Herald Cried Out" — the Accusation as Public Theatre**

*Thumma adhdhana muʾadhdhinu ayyuhā al-ʿīru innakum la-sāriqūn*. The verb *adhdhana*, to make something heard, is the same root from which Arabic took the word for the call to prayer, and its object here is a charge of theft; the sūrah's ear is not innocent in choosing it. The *ʿīr* is the caravan — specifically the camels and the men who drive them, a word the Qur'an uses nowhere else — and the accusation is addressed to all of them, not to the accused. A crier who names the thief would end the matter; a crier who charges a company forces the company to engage, to turn and ask "what is it that you have lost?" (12:71), and thus to enter the procedure by which the bowl will be "found." This is Yūsuf's method reduced to a scene: he creates a public process the brothers choose to join, in which they themselves will pronounce the law under which his brother can be kept.

The brothers' response is dignity rather than panic: they turn and ask what is missing (12:71), and then swear by God that they came "not to work corruption in the land, and we are not thieves" (12:73). Our source's notes record that the two parties' speeches are being reported with care: the question "what are you missing?" is theirs, while the declaration of the camel-load reward belongs to the crier (several commentators), and the phrase "I shall be its guarantor" means that whoever produces the bowl gets the load (al-Ṭabarī). The exchange, in other words, is a contract: a reward offered, an oath sworn, a penalty proposed by the accused themselves (12:75). No one is robbed, no one is dragged; the whole affair proceeds as a market quarrel conducted under law.

**A Prophet's Ruse: the Question the Verse Provokes**

The scene has obliged Muslim ethicists to be precise about the difference between deception and lying. The means are undeniably deceptive — a bowl taken, a bag packed, a charge broadcast — yet the sūrah records no wrongdoing and attributes the plan to God, while the outcome is a family that cannot afterwards claim to have been defrauded: what was done to them was done under *their* own stated penalty, "thus do we recompense the wrongdoers" (12:75), and under *their* own father's custom, which our source records: whoever was caught stealing was handed over to the one he stole from, and the same note says that Yaʿqūb's household rule made Benjamin appear culpable in a way that the king's law (beating, then double restitution — al-Rāzī) would not have done. The sūrah is thus explicit that no false judgment was imposed on anyone: the law applied was the one the brothers themselves proposed. To this the tradition adds the general rule by which the incident is read: in the prophetic saying recorded in the ḥadīth collections on Ibrāhīm's "she is my sister," the prophets' indirections (*maʿāriḍ*) are treated as speech whose literal form misleads while its meaning is true, and the ethicists' summary — "in indirections there is room to avoid lying" — is adduced from this kind of case. Modern readers, with their sting operations and their decoys, recognise the shape of the argument: deception whose end is to make a person incriminate himself under a law he has himself endorsed, and which is disclosed afterwards. The sūrah's own verdict on the whole affair is not "he lied" but "We devised," and the devising is what reunited the eleven stars, the sun and the moon (12:4), many years after the boy who saw it was thrown into a dry well.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:71

> **71** *Turning toward them, they said, “What are you missing?”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Composure of the Unafraid**

*Qālū mādhā tafqidūn*. The first thing the sūrah records after the charge of theft is not a denial but a question, and the question asks after the other party's loss. The received translation's "turning toward them" sets the physical scene: the caravan halts, faces the accusers, and asks for particulars. Men who had stolen would have run, bribed, or blustered; the brothers do the thing that only the innocent can afford to do, which is to make the claimant specify what is claimed. This is the sūrah's way of proving their innocence of *this* charge while the reader keeps in mind the older one: they were not thieves in Egypt, and they were, in the only sense the chapter cares about, robbers of a childhood — the accusation they cannot answer, and the one they never dream of answering.

The verb *faqada* (to lose something, to be without it) governs the whole exchange. The brothers ask "what is it you have lost?" and the answer comes back as a thing with a price and a guarantor (12:72), and then the brothers themselves propose the penalty for the loss (12:74-75). The sūrah is careful to record that each step is spoken by a party, in order: the demand for specification, the statement of claim, the offer of reward, the definition of the sanction. Nothing is imposed on them; a prophet's plan is conducted as a negotiation among men who think they are negotiating.

**A Question of Law, Not of Emotion**

In the terms of Islamic jurisprudence, the brothers are insisting on the claimant's duty. A plaintiff who says "you stole" must say what is missing, of whom, and when — the classical rules of *daʿwā* require the claim to be particularised before any oath, search, or penalty is entertained, and the sūrah's scene is quoted by the jurists as one of its scriptural models. Their question is therefore also a piece of self-defence: if a bowl is missing, it may have been mislaid, borrowed, or counted wrongly, and "thieves" was the crier's word, not a verdict. By asking what is lost, they convert an insult into a case.

It is worth noticing that they ask, rather than protest their innocence first. The order of their speech is a moral observation the sūrah handles without comment: honest people reach for facts before reaching for oaths. In the next verse they will appeal to God's name ("by God, you certainly know…," 12:73), and their oath is the second move, not the first; they want the object described before they swear they did not touch it. Compare the household's behaviour when Yūsuf was accused: there was no question of specification, no offer of a search of the accusers' own rooms, and a "penalty" proposed on the spot by the woman whose case was already lost (12:25). The sūrah has arranged the two scenes as a contrast in procedure — an accusation made in a private house with doors locked (12:23-25), and an accusation made in the open with the accused permitted to ask what has gone missing (12:70-71).

**"Turning toward them": the Caravan as a Body with Standing**

The charge was addressed to the whole company — "O you caravan! You are thieves!" (12:70) — and it is the whole company that turns and answers. The sūrah's phrasing keeps the collective voice: they speak as one body in a public transaction, which is why the later negotiation has to be about one member's person: the accusers cannot take the caravan, so they must find an individual, and the finding is what God's device arranges (12:76). The brothers' confidence is in their number and their openness; they have nothing to hide and eleven pairs of sacks to offer.

There is a further irony in the sūrah's placement of the question here, a single line between the accusation and the answer, that the chapter's audience would have felt. The brothers had come to Egypt twice already, and each time the encounter had been about a measure, a payment and a condition (12:58-65); their first instinct, when charged, is to treat the charge as another condition to be clarified. They have learned, in other words, to behave like honest traders in a country that is running a test on them — and the sūrah's point is not that they are naive but that they are, by this stage, capable of being candid. The whole second journey to Egypt exists to produce exactly this: men who turn and ask a question instead of running, who will then, when the bowl is found, offer themselves in their brother's place (12:78). That is the moral trajectory the chapter is measuring, verse by verse, in the language of a market dispute.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:72

> **72** *They said, “We are missing the goblet of the king.” “For whosoever brings it forth, there shall be a camel-load. I shall be its guarantor.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Claim, Stated in Four Words**

*Qālū nafqidu ṣawʿa al-maliki*. The answer to the brothers' question is a statement of loss with an owner attached: "we have lost the king's bowl." The sūrah compresses the charge's whole legal content into that: a specific object, belonging to a specific person, in the custody of the party making the demand. The word *ṣawʿ* names a vessel used for pouring and measuring, and our source records the commentators' gloss that the king's goblet "was used by him not only for drinking, but also for measuring food" (al-Ṭabarī), and that it was said to be gold and set with jewels. The two functions are the point of the verse's machinery: the thing that measures out grain is missing, in a country where the measure is the only thing anyone can live on. An accusation about a measuring-cup, in a famine, is an accusation about the public good, and that is why the penalty offered for its recovery is public too.

Then the reward, and then the surety: *wa-man jāʾa bihi ḥimlu baʿīrin wa-anā bihi zaʿīm*. A camel-load — the unit in which this sūrah counts wealth (the brothers had spoken of "an extra camel-load's measure," 12:65) — and a guarantor for it.

**Who Speaks: the Sūrah's Rapid Change of Voice**

Notice that the verse's three sentences are not all spoken by the same mouth. Our source records the classical division of the lines: the words "we are missing the king's goblet" are put on the lips of Joseph's own companions, while the proclamation of the camel-load — and, on the reading preserved in the note, the guarantee that goes with it — belongs to the herald. The sūrah does not mark the change with "he said"; it lets the pronouns and the register carry it, and the reader has to keep the roles apart: the claim, the bounty, the bond. This is one of several places where the chapter's brevity has produced a genuine exegetical question — the same problem appears at 12:52 (who says "this is that he may know…," our source records both readings) and at 12:53 (the majority say the words are Yūsuf's, others give them to Zulaykhā). The commentators' habit of specifying which speaker says which half is not pedantry; it is the discipline the text's own style demands.

**A Reward and a Guarantee: Two Contracts in a Sentence**

Muslim jurists read this verse as scriptural warrant for two named transactions. The first is *juʿl* — a public offer of reward for a specified service, "for whoever brings it, a camel-load": an offer made to no one in particular, binding on the offeror once someone performs the act, and treated in the contract books under its own chapter because it is the only ordinary way to buy information. The second is *kafāla* or *ḍamān* — suretyship: the words "I am its guarantor (zaʿīm)" are cited for the legitimacy of a person binding himself to produce a thing or a debt owed by another. Both institutions are discussed in the *fuqahāʾ*'s chapters with our verse as one of their proofs, and both are here in service of a single device: the reward makes the bowl findable, and the guarantee makes the reward believable.

The theological use the sūrah itself makes of the vocabulary of surety is worth noting, because the chapter has already set up the contrast. Yaʿqūb refused his sons' assurance and demanded a pledge "from God," and when it was given he said "God is Guardian (wakīl) over what we say" (12:66); here the king's crier offers himself as *zaʿīm* for a camel-load. The chapter therefore has two guarantors in the same journey — one human and one divine — and the plot runs on the difference: the human bond is kept to the letter (the brothers search and accept the penalty), while the human promise that actually mattered (their "we shall surely be his keepers," 12:63) is broken, and only God's keeping holds. That is the verse's quiet contribution to the sūrah's theology of trust: you may accept sureties for cargo; you should not stake your children on them.

**The Stakes of a Camel-Load**

Finally, the verse's arithmetic is part of the chapter's realism. A camel-load of grain in the second year of a seven-year famine is not a finder's fee but a season of survival for a household; and the sūrah makes the reward enormous on purpose, so that the reader understands why an entire caravan would submit to having its bags searched on the spot. Nobody in this scene is being heroic: the accusers want their instrument back and pay for it, and the accused want the reward and offer themselves to the test. That is what makes the device work, and it is also what makes the later self-accusation credible: when the eldest brother finally speaks among themselves, he does not blame the minister but them — "do you not know that your father took from you a solemn pledge in God's name, and how you failed with regard to Yūsuf before?" (12:80) — and he then refuses to leave the land until his father permits it or God judges for him — the same verse (12:80), with the words "and He is the best of those who judge". Men who bargain openly for a camel-load of grain in a famine are, the sūrah shows, the same men who at last measure their own conduct against a covenant. The chapter's moral psychology never separates economics from character, and 12:72 is where the two are welded together most tightly: the reward offered in a famine is the measure of what a family is being asked to risk, and the sūrah has just stated its price in the only unit its audience trusted.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:73

> **73** *They said, “By God, you certainly know that we came not to work corruption in the land, and we are not thieves.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Oath, an Appeal to Knowledge, and Two Denials**

*Qālū wallāhi laqad ʿalimtum mā jiʾnā li-nuffida fī al-arḍi wa-mā kunnā sāriqīn*. The sentence has four parts, and their order is a study in how honest men argue: they swear, then they appeal to what the other side already knows, then they deny a large thing (corruption in the land), then they deny the small one actually charged (theft). The oath is placed before the content, not after it, and the appeal to shared knowledge — *laqad ʿalimtum*, "you have certainly known" — is the sūrah's way of showing that the brothers are not improvising a defence but standing on a record: they have come twice, paid, and been given full measure (12:59, 65). They are, in other words, relying on their conduct. It is the same reliance the chapter has been building since 12:22, "thus do We recompense the beautiful-doers," and the same reliance Yūsuf himself used when he asked the king to reopen the case of the women (12:50).

The verb of their denial, *afsād* — corruption, spoilage, damage done to the order of a place — is the Qur'an's word for the sins that break a society, and its formula "not to work corruption in the land" is the language of revelation rather than of a market quarrel (2:11-12, *"and when it is said to them, do not work corruption in the land, they say: only we are peacemakers"*; 2:205; 7:56; 26:151). The brothers are, without knowing it, borrowing the idiom in which the Qur'an phrases the innocence of prophets, and the sūrah lets its accused speak in the vocabulary of the innocent while a different charge stands against them. The effect is one of the chapter's quietest ironies: they swear by God that they came to do no corruption in Egypt, and the reader knows that they once committed a corruption in Canaan which the sūrah does not shrink from naming either — "do not kill Yūsuf" (12:11), and, at the end, "do you know what you did to Yūsuf and his brother, when you were unaware?" (12:89).

**"We Were Not Thieves" — the Grammar of Character**

*Wa-mā kunnā sāriqīn*. The past of the verb of being with a participle — "we were not, we were never of the character of thieves" — claims more than innocence of this act: it claims a history that makes the act out of the question. The sūrah elsewhere uses precisely this construction to describe people's settled dispositions, and Yūsuf has just used it for the brothers in the kindest possible form: "do not be confounded at what they *used to do*" (12:69: *bimā kānū yaʿmalūn*) — a past habit, a thing that had a course and is over. Their own sentence is the mirror image: what we *were*, we are not.

But the chapter's answer to the claim is the whole plot. They were not thieves in Egypt. They took a brother from his father's hand and sold him for a mean price (12:19-20); that is the theft the sūrah never lets them mention about themselves, and they did it with a story so dressed up that even the father's grief was asked to accept it (12:16-17). That is why 12:73 is read alongside 12:18, where Yaʿqūb, handed the shirt with its false blood, replies: *"Nay, your souls have seduced you in this matter. Beautiful patience! And God is the One Whose help is sought against that which you describe."* He will say the same kind of thing again when the pledge has failed him: "Nay, your souls have seduced you in this matter. Beautiful patience!" (12:83), with the cry *yā-asafā ʿalā Yūsuf* at 12:84 and the hope "perhaps God will bring them all to me" at 12:83, and the sūrah's design is to let a father meet two losses with one sentence. What is remarkable about the brothers, in 12:73, is that their self-presentation is sincere and narrow: they know they did not steal a bowl. The sūrah never says otherwise. It simply keeps the wider theft in view, and lets the reader feel the disproportion between what men swear to and what they have done.

**The Appeal to Reputation as the Sūrah's Test**

There is a further piece of the chapter's moral logic here. The brothers' argument is "you know us," and the sūrah has already shown what public knowledge is worth: the women of the city knew Yūsuf's excellence and still said the worst of him (12:30); Potiphar's household saw the evidence and jailed him anyway (12:35); the king's notables knew they could not interpret and called the dream a jumble (12:44). Reputation, in this chapter, is not a court of appeal but a mob with better information than it uses. Yūsuf, by contrast, has twice now been described as someone whose character is *seen*: "we see you among the beautiful-doers" (12:36) from the two prisoners, "truly we see you among the beautiful-doers" (12:78) from the brothers, in the very next verse. And yet — the sūrah's point — even that seeing is not enough: they can look at the man who gives full measure, call him a muḥsin, and then, in the next breath, allege that his mother's son is a thief (12:77). The chain of these two "we see you" statements, one in a prison and one at a granary, is the chapter's judgment on how people treat the good: they recognise them, rely on them, and accuse them of whatever is convenient, and they are, in the sūrah's eyes, the worse for the convenience rather than the worse for the recognition.

**Practical Sentences**

Two rules are worth extracting. First, an oath without particulars is a weak defence: the brothers swear and assert character, and it is only when they are asked "what is the penalty if you are lying?" (12:74) that they supply the test by which innocence can be proved. Their instinct is to appeal; Yūsuf's is to propose a procedure. Second, the sūrah's irony is a warning about the range of one's denials: people who deny large things ("we came not to work corruption in the land") are often in danger of the small, unacknowledged ones. The chapter's own cure for this is on the father's lips, in the sentence he repeats twice and never retracts: patience is beautiful, and God is the one asked for help against what is described (12:18, 83-84).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:74

> **74** *They said, “And what will be the recompense for it if you be liars?”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Accused Ask for the Law**

*Qālū fa-mā jazāʾuhu in kuntum kādhibīn*. The verse is a single question, and it inverts the ordinary shape of an accusation. The claim has been made in public ("truly you are thieves," 12:70), the loss has been specified ("the king's goblet," 12:72), and now the accused say: and what is the penalty, if the charge turns out to be false? They do not refuse the search; they ask what the search is for. In the sūrah's presentation this is both a piece of legal instinct and a piece of self-confidence — the instinct to make the other side commit to a rule before the facts are established, and the confidence of men who can afford to.

There is a real procedural wisdom here, and the sūrah records it as the turning-point of the scene. In the law of claims, the burden of the allegation is on the claimant, and the party accused of a private injury is entitled to know the sanction he is exposing himself to: a demand for restitution and a demand for a person's liberty are different things, and a search that begins without that distinction is not a search but a seizure. The brothers make the distinction, and the sūrah rewards them for it with the next verse, in which they get to name the penalty themselves.

**"If You Be Liars" — the Sūrah's Second *Jazāʾ***

The word *jazāʾ*, recompense or penalty, has appeared once before in the chapter in an almost identical construction, and the repetition is not accidental. Zulaykhā's household, with a torn shirt and a witness on their side, asked: *"What is the recompense for one who desires ill toward thy wife, save that he be imprisoned, or a painful punishment?"* (12:25). The form of the question is the same — *mā jazāʾu…*, a demand that a penalty be stated — and the situation is the opposite: there, the powerful party proposes the punishment in advance, and the accused man is silent; here, the accused propose that the punishment be defined before the evidence is looked for, and the minister stays silent. Yūsuf has, in other words, been on the receiving end of exactly this device, and the sūrah lets the reader notice that he now runs the same machinery from the other side, under God's direction (12:76). It is a study in how the same legal form can serve revenge or serve instruction: the first *jazāʾ* question was framed to close a case, this one is framed — and answered — to open a conscience.

Our source's note on this exchange records the substance of what follows: the brothers' answer is the custom of their own house and people, that a thief is handed over to the one from whom he stole, and the note adds that the same custom was the rule in Yaʿqūb's family — which is why Yūsuf's men could claim the cup at all without appearing to invent a law. The scene, from this angle, is not a frame-up but a jurisdictional move: the minister of Egypt is obliged to apply Egyptian law, and Egyptian law (as our source preserves in a note from al-Rāzī) punished theft with beating and double restitution — a penalty from which Benjamin would have walked free. So the accusation has to be tried under the brothers' own code, and the brothers' own code has to be volunteered. That is the sūrah's definition of Providence: not the overriding of human choice but its use.

**A Question the Reader Should Keep Asking**

The line also carries the chapter's permanent warning about the price of certainty. The brothers are so sure of their innocence that they invite the test, and the test they invite will cost them their brother; the sūrah does not say that they were wrong to accept it, and does not say they were naive. What it shows is that a fair procedure, honestly entered, can produce a result you never meant: they consented to a search of their bags because a search was the only proof their innocence needed, and the search produced a penalty that had been invented, in the very previous verse, by the men asking for it. Nothing in the exchange was corrupt, and everything in it was rigged — the rigging lying not in the rules but in the bowl already lying in a saddle (12:70). This is why the chapter's later verdict on the whole affair is a statement about knowledge rather than about fault: "You are in a worse position! And God knows best concerning that which you describe" (12:77), and "We raise in degrees whomsoever We will, and above every possessor of knowledge is a knower" (12:76). The lesson for a reader is not to fear honest procedures, but to remember that a person who is sure of himself is, in that very moment, the most available to being taught.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:75

> **75** *They said, “Its recompense will be that he in whose saddlebag it is found—he himself shall be its recompense. Thus do we recompense the wrongdoers.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Penalty That Is a Person**

*Qālū jazāʾuhu man wujida min riḥlihi fa-huwa jazāʾuhu*. The sentence is strange in Arabic and stranger still in English, and the strangeness is the meaning. Instead of saying "the penalty is so many blows, or so much silver," the brothers say: *its penalty is — whoever it is found with, he is its penalty*. The fine is a human being. A nominal equation of crime with a person belongs to a jurisprudence in which a thief who cannot pay is delivered to the party he wronged; our source records exactly this as their custom, that the thief was handed over to the one from whom he had stolen, and the note adds that this was the rule in the household of Yaʿqūb as well, which is why the king's men could demand it without appearing to invent a law for the occasion. (The parallel in Genesis, where the steward proposes that the finder "shall be my servant" and the brothers offer death and enslavement for the whole company, shows how the sūrah has trimmed the story down to the single hard point: the man in whose bag it is *is* the penalty.)

The doubling of the phrase — *jazāʾuhu* at the beginning and *fa-huwa jazāʾuhu* at the end — closes the door on negotiation. The penalty is not attached to the goods, not paid to the treasury, not commutable: it is the person, and the person is the penalty. This is the code the brothers volunteer, and the sūrah's irony is that they volunteer it in defence of an innocence they happen to possess, in a case rigged to catch a boy who possesses it absolutely. Their legal maxim will be executed against the one person in the caravan who cannot be a wrongdoer (12:69, "God is my refuge from that"), and the chapter lets them discover that in the next scene, when they beg to trade themselves for him (12:78).

**"Thus Do We Recompense the Wrongdoers"**

*Kadhalika najzī al-ẓālimīn*. The clause is a judge's summary, and the brothers speak it about themselves as if they were the administration. The sūrah has set the same formula in God's mouth twice already, and its echoes here are the point: "thus do We recompense the beautiful-doers" (12:22), and, at the end of the famine administration, "whoever is reverent and patient — surely God neglects not the reward of the virtuous" (12:90). The men of the caravan use the verb *jazāʾ* of recompense as though justice were their trade; the chapter will spend the last thirty verses proving that they are wrong about one half of the formula, since the one who actually holds power in this story does not, when the moment comes, recompense wrongdoers at all: "There is no reproach against you this day" (12:92). The verse is thus a hinge in the sūrah's theology of desert. Everything in the middle of the chapter runs on *jazāʾ* — the shirt and the price, the prison and the interpretation, the measure and the bowl — and at the end it is switched for *faḍl* and *raḥma*: "God has been gracious unto us" (12:90). What the brothers pronounce in 12:75 is the exact rule the chapter will, with enormous care, set aside.

**The Passive Verb: Where the Plot Hides**

Notice the single verb of the brothers' law: *man wujida* — "whoever it is *found*." In a sequence that has named its actors plainly, this is the only line where no agent appears. The putting was active and human — "he put the cup in his brother's saddlebag" (12:70); the finding is passive and impersonal; and the removal, when it comes, is given a verb again, now with God as its subject: "Thus did We devise a scheme for Joseph" (12:76). The sūrah's grammar therefore performs the lesson the whole scene teaches: an event that enters the world as luck, as "it was found in his bag," is the very event which, seen from the other side, is called planning. Nothing about the finding was unfair to the men who insisted on it — the rule was theirs, the search was open, the reward was announced — and yet the outcome was exactly what the deviser intended. The verse, read this way, is not about theft at all. It is a statement about how evidence works, and how a just procedure can be the instrument of a purpose larger than the parties in it.

**Two Practical Sentences**

First, the danger of pronouncing penalties for hypothetical criminals. Men are generous with the punishment of "wrongdoers" when the category is abstract — the brothers even say *thus do we recompense* with pride, as if they were describing their virtue — and the same men are quick to plead for clemency when the category includes their own (12:78, "so take one of us in his place; truly we see you as being among the virtuous"). A rule one is willing to apply only to other people has, in the sūrah's view, not yet been understood. Second, the verse's other teaching is about the law: our source's note on the following verse preserves the reason the brothers' code was needed at all — under the law of Egypt, the minister could not have detained the boy (the penalty there was beating and the payment of double the value), so God moved the brothers to supply a jurisdiction of their own. The sūrah's confidence that God can use a private law as well as a public court is, in a modern register, its permission to hope that no system, however complete, is closed to a purpose higher than its own clauses.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:76

> **76** *Then he began with their baggage, before the baggage of his brother. Then he removed it from his brother’s baggage. Thus did We devise a scheme for Joseph. Under the king’s law, he could not have taken his brother unless God willed. We raise in degrees whomsoever We will, and above every possessor of knowledge is a knower.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Search, Ordered for the Sake of the Onlookers**

*Fa-badaʾa bi-ʿiʿāatihim min qabli riḥli akhīhi thumma-ntantaʿa al-siqāyata min riḥli akhīhi*. The verse's one information-bearing word is the sequence: the eleven sacks first, the twelfth last. The reader has known since 12:70 where the bowl was, and the sūrah slows the scene down anyway, because its interest is not suspense but fairness — the fairness, that is, as it must have looked to the men of the caravan. A crier who went straight to Benjamin's saddle would have been caught; a crier who opens every bag in turn, and finds nothing in the first eleven, has made the twelfth an act of discovery in the eyes of everyone present. The brothers will still protest (12:77), but their protest will be the language of men searching for an explanation rather than of men who have found a trick.

The phrase *riḥl akhīhi* — "his brother's baggage" — comes twice in one line, and the repetition is the sūrah's way of keeping the human cost in view while it describes a clever procedure: it is his brother, and only his brother's bag is named. The verb of the third occurrence, *jaʿala* ("he put," 12:70), belongs to a man; the verb of finding here, *wujida* (12:75), belongs to no one; and the verb of extraction is now given a subject again, but the subject of the sentence immediately after it is God: "thus did We devise a scheme for Joseph." The verse's four short moves are a lesson in how the sūrah distributes agency.

**"Thus Did We Devise": the Only *Nakīdu* in the Sūrah**

*Kadhalika nakīdu li-Yūsuf*. Here, and only here in this chapter, the verb of *kayd* — devising, plotting, counter-arranging — is put in the first person plural of God. The sūrah has used the noun for every other party in the story: the women's plan, "great indeed is your plan (kaydukunna)" as Yūsuf said to them (12:28); his own prayer, "if You avert from me their plan I incline to them" (12:33); the answer that God "turned away from him their plan" (12:34); Zulaykhā's aim, "that he may know that I did not betray him in secret and that God does not guide the plan of the treacherous" (12:52); and Yūsuf's final word about the brothers, "Satan sowed discord between me and my brothers" (12:100).
