Against that chain, 12:76's claim is startling: the only devising God ascribes to Himself in the sūrah is devising on behalf of a family against their own ignorance, and the note preserved in our source makes the intention explicit — the plan was based on revelation, "because the ultimate goal of the plot was for God to show His solicitude toward Joseph's brothers." The chapter's theology of *kayd* therefore is not that God out-manoeuvres sinners in anger but that He out-manoeuvres a whole family in order to bring them to the point where they can be forgiven and fed. What is "great" about the women's plan (12:28) is its capacity to destroy an innocent man; what is perfect about God's plan here is its capacity to save guilty ones without breaking the rules they themselves supplied.

**"Under the King's Law": a Prophet's Jurisdiction and Its Limits**

*Mā kāna liyaʾkhudha akhāhu fī dīni al-maliki illā an yashāʾa llāh*. The word *dīn* here is law rather than religion in the narrow sense: the code, custom and jurisdiction of the king's court, under which Yūsuf sat as a judge of Egypt and an administrator of its grain. The sūrah's point is blunt — as a magistrate of Egypt, he had no authority to enslave a free man for a theft the state's law did not punish that way, and our source preserves the commentators' specification: the king's law required beating and payment of double the value, which would have let Benjamin go (al-Rāzī). Hence the sentence's clause "unless God willed": what God willed was precisely the brothers' answer in 12:75, their own ancestral law of handing the thief over. The mechanism is worth pausing on: the prophet does not suspend the state's law, does not use his office to invent a penalty, and does not take the boy by force. He accepts the jurisdiction he lives under, and God arranges that the accusers propose a different one — one the accused themselves call just.

This gives 12:76 its place in the sūrah's long argument about *ḥukm*, judgment. The chapter has already put the sentence "judgment belongs to God alone" in the mouth of Yūsuf in prison (12:40) and of Yaʿqūb as he sent his sons on this very journey (12:67). Here the sentence is demonstrated rather than preached: a man of God, seated at the top of a pagan bureaucracy, finds his power bounded by statute, and discovers that the wider jurisdiction he needs must come from God's guidance of other people's choices. That is the practical content of the phrase *tawakkul* as the chapter has used it — not the abolition of means but the certainty that the means are held (12:66-67).

**Degrees of Knowledge, and the Knower Above**

*Na-rfaʿu darajāt(in) man nashāʾu wa-fawqa kulli dhī ʿilmin ʿalīm*. The first half belongs, in the Qur'an's usage, to elevation in prophethood and in nearness to God; the same clause — "We raise in degrees whomsoever We will" — is said elsewhere of Ibrāhīm and his progeny (6:83), and the Qur'an promises the believers "those of you who believe and are given knowledge will be raised in degrees" (58:11). The sūrah has already named the commodity in which Yūsuf is raised: "when he reached his maturity, We gave him wisdom and knowledge (*ḥukman wa-ʿilman*)" (12:22), and it has said of his father's insight that he possessed it because he was taught (12:68). Rank in this chapter is not genealogy and not office — it is a knowledge that has been given, and can therefore be given to somebody else.

The second half, "above every possessor of knowledge is a knower," is the sūrah's check on the whole scene. Two readings have been preserved in the commentaries: each man of knowledge has above him a man who knows more, until the series ends in God; or, more simply, the omniscient One stands above every claimant to knowledge. Either way the target is the certainty the brothers displayed — "you certainly know that we came not to work corruption in the land" (12:73) — and the verse's sequel states the same thing as a verdict: "God knows best concerning that which you describe" (12:77). It also closes the loop the sūrah has been describing since 12:21, where the refrain began: "but most of mankind do not know" (also 12:40, 12:68, and, at the chapter's end, 12:103). The cleverness of the search — eleven bags, then the twelfth — is thus bracketed by a statement about knowledge: the deviser's intelligence is real, it is taught, and it is exceeded. Yūsuf, who has just orchestrated the most elaborate piece of psychological management in the chapter, is told in the same breath that he is not the last word.

**What the Verse Asks of Its Readers**

Read as instruction, 12:76 asks for two habits at once. The first is candour about one's own ingenuity: the verse concedes that the plan was necessary and that it worked, and it declines to call it anything but God's — a prophet's cleverness is named *nikād* and attributed upward, and no moral is drawn about the trick. Modern readers who run any kind of investigation, negotiation, or intervention recognise the situation: the outcome is produced by a procedure one has designed, and the design's success is entirely dependent on the other party's free and honest cooperation, which one cannot manufacture. The second habit is the humility the last clause enforces on anyone who claims to know a case: eleven sacks opened, nothing found, and the twelfth is where the truth lies; and the person who knows which sack it was is not present in the room. That is why the sūrah, having reached the point at which its hero is fully in control, says instead: *We* raise whom We will, and above every possessor of knowledge is a knower.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:77

> **77** *They said, “If he has stolen, a brother of his had stolen aforetime.” But Joseph kept it secret in his soul and disclosed it not unto them. He said, “You are in a worse position! And God knows best concerning that which you describe.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Retort That Betrayed the Men Who Made It**

*Qālū in yasriq fa-qad saraqa akhun lahu min qablu*. Cornered, the brothers reach for the one weapon that a charge of theft always invites: an appeal to background. If this boy took the bowl, they say, then stealing runs in his family — a sentence that sounds like an explanation and is in fact an accusation dressed as an excuse. The sūrah lets them say it without comment, which is the harshest treatment it has yet given them: eleven men, who a verse ago were swearing that thieving was not their character (12:73), now try to locate the character flaw in the household of Rachel's two sons. They do not know that the man they are describing is the one whose brother they destroyed, and the sūrah's silence is the whole of its commentary on slander: it does not have to refute the claim, because the plot has already put the slanderers in the position of the accused.

The report the commentators attach to the phrase is preserved in our source: "a brother of his had stolen aforetime" is taken to refer to Joseph himself, who when younger "is said to have stolen an idol from his maternal grandfather and destroyed it, lest it be worshipped" (Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, and other commentators of the same school). The verb "is said" marks the status of the story: nothing in the verse asserts it, and it belongs to the transmitted material the exegetes cite to explain how a brother could be called a thief without the sūrah's endorsement. Its imaginative logic is sound, however — the young breaker of images is a figure the Qur'an knows from Ibrāhīm, whose people demanded "who has done this to our gods?" and who answered that the biggest of them had done it (21:57-58) — and it puts the accusation in the mouth of brothers who are, in their own eyes, prosecuting an old grudge. The sūrah itself confines the reader to what the line does: it gives the brothers a way of making an innocent boy's misfortune look probable, which is what slander is.

**What He Kept, and How the Verse Says So**

*Wa-asarrahā Yūsufu fī nafsihi lam yubdiḥā lahum*. He hid it — the reply — in his self, and did not show it to them. Then the verse continues: *qāla inna lī sharrun makānan*, "he said: you are in a worse position," which seems, at a glance, to undo the clause before it. Our source records the classical solution: what Joseph kept secret was precisely this retort, which he said to himself and did not voice aloud (al-Zamakhsharī). The verse's own structure supports the reading: disclosure is denied, and then the words are given as a report of the inner speech, the way 12:13 gives the brothers' plan ("he said" for what they concealed) and the way the sūrah elsewhere lets a heart speak without a mouth.

The content of the suppressed reply is the sūrah's verdict on the brothers' case: *innī li-sharrun makānan* — read as "you are in a worse position," and glossed by the commentators as the judgment that they, not Benjamin, are the guilty party, "since they stole him (Joseph) from their father and treated him unjustly, while Benjamin did not participate in this act" (al-Zamakhsharī and others). The word-play is not lost: their argument was that a thief comes from a thievish family, and the reply is that the family's real theft is the one no Egyptian court would try. The sūrah has said this three times already in its own voice — the bloodied shirt and the lie (12:18), the father's "nay, your souls have seduced you in this matter" (12:83), and, at the end, "do you know what you have done with Joseph and his brother, when you were ignorant?" (12:89). What it does here is let the wronged man think the sentence and not say it.

**The Ethics of the Unspoken Retort**

This small detail carries the chapter's most demanding instruction. Yūsuf has the evidence, the office, the moral high ground and the moment; a single spoken sentence would have shamed his brothers permanently in front of a caravan, and it would have been true. He thinks it. The Qur'an's description of the pious includes "those who restrain anger and pardon people, for God loves the beautiful-doers" (3:134: *al-kāẓimīna al-ghayẓa wa-l-ʿāfīna ʿani al-nās*), and the Prophet ﷺ defined strength in the same terms: "the strong man is not the good wrestler; the strong man is he who controls himself at anger" (Bukhārī and Muslim). Neither text requires that the anger not exist; both require that it not be spent. The sūrah's scene shows the difference between the two, since Yūsuf's restraint here is what makes his later words possible: when he is finally able to speak about the past, what he says is "there is no reproach against you this day" (12:92). Had he released the retort in the granary, the pardon at the reunion would have been one more exchange of blows between people keeping score.

And the closing clause is his prayer, not their sentence: *wa-llāhu aʿlamu li-mā taṣifūn* — "God knows best concerning that which you describe." The phrase belongs to the sūrah's family of refrains about description: Yaʿqūb, handed the shirt with its lying blood, ends his verdict with "beautiful patience, and God is the one asked for help against what you describe" (12:18), and at each new loss he returns to the same two words of patience (12:83). The son here uses the father's idiom, without the appeal for help: knowledge is left with God, and the dispute about what really happened is taken out of human hands altogether. It is the sūrah's prescription for a quarrel one can win — you may state your case in the room and leave the judgment of character to the One who actually has access to it.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:78

> **78** *They said, “O viceroy! He has a venerable, aged father; so take one of us in his place. Truly we see you as being among the virtuous.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"O Viceroy": the Title Comes Full Circle**

*Qālū yā-ayyuha al-ʿazīz*. The word *ʿazīz* is the sūrah's most quietly ironic title. For twenty-odd verses it belonged to the Egyptian lord who bought a boy as an article of property — the text names his wife "the wife of the ʿazīz" again and again (12:21-30), and the whole first act of the chapter is conducted under that man's roof, in his house with its locked doors. When Yūsuf asks the king's office for himself, he asks to be set over the storehouse of the land as a *ḥāfiẓ* who *knows* (12:55), and the sūrah's later scenes give him the same honorific that once marked his owner. Now the brothers use it — "O viceroy!" — to ask for a life, and the same address will be used a third time when they come to him as beggars of grain (12:88). The chapter is counting out, in a single word, the distance between being sold and being entreated: the man called *ʿazīz* by Potiphar's household is called *ʿazīz* by his own brothers, and the sūrah lets the identical title do the work of a paragraph about providence.

**Three Moves: Pathos, Bargain, Flattery**

The verse is a perfect specimen of an appeal, and its three parts are worth separating. First the pity: "he has a venerable, aged father." Our source preserves the commentators' gloss on why this argument was chosen: by mentioning their father the brothers were saying that Yaʿqūb loves Benjamin more than the rest, and that since Benjamin "has been a means of solace for Jacob in place of Joseph, not getting Benjamin back would cause him more grief" (the commentators). The pathos is exactly calibrated: they do not claim that Benjamin is useful or young, but that an old man will not survive the loss — the same old man, the sūrah knows, who has already gone white-eyed with grief once (12:84) and who will say that he complains his sorrow only to God (12:86).

Second the bargain: "take one of us in his place." Eleven men offer a substitute for the twelfth. In the parallel account in Genesis the offer comes from the one who gave the guarantee in the first place (Judah, who had said "upon my hand shall thou require him"), and here the sūrah does not even bother to say which of them is meant. What matters is the shape of the proposal, which is a ransom in persons: one free man for another.

Third the compliment: "truly we see you as being among the virtuous (*mina al-muḥsinīn*)." This is the same verb and the same word the prisoners used in the cell — "we see you among the beautiful-doers" (12:36) — and the sūrah's pattern of "we see you" statements (12:36, 73, 78) is now turned to account as a piece of negotiating technique. Flattery in this scene is not dishonest; it is an attempt to make the man's own record bind him to the concession they want. That is the verse's most modern-feeling observation, and its moral is complicated: they appeal to iḥsān in order to ask Yūsuf to act against justice, and the sūrah's answer comes in the very next verse, where he refuses precisely because iḥsān is real.

**The Request the Sūrah Cannot Grant**

Substitution is the moment at which the chapter's ethics of person and responsibility becomes explicit. The brothers' own law, given two verses earlier, punished the man "in whose saddlebag it is found" (12:75); to take another in his place would be to punish a person for a deed he did not do, and the Qur'an's settled doctrine forbids exactly that transfer: "no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another" (6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 53:38). The verse's petition is therefore not a cleverness to be rewarded; it is a request that the whole story would have to refuse in order to mean anything. Yūsuf's reply — "God be my refuge that we should take any save the one with whom we found our property" (12:79) — is the sūrah's ruling on the appeal to mercy when mercy would be injustice: iḥsān is not a licence to be arbitrary, and the "beautiful-doer" is praised for being bound.

And yet the refusal, which sounds like a failure of compassion, is the hinge of the plot. Because Yūsuf will not take a substitute, Benjamin stays in Egypt; because Benjamin stays, the brothers must go back and tell the truth (12:81-82); because they tell it, Yaʿqūb sends them again (12:87-88) and the family is reunited in Egypt (12:99-100). The chapter's arithmetic of hardness is thus the counterpart of its arithmetic of kindness — the goods returned into the saddles (12:62), the cup put in the saddle (12:70) — and both are described in its theology as God's doing rather than Yūsuf's: "God has been gracious unto us" (12:90). Men who beg for an exception in the name of virtue are, in the sūrah's telling, the same men for whom a rule is about to be broken in the name of mercy — the rule of Egypt's law, which God's *nikād* has already stepped over (12:76).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:79

> **79** *He said, “God be my refuge that we should take any save the one with whom we found our property! For then we would surely be wrongdoers.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Words at Two Ages**

*Qāla ʿūdhu bi-llāhi an naʾkhudha illā man wajadnā ʿindahu matāʿanā*. The formula of refuge — *ʿūdhu bi-llāh*, "I seek shelter in God" — has been spoken by this character exactly once before, and the sūrah has not let the reader forget it. Standing in a locked house with a woman who had power over his life and his freedom, the young slave said: "God be my refuge! Truly He is my lord, and has made beautiful my accommodation. Verily the wrongdoers will not prosper!" (12:23). Now, in the language of the same verb of taking and the same word for wrongdoing, the ruler of Egypt's granary says it a second time, and about a different temptation: not a body he could have taken, but a concession he could have granted.

The chapter has therefore built its hero's integrity out of a single repeated phrase at two points of a life. At twenty he refused a pleasure that would have cost him his faith; at forty he refuses a kindness that would have cost another man his liberty. Both refusals are named *ẓulm* in his own mouth, and both are accompanied by the same appeal to God as the one from whom shelter is sought. That is the sūrah's answer to anyone who thinks virtue is a matter of one kind of test: the young man's purity and the old man's justice are the same capacity, exercised at opposite ends of a career, in opposite directions of pressure — once against a desire he was denied, once against a power he was given. Modern institutions have a name for the second test and far less respect for it than for the first: the official who can help a sympathetic family and refuses, because the rule is about a person, not about their circumstances.

**"Save the One with Whom We Found Our Property"**

The refusal is not an arbitrary severity; it is jurisdictional. His sentence, in the sūrah's phrasing, is bounded by what was "found": *man wajadnā ʿindahu matāʿanā* — the one in whose possession our goods were found. Again the verb is impersonal, as it was when the brothers framed their law ("whoever it is *found*," 12:75), and again the constraint is the fact and nothing else: not the household's grief, not the father's age, not the offer of a substitute, not the compliment about his own virtue. The ʿāyah's logic is the logic of every legitimate detention — it exists because of a finding, and it ends where the finding ends. The jurists who worked out the rules for holding a debtor kept this limit visible: seizure answers to the debt and to the debtor, and taking a third party's person or property to settle another's obligation is the paradigm case of *ẓulm*; the delay of a solvent debtor is itself called injustice in the Prophet's saying, and the entire apparatus of the law of *ḥajr* and detention is meant to keep punishment attached to the person to whom it is owed.

It is worth setting the sūrah's line beside the version in Genesis, where Judah offers that all of them become their lord's servants and the lord answers that only the man in whose hand the cup was found shall be his servant, "and get ye up in peace to your father." The two accounts agree on the refusal, and the Qur'anic one is stripped of everything but the principle; even the brothers' offer is answered before it is finished, in a sentence that begins with God's name.

**"For Then We Would Surely Be Wrongdoers"**

*Innā idhan la-ẓālimūn*. The last clause is the verse's theological weight. Yūsuf does not say "the law does not permit it," and he does not say "I would be criticised." He says that doing the right thing for the wrong reason would make *him* one of the wrongdoers — the same category he used when he was a slave, "verily the wrongdoers will not prosper" (12:23), and the same category the brothers had used of themselves when they volunteered their own code, "thus do we recompense the wrongdoers" (12:75). The sūrah's diction is doing something remarkable here: it has made the accused articulate a maxim, and made the prosecutor apply that same maxim to himself in advance, so that both sides in the dispute are reasoning under one word. What divides them is not the standard but the willingness to be bound by it when it costs them.

And what costs Yūsuf here is not small. To hold Benjamin is to guarantee an aged father's grief, and the sūrah has already shown what that grief looks like when it arrives (12:84: "his eyes had turned white with grief, and he was choked with anguish"). A man who knows that his next sentence will produce that scene, and who says it anyway because he has found his property in another man's bag, is described by the chapter as merciful for the very reason that he is just: the sūrah's own summary of the family's deliverance will be "God has been gracious unto us" (12:90), and the last word about the arbitrator will be the father's: "He is the Knowing, the Wise" (12:83). Justice that refuses to bend in the granary is the condition of a reunion in which no one can complain of having been defrauded — which is why the sūrah can, three verses later, let the brothers say the sentence that no defendant ever manages to say about himself: "your father took from you a solemn pledge in God's name, and before this we failed with regard to Yūsuf" (12:80). Yūsuf's "no" is what makes their confession possible.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:80

> **80** *So when they despaired of [swaying] him, they conferred privately. The eldest of them said, “Do you not know that your father has taken a solemn pledge from you before God, and earlier you neglected Joseph? Thus I shall not depart from this land till my father grants me leave, or God renders judgment upon me, and He is the best of judges!"*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Despair, in the Sūrah's Own Vocabulary**

*Fa-lammā istaʾāsū min-hu khalaṣū najiyyan*. The verb of the opening clause is *y-ʾ-s*, to despair, and it is used here of the brothers' assessment of a man who would not move: they despaired of him. The chapter will answer that verb three verses later, on the lips of their father, in the most quoted sentence of the sūrah: "O my sons! Go and inquire about Joseph and his brother and despair not of God's Comfort; truly none despairs of God's Comfort save the disbelieving people" (12:87). The sūrah has placed the two uses deliberately: the men despair of a minister, and the old man forbids despairing of God. The verse's first word is thus a diagnosis of exactly the condition that the next scene will cure, and the cure is not optimism but a change of the party whose will is being measured.

The second verb is rarer and more vivid: *khalaṣū najiyyan*, "they became exclusive, in private counsel." The noun *najwā* in the Qur'an is the word for a conversation drawn aside from others — used of conspirators whispering, and used, in the sura al-Mujādala, of the believers' private consultations with the Prophet, which are to be prepared by an offering (58:12). Here it marks the moment when advocacy ends and honesty begins. In public, before the crier and the caravan, the brothers swore that they had not come to work corruption in the land and were not thieves (12:73); in private, with no audience but themselves, the eldest says that they failed with regard to Yūsuf. The sūrah is noting a permanent fact about groups: a room with outsiders in it produces a case, and a room with only the guilty in it produces a confession.

**The Eldest Turns the Court on His Brothers**

*Qāla kabīruhum a-lā taʿlamūna anna abākum akhadha minkum mawthāqan mina llāhi wa-min qablu mā farraṭnā fī Yūsuf*. The address is a question, and its subject is not the minister's cruelty but their own record. Two clauses, in order: the covenant and the old failure. The first reaches back to 12:66, where Yaʿqūb refused to release Benjamin until they swore "by God" a *mawthāq* — the same word used of the solemn covenants God takes from the prophets (33:7) and from the Children of Israel (2:63, 2:93) — and the second reaches back further, to the day they did not bring a boy home. Our source records the commentators' gloss on the verb: they "neglected Joseph when they failed to take care of him" (al-Baghawī), and, by extension, broke the vow made to Yaʿqūb to return him safely; and the Qur'an's own phrasing of that first assurance was "we shall surely be his keepers" (12:12), answered by the father at 12:64 with a sentence the eldest now proves right: "should I entrust him to you as I entrusted his brother to you aforetime?" (12:64). The chapter's vocabulary of guardianship — *ḥifẓ*, *amāna*, * kafāla*, *wakāla* — has run from 12:11 to this verse, and 12:80 is where the brothers finally use it against themselves.

The sūrah calls the speaker "the eldest of them" and never a name; our source notes that the commentators identify him as Reuben, without the text doing so. That anonymity is a merit: the chapter is not interested in which son said it but in the fact that, at last, one of them does.

**"I Shall Not Depart from This Land"**

*Fa-lan abraḥa al-arḍa ḥatta yaʾdhana lī abī aw yaḥkuma llāhu lī wa-huwa khayru al-ḥākimīn*. The resolution has the shape of a legal pledge: he will stay put until one of two things happens — his father's permission, or God's judgment. Our source records the sense of the alternative: the judgment of God means either that Benjamin is returned to him or that he is sent home without him (al-Baghawī) — that is, the eldest commits himself to remain a suppliant in Egypt until the case is closed, leaving the two outcomes to a judge he cannot manage. In the Qur'an this idiom, "You are the best of those who judge," belongs to a family of petitions in which a hard case is handed to God rather than argued with men, and our source directs the reader to the same expression in 7:87 for its company of cognate phrases. The brothers had begun this whole second journey with an argument about trust (12:64: "we failed to guard him, so who will guard him for us?"); they end it with one of them refusing to argue with anyone but God.

The verbal form he chooses, *abraḥa* (to cease, to leave off), in the negative with "until," is the grammar of an unconditional: he will not stop. It is the same idiom of unceasing that the sūrah puts on the brothers' lips about their father a few verses later — "By God, wilt thou go on remembering Joseph till thou art ill to the point of death, or till thou hast perished?" (12:85) — a clause whose negation the grammarians explain as understood rather than expressed ("you will not cease remembering Yūsuf"), precisely as the eldest's own *lan abraḥu* here states it. The repetition matters: the chapter's characters are distinguished by what they refuse to stop doing. The brothers once refused to leave the well alone; now one of them refuses to leave a country. Between those two refusals is the whole moral education the sūrah has been describing, and it is why the scene that follows — the message to the father, "your son has stolen" (12:81) — can be told without a word of comment. The men carrying it are not the men who carried the shirt.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:81

> **81** *Return unto your father and say, ‘O father! Verily your son has committed theft. And we bore witness to that which we knew, but we are not keepers of the unseen.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Report Drafted Before It Is Delivered**

*Irjiʿū ilā abīkum fa-qūlū yā-abānā inna ibnaka saraqa*. The sūrah rarely tells its characters what to say; here it hands them their text. Two imperatives — go back, say — and then the speech itself, quoted inside the instruction, complete with the vocative ("O father") and the confession's three hedges. The device is a piece of narrative economy with a moral edge: we are made to hear the brothers composing their defence before they deliver it, and we hear it as they hear it in their own rehearsal, where each clause has been chosen to limit damage. The chapter does not wait to describe the journey or the interview; the next thing we see is the old man's reply (12:83). Everything between the drafting and the delivery is left out, because the sūrah's subject is not the scene but the sentence.

That sentence is the first time in this episode that a brother says "your son stole" without a conditional particle. In the presence of the accusers they had said "if he has stolen, a brother of his had stolen aforetime" (12:77) — an argument, hedged and hostile. Now, to their father, the theft is asserted flatly. The shift is a fine observation in the chapter's psychology: men hedge when they are being judged and simplify when they are being believed.

**"We Bore Witness Only to What We Knew"**

*Wa-mā shahidnā illā bimā ʿalimnā*. The clause is the Qur'an's own rule of evidence stated by the men who need it: attestation is bounded by knowledge. The Book says it elsewhere as a prohibition — "do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge; the hearing, the sight and the heart, all of these will be asked about" (17:36) — and the jurists built on it the requirement that a witness testify to what he perceived, not to what he inferred. The brothers' sentence is a claim to have kept that limit: we saw the cup in the bag, and that is all we assert. It is, in its own narrow way, true. They did see it; the sūrah has said so in the passives of the search ("whoever it is found with," 12:75; "the one with whom we found our property," 12:79), and their testimony is reportable without dishonesty.

What the verse exposes, however, is the insufficiency of correct testimony. Between "we found it in his saddlebag" and "your son stole" there is an inference — and the inference is precisely the point at which the sūrah's reader is ahead of everyone in the room, because the reader was told in 12:70 who put it there. The brothers' epistemology is impeccable and their conclusion wrong; the chapter is showing that a truthful report of a finding can carry a false claim across a border, and that a man who restricts himself to what he knows may still say more than he knows. Anyone who has ever given or taken a witness statement, an incident report, or a reference recognises the structure: the account is accurate, the account is complete as far as it goes, and the account is decisive — and the decisive part is the one sentence that was never witnessed.

**"We Were Not Keepers of the Unseen"**

*Wa-mā kunnā li-l-ghaybi ḥāfiẓīn*. The word *ḥāfiẓ* is the sūrah's own old wound. The brothers' first promise about Yūsuf was made in that exact vocabulary: "send him with us tomorrow, to frolic and play; we shall surely be his keepers" (12:12: *la-nakūna la-hu ḥāfiẓīn*). Their father's answer, when they asked for Benjamin on the same terms, used the comparative of the same root — "God is the best of keepers" (12:64) — and the pledge he demanded was hedged with a condition about what they cannot control ("unless you are surrounded," 12:66). Now, having lost the boy they guaranteed, they disavow the office: we were never keepers of the unseen. The chapter's chain of guardianship — Yūsuf's claim when he asks for the storehouse — "set me over the storehouses of the land; truly I am a skilled keeper" (12:55), the father's "who is the best of guardians" (12:64), God as *wakīl* over their words (12:66), and this verse — closes here, with the sons admitting that the only keeping they ever had was nominal.

Our source preserves two notes on the clause that make its legal function plain. First, al-Zamakhsharī glosses it as the brothers' plea that they had no way of foreseeing that Benjamin would steal in Egypt when the pact was made. Second, the same note adds that by saying it they were "implicitly invoking the exceptional condition that Jacob included in his pledge with them" — that is, they are arguing their way back inside the covenant's own escape clause (12:66), the "unless you are surrounded" that exempts from an obligation what human power cannot reach. Whether or not the clause covers them (a theft they could not foresee is not a threat that surrounded them) the sūrah leaves to the reader; what it does leave on the record is that the brothers, unlike their father, do not think of God as the one they are answering. They are answering a contract.

That is the verse's final, quiet instruction. A promise kept only to its letter, and a report that is true up to the edge of what the witness saw, are both forms of the same skill — and skill of that kind, the sūrah has shown from 12:16 onward, is what a family loses when it begins to lie to itself. The father, when he hears all of it, will not test their evidence. He will read them.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:82

> **82** *So ask the town wherein we were, and the caravan with which we approached. Verily we are truthful.’”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Turning to Strangers for Proof**

*Wa-sʾal al-qaryata illāti kunā fīhā wa-l-ʿīra llātī qadimnā fīhā wa-innā la-ṣādikūn*. The second half of the brothers' prepared message is a demand for due diligence, and it is the most striking line in their rehearsal. They do not say "believe us"; they say *ask*. A man who had guaranteed his brother's return and come back without him offers, unprompted, two independent bodies of witnesses — the town where the loss occurred and the company that travelled with them. Within the sūrah's legal scene this is exactly the right move: a claim that can be checked should be checked, and a defendant who invites the check has, by the ordinary rules of testimony, strengthened his standing. The clause also answers a difficulty the chapter has kept open since 12:64, where the father said plainly that he would not trust them with Benjamin as he had trusted them with Yūsuf. Their answer is not to argue for their character but to produce evidence, which is — the sūrah allows — progress.

Our source preserves the commentators' identification of the two parties: "the town refers to the people of Egypt, and the caravan to the people of Canaan," and the note adds a detail that closes the loop of 12:70 — "it is said that when the latter returned to Jacob, they testified to the brothers' truthfulness" (our source records). The company that had been accused in a sentence ("O you caravan! Truly you are thieves!") thus becomes the company that vouches for the accused. The sūrah's single word *ʿīr* links the two scenes, and the reversal is one of the chapter's neatest pieces of irony: the men who shouted the charge, and the strangers who heard it, agree that the brothers are honest; and they are right, and it does not help.

**The Metonymy: Asking a Town**

The command to "ask the town" is, of course, a way of asking its people, and Arabic's rhetoricians classify the expression as a substitution of place for occupant — the *majāz* in which a city stands for its inhabitants. The sūrah's phrasing has a further economy: it does not say "ask so-and-so" or "ask the king's men," because the point is not a witness list but the sheer publicness of the event. Everything had been done in the open — the crier, the demand for the cup, the offer of a camel-load, the search of eleven bags in front of a caravan, the penalty proposed by the accused themselves (12:70-75). The brothers are able to appeal to the visibility of the whole affair because the affair was visible; that is what made the device work, and it is why Yūsuf never has to lie about anything. A modern reader will recognise the shape of the argument from any well-run inquiry: the process was fair, the record is complete, the parties signed their own statements — and the conclusion is nonetheless the one the design required. Publicity is not the same as truth, and the sūrah knows it.

**"Verily We Are Truthful"**

*Wa-innā la-ṣādikūn*. The asseveration closes the message with the strongest available formula: an oath-styled clause, "we are, most certainly, among the truthful." It is not a lie. They did not steal; they did see the bowl; they did search; they are truthful about every item they list. What the sūrah sets against their sentence is the same standard it set against their father's: the chapter's refrain about description and knowledge — "God knows best concerning that which you describe" (12:77), and "He is the Knowing, the Wise" (12:83). Truthfulness, in this family's reckoning, is not the absence of falsehood but the presence of the whole account; and the whole account would have had to include the fact that they were reporting a theft whose evidence had been placed in a bag by the man whose brother they had once thrown into a well. The verse's pathos is that no one in it is able to see that, except the reader.

Yaʿqūb, when the message is delivered, will not send anyone to the town. His reply needs no witnesses: "Nay, your souls have seduced you in this matter. Beautiful patience!" (12:83) — the sentence of a man who has heard the same report before and who knows, without evidence, that something has been left out. That is the sūrah's final comment on verification: inquiry into the town is good for a judge, and useless against a father who can read a son's grief. The chapter has spent two verses teaching the brothers to build a defensible case; the next verse will teach them, again, that some knowledge is not reached by asking.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:83

> **83** *He said, “Nay, your souls have seduced you in this matter. Beautiful patience! It may be that God will bring them to me all together. Truly He is the Knowing, the Wise.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Father Says the Sentence He Said Before**

*Qāla bal sawwalat lakum anfusukum amran fa-ṣabrun jamīlun wa-ʿasā llāhu an yaʾtiyanī bihim jamīʿan innahu huwa al-ʿalīmu al-ḥakīm*. The first half of this reply is, word for word, the reply Yaʿqūb gave when his sons first came to him with the shirt and its false blood (12:18), and the sūrah's repetition is one of its grandest structural strokes. Two losses, twenty-odd years apart, are met with one sentence, and the reader is obliged to compare the two scenes: then, the report came with a fabricated detail and was dismissed by intuition; now, it comes with witnesses offered and is dismissed by the same intuition (12:82). Nothing in the middle of the verse has been improved by evidence, and the father has not changed. He has only waited.

Our source records the note that makes the repetition interpretively decisive: "Jacob's words here are similar to his response to the news of the loss of Joseph in verse 18," and the same note reads the hope that follows as prophecy — "it may be that God will bring them to me all together" is, in the source's words, "a kind of prophecy, since Joseph was still not known to be alive." The sūrah itself has planted that reading in the chapter's opening, where the boy's dream was not a fancy but a fact about the future, withheld from his brothers (12:5-6). Yaʿqūb is now doing with words what he did then with restraint: he declines to accept the report, and he is right for reasons he does not spell out.

**"Your Souls Have Seduced You"**

The accusation he levelles is not theft this time, nor treachery, but self-deception: *sawwalat lakum anfusukum amran* — your own selves made an affair look smooth to you. The verb is the one the sūrah has already used for the mechanism of the chapter's every wrong: the brothers' souls dressed up killing a child, or abandoning him in a well, as a plan (12:10-15); the women's souls dressed up a false testimony (12:30); Zulaykhā, in her own defence, names the same pressure — "I do not absolve myself; truly the soul is a great commandress of evil, except as my Lord has mercy" (12:53). Yaʿqūb's sentence therefore is not a personal insult but the sūrah's diagnosis, spoken by the character who has watched the disease at work in his own house for a generation: an idea becomes "an affair" in a person's imagination, and the imagination flatters it.

Note also that the father's reply is directed to the men, not to the missing boy. He does not say "what have you done to Benjamin"; he says "what your souls have done to you." The chapter's pedagogy of responsibility has reached its last stage: at 12:77 Yūsuf thought, but did not say, that his brothers were worse off than the accused; his father says the equivalent in the language of the self, and in the language of God's knowledge.

**Beautiful Patience, and the Hope Attached to It**

*Ṣabrun jamīlun*. The phrase, first heard in 12:18, is the sūrah's term for a grief that does not advertise itself; the commentary tradition glosses it as patience without complaint to creatures (see 12:85-86). What is remarkable in this verse is what the patience is paired with: not resignation but a request. *ʿAsā llāhu an yaʾtiyanī bihim jamīʿan* — "it may be that God will bring them to me all together." The word *ʿasā* is hope addressed to a specific agent, and "all together" is the detail that undoes the brothers' whole careful report: they had come to speak of one brother's loss, and the father's sentence includes three. Our source preserves the classical answer to the obvious question of reference: "them" means Yūsuf, Benjamin, and the eldest, Reuben, who had refused to leave Egypt (12:80). The father's hope, on this reading, already contains the reunion that the chapter will spend its closing verses producing — the family standing before him in Egypt (12:99) and the prostration of the parents (12:100) — and it contains the son he was not told about.

The closing clause is the sūrah's own seal on the scene: *innahu huwa al-ʿalīmu al-ḥakīm* — "truly He is the Knowing, the Wise." Every verse of the famine, the measuring, the planted cup and the withheld reply has been a demonstration of the two names: someone knows what is in the bags, and someone is wise enough to use it without harming anyone's soul more than it needs to be harmed. Yaʿqūb's patience is beautiful because it is not blind; it is grounded in an attribution — the knowledge is with God, and so is the judgment. That is the sentence on which the next two verses turn, when the sons protest at his weeping and he explains what he is doing with his grief.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:84

> **84** *And he turned away from them and said, “Oh, how great is my grief for Joseph!” His eyes had turned white with grief, and he was choked with anguish.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Turning Away: the One Gesture the Sūrah Allows Him**

*Wa-aʿraḍa ʿan-hum*. Before he speaks, he leaves. The verb *aʿraḍa ʿan* — to turn one's face away from — is not neutral in Qur'anic diction: it is the word used for those who turn from the signs when they are brought to them, and the sūrah's choice to put it here of a father turning from his sons says more than a paragraph would: the company of men who have come with a well-drafted report (12:81-82) is met with a body's refusal to continue. There is no rebuke in the verse, no argument, no demand for the witnesses he was invited to question. Yaʿqūb has heard enough, and he walks out of the conversation.

Then the cry. *Yā-asafā ʿalā Yūsuf* — the vocative of lament, with the name in the place where, if he were composing a sentence, an object would stand. *Asaf* is grief of a particular kind: inward, overwhelming, and here addressed to nobody. This is the only place in the sūrah where a man speaks directly to his own sorrow, and it is placed immediately after two verses of legal prose in which everyone has been careful about what they assert and whom they assert it to. The chapter's rhythm — a case argued, a case abandoned for a groan — is its statement about what lies under procedure.

**"His Eyes Had Turned White with Grief"**

*Wa-bayyaḍat ʿaynāhu mina al-ḥuzni fa-huwa kakīm*. The sūrah gives a medical fact, and it gives it as the consequence of an emotion: grief does something to a body. To "whiten" here is to be blinded, and our source preserves the two graded readings the commentators offer: that in his sadness Yaʿqūb "wept so much that he went blind," or, more moderately, that his eyesight became very weak — both indicated by the words *his eyes had turned white*. The verse's last word, *kakīm*, belongs to the same physical register: a man choked, his voice sealed, grief constricting the throat; the root *k-k-m* gives Arabic its word for something kept silent (a *maktūm* secret, a voice *makmūm*), and the sūrah's grammar is exact — the man whose eyes are destroyed by what he sees is the same man whose voice is closed by what he will not say.

The chapter has already used this economy once. When Yūsuf was taken from him, the text recorded no tears; it recorded a shirt, a sentence about souls, and "beautiful patience" (12:16-18). Now, decades later, it records a body. The sūrah's silence about the earlier weeping is not stoicism but the difference between receiving news and losing the last one: the first loss he was told about in a story, and the second he can no longer imagine living past.

**Grief That Does Not Cross a Line**

The verse is one of the Qur'an's clearest demonstrations of what the tradition means by *ṣabrun jamīl*: the lament is real, the eyes are lost, the throat closes — and nothing is said against God. Yaʿqūb does not ask why, does not accuse, and does not wish for death. Compare the cry of Maryam in Sūrah Maryam (19:28) — "would that I had died before this and become a thing forgotten, utterly forgotten" — which the Qur'an reports without condemnation, and Ayyūb's, which is addressed not to grief but to the one who can lift it: "Affliction has touched me, and You are the most merciful of the merciful" (21:83), answered at once with "so We responded to him and removed the affliction that was in him, and We gave him back his family and the like of them with them" (21:84). The Qur'an's two great cases of a prophet's grief, Yaʿqūb's here and Ayyūb's there, have the same structure: the complaint is spoken, and it is spoken upward, and — as Ayyūb's verse states in the plainest terms the Book allows — the return of the family is the answer. Yaʿqūb's hope in the previous verse, "it may be that God will bring them to me all together" (12:83), is the same petition in the same key.

The Prophet's own practice, recorded in Bukhārī and Muslim when his son Ibrāhīm died, is the tradition's commentary on this verse: "the eyes tear and the heart grieves, and we say only what pleases our Lord; we are saddened by your parting, Ibrāhīm." The hadith is cited by the exegetes exactly here because it draws the sūrah's own line: tears are not a complaint against God; the grief that is beautiful is grief that stays inside the address. A modern reader, watching an old man ruined by a report he cannot verify, may notice that the text's sympathy is entirely with him, and that the sūrah records the physical cost without ever calling the emotion a failure of faith. It says nothing about his weeping except that it happened, and it says nothing about the eyes except that they whitened — a chapter that has spent its whole middle section on the ethics of speech here concedes that some sorrows are only available in the body.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:85

> **85** *They said, “By God, wilt thou go on remembering Joseph till thou art ill to the point of death, or till thou hast perished?”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sons’ Oath, and What It Actually Says**

*Qālū tā-llāhi taf-taʾu taʾkuru Yūsufa ḥatta takūna ḥaraḍan aw takūna mina al-hālikīn*. Read closely, the verse contains no reproach. There is no mention of Benjamin, no defence of their conduct, no reference to the Egyptian minister: they swear by God that their father's remembering will kill him. Our source records exactly this reading — the question was "motivated by love and compassion for their father," and it is "a figurative way of telling him that they fear for his demise if he continues to grieve over Joseph" (the commentators preserve both the motive and the rhetorical form) — and it is a real achievement of the sūrah's characterization that the same men who sold a brother now speak as sons.

Their oath has the same shape as their last one. Before the caravan they swore "by God, you certainly know that we came not to work corruption in the land" (12:73); here they swear "by God" about an old man's memory. Both oaths are about Yūsuf, and both are conducted in the register of certainty: the first claimed to know what the Egyptians knew, the second claims to know where the father's grief is heading. Between them lies the private conference in which the eldest said the thing nobody had said aloud — "before this we failed with regard to Yūsuf" (12:80). The sūrah has arranged the oaths so that the reader measures them: men who are certain about other people's knowledge, and now certain about death, are men who have not yet reckoned with what they themselves did.

**A Verb Without Its Negation**

The line's most discussed word is *taf-taʾu*, from a verb meaning "to cease," which the received text writes with no explicit negation. The old grammarians and readers dealt with the bareness in two ways, and the sūrah's translators follow one or the other: either a "not" is understood, so that the sentence is a threatened prohibition — "by God, you will not cease remembering Yūsuf until…" — or the clause is taken as the question the received translation adopts: "wilt thou go on remembering Joseph…?" Either way the sense is identical, and the ambiguity is not a defect. An oath whose grammar is half-supplied is precisely what the scene needs: eleven men speaking to a father they cannot order, only press.

The verb governed by it is *dhikr* — "remembering," not "grieving" — and the sūrah's word choice is the point. What they object to is that Yūsuf's name keeps being said. The chapter has already established that the brothers cannot stop thinking about him either: the eldest's self-accusation used the same name (12:80), and their whole journey to Egypt was conducted, as they said to their father, in his shadow ("what is the matter that you do not trust us with Yūsuf as we were trustworthy with his brother?" — 12:64). They are asking their father to stop what they cannot.

**"Till Thou Art Ill … or Perished"**

The two terms of the threat are graded and physical. *Ḥaraḍ* is a word of the Qur'an's rarest vocabulary, which the dictionaries gloss as a wasting sickness, the state of a body or a branch dried out to rottenness; *halāk* is perdition, the end. Between them is a whole old age of deterioration, and the sons name it because they are frightened: they know that the man in front of them has been weeping until his eyes whitened (12:84), and they understand, correctly, that memory is doing this to him. The sūrah allows their fear to be sincere. Its irony is that their diagnosis is accurate while their remedy is impossible — you cannot ask a man to stop remembering a son he was told was torn by a wolf, and who, as the chapter's audience has known since 12:13, is not dead.

There is a further layer of Qur'anic diction here that the sūrah's readers have not missed. The word *halāk* — perdition, destruction — is the Book's standard term for the end of communities that defied their messengers, and the brothers reach for it in a casual oath; the sūrah's irony is that its worst word is applied by these sons to the man whose house they themselves ruined. Yaʿqūb will not answer the threat. He will answer the accusation implied in it — that his grief is excessive — with a sentence about to whom it is addressed, and that is 12:86.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:86

> **86** *He said, “I complain of my sorrow and grief to God alone. And I know from God that which you know not.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Words for What Is Wrong, and One Direction**

*Qāla innamā ashkū baʾthī wa-ḥuznī ila llāh*. The verse's first movement is a distinction between two nouns. *Ḥuzn* is the plain word for grief — the sadness that sits in a person and makes him heavy, and the sūrah has already used it of the family's whole condition ("affliction has befallen us," 12:88, and the father's "his eyes had turned white with grief," 12:84). *Baʾth* is rarer and more interior: from the root that also gives *baʿtha*, the rousing and the sending-forth, it names the disordered, scattered state of a mind that grief has set in motion — distress in its restless aspect rather than in its weight. Yaʿqūb therefore complains of both the agitation and the heaviness; he does not simplify his own suffering for the sake of the people who find it inconvenient.

The second movement is the single particle that changes everything: *ila llāh*. Not "about," not "from," but *to*. The sūrah's answer to his sons' reproach is not that he is not complaining — he complains, and he tells them so — but that the complaint has an addressee. The commentary tradition preserves the definition of *ṣabrun jamīl* that the chapter's earlier verse demanded, and it is exactly this: "a form of patience in which one complains only to God about one's suffering and not to other human beings." The phrase had been Yaʿqūb's own formula twice already (12:18, 83); here it is explained from the inside, and the explanation removes the only reading in which it could have been mistaken for endurance-by-numbers, a stiff upper lip. Beautiful patience is not silence about pain; it is pain spoken where it will be heard without diminishing anyone.

**"I Know from God What You Do Not Know"**

*Wa-aʿlamu mina llāhi mā lā taʿlamūn*. The clause is at once a claim and a qualification: the knowledge he has, he has *from God*. Our source records the gloss that some give it: this is Jacob's "intuitive knowledge that Joseph was still alive" (al-Rāzī), the certainty behind his refusal to test their witnesses and behind the hope "it may be that God will bring them to me all together" (12:83). The sūrah's diction has prepared the claim carefully. It has said of Yaʿqūb that he possessed what he did because he had been taught — "he had knowledge of what We taught him" (12:68) — and it has twice contrasted human assurance with divine knowledge in the very passages around this one: "God knows best concerning that which you describe" (12:77), and the chapter's refrain "most of mankind do not know" (12:21, 40, 68, 103). A father who says *I know what you do not know* is, in this sūrah, not a man boasting of insight but a man in possession of a gift — and the gift is what the verse's second half is for.

The verse's place in the Qur'an's wider teaching is fixed by a sentence of the Book's own epistemology of hardship: "perhaps you hate a thing and it is good for you, and perhaps you love a thing and it is bad for you, and God knows and you do not know" (2:216). Yaʿqūb's line is that principle spoken by someone who has lived inside it for years; his sons are still arguing about the surface of events, and he is describing the layer at which they are being managed. The sūrah's readers are also being trained to read at that layer: the audience has known since 12:13 that God "turns their scheming" and since 12:76 that He "devises" for Yūsuf, so the father's claim is not a surprise to anyone except the eleven men in the room.

**A Rule for Grief, and a Rule for Belief**

Two lessons are stated in this verse and are quoted by Muslims to this day. The first is about the direction of complaint. The chapter of patience in al-Baqara links the two together — "give tidings of the patient, those who, when affliction strikes them, say: truly we are God's, and to Him we return" (2:155-156) — and the return in that formula is the same *ila llāh* that Yaʿqūb uses: affliction is answered by re-orientation toward the one who sent it, not by suppression of the feeling. To complain to God is, in this tradition, an act of the belief that He hears and owns; what is forbidden is complaint *against* Him, or complaint that stops at creatures because creatures are all that a person believes in. The second lesson is the verse's most practical one: an inner life can be entirely real and entirely invisible to one's family. Yaʿqūb's sons see an old man wasting and conclude that he is being destroyed by memory; he tells them that the same memory is held by knowledge they do not possess. The sūrah will vindicate him in the plainest physical terms it has, and with his own words. When the bearer of glad tidings casts the shirt over his face "he was restored to sight," and the first thing the healed man says is the clause of this verse, quoted back at his sons: "Did I not say unto you that I know from God that which you know not?" (12:96). The organ destroyed by grief in 12:84 is returned in 12:96, and the claim about seeing what others cannot is answered in the same currency it was stated in.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:87

> **87** *O my sons! Go and inquire about Joseph and his brother and despair not of God’s Comfort; truly none despairs of God’s Comfort save the disbelieving people.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Tender Word as in the First Verse of the Story**

*Yā-baniyya idhhabū fa-taḥassasū min Yūsufa wa-akhīhi*. The vocative is the sūrah's bookend. When Yaʿqūb first heard the dream and feared for the boy, he said "yā-buniyya" — "my dear son" (12:5), the softened plural of address that Arabic uses for affection. Now, with the two he lost and the ten he cannot trust all standing in front of him, the same tenderness returns in the plural: "O my sons." The chapter has spent eighty verses travelling from a father's fear for one son to a father's command to eleven, and it marks the distance by nothing except the warmth of the address.

The verb of the command is *taḥassasa*, to trace a thing out with the senses, to investigate by following traces — and the imperative is worth pausing on because it is the opposite of what the brothers had proposed. They wanted to be believed; he tells them to go and look. He does not send them to negotiate with the minister, to retrieve the bowl by argument, or to plead a family's case: he sends them out to gather facts. The prophetic ethic of the verse is the one the Prophet's saying later articulated as a pair: "do not be on the lookout for faults and do not spy into one another's hidden affairs" (Muslim), where *taḥassus* — hunting for what is outwardly traceable — is set against *tajassus*, prying into what is concealed. Yaʿqūb sends his sons to inquire after the missing, not to rummage in anyone's heart. The distinction is one the sūrah has been making for twenty verses, ever since Yūsuf refused to narrate the shirt's story to the whole town.

**"About Joseph and His Brother"**

The object of the search is the chapter's great confession in the shape of an order. Benjamin is in custody, which is why the expedition is needed; and yet the father's sentence names Yūsuf first — "inquire about Joseph and his brother." Our source records the note exactly: Jacob also told them to inquire about Joseph, "he believed Joseph was still alive," and the commentators preserve for that belief two transmitted grounds, one that he had seen it in a dream and one that the Angel of Death informed him that he had not taken Joseph's soul. The report is not the verse's, and the sūrah leaves the matter where it always leaves the unseen: with the claim of 12:86, "I know from God that which you know not." What the verse does with the belief, though, is decisive — a man who is certain about an impossibility organises a search party on its behalf, and the search party is what delivers the certainty. The chapter's epistemology is a loop: the father's unsupported knowledge becomes, by the end of the plot, knowledge that everyone in the room shares (12:90: "Art thou indeed Joseph?").

**Despair, and Its Name in This Sūrah**

*Wa-lā tayʾasū min rawḥi llāh*. The noun *rawḥ* is relief as a breath of air — from the root of spaciousness and rest — and the sūrah's translation of it, "God's Comfort," preserves the physical image. Our source's note glosses the clause as a prohibition against losing hope in God's mercy, and it points to the verse's twin in Sūrah al-Zumar: "say: O My servants who have transgressed against themselves, do not despair of God's mercy; truly God forgives all sins — He is the Forgiving, the Merciful" (39:53). The pairing is instructive because in both places the prohibition on despair is attached to the possibility of pardon. Yaʿqūb is not telling his sons that the grain will come back or that a prisoner will be released; he is telling them that a case is not closed while the one who forgives and relieves is still being addressed.

The word had been used against them one verse before the reunion, and the sūrah does not waste the echo: "when they despaired of him (*istaʾāsū min-hu*), they conferred privately" (12:80). They had despaired of Yūsuf's softening and gone off among themselves; their father now forbids the only despair that matters — of God — and names it unbelief: "none despairs of God's Comfort save the disbelieving people." The Book elsewhere puts the same prohibition in Ibrāhīm's own conversation with the messengers who brought him news of a son: "they said: we bring you the truth in glad tidings, so do not be of the despairing" (15:57), and Ibrāhīm answers with the rhetorical question that gives the doctrine its edge — "and who despairs of the mercy of his Lord except the astray people?" (15:56). The logic in every case is the same. Despair is not an emotion the Qur'an scolds for being weak; it is a judgment about God, and that is why it is counted among the sins of belief rather than the accidents of mood. The sūrah's own theology has already said why the judgment is wrong: it has told the reader who put the cup in the bag (12:70) and who devised the plan (12:76). A man who knows the chapter knows the answer to despair, and Yaʿqūb has been living on that knowledge for years.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:88

> **88** *So when they entered upon him they said, “O viceroy! Affliction has befallen us and our people. We bring but meager merchandise; yet grant us full measure and be charitable unto us. Truly God shall recompense the charitable.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Entry, and the Second Vocabulary**

*Fa-lammā dakhalaʿalay-hi qālū yā-ayyuha al-ʿazīz massanā wa-ahlanā al-ḍurru wa-jiʾnā bi-biḍāʿatim muzjāʾatin fa-awfi lanā al-kayla wa-taṣaddaq ʿalaynā inna llāha yajzī al-mutaṣaddiqīn*. The verb *dakhala ʿalayhi* carries the reader back to the first audience, when "they came and entered upon him and he recognised them while they were unaware" (12:58). Then, the brothers had spoken as claimants: they reported a family that was straitened, asked for a measure, and paid with what they had (12:58-59, 65). They now speak as suppliants, and their sentences have changed in a way the sūrah does not comment on but does record: they open with an address ("O viceroy"), they state their need before making their request, and they close with a clause about God's recompense. Between the first audience and this one they have lost a guaranteed brother, lied to an old man, been out-argued by a minister, and had their own law turned against them; the speech of 12:88 is what that education sounds like.

**"Affliction Has Befallen Us and Our People"**

*Massanā wa-ahlanā al-ḍurr*. The verb *massa* — to touch — is the Book's habitual understatement for calamity: harm "touches" a person the way a fever touches a body, and it is the word Ayyūb uses for the trial that ruined him, "affliction has touched me, and You are the most merciful of the merciful" (21:83). Here the noun is *ḍurr*, hardship in its narrowing sense. Our source records the gloss: the affliction meant their intense hunger, and the "meager merchandise" was the little goods they had with them to trade for food. The word for "meager," *muzjāʾa*, is vivid in its register: it describes stuff that is short-weight, adulterated or simply unwanted, and later Arabic used it precisely of debased coin. These are men who have been emptied by the famine, arriving at the granary of the world with coin nobody will take.

The order of their request matters too. They ask first for the thing they came for: "grant us full measure," using the sūrah's own word *kayl*, the measure that has been the chapter's unit of justice since 12:59. And only then, as an aside, "and be charitable unto us" — *wa-taṣaddaq ʿalaynā*. Our source records the commentators' reading of what that phrase is doing: it "is understood as a request for the return of Benjamin." The brothers do not dare to ask for the boy. They ask for charity, and mean a person. It is the most careful sentence they have ever spoken, and the sūrah's restraint — no mention of the request being made or refused here, only the word *taṣaddaq* — lets the reader feel how much the men have learned about power.

**A Pun They Did Not Intend**

The verb they use, *taṣaddaq*, is from the same root as *ṣidq*, truth: it means at once "give alms" and "be truthful with us," and the sūrah has set it, one verse earlier in the family's own words, against its sister form: "verily we are truthful (*ṣādikūn*)" (12:82). The chapter's ear for this root has been trained since 12:18, where the shirt and the false statement were juxtaposed, and since 12:25, where a lie was tested by a torn garment. So the plea "be charitable unto us" is heard in this sūrah as "deal truly with us," and its addressee is precisely the brother whose truthfulness they once denied (12:16: "you will not believe us though we are truthful"). The irony is not that they lie here; they are telling the truth about the famine. It is that their word for generosity is a word for veracity, and the man who holds the granary is the one person on earth to whom they have never told the truth.

**"God Shall Recompense the Charitable"**

*Inna llāha yajzī al-mutaṣaddiqīn*. The closing clause invokes recompense — the sūrah's *jazāʾ* — and the brothers have by now made that word a habit: they proposed it as a penalty ("thus do we recompense the wrongdoers," 12:75) and they now propose it as an incentive. Yūsuf's answer, when it comes, will use a higher noun and a different verb: "whoever is reverent and patient — surely God neglects not the reward (*ajr*) of the beautiful-doers" (12:90), and the chapter then shows them a recompense nobody bargained for: no reproach, a shirt for the father's eyes, and the family settled in Egypt (12:92-100). That is the last of the sūrah's lessons about religious language in negotiation. Invoking God's reward is legitimate — the brothers are not being hypocritical in 12:88, and their hunger is real — but the chapter's point is that God's economy is not the minister's. He does not pay for charity; He gives the boy back to the men who lost him, because the reason He took him was the one reason they could not have asked for: that they should come to know, at the end of their own honesty, that they had been kept all along (12:90: "God has been gracious unto us").
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:89

> **89** *He said, “Do you know what you have done with Joseph and his brother, when you were ignorant?”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Replaces the Answer**

The brothers have asked for grain, for relief from a famine, and — in the sūrah's compressed phrase — for the charity that means their brother's release (12:88). Yūsuf does not answer. He asks them a question, and the question is not about the goods: *hal ʿalimtum mā faʿaltum bi-Yūsufa wa-akhīhi idh antum jāhilūn* — "do you know what you did with Yūsuf and his brother, when you were ignorant?" The whole apparatus of the Egypt episode — the measure, the bowl, the guarantee, the search — is here set down, and the chapter returns, in a single sentence, to the day the family broke. This is how the sūrah conducts reconciliation: not by granting or refusing the request, but by making the parties stand for one moment inside the truth of their own history. The request will in fact be granted twice over in the next verses, but only after the question has been asked.

The question's form is important. It is a question of knowledge, not of fact: he does not ask "what did you do?" — they could answer that with a narrative — but "did you *know* what you did?", which is unanswerable except by silence. The sūrah has used this verb-pair on the brothers repeatedly: they came to Egypt and "he recognised them while they were unaware" (12:58), and their own defence before the caravan had been an appeal to what the Egyptians knew, "you certainly know that we came not to work corruption in the land" (12:73). Now the minister reverses the direction of the appeal and asks them what they knew about their own past. The chapter's long argument about knowledge — "God knows best concerning that which you describe" (12:77), "above every possessor of knowledge is a knower" (12:76), and the father's "I know from God that which you know not" (12:86) — arrives at its last application, which is the simplest and the hardest: a man asking his brothers whether they understood what they were doing.

**"Joseph and His Brother"**

The pairing of the two names in one object of the verb is the sūrah's way of keeping the two injuries together. Our source records the commentators on precisely this: the question is "about their separating him from Benjamin when they were young, and about their harming him and perpetrating injustices against Benjamin, such as debasing and reviling him." In other words, the chapter does not allow the story of the older wound to be told without the newer one: the same men who threw a boy in a well kept a brother from his father, humiliated the second family, and treated Rachel's two sons as a single case. Yūsuf will not be flattered by being acknowledged as the greater victim; the sentence binds him to Benjamin, and in the next breath calls both of them "my" — "I am Yūsuf and this is my brother" (12:90).

**"When You Were Ignorant" — the Victim's Excuse**

The final clause is the verse's moral centre. *Idh antum jāhilūn* — at a time when you did not know — and our source records both the function and the gloss: Yūsuf "then makes an excuse for his brothers' wrong actions," and the ignorance referred to is either "their ignorance of the vileness of their actions" or "their general state of ignorance on account of their youth." The sūrah lets the wronged man characterise the wrong as done in a condition of not-knowing, and the move is worth measuring: he could have said "when you were cruel," or "when you hated him," and the chapter would have had to report it. Instead the crime is attributed to *jahl* — ignorance, in the sense of not grasping what an act is — which is the Qur'an's own most generous account of why people do what they do, and the ground on which it asks God not to hold a community to what it says in error ("our Lord, do not take us to task if we forget or make a mistake," 2:286). It is not a denial that the act was wrong; it is a refusal to make the wrong the man's permanent identity.

**What Made Them Know Him**

The sūrah gives no scene of recognition: the question is asked, and the answer comes in the next verse as an astonishment ("Art thou indeed Joseph?"). The commentators, as our source records, fill the gap in three graded ways: the brothers understood who he was when he spoke these words; or when he smiled; or — in a further report — when he removed his crown. What is striking is the first and most sober of these: the words themselves are the disclosure. Nothing in the chapter requires a miracle of recognition, because the sūrah has already established that only Yūsuf speaks this way about his brothers' crime — a man who, having every means to take a reproach, hands them an excuse instead.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:90

> **90** *They said, “Art thou indeed Joseph?” He said, “I am Joseph and this is my brother. God has been gracious unto us. Verily whosoever is reverent and patient—surely God neglects not the reward of the virtuous.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question in the Double Intensifier**

*Qālū a-innaka la-anta Yūsuf*. Arabic can ask "Are you Yūsuf?" in a plain way; the sūrah piles on three emphatics at once — the interrogative, the particle *inna*, and the second *la-* — and the effect is the sound of a man trying to make a fact take hold. It is the only question the brothers are recorded as asking in the whole chapter, and it is not about the famine, the measure, or the bowl. Everything they have said in Egypt up to this point has been defensive argument; the argument ends in one line of astonishment. The sūrah does not describe their faces, and does not tell us whether they fell.

**"I Am Yūsuf, and This Is My Brother"**

*Qāla anā Yūsuf wa-hādhā akhī*. The disclosure has two clauses and no third. He identifies himself; he identifies the brother standing with him — using the demonstrative, as though pointing to a boy who had been in this room as an accused thief, and to whom the chapter's last twenty verses have been devoted. And he does not say "your brother," though he could have: the possessive he chooses throughout is *my* — "my brother," "Yūsuf and his brother" (12:89), and, in the verse's next clause, "God has been gracious unto *us*." The sūrah has Yūsuf speak as the elder brother of Benjamin and as a member of the same family that was wronged, not as a plaintiff with defendants in front of him. The word for graciousness is *manna*: a favour conferred, not a debt repaid — "God has bestowed His grace upon us," meaning that what looks like a reconciliation between eleven brothers is, in the chapter's own account, an act of divine solicitude toward the household (12:76, 12:90). It is the last of the sūrah's many reminders that its plot has a single author; the minister, the granary, the bowl, the guarantee, and the father's white eyes were the means.

**A Rule Spoken over His Own Experience**

*Inna man ittaqā wa-ṣabara fa-inna llāha lā yuḍīʿu ajra al-muḥsinīn*. Yūsuf does not congratulate himself and does not lecture them about the past; he states a principle, in the grammar of a general rule: whoever guards against God and is patient — God does not waste the reward of those who do beautiful things. Three elements deserve attention.

First, the two qualities are the two the chapter has spent its length dramatising: *taqwā*, which was his refusal in the house ("my Lord has made beautiful my accommodation; verily the wrongdoers will not prosper," 12:23), and *ṣabr*, which was his preaching in the cell ("Judgment belongs to God alone," 12:40) and the prisoners' estimate of him ("we see you among the beautiful-doers," 12:36) and his father's twice-repeated formula ("beautiful patience," 12:18, 83, 84). The verse is a summary of the sūrah's own vocabulary, and its hero is quoting his own life.

Second, the reward named is *ajr*, wages due, and the verb is the striking one: God does not *lose*, does not *waste*, does not allow to go missing the reward of the beautiful-doer. This is exactly the clause the chapter has already put in God's mouth as He described the maturity of Yūsuf — "We gave him wisdom and knowledge; thus do We recompense the beautiful-doers" (12:22) — and our source records that the same sentence, in this or a closely cognate form, belongs to a family of Qur'anic assurances, pointing the reader to al-Tawba 9:120 and Hūd 11:11 and 115, alongside this sūrah's own 12:56. The theology of the promise is that of 11:115, "and be patient, for God does not waste the reward of the beautiful-doers," and its past tense is important: in this world the beautiful-doer may be in prison for years; the verse guarantees only that nothing is lost.

Third, the clause is offered to the brothers as much as to himself. If God does not waste the reward of those who do good, then the repentance that is about to be spoken ("God has preferred you over us, and we were at fault," 12:91) is itself a kind of *iḥsān* — the honesty to name the other's excellence as God's giving — and it will be rewarded with the words of 12:92. The chapter's reward-system is, at its end, a mercy-system: the last word Yūsuf says about the debt between them is not *jazāʾ* but *maghfira* (12:92), and the verse's own logic explains why: what God does not waste, He does not need to collect.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:91

> **91** *“By God!” they said, “God has preferred thee over us, and we were at fault.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Oath They Can Now Keep**

*Qālū tā-llāhi laqad ātharaka llāhu ʿalaynā wa-in kunnā li-khāṭiʾīn*. The sūrah has heard these men swear three times in Egypt and watched each oath collapse into self-justification: "by God, you certainly know that we came not to work corruption in the land" (12:73); "by God, wilt thou go on remembering Joseph till thou art ill" (12:85); and the message's "verily we are truthful" (12:82). This fourth oath is the first that indicts the swearer. They do not swear that they are innocent of anything; they swear that God has done something to one other than themselves. The chapter's ethics of speech has been leading here since the bloodied shirt (12:18), where the father's reply was that God is the one asked for help "against that which you describe": an oath becomes truthful when it stops describing a person's own excellence and starts naming what God has given somebody else.

**"God Has Preferred Thee over Us": the Word for Selflessness**

The verb is *āthara*, to give another a greater share, and its verbal noun *īthār* is the name in Islamic ethics for preferring others to oneself — the quality the Qur'an praises in the citizens of Madina, who "prefer others over themselves even when they are in privation" (59:9). The sūrah's choice is precise, because it is answering the word that started the whole affair. What the brothers complained of as boys was not injustice but affection: Yūsuf and his brother were *loved more* by the father than they were (12:8), and the grievance at being second in someone's heart is the seed of everything the chapter goes on to describe. Now, after the twenty-odd years the chapter has spent in Egypt, they use the language of preference again — but the one who prefers is God, the preference is granted to the victim, and it is affirmed without resentment. The same men, the same subject, a different verb and a different agent: the sūrah marks the death of envy in a single word.

Their confession has a further quality worth noting. They do not say "you are better than us," which is what flattery would have supplied; they say God has preferred you, which is a statement about God's giving and therefore a statement about themselves as recipients. Envy, in the sūrah's psychology, is a refusal of God's distribution; its cure is not a smaller opinion of oneself but a recognition that the good in another person was put there by the one who divides. The sentence is thus also a piece of theology of the kind the chapter has been building since 12:53, where Zulaykhā refused to absolve herself, and 12:90, where Yūsuf refused to take credit for his own preservation: "God has been gracious unto us."

**"We Were at Fault"**

*Wa-in kunnā li-khāṭiʾīn*.
