The word *khaṭīʾa* is error, miss, the sin that is a wrong aim rather than a settled malice — and the sūrah will use it once more, in the same construction, when the brothers stand before their father: "seek forgiveness for us for our sins; truly we were at fault" (12:97). The repetition is a structure, not a rhyme: the men who said to Yūsuf "you certainly know that we came not to work corruption in the land" (12:73) end by saying, to the two people they wronged most, that they were wrongdoers. Two things about their phrasing matter. They do not minimise ("we did what we had to do") and they do not dramatise ("we are the worst of sinners"); they state a fact about their own record, in a noun that leaves room for repentance. And they do not ask, in this verse, to be forgiven — that request goes to the father, and comes in 12:97 — while the forgiveness they receive here comes from Yūsuf's lips as a decision about conduct rather than an absolution (12:92).

For the reader, the sūrah's verdict on this confession is given by its placement. The brothers' sentence stands between Yūsuf's question — "do you know what you have done… when you were ignorant?" (12:89) — and his reply that there is no reproach against them that day (12:92). Recognition, confession, pardon: the three lines of the reconciliation, and not one word about the punishment the same chapter's own criminal code had prescribed at the brothers' demand ("thus do we recompense the wrongdoers," 12:75). The sūrah has kept that law in the record so that its cancellation could be seen for what it is.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:92

> **92** *He said, “There is no reproach against you this day. God will forgive you. And He is the most Merciful of the merciful.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Legal Sentence, Not a Sentiment**

*Qāla lā tathrība ʿalaykumu l-yawma*. The words are a ruling. *Tathrīb* is blame in the sense of a strike: our source records that the word here "can also mean physical punishment (*ʿiqāb*)," and that the saying as a whole is described in some sources as "a beautiful form of forgiveness." Taken together the two glosses give the sentence its full range: no blow and no rebuke falls on you today. The sūrah has been explicit about the machinery the minister could have used. The brothers had themselves proposed the code by which a man is surrendered for a theft ("thus do we recompense the wrongdoers," 12:75), the father's pledge was in their hands (12:66, 80), and the Egyptian law of the land had been over-ridden once already on God's instruction (12:76). Yūsuf holds the letter of the law, the evidence, and the office, and closes the case with a negative: no reproach — that is, nothing is owed, nothing will be extracted, no record will be kept open.

The qualifier "this day" has struck readers as oddly narrow, and the commentators' answer is that the day is named because it is the day of favour and of the end of the affair: what is suspended today is not merely one instance of blame but the whole practice of counting. The sūrah, whose hero has already declined to voice the reproach he was entitled to make (12:77), now declines to make it once, in public, in the language of law.

**"God Will Forgive You"**

*Yaghfiru llāhu lakum*. He does not say "I forgive you," and the omission is the verse's deepest teaching. In the Qur'an's moral universe a wrong between persons is not an offence the victim can cancel at will, because the injury is also a sin, and the sin belongs to God. What the wronged party can do is two things he does here: renounce the punishment that was his right, and ask forgiveness for the one who hurt him. Yūsuf's pardon is therefore a decision about conduct plus a prayer — and it is the prayer that is the harder part, since to ask God to forgive a person is to wish them well at the very point where they have made themselves unable to be wished well. The sūrah has trained the reader to hear this properly, because it made him say, decades earlier, "my Lord! Prison is dearer to me than that to which they call me" (12:33): a man whose refusals and whose mercies both run through the same channel of asking God.

The closing clause puts the request in the sūrah's own superlative: *wa-anta arḥamu r-rāḥimīn* — "and He is the most Merciful of the merciful." In this chapter that exact phrase occurs only twice, here and on the father's lips when he consented to the journey that was going to take Benjamin from him: "God is the best of keepers, and He is the most Merciful of the merciful" (12:64). The sūrah's architecture joins the two: the sentence the father said in trust is the sentence the son says in forgiveness, and both are addressed to the being whose mercy the chapter has, line by line, demonstrated.

**The Verse the Prophet Recited**

The saying became, in Islam, the standing formula for a conquest that could have been a reckoning. Our source records the report attached to it: at the taking of Makkah, when the city's idolaters had no hope of escape, the Prophet asked them what they supposed he would do with them; they answered, "good — a noble brother, son of a noble brother," and he replied: "I say to you as Yūsuf said to his brothers: no reproach against you this day; go, for you are free." The report is preserved in the sīra literature and in the ḥadīth collections, and its value for the reading of this verse is that it treats 12:92 as a rule of conduct rather than a story. The one who is strong, holding the innocent and the guilty in one hand, says what the victim of a family's jealousy said at the height of his own power. Where the sūrah's own sequel continues, the amnesty is completed in the most material terms available to it: a shirt sent to a blind father's face, the family brought "all together" (12:93), and Egypt opened to them as a place of settlement (12:99) — the chapter's final proof that a pardon, to be real, has to leave the pardoned with somewhere to live.

Two modern sentences follow from the verse. First, an apology is not a payment: the brothers ask for nothing here and receive a release, which is why their request for forgiveness comes only afterwards, and from the father (12:97-98). Second, the strongest form of closing a grievance is not to declare it closed but to state the terms on which it stays closed — no reproach today — and to ask for the other person's absolution from a being more merciful than the injured party can manage to be.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:93

> **93** *Take this shirt of mine and cast it upon my father’s face; he will come to see. And bring me your family, all together.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Object That Has Been the Chapter's Evidence**

*Ḥammilū qamīṣī hādhā fa-alqūhu ʿalā wajhi abī yaʾti baṣīran*. The shirt has been the sūrah's court of appeal from the beginning. It was brought to Yaʿqūb with "false blood" and answered with the sentence he would repeat twice (12:18); it was torn from behind in a locked house, and that tear — not the man's word, not the woman's — was accepted as the proof of Yūsuf's innocence (12:26-27). Now the same kind of object is despatched as the instrument of healing, and the chapter's logic is exact: what carried a lie becomes the carrier of relief, what convicted a boy becomes the medicine of his father. The sūrah's realism about evidence is thus completed by a realism about consolation: objects do not change their nature, only their use, and a family's history is stored in things.

Notice the possessive and the demonstrative — "this shirt of mine." He does not send a letter, a physician, gold, or an order; he sends a garment with his identification on it, so that the old man will receive, in the same gesture, the proof that his son lives and the touch of what belonged to him. The verse's second sentence, "he will come to see," is left unexplained: the cloth is cast on a face and sight returns. The mechanism is not specified, and that restraint is deliberate.

**Inspiration, and the Reports around It**

Our source preserves two notes on the verse. The first is that Yūsuf's order to have the shirt cast over Yaʿqūb's face "was reportedly based on divine inspiration" — that is, the medicine is not a folk remedy but a disclosed instruction, and the sūrah has already given Yūsuf the standing of a man taught by God ("this is among the things my Lord has taught me," 12:37; and of his father, "he had knowledge of what We taught him," 12:68). The second is a legend the commentators transmit rather than assert: that this is the same shirt made of the silk of Paradise which Gabriel brought to Yūsuf when he was in the well. The embellishment is a piety, not a proof-text; its imaginative function is nonetheless visible, since it ties the well (12:15) to the reunion by a single article of clothing, and the sūrah has done something similar with its own means — the shirt that saved him from a charge, the shirt that will save his father from blindness, one garment in both directions.

**"Bring Me Your Family, All Together"**

*Waʾtūnī bi-ahlikum ajmaʿīn*. The second half of the command converts a family's rescue into a migration. Everything the chapter has been working toward — the eleven sons' transformation, the father's preserved certainty, Benjamin's restored honour — is now to be gathered into one household in Egypt, and the verse's word for it is "all together," the same totalising plural the father had used in his prayer of hope: "it may be that God will bring them to me all together" (12:83). The sūrah's audience has been waiting for the two "all together"s to meet, and they do, in a single unremarkable instruction carried by one of the brothers.

There is a discipline of leadership in the ordering of the verse too. Yūsuf, with the treasury of a famine under his hand, does not send an escort to fetch his family in state; he gives his brothers a task. He had seen what trust did to them when they were given a boy to keep (12:12, 64, 80), and the chapter's solution is not to set them aside but to employ them in the one errand they cannot fail and would not wish to fail. A man who has learned, at the cost of a generation, how fragile his family's honesty is, rebuilds it by giving them responsibility for its healing. That is the sūrah's last word about the management of wrongdoers before it turns, in the next verses, to the forgiveness of them.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:94

> **94** *And as the caravan set off, their father said, “Truly I sense the scent of Joseph, if you think me not senile!”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Departure, and a Smell**

*Wa-lammā faṣalat al-ʿīru qāla abūhum innī la-ajidu rīḥa Yūsuf*. The verse begins with a caravan and ends with a scent, and between them the sūrah has moved its whole theology of providence into a nose. *Faṣalat* is the verb of separation and setting out — the caravan "left," cut loose from the edge of Egypt — and the word for the caravan, *ʿīr*, is the same the chapter used once before, when a herald shouted at it: "O you caravan! Truly you are thieves!" (12:70). The road that carried the accusation now carries the cure, and the sūrah's single repeated noun makes the connection without a word of comment.

What Yaʿqūb says is not that he sees, but that he "finds" (*ajidu*) the *rīḥ* of Yūsuf. *Rīḥ* is wind before it is smell: the word names the thing that arrives from a distance, carrying what it touched. Our source records the commentators' explanation of how a blind man in Canaan smells a shirt in transit — the scent was the heavenly smell that came from the shirt (al-Baghawī and others), and "some say that this was a miracle because Jacob and Joseph were prophets." The sūrah itself says nothing of the mechanism, and the restraint is characteristic: the verse does not report a vision, a voice, or an angel, only an old man's statement about an odour, made in the first person and hedged at once.

**"If You Think Me Not Senile"**

*Law lā an tu-fannidūn*. The verb *fanīd* is the dotage of age, the state of a man whose opinions no longer count because he is no longer thought to be in possession of himself; the brothers had used a near-equivalent insult at 12:95, and the father knows they will use it again. His sentence is therefore constructed like a claim that expects to be dismissed: "I find the scent of Yūsuf — unless you insist on treating me as a fool." The grammar is an apology and, at the same time, a defiance: he will not withdraw the assertion, only concede that the evidence for it is unavailable to the people he is talking to.

That concession is the verse's psychological masterpiece. Yaʿqūb has staked everything on a knowledge "from God that which you know not" (12:86), and the sūrah shows what such certainty costs inside a family: it is indistinguishable, from outside, from the obsession of a grieving old man. The chapter has already marked his persistence as a kind of illness in the mouths of his sons ("wilt thou go on remembering Joseph till thou art ill," 12:85). Now he names the diagnosis himself, before they can, and hands it back: you may call me senile, but I smell him. The hedge is not doubt. It is the courtesy of a man who knows that his best evidence will sound like madness.

**The Breath of God, in the Same Root**

One word-links in the passage is invisible in translation and decisive in Arabic. At 12:87 the father forbade despair "of God's *rawḥ*" — relief, spaciousness, a breath — and here he finds the *rīḥ* of Yūsuf. Both words are from the same root of air and rest (*r-w-ḥ*), and the sequence is exact: a prohibition against despairing of relief, followed immediately by relief arriving as air. The sūrah's theology of hope is thus not an attitude but a physiology — breath, scent, eyes whitened and returned — and this is how the chapter has argued its whole case since 12:64, when Yaʿqūb consigned his sons to "God, the best of keepers." What is kept, in this verse, is not the boy in a saddlebag but the old man's perception, which the plot will vindicate to the letter in the next two verses: the bearer of glad tidings arrives (12:96) and the father's claim turns out to have been knowledge rather than fancy.

For readers, 12:94 is the sūrah's lesson in the social risk of conviction. A person who knows something he cannot demonstrate is always tempted either to overstate it ("believe me") or to abandon it ("I am only an old fool"); Yaʿqūb does neither. He states what he perceives, attaches the discount his hearers will apply, and keeps the perception intact. The chapter rewards that exactness — not by removing the brothers' scepticism first, but by letting the smell precede the shirt by a whole verse, so that the family learns, in the order of the narrative, that their father had been seeing clearly the entire time.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:95

> **95** *They said, “By God! Truly thou art astray as of old.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same Charge as at the Beginning**

*Qālū tā-llāhi innaka la-fī ḍalālika al-qadīm*. The sentence is a bracket, and the sūrah has placed its twin at the start of the story. When the brothers first asked to take Yūsuf out, their reason was not hatred but a judgment about their father: "Joseph and his brother are more loved by our father than we are… surely our father is plainly in error" (12:8). Now, with the second son lost and the first one a minister in Egypt, they say the identical thing in the identical word — *ḍalāl* — and add the adjective *qadīm*, ancient, long-standing, incurable: "you are in your error of old." Between 12:8 and 12:95 the chapter has told the reader everything about who was mistaken. That is the whole of the verse's art: nothing needs to be answered.

Our source records the commentators' gloss on what the insult means here: by "astray" the brothers mean Jacob's overabundant love for Joseph and his hope to meet with him, "which they thought unreasonable under the circumstances as they knew them." The phrase "as they knew them" is the key to the verse. The brothers are not being cruel for no reason; they are reasoning correctly from a defective docket. A son torn by a wolf (12:17), a second son surrendered under an oath and lost in a foreign court over a stolen bowl, an old man who weeps until his eyes whiten (12:84) and refuses to send witnesses — on the evidence, the smell of a boy a hundred miles away is the last utterance of a mind coming apart. The sūrah lets their inference stand as an inference. What it shows is that a reasonable conclusion drawn from an incomplete account is how sensible people end up speaking nonsense about a prophet.

**"Of Old": the Word Each Side Uses About the Other**

The adjective *qadīm* accuses Yaʿqūb of a fixed state — this is who you have always been, a man absorbed in Yūsuf. But the chapter has already applied the same temporal diagnosis to the brothers themselves, in their own mouth, in the private council: "do you not know that your father took a solemn pledge from you before God, and *earlier* we failed with regard to Yūsuf?" (12:80). Their error, too, is of long standing; their fault, too, has a history. The sūrah's even-handedness is severe and quiet: each party in the family is describing the other in terms of an old, settled failing, and each is right about the past and wrong about what follows from it. The father's constancy looks like obsession and is knowledge; the brothers' candour looks like sanity and is the residue of a crime they had not yet finished confessing.

There is a further point of diction. The reproach "as of old" is one of a chain of adverbs in this stretch of the sūrah — "as I entrusted his brother to you *before*" (12:64), "a brother of his had stolen *aforetime*" (12:77), "*earlier* you neglected Joseph" (12:80), and here "your *ancient* error" (12:95). The chapter keeps dragging the past into the present tense of the argument, and each time it does so, the party making the point is throwing stones at a history it shares. Yūsuf's father is charged with remembering; the men doing the charging are the ones who cannot get rid of a memory they have never once mentioned to him.

**The Pathology of a Family's Hope**

The verse is a case study in how households interpret one another. Strong feelings in a person who has suffered are read by the relatives as symptoms, and the diagnosis is delivered with an oath ("by God") because the family believes its concern is love. Their concern *is* love — the sūrah has already said as much, noting that this line is a figurative way of telling their father they fear his death (see 12:85) — and that is what makes it effective: the strongest pressure on a convinced person does not come from opponents but from people who are worried about him. Yaʿqūb's answer, in the next verse, is not to argue. The bearer of glad tidings arrives, and he says only: "did I not tell you that I know from God what you know not?" (12:96). A chapter that has spent its length on the ethics of speech gives the old man one triumph, and it is the mildest triumph available: not "you were wrong," but "what I said was true."
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:96

> **96** *And when the bearer of glad tidings came, he cast it upon his face and he was restored to sight. He said, “Did I not say unto you that I know from God that which you know not?”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Man Who Brought the Good News**

*Fa-lammā an jāʾa al-bashīru alqāhu ʿalā wajhihi fa-rtadda baṣīran*. The sūrah names its messenger by his function: *al-bashīr*, the bearer of *bushrā*, glad tidings — the same word the Qur'an uses for the angels who come to Ibrāhīm with the news of a son ("we bring you glad tidings of a knowing boy," 15:53), and the word that belongs to every prophet in the Book's standard pairing, "a bearer of tidings and a warner" (2:119). The choice is not decoration. A verse that could have said "the brother came" or "the one sent came" says instead that a piece of good news arrived, and the news is carried by a human being.

Our source records who that human being is, in a note that repays attention: the bearer of glad tidings who cast the shirt over Yaʿqūb's face was, on one reading, the same brother who had interceded with the others when they were plotting to kill Yūsuf — the unnamed voice of 12:10, "slay not Joseph, but cast him into the depths of the well." The sūrah had kept that man anonymous in the conspiracy; here it gives him the verse's only title. The moral economy is the chapter's own: one merciful sentence spoken inside a bad plan is not lost, and in time it is the same mouth that brings the family its relief. Yūsuf's teaching that "God does not waste the reward of the beautiful-doers" (12:90) has, in other words, already been demonstrated on a bystander who declined to be an executioner.

**"He Was Restored to Sight"**

The healing is told in four words, and they answer, word for word, the scene of the loss. The face on which the shirt is laid is the face that was turned away from the sons (12:84); the eyes that now return are the eyes whose whitening was the chapter's last word about grief (12:84: "his eyes had turned white with grief, and he was choked with anguish"). *Irtadda baṣīran* — he became, again, a seer — and the sūrah's brevity is reverent: it does not say the shirt healed, it does not narrate a miracle, it reports the order of events (cast, restored) and lets the reader put God in the gap, as the whole chapter has asked them to do since the dry well and the caravan (12:19, 70). What it does make clear is that the cure is physical. Yaʿqūb was not consoled; he was healed, and his eyes are the evidence.

The comparison the sūrah invites with the scriptural parallel is instructive. In Genesis, the report of Yūsuf's survival is at first not believed — Jacob's spirit fails him because he cannot take it in — and only when he hears "all the words of Joseph… the spirit of Jacob their father revived" (Genesis 45:26-27). The Qur'an reverses the sequence entirely: the father believes before any evidence, senses the shirt while the caravan is still leaving, is reproached as an old fool (12:95), and is then vindicated by the same means he never doubted. Revelation here is not what produces faith; faith is what survives until the revelation arrives.

**"Did I Not Say Unto You"**

*Qāla a-lam aqul lakum innī aʿlamu mina llāhi mā lā taʿlamūn*. The vindication repeats the father's earlier sentence verbatim (12:86), and the repetition is the point of the verse's rhetoric: he does not add a word. He does not say "and you insulted me," nor "you owe me an apology," nor even "what did I tell you about Yūsuf." He restates the epistemic claim — *I know from God what you do not know* — because everything he needs is inside it. A chapter in which the wronged brother suppressed a legitimate reproach (12:77) has taught its father the same self-restraint at the moment when he has, for once, the last word.

The sūrah's sequel makes the restraint pay. The brothers' next sentence is not a defence but a petition: "O father! Seek forgiveness for us from our sins; truly we were at fault" (12:97). Vindication, in the economy of this family, is worth less than intercession; and the father who could have collected a debt of humiliation instead undertakes to ask God for something on their behalf (12:98). That is why the last verse of this healing scene is not about eyes at all. The father was right; he says so once; and then the chapter moves on to the harder and more religious question of what a man does with being right when the people who doubted him are his sons.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:97

> **97** *They said, “O father! Seek forgiveness for us from our sins. Truly we were at fault.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Request They Had Not Yet Made**

*Qālū yā-abānā-ghfir lanā dhunūbanā innā kunnā khāṭiʾīn*. The chapter has, in nine verses, extracted every kind of statement from these men: an oath of innocence before a caravan (12:73), a draft of a report to their father limited to what they knew (12:81), a demand that he accept the town as a witness (12:82), an oath about his preference for the lost son (12:91), and a diagnosis of his grief as madness (12:95). Now they ask for the one thing they had never asked for: forgiveness. The order of the sūrah's scenes is a lesson in what repentance requires. The brothers could not have requested this from their father a hundred verses earlier — not because he would have refused, but because they would not have known what they were asking. It takes the bowl, the guarantee, the search and the recognition to bring eleven men to the sentence "our sins," in the plural, without an excuse attached.

Two words in the sentence are worth weighing. *Dhunūb* — sins — is plural and undifferentiated; they do not itemise, and the sūrah does not itemise for them: the chapter's way of keeping the crime general is not leniency but realism about how remorse works, since a person who lists his offences one by one is often bargaining. And *khāṭiʾīn* — "we were at fault," from *khaṭīʾa*, an error, a miss — is the same word they had used in front of Yūsuf (12:91). The sūrah has them confess to the two people they wronged in identical terms, so that the record shows the confession was not adjusted to the audience: the men do not claim necessity before the father and modesty before the minister.

**"Seek Forgiveness for Us": Asking Another to Pray**

The verb is not "forgive us" but "seek forgiveness for us" — *-ghfir lanā* addressed to a human being who cannot forgive, meaning *staghfir lanā*. The sons know the limit of their father's authority and the reach of his prayer, and the Qur'an itself treats that as a real thing: "if only, when they wronged themselves, they had come to you and asked God's forgiveness, and the Messenger had asked forgiveness for them, they would have found God accepting repentance, merciful" (4:64). The verse establishes in the Book's own practice what the sūrah here shows in a family: an intercession is not a substitute for repentance but its completion, the moment a person admits that the harm he did is between the one he wronged and God, and asks the wronged party to speak on his behalf.

There is a further delicacy in the request's direction. Yūsuf has already said "God will forgive you" over them (12:92); they now go to Yaʿqūb with the same request, and the sūrah permits both — as if to say that a wronged brother's amnesty and a father's prayer are different debts, and the second had to be asked for. The chapter's reconciliation is therefore not a single event but a set of settlements: the granary's account closed (12:92), the house's account not yet (12:97-98), and both handled without any of the parties presuming to do what belongs to God.

**The Scene the Sūrah Skips**

Our source records that this conversation took place after the brothers came back home from Egypt; the sūrah, which has narrated every stage of the two journeys in dialogue, here moves from the father's healing (12:96) to the sons standing before him with no scene at all. The omission is the chapter's last and gentlest piece of craft. Everything that the brothers did — the well, the shirt, the lie, the bowl — has been shown, and the one thing the text will not stage is the moment of a father's forgiveness, since it belongs to God and will be spoken of, in the next verse, only as a promise. What the sūrah does show is the sons' side of the transaction, because that is the part a listener can imitate: go to the person you hurt, say what you did in the plural, do not argue about the reasons, and ask him to pray that the matter be closed above your heads.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:98

> **98** *He said, “I shall indeed seek forgiveness for you from my Lord. Truly He is Forgiving, Merciful.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Promise in the Future Tense**

*Qāla sa-wafa astaghfiru lakum min rabbī*. The father does not forgive them. He promises to ask God to forgive them, and the promise is placed in the future with the particle Arabic uses when a commitment is both certain and deferred. Every element of that sentence is instruction. To say "I forgive you" is, in the sūrah's theology, to claim an authority no creature has: the wrong done to Yaʿqūb was also a sin against God, and only He remits it. To say "I shall ask" is to keep one's place — the injured party as suppliant, not as judge. And to say it in the future is to decline to close a matter that requires time.

Our source preserves the reason the commentators give for the deferral: some record that Jacob "delayed his prayer for forgiveness a certain period of time," and one explanation offered is that he did so "to ascertain whether they were being sincere and truthful." The note is not a legend about the verse; it is drawn from the verse's own grammar, since a man who intended to pray at once would have said so. Repentance, in this reading, is proved by being lived with, and a father's absolution is withheld not in spite of the sons' grief but because it is a real gift that cannot be handed over in the same moment it is requested — otherwise it is only the ending of a quarrel.

**"From My Lord": the Ground of the Hope**

*Innahu huwa al-Ghafūru al-Raḥīm*. The closing clause is a statement about God's names, and the sūrah's placement of it is what makes the verse consoling rather than severe. The promise is deferred; the attribute is not. Yaʿqūb does not say "perhaps He will," but names Him as the Forgiving, the Merciful — the two names that, in the Qur'an's usage, answer precisely the case of people who have wronged themselves and come asking. The chapter has already set forgiveness in this vocabulary: Yūsuf, with power in his hands, had said "God will forgive you; and He is the most Merciful of the merciful" (12:92), and the sūrah's two pardon-speeches — the son's and the father's — are built on the same formula, and both are requests and prophecies rather than verdicts. What the verse supplies to the eleven brothers, then, is not the relief of a concluded transaction but something firmer: a promise made by a weak man, secured by a description of God that he cannot control.

**The Reconciliation the Sūrah Does Not Narrate**

One of the chapter's most noticed silences is here. The sūrah tells us of the father's undertaking, and then it moves, without a word about the interval, to the family standing before Yūsuf in Egypt (12:99). It never reports Yaʿqūb praying for his sons, and never reports the answer. The effect is not omission but distribution: the story of the reconciliation has already been told in the only form that could be verified — what each party said and did — while the acceptance of the prayer is left with the One whose name it was asked under. Readers of the sūrah have taken the lesson literally, since the two names of 12:98 are among the most frequently invoked in Islam for exactly this situation: the sinner's hope is not his own sincerity but the description God gives of Himself.

For a family, the verse's practical content is about the difference between two acts that are usually conflated. Yūsuf's "no reproach against you this day" (12:92) settled conduct: nobody would be punished, the accounts were closed, the granary would be open. Yaʿqūb's "I shall seek forgiveness for you" (12:98) left the inner matter to its proper address, in God's hands, on a horizon he did not fix. A household can survive the first without the second, but it does not heal. The chapter's next verse — the family entering Egypt "in security, if God wills" (12:99) — is the sūrah's way of showing what the second act eventually produced: not a declaration that nothing happened, but a place where the people who did it could live together.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:99

> **99** *So when they entered upon Joseph, he drew his parents close to himself and said, “Enter Egypt in security, if God wills!”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verb of Shelter, Used Twice in a Generation**

*Fa-lammā dakhalaʿalā Yūsufa āwā ilayhi abawayhi*. The verb *āwā ilā* — to take to oneself as a shelter — is the word the sūrah used when Benjamin arrived: "he took him into his shelter (*āwā ilayhi*)" (12:69), and it is the word behind *mathwā*, the "accommodation" Yūsuf praised in Potiphar's house ("my Lord has made beautiful my accommodation," 12:23) and which the chapter has used for a seat, a home and a resting-place (12:21, 59). Now the same verb is turned on the parents, and the effect is a chapter closing in the vocabulary it opened with: the boy who was given shelter is the man who gives it, and the household that lost a child to a well is received into a country. The dual *abawayhi* — "his two parents" — is the sūrah's small precision; it does not say "his father" alone, and the two who will be raised to the throne in the next verse are named as a pair, since both were destroyed by the same grief.

**"Enter Egypt in Security, If God Wills"**

*Udkhulū Miṣra in shāʾa llāhu āminīn*. The sentence has a form the Qur'an elsewhere treats as a rule of speech: a future or an imperative concerning what is not yet in one's power is to be hedged with the exception. "And never say of a thing, 'truly I will do that tomorrow,' unless [you add] 'if God wills'" (18:23-24) — and the sūrah's audience is meant to notice that a man who had become the de facto authority of the most organised state of his age, holding the grain of seven years, uses the exception when he invites his family into a city. It is a prophet's education of the reader at the moment of the chapter's greatest triumph: security is not a possession; it is something asked for, and its grant depends on a will other than his.

The word *āminīn* — secure — closes the sūrah's long argument about safety. Yaʿqūb had feared for a son in the desert: "truly it grieves me that you should go with him. And I fear that the wolf may eat him, while you are heedless of him" (12:13), and when he released Benjamin he handed the case to "God, the best of keepers" (12:64). Both fears were realised in the letter and defeated in the end: a son was lost and a son was kept, and the family stands now in the land of the famine without hunger. The verse's promise is also, historically, the sūrah's etiology of Israel in Egypt — the entry that will, in other chapters, become the sojourn from which Mūsā is sent — and our source notes that the same construction returns in Sūrah al-Fatḥ, where God fulfils His Messenger's vision with the words that the believers "will enter the Sacred Mosque, if God wills, secure" (48:27). The parallel is exact and its point is doctrinal: a vision given by God is executed by God, the exception clause is attached to both entries, and in both cases the security is the thing promised, not the destination.

**A Greeting Without a Speech**

Notice what the verse does not contain. There is no account of the journey from Canaan, no description of the parents' reaction to seeing their son in his office, no conversation about the years. The sūrah, which has given the family dozens of lines of argument in the previous fifty verses, gives them here one embrace and one sentence of permission. It is the chapter's last word on how reconciliation actually looks: after twenty years of grievance, what remains to be said is an invitation, and the invitation is phrased as a prayer. What follows — the parents raised to the throne, the brothers falling, the dream named at last (12:100) — is left to the sūrah's single public act, so that the family's history is ended in front of everybody, with nobody's past recited.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:100

> **100** *And he raised his parents up to the throne, and they [all] fell prostrate toward him. He said, “O my father! This is the fulfillment of my vision; my Lord has made it come true. He was good to me when He brought me forth from prison, and brought thee from the desert, after Satan incited evil between my brothers and me. Indeed, my Lord is Subtle in that which He wills. Truly He is the Knower, the Wise.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Dream, at Last, in Front of the Brothers**

*Wa-rafaʿa abawayhi ʿala al-ʿarshi wa-kharrū la-hu sujjadā*. The chapter's first scene was a boy telling his father a dream and being told not to tell his brothers (12:4-5); its last public act is the dream performed in their presence, and the sūrah's point is that the revelation was kept in order to be revealed at the right hour. Yūsuf does not say, in this moment, "do you remember the eleven stars?" — the sentence is addressed to the father, not to the brothers: "O my father! This is the interpretation of my vision of old; my Lord has made it true." The eleven who heard it as children will have worked out the arithmetic themselves, and the sūrah's commentaries spell out what the verse leaves implied: the parents and the eleven brothers correspond to the sun, the moon and the eleven stars. The prostration is therefore not an act of worship but the fulfilment of a sign, and it is directed "toward him" (*lahu*), in the direction of the man, as the received translation's "toward" keeps it.

The verse's first verb is also a piece of ethics: *rafaʿa abawayhi* — he raised his two parents. Our source records the commentators' gloss that this means he seated them with him on the king's throne, an honour that a ruler of Egypt would not have given to foreign shepherds except, as the sūrah has made him say, by the grace of God. The boy who was thrown down a well and sold downward through a house of slaves raises his parents up to the seat of power: the chapter's vertical imagery — the "degrees" God raises whom He wills (12:76) — ends in a physical elevation of the old.

**A Prostration of Honour, Not of Worship**

Muslim readers of this verse have always had a doctrinal question to settle, since prostration to another human being is the one act the Qur'an reserves to God: "do not prostrate to the sun or the moon, but prostrate to God who created them" (41:37). The answer the tradition gives is that the prostration here was a greeting and an honour, permitted in those laws and not in this one — our source records exactly that the act "was a way of greeting and honouring that person, much like standing up for someone out of respect or kissing the hand." The same principle governs the other prostrations of the Qur'an's stories: the angels falling to Adam at God's command (2:34, 15:29) and Yūsuf's parents here are not acts of devotion to a creature but obeisance performed because God willed it, and the sharīʿa of this community closed the practice so that the gesture could not slide into what it resembles. That is why the verse says *sujjad* without saying *lillāh*: the sūrah narrates, and the law of the Prophet ﷺ regulates.

**"He Brought Me Forth from Prison": the Tact of the Grateful**

Read Yūsuf's inventory of mercy with the chapter behind you. He thanks God for two exits — out of the prison, and out of the desert, the latter meaning his father's emigration from Canaan — and he does not thank God for the thing his audience is waiting to hear: deliverance from the well. Our source preserves the note that makes the omission the sūrah's finest moral detail: Joseph "does not mention the well from which he was delivered, only the prison, since he had already told his brothers, 'there is no reproach against you this day.'" A man who has publicly forgone the right to blame them does not seize the chance to describe their crime as his own deliverance, even in a sentence addressed to God. Repentance, in this chapter, is protected by the tongue of the forgiven.

The second half of his sentence performs the same courtesy in theology: "after Satan incited evil between me and my brothers." The verb *nazagha* is of stinging and provocation — the same root gives the Qur'an its word for a goad of Satan that a believer answers by seeking refuge ("and if an incitement from Satan incites you, seek refuge in God," 7:200) — and by transferring the agency to Satan Yūsuf does two things at once: he tells the truth about the mechanism of a family's quarrel and he refuses to assign it to the persons present. Our source records the exegetes' reading of the clause in precisely these terms: his blaming of Satan "is tantamount to his making an excuse for them for their wrong actions," the same courtesy he had shown at 12:89 ("when you were ignorant").

**"My Lord is Subtle in That Which He Wills"**

*Inna rabbī laṭīfun limā yashāʾu innahu huwa al-ʿalīmu al-ḥakīm*. The name *laṭīf* carries both senses Arabic holds in one word: fine, and therefore penetrating; gentle, and therefore unobtrusive. Our source records the gloss the commentators prefer: "the manner in which God carries out His will is subtle." Nothing in the chapter has been coarse. A cup in a saddle, a torn shirt, a returned payment, a promise hedged with "unless you are surrounded," a bowl "found," a father's eyes whitened and then returned — every turn of the plot has moved through the smallest available opening, and the man who has lived inside that craft names it, at the end, as an attribute of God. It is fitting that the sūrah's final theological sentence should repeat exactly the pair the father used when there was nothing left but hope — "truly He is the Knowing, the Wise" (12:83) — so that the chapter ends where Yaʿqūb stood: with the knowledge and the wisdom of God asserted over a family's entire history, now no longer on trust.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:101

> **101** *My Lord! Thou hast given me something of sovereignty, and taught me the interpretation of events. Originator of the heavens and the earth! Thou art my Protector in this world and the Hereafter! Take me as a submitter unto Thyself, and admit me to the company of the righteous.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hero's Last Words Are a Petition, Not a Speech**

*Rabbi qad ātaytanī mina al-mulki wa-ʿallamtanī min taʾwīli al-aḥādīth*. The chapter that has been built almost entirely out of dialogue — a boy and a father, brothers and a father, a woman and a household, prisoners and a minister, a king and his notables — ends with its hero speaking to nobody in the room. Yūsuf's final recorded sentence is a prayer addressed to God, and it has no addressee among the eleven brothers who have just prostrated, no father to thank, no court to harangue. That is the sūrah's last instruction about how a story of providence is to be received: the man who has been through the well, the slave-market, the accusation, the prison and the throne takes the achievement of his life as the material of a supplication.

Two grammatical details do the theological work. He says "you have given me *of* sovereignty" and "you have taught me *of* the interpretation of events," and the partitive "of" governs both: our source records that the phrase is to be read against his standing — Yūsuf "was granted something of sovereignty since he had the highest standing in Egypt next to the king" (see 12:56-57) — so the most successful political career in the chapter is described as a portion, not a possession. And the two gifts he names are precisely the two the chapter has been testing him with: power over grain and people, and knowledge of what dreams mean. He does not claim either; he thanks for both, and gratitude, in the sūrah's own earlier arithmetic, is the condition of increase ("if you are thankful, I will surely increase you," 14:7, in the Book's phrase; the sūrah itself says "thus do We recompense the beautiful-doers," 12:22).

**"Originator of the Heavens and the Earth"**

*Fāṭira al-samāwāti wa-al-arḍ*. The vocative is not a general compliment; it names the Maker of the very bodies that began the story. A child told his father, "I saw eleven stars, and the sun and the moon; I saw them prostrating before me" (12:4), and the chapter has spent a hundred verses arriving at the hour when the sun and moon are represented by two aged parents on a throne in Egypt. At that moment Yūsuf addresses God as the One who originated those lights — the same title the Qur'an uses in a family of verses where creation is spoken of as an act without a model (2:117; 6:14 and 6:101; 35:1; 39:46; 42:11). The theology is exact: a dream about astral bodies is fulfilled by the power of the One who made bodies, so that the honour that falls to a prophet is turned, in the same breath, away from him. The sūrah also names its God as *walī* — "You are my Protector (*walīyy*) in this world and in the Hereafter" — and our source records the logic of the word: the believers' Lord is their Protector (2:257), those under His protection are His *awliyāʾ*, derived from the same root, "upon whom no fear shall come" (10:62) and "for them are the glad tidings in the life of this world and in the Hereafter" (10:64).

**"Take Me as a Submitter"**

*Tawaffanī musliman wa-alḥiqnī bi-l-ṣāliḥīn*. The prophet who has everything asks, at the end, for two things he does not have: the manner of his death and the company after it. *Tawaffā* is the verb of taking a soul in death, and the request is that the taking find him in submission — our source records the sense exactly: "a request to God that he not die except in a state of complete submission to God's will," echoing the command "you must not die except as submitters" (3:102), and says the prayer is "in accordance with the creed of Abraham" (22:78, where the name *muslimūn* is given to the community of Ibrāhīm). The second half asks to be joined to the righteous — the word *alḥiq* is an adjuring, as if a servant were asking to be attached to a household he admires. And it is the sūrah's own ring: at 12:6 the promise to Yūsuf was that God would "complete His blessing upon you and upon the house of Yaʿqūb, just as He completed it upon your two forefathers, Ibrāhīm and Isḥāq"; at 12:101 the man asks, at the height of that completion, to be appended to the righteous — the forefathers he was promised. The chapter ends where its promise was made.

The practical sentence the tradition has drawn from this is the one the verse makes unavoidable: a good life is not a conclusion, and a believer's last petition is about the ending. "Deeds are judged by their closings," the maxim says, and the sūrah shows why the phrase belongs to the prosperous rather than only to the wicked — Yūsuf, with the treasury of a famine at his back and his parents seated beside him, does not ask for more years. He asks to be taken as one who has submitted.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:102

> **102** *These are among the accounts of the unseen which We reveal unto thee. And thou wert not with them when they decided upon their affair and plotted.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Narrator Steps Back into the Room**

*Dhālika min anbaʾi al-ghaybi nūḥīhi ilayka wa-ma kunta ladayhim idh ajmaʿū amra-hum wa-hum yakīdūn*. The address changes for the first time since the sūrah's opening, and the change is the point. The chapter began with "We narrate to you the best of narratives" (12:3) and it now closes with "these are of the accounts of the unseen that We reveal to you" — the same first-person plural, the same verb of disclosure, the same unnamed hearer. The sūrah is an inclusion: the story of Yūsuf is framed, at both ends, not as a moral fable retold but as something being delivered right now, in a particular room, to a particular man, by God. Everything the reader has been asked to feel for a hundred verses is, in this sentence, re-labelled as evidence.

The claim is narrow and testable. "You were not with them." The Prophet ﷺ was not present at the well, in the caravan, in the house with the locked doors, at the granary, or in the private council of the brothers (12:80), and the sūrah has been reporting, all the way through, things that only an eyewitness or an informant could know: the resolve the brothers took among themselves ("when they settled their affair"), the thought Yūsuf hid and did not voice (12:77), the disclosure made to Benjamin alone ("thus did your Lord choose you," 12:6). Our source states the argument plainly: the verse "argues for the miraculous nature of the Prophet's knowledge, since he was given the unseen details of the story of Joseph without being physically present during its unfolding and, according to Islamic teachings, without learning of it from human teachers or through books," since he is the unlettered prophet (7:157).

**A Formula the Qur'an Uses Elsewhere**

The sentence is not unique; it belongs to a family of the Book's own proofs, in which a narrative of past events is followed by the statement that the reciter was not there. The same construction comes at the end of the story of Maryam: "this is of the accounts of the unseen which We reveal to you; you were not with them when they cast their pens to decide which of them would be Maryam's guardian" (3:44), and again in Hūd, "this is of the accounts of the unseen which We reveal to you, and you were not with them" (11:49). The pattern matters for reading 12:102, because in each case the detail named is one that only participants could have reported — the casting of lots to decide a guardianship, the exchange of blows among a people, the settling of an affair in council. The Qur'an is saying that the unobservable interior of a past event is what its knowledge consists in. A historian can reach the outcome; disclosure reaches the room where the decision was made.

**"They Decided Their Affair and Plotted"**

The last clause puts the whole sūrah's theme of scheming into the mouth of the address. The brothers' "planning" is the same current of words that runs through the chapter — the women's "great" plan that Yūsuf answered (12:28), his own prayer that God avert it (12:33), the answer that God turned it away (12:34), the assertion that God does not guide the scheming of the treacherous (12:52), and the divine counter-devising for Yūsuf (12:76). Here the verb is pointed at the brothers in the very moment of their resolve by the well, which means the final word of the narrative section is the one the chapter has used for every party in it, human and divine. The sūrah's moral logic is contained in that symmetry: plotting is what people do when they cannot see the whole board, and the only plan that succeeds is the one that takes account of everything — hence, in the chapter's own sequence, "We raise in degrees whomsoever We will, and above every possessor of knowledge is a knower" (12:76).

**What the Verse Asks of Its Listeners**

Read as an argument, 12:102 is an unusual piece of theology: a text appealing to the impossibility of its own information. A Meccan and Medinan audience lived next to people who possessed older scriptures and could check the outline of a patriarchal story; the sūrah's claim is not that its account matches theirs — the chapter differs from Genesis at every decisive point, refusing the attempted seduction's outcome as the Bible tells it and refusing the name of the woman, refusing the death of the father in Canaan — but that its access is unmediated. It invites the hearer to weigh what it knows against what its narrator could have learned, and then to draw the conclusion the verse draws. For a modern reader the force of the sentence is at least this: the chapter that has spent a hundred verses on the difference between what people can prove and what is actually happening (a shirt, a bowl, a "finding," a father who smells a boy on the wind) closes by saying that its own knowledge is of the second kind.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:103

> **103** *And most of mankind, however much thou mightest desire, are not believers.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain, for the Last Time**

*Wa-mā aktharu al-nāsi wa-law ḥariṣta ʿalayhi bi-muʾminīn*. The sūrah has said this four times, in a series the reader can hear closing. The first was after the sale of Yūsuf: "and most of the people do not know" (12:21, said at the moment an Egyptian bought a prophet as a slave and told his wife to make his lodging honourable). The second came in the prison sermon, when Yūsuf told two men that judgment belongs to God alone — "that is the upright religion, but most of mankind do not know" (12:40). The third was at the father's foresight about the gates (12:68). And here, after the bowl and the forgiveness, the sentence changes its verb: not "do not know" but "are not believers." The chapter's own theology of belief has been announced in a single word, because the sūrah has spent a hundred verses showing that belief is not a failure of information. Its characters knew things and did not see: the brothers knew the boy was their brother; the women knew Yūsuf's excellence (12:30) and said the worst of him; the king's court knew the dream was uninterpretable and called it a jumble (12:44); and the one man who knew the truth — the father — was told to his face that he was in his ancient error (12:95). "Most of mankind are not believers" is not a complaint about evidence. It is the sūrah's conclusion about the hearts that receive it.

**"However Much You Desire"**

The conditional clause is addressed to the Prophet ﷺ, and its word for desire — *ḥarṣ* — is not ordinary wishing but eagerness, the state of a person who wants a thing so much that he presses on it. The Qur'an knows that condition in him: "it is hard on you what they say; perhaps you will destroy yourself with grief that they do not believe" (18:6), "perhaps you will go away after them, grieving" (26:3), and "a messenger from among yourselves; what you suffer is hard upon him, he is full of concern for you, and with the believers he is compassionate, merciful" (9:128). Our source's note on this verse preserves exactly that context: the Prophet's concern for his people is addressed in these passages, together with the principle that "it is only God who guides and only God who causes people to believe," with its own proofs: "you do not guide whom you love, but God guides whom He wills" (28:56), and, in Yūnus, the doctrine settled by a question — "and if your Lord had willed, whoever is on earth would all believe; will you then compel the people to be believers?" (10:99).

The verse's function in the sūrah is thus consolatory before it is doctrinal. The chapter's final scene was a man whose own family needed a generation of hardship, a planted bowl and an administrative crisis before they could tell the truth to each other; the one who knows what belief costs is not being told that his effort was wasted, but that its outcome was never his to produce. That is also why the verse is placed immediately before 12:104 — "you ask them for no reward for it" — the two lines belong to one instruction: the wage is not your leverage, so the result is not your burden.

**Sentences for the Eager**

Three practical rules are inside the verse. The first is that the desire for people to believe, in a teacher, a parent or a daʿī, becomes counter-productive at the exact point when it turns from care into pressure; the sūrah's own story proves it, since the father's tender authority over his sons did nothing for twenty years and the honest humiliation of the granary did everything. The second is the limit of the messenger's office, which the Book states in the plainest terms elsewhere — "remind them, you are only one who reminds; you are not a taskmaster over them" (88:21-22) — and which this verse grounds in God's exclusive ownership of faith. The third is a mercy to the disappointed: "most of mankind" is a generalisation meant to break the expectation that success is the norm. A man who expects the many to believe will be embittered; a man told in advance that even the eager heart will not move most people is free to keep telling the truth kindly. The chapter's ending will draw the same lesson upward, to the unseen again: help comes when the messengers despair (12:110), and the narrative exists as a lesson for the people of hearts (12:111).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:104

> **104** *And thou askest of them no reward for it; it is naught but a reminder for the worlds.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fee That Is Not Charged**

*Wa-mā tasʾaluhum ʿalayhi min ajr*. The verse repeats, almost letter for letter, a sentence the Qur'an puts in the mouth of the Prophet elsewhere and which our source notes explicitly: at the end of Sūrah al-Anʿām the instruction is "say: I ask of you no reward for it; it is naught but a reminder for the worlds" (6:90), and the same denial of payment runs through the Book as the mark of a messenger's honesty — "I ask you for no wealth, and my reward is only upon God" (Hūd 11:29) — and the rejection of the Meccan slurs, "you are not, by the grace of your Lord, a soothsayer or a madman" (Ṭūr 52:29, in the same passage that refuses the charge that he is a poet waiting for death: "say: wait, for I too am among those who wait," 52:31). The point of mentioning payment here, at the end of a sūrah about a man who administered a national grain reserve, is not accidental: this chapter has been about measure, price, payment, and a cargo of goods returned to its owners (12:59, 65). The one thing that will not be transacted in this economy is the reminder.

Yūsuf's career is the sūrah's illustration. He sold nothing, and he did not let himself be bought: the price of a boy (12:20) was the transaction the chapter opens with, and the verse's "no reward" is the frame's other end — the narrative itself, now being delivered to the Prophet ﷺ, is free, and the messenger is not to make a business of it.

**"It Is Naught but a Reminder for the Worlds"**

*In huwa illā dhikrun li-l-ʿālamīn*. Two things are being said at once. First, what the Qur'an is: *dhikr*, a reminder — the noun of memory, of making present again what a person already knows and has forgotten. The sūrah's whole craft supports the word: it has told a story whose every episode is a case of knowing without seeing (the brothers who "knew" what they did, the father who knew and was called mad, the minister who knew and waited years), and it began by announcing that it was narrating "the best of stories" (12:3), not inventing one. The chapter's ending confirms the opening description by calling the same material a reminder, so that the sūrah's genre is settled inside its own last lines: narrative as admonition, not narrative as entertainment.

Second, whom it is for: "the worlds." The story has been about one family in Canaan and Egypt, with no name given for the woman, no name for the city, no name for the king — an Israelite household in the dress of a court intrigue. At the end the address widens past the people of the book of Yūsuf to everybody, and the sūrah has prepared that too: its God has been called, in the prison sermon, the one to whom "the upright religion" belongs (12:40) and, in the hero's last prayer, "Originator of the heavens and the earth" (12:101); the chapter's closing word for Him is the same one its opening used for the Lord of Yaʿqūb's house. A chapter that ends by telling its listener that the material in its hands belongs to all the worlds has effectively said that a family's reconciliation, a famine's management, and a prophet's patience are public property.

**What the Verse Gives the Preacher**

For the reader who is not the Prophet, the two clauses are still rules. A reminder that is paid for stops being a reminder — the listener begins to suspect the seller, and the seller begins to need the sale. And a reminder that must succeed becomes a burden; the previous verse had already told the eager man that most of mankind will not believe however much he wants it (12:103), and here the compensation: the message is not a commodity whose value is fixed by the number of buyers. The sūrah's practical theology of communication is that one speaks the truth because it is true and the hearers are free, and one's own reward is not in the transaction. That is why the chapter, having shown a man who refused a sin he would have had to buy and who then refused an advantage he could have taken for free (12:23, 79), can end its instructions with the least dramatic of virtues: working without charging, and reminding without owning the result.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:105

> **105** *How many a sign is there in the heavens and on the earth by which they pass; yet they turn away from them!*

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Exclamation, Not a Report**

*Wa-kaʾayyin min āyatin fi al-samāwāti wa-al-arḍi yamurrūna ʿalayhā wa-hum ʿanhā muʿriḍūn*. The phrase *wa-kaʾayyin min* is Arabic's idiom for "and how many a…!", an exclamation of quantity that the Qur'an uses when it wants a listener to imagine an uncounted number: "how many a small band has overcome a great band by God's permission" (2:249), and "how many a prophet fought with him a company of devout men" (3:146). Here the uncountable thing is *āyāt* — signs — and the sentence's emotion is not anger but astonishment: the world is full of evidence, and it is walked past.

The word *āya* in this chapter has been doing quiet work throughout. A torn shirt behind was a sign of innocence in a house where a stronger sign — a man's own word — was ignored (12:26-27); seven lean ears of corn devoured by seven fat ones were a sign read by a prisoner in a cell when the court could not read it at all (12:43-49); a father knew a son was alive from an odour on the wind (12:94), and the shirt that had carried a false accusation carried a healing (12:93, 96). The chapter has, in other words, been teaching its audience what a sign looks like in the middle of an ordinary life: it is not an interruption of the course of events but a configuration of them, legible only to somebody.

**Passing By**

The verb of indifference is a verb of travel: *yamurrūna ʿalayhā*, "they pass over them, by them." The sūrah's whole plot has been a set of journeys — to the well, to Egypt, back to Canaan and again (12:8, 58, 70, 94) — and the same word has described the caravan that carried the cup. In the Qur'an's usage the phrase "passing by" belongs to the ruins and remains of earlier nations, which the traveller crosses without stopping: "did they not travel in the land and see what was the end of those before them?" (40:21), and again in the chapter of the Romans: "did they not travel in the land and see what was the end of those before them? They were stronger than they were, and they turned up the earth and peopled it more than these have peopled it" (30:9). Our source records that note against this verse too: the signs people pass by are, specifically, the vestiges left by past civilisations, "from which those who come later are meant to take a lesson, but instead fail to do so." The verse therefore has a double field: the sky and the earth carry the signs of the present, and the ground of history carries the signs of the past, and human beings move through both without reading either.

The second word of the pair, *muʿriḍūn* — turning away — is the same verb the chapter used for the father in the depth of his sorrow: "he turned away from them and said: oh, how great is my grief for Joseph!" (12:84). The sūrah's ear will catch the collision. The same physical figure — a face turned aside — is a prophet's grief in one verse and a people's culpability in another; what divides them is not the act but the will. Yaʿqūb turned from his sons because he could not argue with them, and God's sign reached him anyway; the people described here turn from the sign itself, and nothing reaches them, because the turning is the point.

**A Verse About Attention**

The verse has no imperative in it, and that is its method. It does not say "look" — it states that people pass and that they turn away, leaving the reader to do the opposite. The theology of *āyāt* in the Qur'an depends on exactly this: signs do not compel, they invite; if they forced assent they would not be signs but proofs in a demonstration, and the Book insists that belief earned by compulsion is worth nothing ("and if your Lord had willed, whoever is on earth would all believe," 10:99). Our source records the commentators' gloss on the verse's diagnosis: human beings "continuously encounter signs and proofs that indicate the being of their Creator as well as His wisdom, power and oneness, yet they do not reflect upon the meaning of these signs; nor do they take a lesson from them." Unreflection, not unavailability, is the disease.

For a modern reader the sentence is a description of an over-seen world. The heavens and the earth are, in our experience, more full of legible structure than at any previous time — the age of the light, the scale of the atom, the record of extinct cities under the plough — and attention is scarcer than information. The verse's claim is that the problem is not the density of signs but the habit of passing: a person can live inside evidence and travel straight through it. The chapter's own cure, stated in its last verses, is the narrative it has just told: a story is a device for making somebody stop, look at one human life, and find in it "a lesson for the people of hearts" (12:111).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:106

> **106** *And most of them believe not in God, save that they ascribe partners unto Him.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Puts Belief and Idolatry in the Same Sentence**

*Wa-mā yuʾminu aktharuhum li-llāhi illā wa-hum mushrikūn*. Read slowly, this is one of the Qur'an's most unsettling statements about religion, because it does not describe unbelievers. It says that most people *do* believe in God — the verb of faith is in the present tense, affirmed — and that this same majority are, in the same breath, associating partners with Him. The sentence's exception ("save that they ascribe partners") is not a qualification of their faith but of its quality: their monotheism is real and incomplete, and the incompleteness is what the Book calls *shirk*. Unbelief, in other words, is not the majority condition of humanity in this verse's account; corrupted belief is. That is why the sūrah pairs it with 12:103, where the diagnosis was "most of mankind are not believers": the two verses describe the same crowd from two sides, and the second is the harder, because the people in 12:106 are religious, and the problem is not that they have no God but that they have company.

Our source records the occasion the commentators give: "it is said that this verse was revealed about the Makkan idolaters, who believed in God but also worshipped other gods alongside Him." The Meccan context is exactly right — the Quraysh's shrine was full of gods while they affirmed a high God who created and sustained — and the sūrah's own prison sermon has already described the mechanism: "you worship apart from Him naught but names that you have named — you and your fathers — for which God has sent down no authority. Judgment belongs to God alone" (12:38-40). Yūsuf's line in the cell and the chapter's last diagnosis are the same sentence at two ends of the story: associates are names, and names have no authority.

**The Weight of the Charge**

The Qur'an treats *shirk* as the one sin whose structure puts it outside the range of automatic pardon: "God does not forgive that partners be ascribed to Him, and He forgives what is besides that for whom He wills" (4:48, repeated at 4:116). The severity is not about God's vanity but about the act's shape: associating a partner is the one wrong that is done to reality itself, a mis-sizing of what is, and it is committed not by the irreligious but by people who need an intermediary they can manage — a saint, a tribe, a ruler, an ancestor, a fortune. The sūrah of Yūsuf has been about precisely that hunger for a manageable intermediary: men who relied on a promise (12:12, 63), on a guarantee (12:72), on a father's authority, on the king's law (12:76) — and each of those had to be broken open so that the only reliance left was the one that holds. When Yaʿqūb says "God is the best of keepers" (12:64) and Yūsuf says "You are my Protector in this world and the Hereafter" (12:101), the chapter is stating the alternative to *shirk* in the positive.

**Idols Without Statues**

Our source extends the verse's range in the way the commentators habitually do: the gods placed beside God "are not limited to physical idols, but can also be other elements that one idolizes, such as one's desires, power, or ideas," and the Book itself makes the first of these identifications: "have you seen the one who takes his desire for his god?" (25:43), with the same diagnosis in the chapter of the Crouching (45:23), where a man is left "in his desire" and God seals what he has chosen. This is the verse's practical edge for people who have never knelt to a statue: whatever a person obeys when obeying it conflicts with God — the appetite, the career, the opinion of a crowd, the ideology that cannot be questioned — is functioning as a partner, and the Qur'an's word for the arrangement is idolatry.

The sūrah's own characters supply the test case. Zulaykhā's household worshipped respectability enough to destroy an innocent man with it (12:18-29); the king's court worshipped the uninterpretable dream enough to leave a prophet in prison for years (12:42, 44-46); the brothers, at the beginning, worshipped their own claim on their father's love (12:8). In each case there was no statue, and in each case the thing they feared or loved most was, in fact, governing. That is why 12:106 belongs at the end of this narrative rather than in a chapter of argument: the story has just shown that belief in God survives even a generation of injustice, and that what destroys people is not disbelief but the second god they keep beside Him — and, by the sūrah's last words, that the remedy is to look at the story and take the lesson (12:111).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:107

> **107** *Do they feel secure from the coming of an overwhelming punishment from God upon them? Or from the coming of the Hour suddenly, while they are unaware?*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question About Safety, in a Chapter About Trust**

*A-fa-amintu an taʾtiya-hum ghāshiyatu ʿadhābi llāhi aw taʾtiya-humu al-sāʿatu baghtatan wa-hum lā yashʿurūn*. The verb at the head of the verse, *amina* — to feel secure, to be safe — is one of the chapter's load-bearing words. The sons used it for their claim to be trusted — "what ails you that you do not trust us (lā taʾmannūnā) with Yūsuf?" (12:11) — and the father turned the same verb into the chapter's central rebuke: "should I entrust him to you as I entrusted his brother to you aforetime?" (12:64: *kamā amanna-ka qablu ʿalā akhīhi*). The family entered Egypt "secure" (*āminīn*, 12:99) because a prophet asked it of God, and now the sūrah's last threat turns the same root against its audience: the sense of safety that is taken without asking is delusion. The chapter has, in other words, spent a hundred verses distinguishing a real security, which is given, from a felt security, which is stolen — and 12:107 is the second kind.

The punishment is named *ghāshiyat*, from a root of covering: the darkness that wraps a person whole, the word used in the last sūrah of the Qur'an for "the night when it closes in" (113:3, *ghāsiq*). Our source records the commentators' two glosses here: a thunderbolt, or "some kind of enveloping punishment" (al-Baghawī) — and the image of a storm-front or a flood-front arriving with no gaps is the point: *ghāshiya* is what you cannot stand outside of. The second alternative, the Hour coming *baghtatan*, "suddenly," belongs to the Book's fixed phrase for the Last Day's arrival and our source's note directs the reader to the verses on the suddenness of the Hour and the heedlessness in which it will find most people (cf. "it will come upon them suddenly and confound them, so they cannot ward it off, nor will they be reprieved," 21:40).

**"While They Are Unaware" — the Sūrah's Oldest Word**

*Wa-hum lā yashʿurūn*. This is the last appearance of a phrase that has been present since the well. When the brothers lifted Yūsuf toward the pit and settled on what they would do, the sūrah says God revealed to the boy: "you will surely tell them of this their affair, while they are unaware" (12:15: *wa-hum lā yashʿurūn*). The verse's final clause here is the same, with the world in place of the brothers. The chapter's structure makes the parallel meaningful: unawareness is not a moral failure of intelligence but the human condition — the brothers were not stupid, they were inside their own grievance; the Meccans were not blind, they were inside their own security. And the revelation at the well came true: a boy who was thrown away as worthless sat in front of those same men and said "do you know what you have done with Yūsuf and his brother, when you were ignorant?" (12:89). To be told "you were unaware" is, in this sūrah, the beginning of faith, because it is the moment a person discovers that his own account of events was not the whole of them.

**The Rhetoric of a Question**

The verse asks; it asserts nothing. The idiom "do they feel secure…?" (a-amintu) is the Qur'an's own standing form for a warning that cannot be delivered as a fact without removing the choice it is meant to protect: "do you feel secure that He who is in heaven would cause the earth to swallow you and then it would surge?" (67:16), and "do you feel secure that He would send against you a storm of stones?" (67:17); our source lists the same construction in 16:45-47 and 17:68-69, and says the verse here is about the idolaters of Makkah. A threat stated as a question does three things at once: it leaves the hearer free, it forces him to formulate his own answer, and it says that the danger is not invisible — only unanswered. The chapter has already proved the point by narrative: every person in this sūrah who felt secure was wrong about the extent of what he knew (a woman who thought the doors were locked, brothers who thought a well would be a grave, a court that thought a slave could not read a king's dream), and the only person who was right about everything was the one who never claimed to be secure but kept saying "God is the best of keepers" (12:64).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:108

> **108** *Say, “This is my way. I call unto God with clear sight—I, and those who follow me. Glory be to God! And I am not among those who ascribe partners unto God.”*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"This Is My Way": A Path, Not a Property**

*Qul hādhihi sabīlī adʿū ila llāhi ʿalā baṣīratin anā wa-man tabiʿanī*. The imperative *qul* — "say" — returns at the sūrah's end, and the sentence it introduces is a self-description in four clauses: a way, a call, a ground, and a disclaimer. The word for the way, *sabīl*, is the road a traveller takes, not a possession or a sect; our source records that the commentators gloss it either as the Prophet's "wont" or as "the religion he brought," and both notes point to the same instruction elsewhere in the Book: "call to the way of your Lord with wisdom and beautiful exhortation" (16:125). A *sabīl* is meant to be walked and, if it is good, to be walked by others — which is why the very next clause says "I, and whoever follows me." The claim of the verse is not that the path is exclusive in the sense of being closed, but that it is definite: this one, not any one.

Notice the humility inside the confidence. He says "this is my way," not "this is God's way," and yet the call is not to himself: *adʿū ila llāh* — "I call to God." The preacher is not the destination; the sūrah has spent its length on a man who refused to be the object of what he had to offer (Yūsuf, who would not let the prisoners trust him for food but only for interpretation, 12:37; who asked the king to reopen a case so that his innocence be known, 12:50-52; and who at the top of the state attributed everything to God's *manna*, 12:90, 101). The verse's "I call to God" is the last of those gestures: the one who is followed points away from himself.

**"With Clear Sight": What the Chapter Has Been About**

*ʿAlā baṣīra*. The word is from seeing — *baṣīra* is insight, the eye's work done by the mind — and our source records the gloss: "by clear sight is meant that by which one can distinguish with certainty between truth and falsehood, namely, certainty" (al-Baghawī); the Book uses the noun in exactly that sense where it says of its own address that it is "insights for mankind, and guidance and mercy for people who are certain" (45:20). That the sūrah should end on a word about sight is the last of its structural ironies. It began with a boy who saw, in a dream, eleven stars prostrating; it told of a father whose inner sight was so sound that he refused a report he had every reason to believe (12:18, 83, 86), and who was called a fool for it (12:95); it recorded his eyes whitening with grief (12:84) and their return when the shirt touched his face (12:96); and it pronounced, at 12:76, that "above every possessor of knowledge is a knower." The faith offered at the end is not belief in the absence of evidence but evidence rightly read: certainty that distinguishes, which is why "clear sight" is stated as the manner of the call rather than as its content. Anyone who has tried to persuade another person of something important knows the alternative: urgency without clarity, conviction that cannot say what it sees.

**"Glory Be to God! And I Am Not of the Associators"**

*Wa-subḥāna llāhi wa-mā anā mina al-mushrikīn*. The chapter's last word about God is the word that puts Him outside whatever people attach to Him — *subḥān*, glory/transcendence, said here immediately after the sentence on following, as if to close the loophole that movements develop: the followers of a man are always in danger of making him a partner. The disclaimer is the sūrah's own theme restated in the first person, because the man whose career the chapter has narrated said the same thing in a prison cell: "I forsake the creed of a people who believe not in God" (12:37), followed by his denial that anything at all may be associated with Him (12:38), and followed it with the sentence both father and son preached, "judgment belongs to God alone" (12:40). The Meccan preacher's closing declaration and the enslaved preacher's opening one are the same words, at the two ends of the story: the religion of Yūsuf and the religion of the Prophet ﷺ are one *sabīl*, which is the sūrah's own definition of what "confirmation of what came before" means (12:111).

Three rules are left in the verse for whoever repeats it. State your way plainly and call to God, not to yourself; keep your ground in evidence rather than in pressure, since a call made "on clear sight" can be examined by the person being called; and end by removing yourself from the company of those who make partners — which includes every hearer who would make an idol of the caller.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:109

> **109** *And We sent none before thee, save men unto whom We sent revelation among the people of the towns. Have they not journeyed upon the earth and observed how those before them fared in the end? And the Abode of the Hereafter is better for those who are reverent. Do you not understand?*

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Answer to an Objection the Sūrah Never Quotes**

*Wa-mā arsalnā min qablika illā rijālan nūḥī ilayhim min ahl al-qurā*. The verse answers a complaint stated elsewhere: "why has not an angel been sent down upon him?" (6:8), and "nothing prevented people from believing when the guidance came to them except that they said: has God sent a human being as a messenger?" (17:94).
