Our source records that "some have said that this verse was revealed as a response to the Makkans' question in 6:8," and it notes the wider cluster in which the same answer appears (6:8-9; 14:10; 17:94; 18:110; 25:7; 41:6): a messenger has to be one of the people he addresses, speaking their language and sharing their condition, or the claim would be that guidance is a matter of nature rather than of address. The same reasoning stands behind the verse's near-twin: "we sent before you only men to whom We revealed, so ask the people of the Reminder, if you do not know" (16:43).

To this the chapter adds its own proof, because it has been arguing the case in narrative for a hundred verses. Its hero is a human being with a body that was sold, desired, jailed, and fed on rumour, and the sūrah's audience has watched prophecy work through a career: a dream read in a cell, an administrative reform during a famine, a family reorganised by an audit. No angel appears in this chapter at all — the only heavenly speech in it is the *waḥy* "We revealed to you" addressed first to the Prophet ﷺ (12:3) and last to the boy in the well (12:15). The verse's claim about the messengers of the past is the sūrah's description of itself.

**"Among the People of the Towns"**

The phrase *ahl al-qurā*, the people of settled towns, has been read by the commentators as more than scenery. Our source records their inference: many understand it to mean that nomads were never chosen as prophets, "since town dwellers were, according to the commentators, more knowledgeable and refined" (Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī), because a message meant for record needs a society with permanence — houses, markets, contracts, archives, and neighbours who will remember. The chapter has been silently making the same point: Egypt is a state of documents, measures, and storehouses (12:49, 55-56), and it is there that a dream is verified against seven years of bookkeeping, and that a boy's innocence can be established by evidence rather than by force, as the torn shirt proves in a household where a witness is called to testify (12:26-27). The alternative to the town, in this sūrah, is the empty wilderness where a boy can be thrown into a pit and nobody sees (12:15) — the place of the brothers' chance. Revelation chooses the place where what is done can be known, and where what is known can be handed on.

The same clause has carried a heavier dispute: whether "men" (*rijālan*) excludes women from prophethood. Our source preserves both positions — the commentators' inference that women were not chosen, and al-Zamakhsharī's counter-reading that the word's work in the verse is to say *human* rather than angelic, since the objection being answered is about a man, not about a sex. The argument is not settled by this verse, and the Book's own usage complicates a rule drawn from it: the same verb of revelation, *awḥā*, is used of God's direction to Mūsā's mother ("and We revealed to the mother of Mūsā: nurse him," 28:7). A reader of 12:109 is on safer ground taking the verse for what it plainly asserts — that heaven's address comes to human beings living among other human beings — than for a taxonomy it was never written to supply.

**Travel, Look, and Weigh**

*A-falam yasīrūna fī al-arḍi fa-yanẓurū kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu man qabla-hum*. The command to travel and observe is the sūrah's second use of the reflex (the first was 12:105's signs people pass without reading), and the phrase is the Book's standing appeal to the record of destroyed societies ("did they not travel in the land and see what was the end of those before them?" 30:9; 40:21). What is added here is the scale against which the past is measured: "the Abode of the Hereafter is better for those who are reverent." Our source notes the delicacy of the wording: the chapter's earlier version of the promise spoke of the *reward* of the Hereafter as better for whoever believes and guards himself (12:57); here it is the *Abode* itself — not a payment at the end of an account, but a place to live in, which is the last word of a sūrah about a family who had to move country to survive. The closing question — "do you not understand?" — is the chapter's final address to the crowd it has described passing, uncomprehending, by the signs.
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:110

> **110** *Till, when the messengers despaired and thought that they were deemed liars, Our Help came unto them, and whosoever We willed was saved. And Our Might shall not be turned back from the guilty people.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence That Makes the Reader Wait**

*Ḥattā idhā-staʾasa al-rusulu wa-ẓannū anna-hum qad kuḍḍhibū jāʾa-hum naṣrunā*. The verse opens with "until," and the grammar does the theology: the clause hangs, unanswered, through the whole first half of the sentence, and the reader is made to carry the suspense of history inside a single period. The messengers despair; they suppose; and only then — the third clause — "Our help came to them." Nothing about the delay is explained, and nothing is apologised for. The sūrah's claim is that the interval between the promise and its arrival is the place where revelation's credibility is tested, and the verse reproduces the test in its syntax.

The despair is real and it is not a failure. The verb *istaʾasa* is the one this chapter has already used twice in a domestic key: the brothers "despaired of" Yūsuf's softening and went off into private counsel (12:80), and their father forbade them to "despair of God's Comfort" (12:87). Now the same verb is applied to messengers about their peoples, and the sūrah's audience is left with its most dangerous-sounding sentence: the ones who despair are not the weak; the difference is only in what is despaired of and in whose hands the case is then left. The Book's parallel for the human reaction is the verse the commentators cite here (our source points to 2:214, 21:83, 26:3): "or did you suppose that you would enter the Garden while there has not yet come upon you the like of those who passed before you? Affliction and hardship touched them, and they were shaken, until the messenger and those with him said: when is God's help? — surely God's help is near" (2:214). What the verse calls despair is, in that telling, the last stretch before the question turns from "will it come" to "when."

**"They Thought That They Had Been Lied To"**

The middle clause, which the received translation renders as "thought that they were deemed liars," is one of the Qur'an's famous cruxes, and the unpointed script preserves two senses. On one reading the messengers supposed that their people had been told lies — that the promise of help had been falsified in the account given to them; on the other, that the people had been lied to by the messengers' own claims, so that the messengers feared the failure of their credibility. Our source's note does not adjudicate the grammar, and neither does the verse; what it does say is that the verb is *ẓanna*, supposition, not *yaqīna*, knowledge. The prophets are described as reaching a state in which they *thought*, and the thought was not knowledge of the outcome. That is the sūrah's final word on the interior life of a mission: the man with revelation in his hands does not have the conclusion in his head, and the chapter's own narrative had already said so of a lesser grief — "it may be that God will bring them to me all together" (12:83), where a father's certainty was framed as hope, and then proved as fact.

**"Our Help Came to Them"**

*Jāʾa-hum naṣrunā fa-nujjiya man nashāʾ*. The help arrives as an act of "We," and the rescue is then expressly limited by a will that is not the messenger's: "whomsoever We willed was saved." The chapter has said the same thing about elevation — "We raise in degrees whomsoever We will" (12:76) — and the correspondence matters: the same divine will that lifts a prophet from a well to a throne decides who, in a destroyed city, walks out alive. Salvation in this sūrah is never the automatic dividend of a mission; Lūṭ's people, Nūḥ's son, and Egypt's starving town are all, in the Book's other tellings, cases where the preacher's presence did not determine the outcome. The verse's second half is the mirror of that freedom: "and Our punishment shall not be turned away from the guilty people" — our source notes the parallel in the prostration chapter, "who does greater wrong than he who is reminded of the signs of his Lord and then turns away from them? Indeed, from the guilty We shall take vengeance" (32:22). A sūrah that has spent its length showing mercy arriving late and complete ends by refusing to make mercy mechanical: the help is real, the respite is not a reprieve, and the verdict on the unrepentant is the one the guilty themselves announced when they proposed it for a thief (12:75).

For the chapter's first hearers — a small community in Makkah counting their dead and their converts — the verse is not a doctrine but a permission: to be honest about how far despair can reach, and to keep the outcome where the sūrah has kept it, in the hands of the "Knowing, the Wise" (12:83, 100).
## Sūrah Yūsuf 12:111

> **111** *Certainly in their stories is a lesson for those possessed of intellect. It is not a fabricated account; rather, it is a confirmation of that which came before it, and an elaboration of all things, and a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Word for a Lesson Is a Word for Crossing**

*La-qad kāna fī qaṣaṣihim ʿibratun li-ulī al-albāb*. The sūrah's last verdict on its own material is a description of what a reader is supposed to do with it, and the Arabic word is physical: *ʿibra*, a lesson, is from the root of crossing over — *ʿabara* — the same movement by which a person passes from one bank to another. It answers, exactly, the verse two places before, where people "pass by" (*yamurrūna*) the signs in the heavens and the earth and turn away (12:105). There is a crossing that is merely travelling, and a crossing that is understanding; the sūrah has spent a hundred verses on the difference, and the last one names the faculty for the second: *ulū al-albāb*, the possessors of *albāb*, the plurals of *lubb* — the core, the pit inside a fruit, the kernel of a matter. The Qur'an uses the phrase where it asks its hearers to think about creation and history together: "in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and the alternation of night and day, are signs for those possessed of intellect, who remember God standing and sitting and lying down, and reflect on the creation of the heavens and the earth" (3:190-191). Our source records that the same idiom recurs at 5:100, 38:29 and 39:9, and that "their stories" in this verse is taken by the commentators to mean the stories of the messengers told throughout the Qur'an — the sūrah's closing sentence is about the book as a whole, not only about Yūsuf.

That widening is the chapter's own design. It began by calling itself "the most beautiful of stories" (12:3) and it ends by calling the same material a lesson — and the story's plot has been, from the first verse to the last, a set of crossings: a boy lifted out of a well into a house, a shirt that proved a case from behind, a dream that crossed from a child's sleep to a king's council, a family that crossed a desert to a granary, and a father whose eyes went and came back. Everything the sūrah asks of its audience is that they do what its characters eventually did — read the event they are inside.

**"Not a Fabricated Tale"**

*Mā kāna ḥadīthan yuftarā*. The word for what the narrative is not — *iftirāʾ*, fabrication — is literally a stretching-out of a lie, and the sūrah has been answering the accusation all along: this is a story told as a story, with its sources named, its ignorance admitted ("we bear witness only to what we knew," 12:81), and its unseen left to God ("God knows best concerning that which you describe," 12:77). A tale that knows it is being listened to by people who can check it is not a myth; the sūrah's restraint about names and places, its refusal to settle the question of who said what in Potiphar's house, and its habit of marking transmitted reports as reports are part of the same claim. The chapter's truthfulness is not that it is unverifiable but that it invites the comparison and survives it.

The comparison is the next clause: *lākin taṣdīqa lladhī bayna yaday-hi* — "rather, a confirmation of that which is before it." Our source's note points to the verse of Yūnus where the same claim is made — "and it was not for a human being that God should speak to him except by revelation, or from behind a veil, or by sending a messenger" (42:51 in the Book's own cluster on revelation) — and to 10:37, where the Qur'an describes itself as confirming what came before. In this sūrah the claim has a body. The chapter's version of the Joseph story agrees with Genesis on the shape of a life — favoured son, pit, house of a ruler, false accusation, prison, dreams read, grain stored, recognition, forgiveness — and refuses the details in which the older telling makes a prophet smaller: no stolen blessing among the patriarchs, no deception practised on a dying father by the family that is about to become a nation. A revelation that confirms is also one that corrects, and 12:111 does not pretend otherwise.

**"An Elaboration of All Things, and Guidance and Mercy"**

*Wa-tafṣīla kulli shayʾin wa-hudan wa-raḥmatan li-qawmin yuʾminūn*. The second half of the sentence is the sūrah's most startling claim, and our source supplies its key: the same phrase — an elaboration of everything, guidance and mercy — is said in the Qur'an of the Torah given to Mūsā ("and We wrote for him on the tablets, exhortation and an elaboration of all things," 6:154), and of the Book sent to the Prophet (16:64, 89); the attributes belong to revelation as such, not to one scripture. "Elaboration" (*tafṣīl*) means the work of separating things into their parts, and the chapter has done nothing else: a guarantee drafted with an exception clause (12:66); a fear answered by spacing the brothers across several gates (12:67); a promise of a camel-load and a surety (12:72); a penalty chosen by the accused (12:75); a search ordered so that fairness would be visible (12:76); a reproach thought but not spoken (12:77). Every one of these is a small *tafṣīl* of what a person owes to a promise, and "the elaboration of all things" is, in a sūrah, not encyclopaedic information but the detail of what a human life requires to be lived well.

The last two words are *hudan wa-raḥma* — guidance and mercy — "for a people who believe," and it is exact that the sūrah of Yūsuf should close on mercy. Its penultimate theological sentence said that "most of mankind are not believers" (12:103) and that even those who believe associate partners with God (12:106); the book is therefore not described as a coercion but as a gift whose benefit is conditional on the posture of the receiver, and the condition is the one the brothers finally met at the granary: honesty about what they had done. The chapter's own last word before this verse was the hero's prayer to be taken as a submitter and joined to the righteous (12:101), and its most quoted line about God's character was said at the moment forgiveness became possible — "He is the most Merciful of the merciful" (12:64, 92). "A guidance and a mercy" is the sūrah's summary of itself, and the reader has been shown, verse by verse, what both words mean: guidance that a father recognises in the measure of a sack of corn, and mercy that lets eleven men live down what they did to a brother, in a country where they were needed.
